## Foreword / 序言
===Original===

Only the more fantastic and improbable events contained in this book are true. There is no attempt to set down a literal history of Oklahoma. All the characters, the towns, and many of the happenings contained herein are imaginary. But through reading the scant available records, documents, and histories (including the Oklahoma State Historical Library collection) and through many talks with men and women who have lived in Oklahoma since the day of the Opening, something of the spirit, the color, the movement, the life of that incredible commonwealth has, I hope, been caught. Certainly the Run, the Sunday service in the gambling tent, the death of Isaiah and of Arita Red Feather, the catching of the can of nitroglycerin, many of the shooting affrays, most descriptive passages, all of the oil phase, and the Osage Indian material complete﻿—these are based on actual happenings. In many cases material entirely true was discarded as unfit for use because it was so melodramatic, so absurd as to be too strange for the realm of fiction.
There is no city of Osage, Oklahoma. It is a composite of, perhaps, five existent Oklahoma cities. The Kid is not meant to be the notorious Billy the Kid of an earlier day. There was no Yancey Cravat﻿—he is a blending of a number of dashing Oklahoma figures of a past and present day. There is no Sabra Cravat, but she exists in a score of bright-eyed, white-haired, intensely interesting women of sixty-five or thereabouts who told me many strange things as we talked and rocked on an Oklahoma front porch (tree-shaded now).
Anything can have happened in Oklahoma. Practically everything has.
Edna Ferber

===Chinese===

书中只有那些更为离奇、更不可信的事件才是真实的。本书无意记下一部字面上的俄克拉何马信史。书中所有人物、城镇以及许多事件均属虚构。但通过披阅现存为数不多的记录、文献和史籍（包括俄克拉何马州立历史图书馆的收藏），通过与许多自“开放”之日起就居住在俄克拉何马的男人女子的多次交谈，那个不可思议的州的精神、色彩、动荡与生活，我希望，总算捉住了一些。当然，“大竞跑”、赌帐帐篷里的礼拜、以赛亚和艾丽塔·红羽之死、接住那罐硝化甘油、许多起枪战、大部分描写性段落、整个石油篇章，以及完整的奥色治印第安人素材——这些都有真实发生的事为据。有许多场合，完全真实的材料因为太像情节剧、荒唐得小说都不敢写而被弃置不用。

俄克拉何马并没有奥色治这么一座城。它也许是五座现存的俄克拉何马城市的合成。“小子”并非指早年间那个恶名昭著的比利小子。世上本没有扬西·克拉瓦特——他是往昔与当今若干个神采飞扬的俄克拉何马人物的混合。世上也没有萨布拉·克拉瓦特，但她存在于十几位目光明亮、白发苍苍、六十五岁上下、极有意思的女人身上——我们在俄克拉何马一座前廊（如今绿树成荫）上一边摇着摇椅一边闲谈时，她们对我讲过许多奇事。

在俄克拉何马，什么事都可能发生过。事实上，什么事都发生过了。

埃德娜·费伯

## I / 一
===Original===

All the Venables sat at Sunday dinner. All those handsome inbred Venable faces were turned, enthralled, toward Yancey Cravat, who was talking. The combined effect was almost blinding, as of incandescence; but Yancey Cravat was not bedazzled. A sun surrounded by lesser planets, he gave out a radiance so powerful as to dim the luminous circle about him.
Yancey had a disconcerting habit of abruptly concluding a meal﻿—for himself, at least﻿—by throwing down his napkin at the side of his plate, rising, and striding about the room, or even leaving it. It was not deliberate rudeness. He ate little. His appetite satisfied, he instinctively ceased to eat; ceased to wish to contemplate food. But the Venables sat hours at table, leisurely shelling almonds, sipping sherry; Cousin Dabney Venable peeling an orange for Cousin Bella French Vian with the absorbed concentration of a sculptor molding his clay.
The Venables, dining, strangely resembled one of those fertile and dramatic family groups portrayed lolling unconventionally at meat in the less spiritual of those Biblical canvases that glow richly down at one from the great gallery walls of Europe. Though their garb was sober enough, being characteristic of the time﻿—1889﻿—and the place﻿—Kansas﻿—it yet conveyed an impression as of purple and scarlet robes enveloping these gracile shoulders. You would not have been surprised to see, moving silently about this board, Nubian blacks in loincloths, bearing aloft golden vessels piled with exotic fruits or steaming with strange pasties in which nightingales’ tongues figured prominently. Blacks, as a matter of fact, did move about the Venable table, but these, too, wore the conventional garb of the servitor.
This branch of the Venable family tree had been transplanted from Mississippi to Kansas more than two decades before, but the midwest had failed to set her bourgeois stamp upon them. Straitened though it was, there still obtained in that household, by some genealogical miracle, many of those charming ways, remotely Oriental, that were of the South whence they had sprung. The midday meal was, more often than not, a sort of tribal feast at which sprawled hosts of impecunious kin, mysteriously sprung up at the sound of the dinner bell and the scent of baking meats.

===Chinese===

星期天的正餐桌上，维纳布尔家的人到齐了。所有那些俊美的、血脉相通的维纳布尔家的脸，都出神地转向正在说话的扬西·克拉瓦特。这合力的一望几乎叫人睜不开眼，像一片白炽；可扬西·克拉瓦特不为所眩。他是一轮太阳，四周环绕着较小的行星，他放出的光焰那样强，竟把他周身那一圈光都罩淡了。

扬西有个叫人窘迫的习惯：一顿饭常常曷然而止——至少对他自己是如此——餐巾往盘边一攞，站起身，在屋里大步走动，甚至干脆走出去。这不是存心无礼。他吃得少。食欲一足，他本能地不再吃；连看也不愿再看食物。可维纳布尔家的人一坐几个钟头，从容地剥杏仁，呷雪利酒；达布尼·维纳布尔表兄给贝拉·法兰奇·维安表姐剥一只椩子，那份专注，像雕塑家揉他的黏土。

维纳布尔家的人用起饭来，活像欧洲大画廊里那些《圣经》巨画上不拘礼法地横陈宴饮的丰肥而富于戏剧性的家族群像——那些画在稍欠灵性的画布里，从高墙上油光光地俯视着你。他们的衣装固然朴素，是当时——一八八九年——当地——堪萨斯——的典型，却给人一种紫袍绛衣笼着这些纤秀肩膀的印象。看见努比亚黑人系着兜裆布，悄没声地在席间走动，高举金盘，堆着异域果子，或冒着热气、里面醒目地摆着夜莺舌头的古怪馅饼，你也不会惊讶。事实上，黑人确实在维纳布尔家的餐桌旁走动，只是这些也穿着仆役的惯常号衣。

维纳布尔家族的这一支，二十多年前从密西西比移植到了堪萨斯，可中西部没能在他们身上打下她那市侩的印记。家道固然艰难了，那户人家却凭着某种谱系的奇迹，保留着许多从他们发源的南方带来的、微带东方色彩的可爱做派。正午这一餐，十有八九是一种部落宴会，满桌瘫坐着成群的穷亲戚，听见开饭铃响、闻到烤肉的香气，便神秘地冒了出来。

===Original===

Unwilling émigrés, war ruined, Lewis Venable and his wife Felice had brought their dear customs with them into exile, as well as the superb mahogany oval at which they now sat, and the war-salvaged silver which gave elegance to the Wichita, Kansas, board. Certainly the mahogany had suffered in transit; and many of their Southern ways, transplanted to Kansas, seemed slightly silly﻿—or would have, had they not been tinged with pathos. The hot breads of the South, heaped high at every meal, still wrought alimentary havoc. The frying pan and the deep-fat kettle (both, perhaps, as much as anything responsible for the tragedy of ’64) still spattered their deadly fusillade in this household. Indeed, the creamy pallor of the Venable women, so like that of a magnolia petal in their girlhood, and tending so surely toward the ocherous in middle age, was less a matter of pigment than of liver. Impecunious though the family now was, three or four Negro servants went about the house, soft-footed, slack, charming. “Rest yo’ wrap?” they suggested, velvet voiced and hospitable, as you entered the wide hallway that was at once so bare and so cluttered. And, “Beat biscuit, Miss Adeline?” as they proffered a fragrant plate.
Even that Kansas garden was of another latitude. Lean hounds drowsed in the sun-drenched untidiness of the doorway, and that untidiness was hidden and transformed by a miracle of color and scent and bloom. Here were passion flower and wistaria and even Bougainvillea in season. Honeysuckle gave out its swooning sweetness. In the early spring lilies of the valley thrust the phantom green of their spears up through the dead brown banking the lilac bushes. That coarse vulgarian, the Kansas sunflower, was a thing despised of the Venables. If one so much as showed its broad face among the scented élégantes of that garden it suffered instant decapitation. On one occasion Felice Venable had been known to ruin a pair of very fine-tempered embroidery scissors while impetuously acting as headsman. She had even been heard to bewail the absence of Spanish moss in this northerly climate. A neighboring midwest matron, miffed, resented this.
“But that’s a parasite! And real creepy, almost. I was in South Carolina and saw it. Kind of floating, like ghosts. And no earthly good.”
“Do even the flowers have to be useful in Kansas?” drawled Felice Venable.

===Chinese===

刘易斯·维纳布尔和他的妻子菲莉丝，这对不甘心的移民、战争的破产者，把他们的好习惯带进了流亡地，也带来了他们此刻围坐的那张精美的椭圆形红木餐桌，和那套从战火里抢救出来的银器——正是它们，给堪萨斯威奇塔的餐桌添了风雅。红木桌在运输中固然受了伤；他们的许多南方做派，移栽到堪萨斯，也显得有几分可笑——若不是染着一层悲怆，本会可笑的。南方那种每餐堆得高高的热面饼，照旧在肠胃里作孽。煎锅和油炸锅（这两样，也许同别的任何东西一样，该对六四年那场悲剧负责）照旧在这户人家里溅着它们致命的弹雨。的确，维纳布尔家女人们那奶油似的白皙——少女时代像木兰花瓣，到中年又那样肯定地转向蜡黄——与其说关乎肤色，不如说关乎肝脏。家道固然穷了，屋里照旧有三四个黑仆出出进进，脚步轻软，懒懒散散，讨人喜欢。“把您的外套脱了吧？”你一走进那间又空旷又堆塞的门厅，他们便这样殷勤地提议，嗓音像丝绒。“来块捶打饼干吧，艾德琳小姐？”他们端上一盘香喷喷的饼干时这样说。

连那座堪萨斯的园子也属于另一个纬度。瘦瘦的猎犬在洒满阳光的乱糟糟的门口打盹，那份乱却被一场色彩、香气和花朵的奇迹掩盖了、点化了。这里有西番莲、紫藤，应季时甚至有叶子花。忍冬放出叫人晕眩的甜香。早春时节，铃兰把它们的矛尖那幽灵似的绿，从紫丁香丛脚下枯死的褐叶里顶出来。堪萨斯的向日葵，那个粗俗的暴发户，是维纳布尔家鄙视的东西。它那宽脸盘只要敢在那园子的香闺名媛中间露一露，立刻问斩。有一回，菲莉丝·维纳布尔一时性起亲刽子手，竟毁掉一把脾气极好的绣花剪刀，这是有案可查的。甚至有人听见她哀叹，这北方气候里没有西班牙苔藓。邻近的一位中西部太太听了气不忿。

“可那是寄生植物呀！简直就是瘆人。我去南卡罗来纳见过。飘飘荡荡的，像鬼。一点用处没有。”

“在堪萨斯，连花也得有用么？”菲莉丝·维纳布尔慢悠悠地说。

===Original===

She was not very popular with the bustling wives of Wichita. They resented her ruffled and trailing white wrappers of cross-barred dimity; her pointed slippers, her arched instep, her indifference to all that went on outside the hedge that surrounded the Venable yard; they resented the hedge itself, symbol of exclusiveness in that open-faced Kansas town. Sheathed in the velvet of Felice Venable’s languor was a sharp-edged poniard of wit inherited from her French forbears, the old Marcys of St. Louis; Missouri fur traders of almost a century earlier. You saw the Marcy mark in the black of her still bountiful hair, in the curve of the brows above the dark eyes﻿—in the dark eyes themselves, so alive in the otherwise immobile face.
As the family now sat at its noonday meal it was plain that while two decades of living in the Middle West had done little to quicken the speech or hasten the movements of Lewis Venable and his wife Felice (they still “you-alled”; they declared to goodness; the eighteenth letter of the alphabet would forever be *ah* to them) it had made a noticeable difference in the younger generation. Up and down the long table they ranged, sons and daughters, sons-in-law and daughters-in-law; grandchildren; remoter kin such as visiting nieces and nephews and cousins, offshoots of this far-flung family. As the more northern-bred members of the company exclaimed at the tale they now were hearing you noted that their vowels were shorter, their diction more clipped, the turn of the head, the lift of the hand less leisurely. In all those faces there was a resemblance, one to the other. Perhaps the listening look which all of them now wore served to accentuate this.
It was late May, and unseasonably hot for the altitude. Then, too, there had been an early pest of moths and June flies this spring. High above the table, and directly over it, on a narrow board suspended by rods from the lofty ceiling sat perched Isaiah, the little black boy. With one hand he clung to the side rods of his precarious roost; with the other he wielded a shoofly of feathery asparagus ferns cut from the early garden. Its soft susurrus as he swished it back and forth was an obbligato to the music of Yancey Cravat’s golden voice. Clinging thus aloft the black boy looked a simian version of one of Raphael’s ceilinged angels.

===Chinese===

她在威奇塔那些忙忙碌碌的太太们中间不太得人心。她们看不惯她那细条纹麻纱的、缀着褶边、拖着下摆的白色家常长袍；看不惯她的尖头便鞋、她高高的足背、她对维纳布尔家院墙外一切世事的不闻不问；她们连那道院墙本身也看不惯——在那座脸面敞开的堪萨斯城里，它正是排他的象征。可菲莉丝·维纳布尔那慵懒的天鹅绒底下，鞘着一柄锋利的短剑：一份从她法兰西先祖——将近一个世纪前圣路易斯的毛皮商人、老马西家——传下来的机智。你在她依然丰厚的黑发里看得出马西家的印记，在黑眼睛上那两弯眉弓里看得出——在那双黑眼睛本身里更是，它们在那张惯常纹丝不动的脸上那样活泛。

此刻一家人正用正餐。看得出，在中西部住了二十年，对刘易斯·维纳布尔和妻子菲莉丝的谈吐举止没起什么催促作用（他们照旧“尤奥尔、尤奥尔”地称呼人；照旧“老天爷在上”；字母表上第十八个字母在他们嘴里永远是 *ah*），对年轻一代却大见成效。长桌两侧坐满了儿子女儿、女婿儿媳、孙儿孙女，还有远房的亲戚——来做客的侄男女、表兄妹，这个枝蔓四出的家族的旁支。听见他们此刻听着的这个故事，这伙人中北方血统多一些的惊叫起来，你便注意到：他们的元音短些，吐字利落些，转头的姿势、抬手的动作也不那么雍容了。所有那些脸彼此相像。此刻人人都带着的那种倾听的神色，也许把这相像衬托得更分明了。

时当五月下旬，就这个海拔来说热得反常。再说，今春蛾子和六月蝇也早早地闹了起来。餐桌上方，恰在正中，一块用吊杆从高顶上悬下来的窄板上，蹲着小黑孩以赛亚。他一只手抓着他那摇摇欲坠的栖架的边杆；另一只手挥着一柄蝇拂，是拿早春园子里的文竹羽毛枝扎成的。那蝇拂来回摆动，沙沙的低响，正给扬西·克拉瓦特金嗓子的话音作伴奏。黑孩子这样高高地攀着，活像拉斐尔天花板上的天使换了一只猴子。

===Original===

His round head, fuzzed with little tight tufts, as of woolly astrakhan through which the black of his poll gleamed richly, was cocked at an impish angle the better to catch the words that flowed from the lips of the speaker. His eyes, popping with excitement, were fixed in an entrancement on the great lounging figure of Yancey Cravat. So bewitched was the boy that frequently his hand fell limp and he forgot altogether his task of bestirring with his verdant fan the hot moist air above the food-laden table. An impatient upward glance from Felice Venable’s darting black eyes, together with a sharply admonitory “*Ah*‑saiah!” would set him to swishing vigorously until the enchantment again stayed his arm.
The Venables saw nothing untoward in this remnant of Mississippi feudalism. Dozens of Isaiah’s forbears had sat perched thus, bestirring the air so that generations of Mississippi Venables might the more agreeably sup and eat and talk. Wichita had first beheld this phenomenon aghast; and even now, after twenty years, it was a subject for local tongue waggings.
Yancey Cravat was talking. He had been talking for the better part of an hour. This very morning he had returned from the Oklahoma country﻿—the newly opened Indian Territory where he had made the Run that marked the settling of this vast tract of virgin land known colloquially as the Nation. Now, as he talked, the faces of the others had the rapt look of those who listen to a saga. It was the look that Jason’s listeners must have had, and Ulysses’; and the eager crowd that gathered about Francisco Vasquez de Coronado before they learned that his search for the Seven Cities of Cibolo had been in vain.
The men at table leaned forward, their hands clasped rather loosely between their knees or on the cloth before them, their plates pushed away, their chairs shoved back. Now and then the sudden white ridge of a hard-set muscle showed along the line of a masculine jaw. Their eyes were those of men who follow a game in which they would fain take part. The women listened, a little frightened, their lips parted. They shushed their children when they moved or whimpered, or, that failing, sent them, with a half-tender, half-admonitory slap behind, to play in the sunny dooryard. Sometimes a woman’s hand reached out possessively, remindingly, and was laid on the arm or the hand of the man seated beside her.

===Chinese===

他那颗圆脑袋，覆着一绺绺紧卷的绒毛，像黑色的阿斯特拉罕羔皮，皮缝里透出乌亮，此刻歪成一个顽童的角度，好把说话人唇边流出的字句听得更真。他兴奋得暴突的两眼，出神地钉在扬西·克拉瓦特那伟岸闲适的身影上。这孩子迷得太狠，手里的拂子时时垂下来软了，全忘了拿他那一柄绿拂去搅动满桌菜肴上头湿热的空气。菲莉丝·维纳布尔那双飞梭似的黑眼睛不耐烦地向上一扫，外带一声断喝“以——赛亚！”他便重新猛挥起来，直到那魔法再度停住他的胳膊。

维纳布尔家对这密西西比封建制度的残余不以为奇。以赛亚的几十代先人都这样蹲踞过，替一代代密西西比的维纳布尔家扇凉空气，好让他们更惬意地喝汤、吃饭、说话。威奇塔初见这景象时骇然；即便现在，过了二十年，它还是本地嚼舌头的题目。

扬西·克拉瓦特正说着。他已经说了大半个钟头。就在今天早晨，他从俄克拉何马那边回来了——那新近开放的印第安领地；在那儿，他参加了“大竞跑”，正是它标志着这片俗称“国”的大片处女地的定居。此刻他说着，其余人的脸上便都有了听英雄史诗的那种痴迷。伊阿宋的听众想必是这样，尤利西斯的听众想必也是；还有那群热切地围住弗朗西斯科·巴斯克斯·德·科罗纳多的人——在他们得知他寻找西波拉七城的努力终归徒劳之前。

桌上的男人们前倾着身子，双手松松地交握在膝间或面前的桌布上，盘子推开，椅子后挪。时而，一条咬紧的肌肉沿着哪条汉子的下颌骤然凸起一道白棱。他们的眼睛，是观看一场自己恨不得下场的一局的人的眼睛。女人们听着，有点害怕，嘴唇微启。孩子一动、一哼，她们便嘘他们；嘘不住，就半疼半训地在屁股上拍一巴掌，打发到洒满阳光的前院里去玩。有时一只女人的手伸出去，带着占有和提醒，搭在她身旁那个男人的手臂或手上。

===Original===

“I am here,” the hand’s pressure said. “Your place is with me. Don’t listen to him like that. Don’t believe him. I am your wife. I am safety. I am security. I am comfort. I am habit. I am convention. Don’t listen like that. Don’t look like that.”
But the man would shake off the hand, not roughly, but with absentminded resentment.
Of all that circlet of faces, linked by the enchantment of the tale now being unfolded before them, there stood out lambent as a flame the face of Sabra Cravat as she sat there at table, her child Cim in her lap. Though she, like her mother Felice Venable, was definitely of the olive-skinned type, her face seemed luminously white as she listened to the amazing, incredible, and slightly ridiculous story now being unfolded by her husband. It was plain, too, that in her, as in her mother, the strain of the pioneering French Marcys was strong. Her abundant hair was as black, and her eyes; and the strong brows arched with a swooping curve like the twin scimitars that hung above the fireplace in the company room. Sabra was secretly ashamed of her heavy brows and given to surveying them disapprovingly in her mirror while running a forefinger (slightly moistened by her tongue) along their sable curves. For the rest, there was something more New England than Southern in the directness of her glance, the quick turn of her head, the briskness of her speech and manner. Twenty-one now, married at sixteen, mother of a four-year-old boy, and still in love with her picturesque giant of a husband, there was about Sabra Cravat a bloom, a glow, sometimes seen at that exquisite and transitory time in a woman’s life when her chemical, emotional, and physical makeup attains its highest point and fuses.
It was easy to trace the resemblance, both in face and spirit, between this glowing girl and the sallow woman at the foot of the table. But to turn from her to old Lewis Venable was to find one’s self baffled by the mysteries of paternity. Old Lewis Venable was not old, but aged; a futile, fumbling, gentle man, somewhat hag-ridden and rendered the more unvital by malaria. Face and hands had a yellow ivory quality born of generations subjected to hot breads, lowlands, bad liver, port wine.

===Chinese===

“我在这儿呢，”那手上的压力说。“你的位置在我身边。别那样听他。别信他。我是你的妻子。我是安稳。我是保障。我是舒适。我是习惯。我是规矩。别那样听。别露出那样的神色。”

可那男人会把手甩开——不粗鲁，却带着一种心不在焉的怨气。

在那一圈被眼前展开的故事施了魔法的脸中间，萨布拉·克拉瓦特的脸像一簇火苗般跳出来。她坐在桌边，膝上抱着她的儿子西姆。她虽然同母亲菲莉丝·维纳布尔一样，分明属于橄榄肤色的一型，此刻听丈夫展开这个惊人、难以置信、又略带荒唐的故事时，她的脸却白得发亮。同样分明的是，她身上同她母亲一样，开疆的法兰西马西家的血统很强。她那一头浓发同她的眼睛一样黑；两道浓眉弯成一道俯冲的弧线，像客厅壁炉上方挂着的那对弯刀。萨布拉暗暗为自己的浓眉害臊，常对着镜子不满地打量它们，一边用食指（舌尖略微舔湿过）顺着那两道乌黑描过去。其余方面——直视的目光、敏捷的转头、说话行事的干脆——她身上新英格兰的成分多于南方的。她如今二十一岁，十六岁出嫁，是一个四岁男孩的母亲，仍爱着她那图画般的巨人丈夫；萨布拉·克拉瓦特周身有一种花期般的光彩，一个女人一生中那段精妙而短暂的时光——她的化学的、情感的、身体的构成达到顶点并熔于一体的时候——有时正有这样的光彩。

这张光艳的脸同桌脚下那个蜡黄的女人之间，容貌和精神上的相像都一望即知。可从她转向老刘易斯·维纳布尔，你就得对父系的神秘甘拜下风了。老刘易斯·维纳布尔不算老，算衰老；一个无能的、笨手笨脚的、温和的人，多少被疟疾缠磨得没了生气。脸和手是一种黄象牙的质地，那是好几代的热面饼、洼地、坏肝脏和波尔图酒养出来的。

===Original===

To say nothing of a resident unexplored bullet somewhere between the third and fifth ribs, got at Murfreesboro as a member of Stanford’s Battery, Heavy Artillery, long long before Roentgen had conceived an eye like God’s.
Lewis Venable, in his armchair at the head of the table, was as spellbound as black Isaiah in his high perch above it. Curiously enough, even the boy Cim had listened, or seemed to listen, as he sat in his mother’s lap. Sabra had eaten her dinner over the child’s head in absentminded bites, her eyes always on her husband’s face. She rarely had had to say, “Hush, Cim, hush!” or to wrest a knife or fork or forbidden tidbit from his clutching fingers. Perhaps it was the curiously musical quality of the storyteller’s voice that lulled him. Sabra Venable’s disgruntled suitors had said when she married Yancey Cravat, a stranger, mysterious, out of Texas and the Cimarron, that it was his voice that had bewitched her. They were in a measure right, for though Yancey Cravat was verbose, frequently even windy, and though much that he said was dry enough in actual content, he had those priceless gifts of the born orator, a vibrant and flexible voice, great sweetness and charm of manner, an hypnotic eye, and the power of making each listener feel that what was being said was intended for his ear alone. Something of the charlatan was in him, much of the actor, a dash of the fanatic.
Any tale told by Yancey Cravat was likely to contain enchantment, incredibility (though this last was not present while he was telling it), and a tinge of the absurd. Yancey himself, even at this early time, was a bizarre, glamorous, and slightly mythical figure. No room seemed big enough for his gigantic frame; no chair but dwindled beneath the breadth of his shoulders. He seemed actually to loom more than his six feet two. His black locks he wore overlong, so that they curled a little about his neck in the manner of Booth. His cheeks and forehead were, in places, deeply pitted, as with the pox. Women, perversely enough, found this attractive.
But first of all you noted his head, his huge head, like a buffalo’s, so heavy that it seemed to loll of its own weight. It was with a shock of astonishment that you remarked about him certain things totally at variance with his bulk, his virility, his appearance of enormous power.

===Chinese===

更不必说胸腔里第三和第五根肋骨之间还住着一颗未探明的子弹，那是当年在默弗里斯伯勒，作为斯坦福德重炮连的一员留下的——远远在伦琴构想出那只上帝般的眼睛之前。

刘易斯·维纳布尔坐在桌首的扶手椅里，同高栖在桌上方的黑孩子以赛亚一样听得入魔。说也奇怪，连西姆这孩子坐在母亲膝上，也听着，或者像是听着。萨布拉伏在孩子头顶心不在焉地一口口吃着饭，眼睛始终在丈夫脸上。她难得需要说一声“嘘，西姆，嘘！”也难得从他攥紧的手指里夺刀叉、夺犯禁的美味。也许是讲故事人嗓音里那奇妙的音乐哄住了他。萨布拉·维纳布尔那些悻悻的求婚者当年说，她嫁给扬西·克拉瓦特——一个陌生人，神秘兮兮，来自得克萨斯和西马龙——是被他的嗓音迷住了。这话有几分对。因为扬西·克拉瓦特虽然话多，甚至常常夸夸其谈，虽然他说的许多东西内容上干瘪得很，他却有天生演说家那几样无价的天赋：一副嘹亮而多变的嗓子，风度上极大的甜美与魅力，一只催眠的眼睛，还有一种本领，能使每一个听者都觉得，这话是单单说给他一个人的耳朵听的。他身上有几分江湖气，许多戏子气，还有一撮狂热。

扬西·克拉瓦特讲的任何故事，多半都带着魔力、不可信（不过讲的时候，这不可信并不在场）和一丝荒唐。扬西本人，即使在这样早的时候，也已是传奇般的、光艳照人的、略带神话色彩的人物。任何屋子似乎都容不下他巨大的身架；任何椅子在他那宽阔的肩膀底下都缩成了小椅子。他实际上比他的六英尺二更显高。他的黑发留得过长，在颈边微微卷起，是布斯的做派。他的面颊和额头上，一处一处有深深的痘疤。女人们偏偏觉得这好看。

可你首先注意到的是他的头，他那颗硕大的头，像野牛的头，重得仿佛会凭自身的分量耷拉下来。而当你发现他身上有些东西同他的块头、他的雄悍、他那一身蛮力全然相反时，你会惊讶得一震。

===Original===

His mouth, full and sensual, had still an expression of great sweetness. His eyelashes were long and curling, like a beautiful girl’s, and when he raised his heavy head to look at you, beneath the long black locks and the dark lashes you saw with something of bewilderment that his eyes were a deep and unfathomable ocean gray.
Now, in the course of his story, and under the excitement of it, he left the table and sprang to his feet, striding about and talking as he strode. His step was amazingly light and graceful for a man of his powerful frame. Fascinated, you saw that his feet were small and arched like a woman’s, and he wore, even in this year of 1889, Texas star boots of fine soft flexible calf, very high heeled, thin soled, and ornamented with cunningly wrought gold stars around the tops. His hands, too, were disproportionate to a man of his stature; slim, pliant, white. He used them as he talked, and the eye followed their movements bewitched. For the rest, his costume was a Prince Albert of fine black broadcloth whose skirts swooped and spread with the vigor of his movements; a pleated white shirt, soft and exquisite material; a black string tie; trousers tucked into the gay boot-tops; and, always, a white felt hat, broad-brimmed and rolling. On occasion he simply blubbered Shakespeare, the Old Testament, the *Odyssey*, the *Iliad*. His speech was spattered with bits of Latin, and with occasional Spanish phrases, relic of his Texas days. He flattered you with his fine eyes; he bewitched you with his voice; he mesmerized you with his hands. He drank a quart of whisky a day; was almost never drunk, but on rare occasions when the liquor fumes bested him he would invariably select a hapless victim and, whipping out the pair of mother-o’-pearl-handled six-shooters he always wore at his belt, would force him to dance by shooting at his feet﻿—a pleasing fancy brought with him from Texas and the Cimarron. Afterward, sobered, he was always filled with shame. Wine, he quoted sadly, is a mocker, strong drink is raging. Yancey Cravat could have been (in fact was, though most of America never knew it) the greatest criminal lawyer of his day. It was said that he hypnotized a jury with his eyes and his hands and his voice.

===Chinese===

他的嘴丰满而性感，却仍带着一种极甜的表情。他的睫毛又长又卷，像漂亮姑娘的；当他抬起那沉重的头来看你，在长长的黑发和乌黑的睫毛底下，你有些迷乱地看见，他的眼睛是一种深邃莫测的海灰色。

此刻，讲到故事的紧要处，兴头上来，他离了席，一跃而起，一边踱一边讲。以他那样雄健的身架，他的步子轻巧得出奇、优雅得出奇。你出神地看着，发现他的脚小而足弓高，像女人的脚；而且，即使在这已是一八八九年的年头，他穿的还是得克萨斯星式靴，精致柔软的小牛皮，高跟，薄底，靴口镶着一圈巧手打制的金星。他的手也同他的身量不相称：纤细，柔韧，白皙。说话时他用着它们，眼睛着魔似的追着它们的动作。至于其余的装束，是一件黑色细呢的普林斯·艾伯特长礼服，衣裾随他动作的劲头翻飞张开；一件打褶的白衬衫，料子柔软精美；一条黑绳领带；裤脚掖进花哨的靴筒；还有，永远一顶白毡帽，帽檐宽大而翻卷。兴之所至，他会整段整段地涌出莎士比亚、《旧约》、《奥德赛》、《伊利亚特》。他的谈吐间溅着拉丁文碎句，间或还有几句西班牙语——那是他得克萨斯岁月的遗物。他用漂亮的眼睛奉承你；用嗓子迷住你；用手催眠你。他一天喝一夸特威士忌；几乎从不醉，只是偶尔酒气占了上风，那时他必定挑中一个倒霉鬼，唰地抽出腰间永远佩着的那对珍珠母柄六响枪，朝那人脚边射击，逼他跳舞——这是他从得克萨斯和西马龙带来的一个可爱的雅兴。事后酒醒，他总是满怀羞惭。他悲哀地引用道：酒能使人亵慢，浓酒使人喧嚷。扬西·克拉瓦特本可以成为（事实上已是，尽管多数美国人从不知情）他那个时代最伟大的刑法律师。据说他能用眼睛、手和嗓音催眠陪审团。

===Original===

His law practice yielded him nothing, or less than that, for being sentimental and melodramatic he usually found himself out of pocket following his brilliant and successful defense of some Dodge City dance-hall girl or roistering cowboy whose six-shooter had been pointed the wrong way.
His past, before his coming to Wichita, was clouded with myths and surmises. Gossip said this; slander whispered that. Rumor, romantic, unsavory, fantastic, shifting and changing like clouds on a mountain peak, floated about the head of Yancey Cravat. They say he has Indian blood in him. They say he has an Indian wife somewhere, and a lot of papooses. Cherokee. They say he used to be known as “Cimarron” Cravat, hence his son’s name, corrupted to Cim. They say his real name is Cimarron Seven, of the Choctaw Indian family of Sevens; he was raised in a teepee; a wickiup had been his bedroom, a blanket his robe. It was known he had been one of the early Boomers who followed the banner of the picturesque and splendidly mad David Payne in the first wild dash of that adventurer into Indian Territory. He had dwelt, others whispered, in that sinister strip, thirty-four miles wide and almost two hundred miles long, called No-Man’s-Land as early as 1854, and, later, known as the Cimarron, a Spanish word meaning wild or unruly. Here, in this strange unowned empire without laws and without a government, a paradise for horse thieves, murderers, desperadoes it was rumored he had spent at least a year (and for good reason). They said the evidences of his Indian blood were plain; look at his skin, his hair, his manner of walking. And why did he protest in his newspaper against the government’s treatment of those dirty, thieving, lazy, good-for-nothing wards of a beneficent country! As for his newspaper﻿—its very name was a scandal: The Wichita *Wigwam*. And just below this: “All the news. Any Scandal Not Libelous. Published Once a Week if Convenient.” For that matter, who ever heard of a practising lawyer who ran a newspaper at the same time? Its columns were echoes of his own thundering oratory in the courtroom or on the platform. He had started his paper in opposition to the old established Wichita *Eagle*. Wichita, roaring, said he should have called his sheet the *Rooster*.

===Chinese===

他的律师行当挣不来钱，或者说连钱都不够赔；因为他多情又爱戏，替某个道奇城舞厅姑娘、或者某个六响枪指错了方向的放牛郎打赢了漂亮官司之后，贴钱的通常是他自己。

他来威奇塔之前的过去，笼罩在神话和猜测里。闲话说这个，造谣传那个。流言——浪漫的、龌龊的、荒诞的，像山峰上的云一样变幻不定——在扬西·克拉瓦特头顶上飘来飘去。说他身上有印第安血。说他在哪儿有个印第安老婆，还有一群娃儿。切罗基的。说他从前人称“西马龙”克拉瓦特，所以他儿子的名字，讹成了西姆。说他真名是西马龙·七，乔克托印第安人“七”家的；他是在梯皮里长大的；一间草棚做他的卧房，一条毯子做他的长袍。人所共知，他是早年的“轰鸣者”之一，追随那位气派堂堂、疯得漂亮的戴维·佩恩的大旗，在那一伙冒险家第一次冲进印第安领地的疯狂突进里就有一份。还有人咬耳朵说，他住过那条阴森的地带——三十四英里宽、将近两百英里长，早在一八五四年就叫做“无人地”，后来改叫西马龙，一个西班牙词，意思是野、不驯。就在这里，在这块没有法律、没有政府的奇异的帝国——这个偷马贼、杀人犯、亡命徒的天堂——据传他至少住过一年（而且大有缘故）。说他印第安血的证据明摆着；瞧他的皮肤、他的头发、他走路的做派。还有，他干嘛在他那张报上抗议政府虐待那些又脏、又偷、又懒、一无是处的、这个仁慈国家的被监护人！至于他那张报——报名本身就是桩丑闻：《威奇塔维格沃姆报》。名字底下还有一行：“所有新闻。凡不涉诽谤的丑闻一概照登。方便时每周出一期。”再说，谁听说过一个执业律师同时办报的？它的专栏，不过是他自己在法庭上、讲台上雷霆般雄辩的回声。他办这张报，是跟威奇塔的老字号《鹰报》唱对台。威奇塔吼着说，他该给他的报取名《公鸡报》。

===Original===

The combination law and newspaper office itself was a jumble and welter of pied type, unopened exchanges, boilerplate, legal volumes, paste pots, loose tobacco, old coats, and racing posters. Wichita, professing scorn of the *Wigwam*, read it. Wichita perused his maiden editorial entitled “Shall the Blue Blood of the Decayed South Poison the Red Blood of the Great Middle West?” and saw him, two months later, carry off in triumph as his bride Sabra Venable, daughter of that same Decay; Sabra Venable, whose cerulean stream might have mingled with the more vulgarly sanguine life fluid of any youth in Wichita. In spite of the garden hedge, the parental pride, the arched insteps, the colored servants, and the general air of what-would-you-varlet that pervaded the Venable household at the entrance of a local male a-wooing, Sabra Venable, at sixteen, might have had her pick of the red-blooded lads of Kansas, all the way from Salina to Winfield. Not to mention more legitimate suitors of blue-blooded stock up from the South, such as Dabney Venable himself, Sabra’s cousin, who resembled at once Lafayette and old Lewis, even to the premature silver of his hair, the length of the fine, dolichocephalic, slightly decadent head, and the black stock at sight of which Wichita gasped. When, from among all these eligibles, Sabra had chosen the romantic but mysterious Cravat, Wichita mothers of marriageable daughters felt themselves revenged of the Venable airs. Strangely enough, the marriageable daughters seemed more resentful than ever, and there was a noticeable falling off in the number of young ladies who had been wont to drop around at the *Wigwam* office with notices of this or that meeting or social event to be inserted in the columns of the paper.
During the course of the bountiful meal with which the Venable table was spread Yancey Cravat had eaten almost nothing. Here was an audience to his liking. Here was a tale to his taste. His story, wild, unbelievable, yet true, was of the opening of the Oklahoma country; of a wilderness made populous in an hour; of cities numbering thousands literally sprung up overnight, where the day before had been only prairie, coyotes, rattlesnakes, red clay, scrub oak, and an occasional nester hidden in the security of a weedy draw.
He had been a month absent. Like thousands of others he had gone in search of free land and a fortune.

===Chinese===

这间律师兼报馆的办公室本身，就是一锅大杂烩：倒散的铅字、没拆封的交换报纸、印版、法律书、浆糊罐、散装烟丝、旧外套、赛马海报。威奇塔嘴上鄙夷《维格沃姆报》，读倒读它。威奇塔细读了他的社论处女作——《腐朽南方的蓝血，可容其毒害大中西部的红血？》——又眼看他两个月之后，凯旋般娶走了那位“腐朽”的掌上明珠萨布拉·维纳布尔做新娘；萨布拉·维纳布尔，她那条蔚蓝的血脉，本可以同威奇塔随便哪个小伙子更俗红的血液汇流的。尽管有园中的树篱、父母的骄傲、高高的足弓、有色人种的仆役，还有本地男子一上门求婚、维纳布尔全家便笼罩的那种“你这莽汉想干什么”的气派，萨布拉·维纳布尔十六岁上，原可以从萨莱纳到温菲尔德一路的堪萨斯热血少年里任挑任选。更不必提从南方上来的那些更门当户对的蓝血求婚者，比如达布尼·维纳布尔本人——萨布拉的表哥，他一举像拉斐特、又像老刘易斯，连那过早花白的头发、那颗修长的、长颅型的、略见颓败的脑袋、那副叫威奇塔倒抽冷气的黑领带都像。萨布拉从这一干良人中偏偏挑了浪漫而神秘的克拉瓦特，威奇塔有待嫁女儿的母亲们觉得，维纳布尔家的架子总算遭了报。说来奇怪，待嫁的女儿们反而更记恨了；往常爱往《维格沃姆报》馆跑、送个集会通知或社交启事去登报的小姐们，明显少了。

维纳布尔家的餐桌上这顿丰盛的饭，扬西·克拉瓦特几乎什么也没吃。这是一群对他胃口的听众。这是一个合他口味的故事。他讲的这个狂野而不可信、却真实的故事，是俄克拉何马地方的开放；是一片荒野在一个钟头里变作人烟稠密；是几千人的城市真真的一夜拔地而起——头一天那里还只有草原、郊狼、响尾蛇、红土、矮橡树，和偶尔一个藏身草沟深处、自以为安稳的擅垦农。

他离开了一个月。同成千上万的人一样，他去找免费的土地和财了。

===Original===

Here was an empire to be had for the taking. He talked, as always, in the highfalutin terms of the speaker who is ever conscious of his audience. Yet, fantastic as it was, all that he said was woven of the warp and woof of truth. Whole scenes, as he talked, seemed to be happening before his listeners’ eyes.

===Chinese===

这里是一个帝国，伸手即得。他说话，同往常一样，用的是那种时时惦记着听众的演说家的高调。然而，尽管天花乱坠，他说的一切都由真实的经纬织成。他说着说着，一幕一幕整景，仿佛就在听众眼前发生了。

## II / 二
===Original===

Coat tails swishing, eyes flashing, arms waving, voice soaring.
“Folks, there’s never been anything like it since Creation. Creation! Hell! That took six days. This was done in one. It was History made in an hour﻿—and I helped make it. Thousands and thousands of people from all over this vast commonwealth of ours” (he talked like that) “traveled hundreds of miles to get a bare piece of land for nothing. But what land! Virgin, except when the Indians had roamed it. ‘Lands of lost gods, and godlike men!’ They came like a procession﻿—a crazy procession﻿—all the way to the Border, covering the ground as fast as they could, by any means at hand﻿—scrambling over the ground, pushing and shoving each other into the ditches to get there first. God knows why﻿—for they all knew that once arrived there they’d have to wait like penned cattle for the firing of the signal shot that opened the promised land. As I got nearer the line it was like ants swarming on sugar. Over the little hills they came, and out of the scrub-oak woods and across the prairie. They came from Texas, and Arkansas and Colorado and Missouri. They came on foot, by God, all the way from Iowa and Nebraska! They came in buggies and wagons and on horseback and muleback. In prairie schooners and ox carts and carriages. I saw a surrey, honey colored, with a fringe around the top, and two elegant bays drawing it, still stepping high along those rutted clay roads as if out for a drive in the Presidio. There was a black boy driving it, brass buttons and all, and in the back seat was a dude in a light tan coat and a cigar in his mouth and a diamond in his shirtfront; and a woman beside him in a big hat and a pink dress laughing and urging the horses along the red dust that was halfway up to the wheel spokes and fit to choke you. They had driven like that from Denver, damned if they hadn’t. I met up with one old homesteader by the roadside﻿—a face dried and wrinkled as a nutmeg﻿—who told me he had started weeks and weeks before, and had made the long trip as best he could, on foot or by rail and boat and wagon, just as kindhearted people along the way would pick him up. I wonder if he ever got his piece of land in that savage rush﻿—poor old devil.”
He paused a moment, perhaps in retrospect, perhaps cunningly to whet the appetites of his listeners. He wrung a breathless, “Oh, Yancey, go on! Go on!” from Sabra.

===Chinese===

衣摆翻飞，目光闪闪，手臂挥舞，嗓音越拔越高。
“乡亲们，开天辟地以来就没见过这样的事。开天辟地！见鬼！那还用了六天呢。这个，一天就成了。这是一个钟头里创造的历史——而我参与了创造。成千上万的人，从我们这个泱泱联邦的四面八方”（他说话就是这个调调）“赶了几百英里路，就为白得一块光秃秃的地。可那是怎样的地啊！处子之地，只除了印第安人曾经游猎过。‘失落的神祇与神一样的男儿的土地！’他们像一支长队——一支疯长队——一路涌到边界，凭手边的一切办法拼命赶路——连滚带爬，互相往沟里推搡，只为头一个到。天知道为什么——因为他们全知道，一到那儿，就得像圈里的牛一样等着，等那一声开跑的信号枪，打开那片应许之地。我越走近那道线，越像蚂蚁扑在糖上。他们翻过一座座小丘，钻出矮栎林，穿过草原。他们从得克萨斯来，从阿肯色、科罗拉多、密苏里来。有人靠两条腿走来的，天哪，一路从艾奥瓦、内布拉斯加走来的！他们坐单马马车、大篷车来，骑马来，骑骡子来。坐草原大篷车、牛车、四轮马车来。我还见过一辆四轮敞篷马车，蜜糖色的，顶上一圈流苏，两匹骏丽的枣红马拉着，在车辙纵横的红土路上依旧高抬着步子，像在普雷西迪奥兜风。赶车的是个黑小子，铜纽扣一应俱全；后座上是个花花公子，浅驼色外衣，嘴里叼着雪茄，衬衫前襟镶着钻石；他旁边一个女人，大帽子，粉红裙子，笑着催马赶路，红土的尘没到半只车辐，哈得你要死。他们就是这样一路从丹佛赶来的，千真万确。路边我遇见一个老垦户——一张脸干得像颗肉豆蔻，皱纹纵横——他说他几个星期几个星期前就动身了，一路能怎么走就怎么走，步行、火车、船、马车，全凭沿途好心人捎他一段。不知那老伙计在那场疯狂的抢夺里到底得没得着他那块地——可怜的老鬼。”
他停了停，也许是回味，也许是狡猾地吊听众的胃口。他从萨布拉口中挤出一声屏着气的：“哦，扬西，往下讲！往下讲！”

===Original===

“Well, the Border at last, and it was like a Fourth of July celebration on Judgment Day. The militia was lined up at the boundary. No one was allowed to set foot on the new land until noon next day, at the firing of the guns. Two million acres of land were to be given away for the grabbing. Noon was the time. They all knew it by heart. April twenty-second, at noon. It takes generations of people hundreds of years to settle a new land. This was going to be made livable territory overnight﻿—was made﻿—like a miracle out of the Old Testament. Compared to this, the Loaves and the Fishes and the parting of the Red Sea were nothing﻿—mere tricks.”
“Don’t be blasphemous, Yancey!” spoke up Aunt Cassandra Venable.
Cousin Dabney Venable tittered into his stock.
“A wilderness one day﻿—except for an occasional wandering band of Indians﻿—an empire the next. If that isn’t a modern miracle﻿—”
“Indians, h’m?” sneered Cousin Dabney, meaningly.
“Oh, Dabney!” exclaimed Sabra, sharply. “Why do you interrupt? Why don’t you just listen!”
Yancey Cravat raised a pacifying hand, but the great buffalo head was lowered toward Cousin Dabney, as though charging. The sweetest of smiles wreathed his lips. “It’s all right, Sabra. Let Cousin Dabney speak. And why not? *Un cabello hace sombra.*”
Cousin Dabney’s ivory face flushed a delicate pink. “What’s that, Cravat? Cherokee talk?”
“Spanish, my lad. Spanish.”
A little moment of silent expectation. Yancey did not explain. A plump and pretty daughter-in-law (not a Venable born) put the question.
“Spanish, Cousin Yancey! I declare! Whatever in the world does it mean? Something romantic, I do hope.”
“Not exactly. A Spanish proverb. It means, literally ‘Even a hair casts a shadow.’ ”
Another second’s silence. The pretty daughter-in-law’s face became quite vacuous. “Oh. A hair﻿—but I don’t see what that’s got to do with﻿ ﻿…”
The time had come for Felice Venable to take charge. Her drawling, querulous voice dripped its slow sweetness upon the bitter feud that lay, a poisonous pool, between the two men.
“Well, I must say I call it downright bad manners, I do indeed.

===Chinese===

“好，终于到了边界，那光景就像末日审判日遇上独立纪念日。民兵沿线把守着边界。明天正午鸣枪之前，谁也不许踏进新土地一步。两百万英亩土地，等着人白抓白拿。正午是时刻。人人背得滚瓜烂熟。四月二十二日，正午。一块新土地的开拓，寻常要几代人几百年。这个呢，一夜之间就要变成人住的地方——也真变成了——像《旧约》里的奇迹。比起这个，五饼二鱼、红海分开都算不得什么——不过是小戏法。”
“别亵渎，扬西！”卡珊德拉·文纳尔姑婆开腔了。
达布尼·文纳尔表哥对着他的领巾吃吃窃笑。
“昨天还是荒野——只偶尔有一伙游荡的印第安人——今天就成了帝国。这若不算现代奇迹——”
“印第安人，嗯哼？”达布尼表哥讥诮地说，话里有话。
“哦，达布尼！”萨布拉厉声道，“你打什么岔？好好听不行吗！”
扬西·克拉瓦特举起一只安抚的手，可那颗硕大的野牛头却朝达布尼表哥低了下去，作势要冲。他的唇边漾着最甜的微笑。“没关系，萨布拉。让达布尼表哥说嘛。为什么不让？*Un cabello hace sombra*（一根头发也投影子）。”
达布尼表哥那象牙色的脸泛起一层薄薄的红。“什么话，克拉瓦特？切罗基话？”
“西班牙话，我的伙计。西班牙话。”
片刻无言的期待。扬西不作解释。一位丰满漂亮的儿媳（不是文纳尔家生的）发问了。
“西班牙话，扬西表哥！哎呀！到底是什么意思？但愿是浪漫的什么。”
“谈不上。一句西班牙谚语。字面的意思是，‘连一根头发也投下影子。’”
又是片刻沉默。漂亮儿媳的脸变得全然茫然。“哦。头发——可我不明白这同……”
到了菲莉斯·文纳尔出面主持的时候了。她那拖长的、絮聒的嗓音，把它缓慢的甜腻，滴在两人之间那泊毒潭似的宿怨上。
“我说，这简直是彻头彻尾的没规矩，千真万确。

===Original===

Here we all are with our ears just a-flapping to hear the first sound of the militia guns at high noon on the Border, and here’s Cousin Jouett Goforth all the way up from Louisiana the first time in fifteen years, and just a-quivering with curiosity, and what do we hear but chitchat about Spanish proverbs and shadows.” She broke off abruptly, cast a lightning glance aloft, and in a tone that would have been called a shout had it issued from the throat of any but a Venable, said, “*Ah*‑saiah!”
The black boy’s shoofly, hanging limp from his inert hand, took up its frantic swishing. The air was cleared. The figures around the table relaxed. Their faces again turned toward Yancey Cravat. Yancey glanced at Sabra. Sabra’s lips puckered into a phantom kiss. They formed two words, unseen, unheard by the rest of the company. “Please, darling.”
“*Cede Deo*,” said Yancey, with a little bow to her. Then, with a still slighter bow, he turned to Cousin Dabney. “ ‘Let there be no strife, I pray thee, between thee and me.’ You may not recognize that either, Dabney. It’s from the Old Testament.”
Cousin Dabney Venable ran a finger along the top of his black silk stock, as though to ease his throat.
With a switch of his coat tails Yancey was off again, pausing only a moment at the sideboard to toss off three fingers of Spanish brandy, like burning liquid amber. He patted his lips with his fine linen handkerchief. “I’ve tasted nothing like that in a month, I can tell you. Raw corn whisky fit to tear your throat out. And as for the water! Red mud. There wasn’t a drink of water to be had in the town after the first twenty-four hours. There we were, thousands and thousands of us, milling around the Border like cattle, with the burning sun baking us all day, nowhere to go for shade, and the thick red dust clogging eyes and nose and mouth. No place to wash, no place to sleep, nothing to eat. Queer enough, they didn’t seem to mind. Didn’t seem to notice. They were feeding on a kind of crazy excitement, and there was a wild light in their eyes. They laughed and joked and just milled around, all day and all night and until near noon next day. If you had a bit of food you divided it with someone. I finally got a cup of water for a dollar, after standing in line for three hours, and then a woman just behind me﻿—”
“A woman!” Cousin Arminta Greenwood (of the Georgia Greenwoods).

===Chinese===

我们大伙儿竖着耳朵，就等着听正午时分边界的民兵放头一枪，朱厄特·戈福思表哥十五年头一遭从路易斯安那远道赶来，好奇心憋得直发抖，结果我们听见了什么？西班牙谚语和影子之类的闲磕牙。”她猛地刹住，朝上方飞快一瞥，用一种出自任何旁人喉咙都该算喊叫、唯独出自文纳尔家不算的声调，唤道：“以——赛亚！”
黑小子手里那把软塌塌的驱蝇扇，立刻疯狂地摇了起来。空气清了。围桌的人影松弛下来。一张张脸又转向扬西·克拉瓦特。扬西瞥了萨布拉一眼。萨布拉的唇努成一个无形的吻。它们做了两个字形，全桌无人看见、无人听见：“求你了，亲爱的。”
“*Cede Deo*（退让于神），”扬西说着，朝她微微一躬。随后，带着更浅的一躬，转向达布尼表哥。“‘我们彼此不可相争。’这句话你恐怕也不认得，达布尼。出自《旧约》。”
达布尼·文纳尔表哥一根手指沿黑丝领巾的边抹了抹，像要让喉咙松快些。
扬西衣摆一甩，又开了腔，只在餐具柜旁略停一停，仰脖灌下三指宽的一口西班牙白兰地，像烧着的液态琥珀。他用精美的亚麻手帕按了按唇。“一个月来没尝过这样的东西，告诉你们吧。生玉米威士忌，剌得你喉咙冒血。至于水！红泥汤。头一个二十四小时一过，那镇上就再弄不到一口水喝。我们成千上万的人就在那儿，围着边界打转，像牲口，毒日头烤了我们一整天，没处躲阴凉，厚厚的红尘堵着眼睛鼻子嘴。没地方洗，没地方睡，没东西吃。说来怪，他们倒像不觉得。像没察觉。他们吃的是一种疯狂的兴奋，眼睛里烧着一股野光。他们笑啊，说笑啊，就这么打转，一整天一整夜，直到第二天将近正午。你有一口吃的，就分给旁人一半。我排了三个钟头队，最后一块钱弄到一杯水；这时我身后一个女人——”
“一个女人！”阿敏塔·格林伍德表姨（佐治亚格林伍德家的）。

===Original===

And Sabra Cravat echoed the words in a shocked whisper.
“You wouldn’t believe, would you, that women would go it alone in a fracas like that. But they did. They were there with their husbands, some of them, but there were women who made the Run alone.”
“What kind of women?” Felice Venable’s tone was not one of inquiry but of condemnation.
“Women with iron in ’em. Women who wanted land and a home. Pioneer women.”
From Aunt Cassandra Venable’s end of the table there came a word that sounded like, “Hussies!”
Yancey Cravat caught the word beneath his teeth and spat it back. “Hussies, heh! The one behind me in the line was a woman of forty﻿—or looked it﻿—in a calico dress and a sunbonnet. She had driven across the prairies all the way from the north of Arkansas in a springless wagon. She was like the women who crossed the continent to California in ’49. A gaunt woman, with a weather-beaten face; the terribly neglected skin”﻿—he glanced at Sabra with her creamy coloring﻿—“that means alkali water and sun and dust and wind. Rough hair, and unlovely hands, and boots with the mud caked on them. It’s women like her who’ve made this country what it is. You can’t read the history of the United States, my friends” (all this he later used in an Oklahoma Fourth of July speech when they tried to make him Governor) “without learning the great story of those thousands of unnamed women﻿—women like this one I’ve described﻿—women in mud-caked boots and calico dresses and sunbonnets, crossing the prairie and the desert and the mountains enduring hardship and privation. Good women, with a terrible and rigid goodness that comes of work and self-denial. Nothing picturesque or romantic about them, I suppose﻿—though occasionally one of them flashes﻿—Belle Starr the outlaw﻿—Rose of the Cimarron﻿—Jeannette Daisy who jumped from a moving Santa Fe train to stake her claim﻿—but the others﻿—no, their story’s never really been told. But it’s there, just the same. And if it’s ever told straight you’ll know it’s the sunbonnet and not the sombrero that has settled this country.”
“Talking nonsense,” drawled Felice Venable.
Yancey whirled on his high heels to face her, his fine eyes blazing. “You’re one of them. You came up from the South with your husband to make a new home in this Kansas﻿—”
“I am not!” retorted Felice Venable, with enormous dignity.

===Chinese===

萨布拉·克拉瓦特也倒抽一口气，把这两个字倒了一遍。
“你不会信吧，那种乱局里，女人也会单枪匹马去闯。可她们真去了。有的跟着丈夫，可也有女人，独自一个参加了抢地。”
“哪样的女人？”菲莉斯·文纳尔的口气不是询问，是定罪。
“骨头里有铁的女人。想要土地、想要个家的女人。拓荒的女人。”
卡珊德拉姑婆那头的桌上，滚出一个词，听来像：“野丫头！”
扬西·克拉瓦特把那个词咬在牙间，又啐了回去。“野丫头，嘿！排在我身后的那一个，是个四十岁的女人——或者看着像四十——印花布裙子，遮阳帽。她坐一辆没有弹簧的篷车，从阿肯色北边一路穿过草原赶来。她就像四九年横穿大陆去加利福尼亚的那些女人。瘦削的脸，风霜剥蚀；皮肤糟蹋得不像话”——他瞥了一眼萨布拉那奶油似的肤色——“那是碱水、日头、尘土和风的意思。头发粗糙，手不好看，靴子上结着泥壳。正是她这样的女人，把这个国家造就成今天这样。我的朋友们，你们读美国的历史”（这些话后来他在俄克拉何马的独立纪念日演讲里又用上了，那时他们想推他当州长）“绕不开那成千上万无名女人的伟大故事——就像我说的这位——穿着泥壳靴子、印花布裙、戴遮阳帽的女人，穿过草原、荒漠和高山，忍受艰辛匮乏。好女人，一种由劳作和克己炼出的、严厉而死板的好。她们身上没有什么画意或浪漫——不过偶尔也闪出一个来——强盗贝尔·斯塔尔——锡马龙的罗丝——珍妮特·黛西，从飞驰的圣菲火车上一跃而下、跑去立标占地——可其余的人——不，她们的故事从来没有真正讲过。但它就在那儿，一样。有朝一日照实讲了，你们就会明白：把这个国家安顿下来的，是遮阳帽，不是阔边帽。”
“胡说八道，”菲莉斯·文纳尔拖长了音。
扬西脚跟一旋，转过来对住她，那双好看的眼睛喷着火。“你就是她们中的一个。你跟着丈夫从南方上来，在这堪萨斯安新家——”
“我才不是！”菲莉斯·文纳尔反驳，尊严大得吓人。

===Original===

“And I’ll thank you not to say any such thing. Sunbonnet indeed! I’ve never worn a sunbonnet in my life. And as for my skin and hair and hands, they were the toast of the South, as I can prove by anyone here, all the way from Louisiana to Tennessee. And feet so small my slippers had to be made to order. Calico and muddy boots indeed!”
“Oh, Mamma, Yancey didn’t mean﻿—he meant courage to leave your home in the South and come up﻿—he wasn’t thinking of﻿—Yancey, do get on with your story of the Run. You got a drink of water for a dollar﻿—dear me!﻿—and shared it with the woman in the calico and the sunbonnet﻿ ﻿…”
He looked a little sheepish. “Well, matter of fact, it turned out she didn’t have a dollar to spare, or anywhere near it, but even if she had it wouldn’t have done her any good. The fellow selling it was a rat-faced hombre with one eye and Mexican pants. The trigger finger of his right hand had been shot away in some fracas or other, so he ladled out water with that hand and toted his gun in his left. Bunged up he was, plenty. A scar on his nose, healed up, but showing the marks of where human teeth had bit him in a fight, as neat and clear as a dentist’s signboard. By the time I got to him there was one cup of water left in the bucket. He tipped it while I held the dipper, and it trickled out, just an even dipperful. The last cup of water on the Border. The crowd waiting in line behind me gave a kind of sound between a groan and a moan. The sound you hear a herd of cow animals give, out on the prairie, when their tongues are hanging out for water in the dry spell. I tipped up the dipper and had down a big mouthful﻿—filthy tasting stuff it was, too. Gyp water. You could feel the alkali cake on your tongue. Well, my head went back as I drank, and I got one look at that woman’s face. Her eyes were on me﻿—on my throat, where the Adam’s apple had just given that one big gulp after the first swallow. All bloodshot the whites of her eyes, and a look in them like a dying man looks at a light. Her mouth was open, and her lips were all split with the heat and the dust and the sun, and dry and flaky as ashes. And then she shut her lips a little and tried to swallow nothing, and couldn’t. There wasn’t any spit in her mouth. I couldn’t down another mouthful, parching as I was. I’d have seen her terrible face to the last day of my life.

===Chinese===

“还请你少说这种话。遮阳帽！我生平从没戴过遮阳帽。至于我的皮肤、头发和手，那是整个南方的骄傲，从路易斯安那到田纳西，在座的随便谁都能作证。脚小到拖鞋都得定做。印花布、泥靴子，亏你说得出！”
“哦，妈妈，扬西不是那个意思——他是说，你当年有勇气离开南方的家、北上来——他没想到——扬西，快接着讲你抢地的事呀。你一块钱弄到一口水——天哪！——还分给了那印花布裙子、遮阳帽的女人……”
他显得有点讪讪的。“嗯，实说吧，后来才知道她连一块钱也拿不出，差得远；可就算有，对她也没用。卖水的家伙是个耗子脸的汉子，独眼，穿墨西哥裤。右手扣扳机的指头不知在哪场乱子里给打没了，于是他用那只手舀水，枪搁在左手拎着。浑身是伤，够瞧的。鼻子上一个疤，愈合了，可人牙咬的印子清清楚楚，像牙医的招牌。轮到我时，桶里只剩一杯水。我端着铁皮勺，他把桶一倾，水细细地淌出来，刚满一勺。边界上最后一杯水。我身后排队的众人发出一种介于呻吟和哀号之间的声音。大旱天里，草原上一群牛渴得舌头耷拉出来时，就是那种声音。我仰起勺，灌下一大口——那味道也够呛。石膏水。舌头上都结得出碱垢。我仰头喝的时候，看了一眼那女人的脸。她的眼睛盯着我——盯着我的喉咙，那儿喉结刚咽完头一口又咕咚了一大口。她眼白全是血丝，那眼神像垂死的人望着灯光。她张着嘴，唇全叫暑气、尘土和日头裂开了，干得像灰，起皮。随后她把唇抿了抿，想咽一口空气，咽不下。她嘴里一点唾沫也没有了。我再咽不下第二口，尽管我自己渴得冒烟。她那张可怕的脸，我要记到咽气的日子。

===Original===

So I righted it, and held it out to her and said, ‘Here, sister, take the rest of it. I’m through.’ ”
Cousin Jouett Goforth essayed his little joke. “Are you right sure she was forty, Yancey, and weather-beaten? And that about her hair and boots and hands?”
Cravat, standing behind his wife’s chair, looked down at her; at the fine white line that marked the parting of her thick black hair. With one forefinger he touched her cheek, gently. He allowed the finger to slip down the creamy surface of her skin, from cheek bone to chin. “Dead sure, Jouett. I left out one thing, though.” Cousin Jouett made a sound signifying, ah, I thought so. “Her teeth,” Yancey Cravat went on thoughtfully. “Broken and discolored like those of a woman of seventy. And most of them gone at the side.”
Here Yancey could not resist charging up and down, flirting his coat tails and generally ruining the fine flavor of his victory over the Venable mind. The Venable mind (or the prospect of escaping it) had been one of the reasons for his dash into the wild melee of the Run in the first place. Now he stood surveying these handsome futile faces, and a great impatience shook him, and a flame of rage shot through him, and a tongue of malice flicked him. With these to goad him, and the knowledge of how he had failed, he plunged again into his story to the end.
“I had planned to try and get a place on the Santa Fe train that was standing, steam up, ready to run into the Nation. But you couldn’t get on. There wasn’t room for a flea. They were hanging on the cowcatcher and swarming all over the engine, and sitting on top of the cars. It was keyed down to make no more speed than a horse. It turned out they didn’t even do that. They went twenty miles in ninety minutes. I decided I’d use my Indian pony. I knew I’d get endurance, anyway, if not speed. And that’s what counted in the end.
“There we stood, by the thousands, all night. Morning, and we began to line up at the Border, as near as they’d let us go. Militia all along to keep us back. They had burned the prairie ahead for miles into the Nation, so as to keep the grass down and make the way clearer. To smoke out the Sooners, too, who had sneaked in and were hiding in the scrub oaks, in the draws, wherever they could. Most of the killing was due to them.

===Chinese===

所以我把勺摆正，递给她，说：‘拿着吧，大姐，剩下的归你。我喝好了。’”
朱厄特·戈福思表哥试着说句俏皮话。“你敢肯定她四十岁吗，扬西？风霜满面？头发、靴子、手，也都确有其事？”
克拉瓦特站在妻子椅后，低头看她；看她那头浓密黑发分缝处一线雪白。他用一根食指轻触她的面颊，任手指顺着她奶油似的肌肤，从颧骨滑到下巴。“千真万确，朱厄特。不过，我漏了一样。”朱厄特表哥发出一声“啊，我就知道”的声音。“她的牙，”扬西·克拉瓦特若有所思地接着说，“又断又黄，像七十岁女人的牙。两边的大都掉了。”
说到这里，扬西忍不住又起身来回冲杀，抖着他的衣摆，把他战胜文纳尔头脑的那份妙趣搅了个稀烂。文纳尔的头脑（或者说逃离它的前景），本就是他一头扎进抢地那场疯狂混战的原因之一。此刻他站在那里，端详着这一张张漂亮而无用的脸，一股巨大的不耐摇撼着他，一道怒火穿身而过，一丝恶毒舐了他一下。有这些鞭策着，又记着自己的功败垂成，他一头扎回故事里，一直讲到底。
“我原打算设法挤上那列圣菲火车——就停在那儿，锅炉烧着，随时准备开进印第安领地。可挤不上去。一只跳蚤的地方都没有。人吊在排障器上，爬满机车，坐在车顶上。车被压得只能开马车的速度。后来连这个也没办到。九十分钟走了二十英里。我决定骑我的印第安小马。我知道它至少给我耐力，纵然没有速度。到头来，要紧的正是这个。
“我们成千上万地站在那儿，站了一整夜。天亮了，大家开始在边界排队，排到人家肯放我们到的最近处。民兵一路拦着。他们提前把前面几英里的草原烧了，一直烧进领地，为的是压住草、把路弄清爽些。也为把抢跑客（Sooner）熏出来——他们偷偷溜进去，藏在矮栎林里、坑洼里，哪儿能藏藏哪儿。死人多半是他们的缘故。

===Original===

They had crawled in and staked the land and stood ready to shoot those of us who came in, fair and square, in the Run. I knew the piece I wanted. An old freighters’ trail, out of use, but still marked with deep ruts, led almost straight to it, once you found the trail, all overgrown as it was. A little creek ran through the land, and the prairie rolled a little there, too. Nothing but blackjacks for miles around it, but on that section, because of the water, I suppose, there were elms and persimmons and cottonwoods and even a grove of pecans. I had noticed it many a time, riding the range.”
(H’m! Riding the range! All the Venables made a quick mental note of that. It was thus, by stray bits and snatches, that they managed to piece together something of Yancey Cravat’s past.)
“Ten o’clock, and the crowd was nervous and restless. Hundreds of us had been followers of Payne and had gone as Boomers in the old Payne colonies, and had been driven out, and had come back again. Thousands from all parts of the country had waited ten years for this day when the land-hungry would be fed. They were like people starving. I’ve seen the same look exactly on the faces of men who were ravenous for food.
“Well, eleven o’clock, and they were crowding and cursing and fighting for places near the Line. They shouted and sang and yelled and argued, and the sound they made wasn’t human at all, but like thousands of wild animals penned up. The sun blazed down. It was cruel. The dust hung over everything in a thick cloud, blinding you and choking you. The black dust of the burned prairie was over everything. We were like a horde of fiends with our red eyes and our cracked lips and our blackened faces. Eleven-thirty. It was a picture straight out of hell. The roar grew louder. People fought for an inch of gain on the Border. Just next to me was a girl who looked about eighteen﻿—she turned out to be twenty-five﻿—and a beauty she was, too﻿—on a coal-black thoroughbred.”
“Aha!” said Cousin Jouett Goforth. He was the kind of man who says, “Aha.”
“On the other side was an old fellow with a long gray beard﻿—a plainsman, he was﻿—a six-shooter in his belt, one wooden leg, and a flask of whisky. He took a pull out of that every minute or two. He was mounted on an Indian pony like mine.

===Chinese===

“他们爬进去，立了标，站在那儿，准备枪杀我们这些正大光明来抢地的人。我看中的那块地，我心里有数。一条废弃的老货运道，还留着深深的车辙，找准了路——尽管它荒草没膝——几乎直通那块地。一条小溪穿地而过，草原在那儿也略略起伏。周遭几英里只有矮黑栎，可偏偏那一块，大概因为有水，有榆树、柿子树、三角叶杨，甚至一小片山核桃林。我放牧时留意过它许多回。”
（哼！放牧！文纳尔全家飞快地各自记下一笔。扬西·克拉瓦特的过去，他们就是这样靠零星碎片，勉强拼出个大概的。）
“十点钟，人群焦躁不宁。我们中间有几百人当过佩恩的追随者，进过老佩恩殖民地的布默（Boomer）垦殖队，被赶出来过，又回来过。全国各地来的几千人，为这一天等了十年，等土地饿汉开饭的日子。他们像饿疯了的人。那副神情我在饿饭的人脸上见过，一模一样。
“到十一点，人们挤着、骂着、为靠近那道线的位置打着。他们喊、唱、嚎、争，那声音根本不像人声，倒像几千头圈起来的野兽。日头毒晒。狠透了。尘土浓云似的罩着一切，呛得你瞎、呛得你噎。烧过的草原的黑灰落满一切。我们红着眼睛、裂着嘴唇、黑着面孔，活像一群恶鬼。十一点半。那是从地狱里直接搬出来的一幅画。吼声越来越响。人们为边界上挪一寸地而打。我紧旁边是个姑娘，看着十八上下——后来知道二十五——还是个美人——骑一匹乌黑的纯血马。”
“啊哈！”朱厄特·戈福思表哥说。他就是那种说“啊哈”的人。
“另一边是个灰白长须的老头——平原人——腰里别着六响枪，一条木腿，还有一瓶威士忌。每一两分钟就抿一口。骑的一匹印第安小马，同我的一样。

===Original===

Every now and then he’d throw back his head and let out a yell that would curdle your blood, even in that chorus of fiends. As we waited we fell to talking, the three of us, though you couldn’t hear much in that uproar. The girl said she had trained her thoroughbred for the race. He was from Kentucky, and so was she. She was bound to get her husband and sixty acres, she said. She had to have it. She didn’t say why, and I didn’t ask her. We were all too keyed up, anyway, to make sense. Oh, I forgot. She had on a getup that took the attention of anyone that saw her, even in that crazy mob. The better to cut the wind, she had shortened sail and wore a short skirt, black tights, and a skullcap.”
Here there was quite a bombardment of sound as silver spoons and knives and forks were dropped from shocked and nerveless feminine Venable fingers.
“It turned out that the three of us, there in the front line, were headed down the old freighters’ trail toward the creek land. I said, ‘I’ll be the first in the Run to reach Little Bear.’ That was the name of the creek on the section. The girl pulled her cap down tight over her ears. ‘Follow me,’ she laughed. ‘I’ll show you the way.’ Then the old fellow with the wooden leg and the whiskers yelled out, ‘Whoop‑ee! I’ll tell ’em along the Little Bear you’re both a-comin’.’
“There we were, the girl on my left, the old plainsman on my right. Eleven forty-five. Along the Border were the soldiers, their guns in one hand, their watches in the other. Those last five minutes seemed years long; and funny, they’d quieted till there wasn’t a sound. Listening. The last minute was an eternity. Twelve o’clock. There went up a roar that drowned the crack of the soldiers’ musketry as they fired in the air as the signal of noon and the start of the Run. You could see the puffs of smoke from their guns, but you couldn’t hear a sound. The thousands surged over the Line. It was like water going over a broken dam. The rush had started, and it was devil take the hindmost. We swept across the prairie in a cloud of black and red dust that covered our faces and hands in a minute, so that we looked like black demons from hell. Off we went, down the old freight trail that was two wheel ruts, a foot wide each, worn into the prairie soil.

===Chinese===

隔一会儿他就仰起头嚎一嗓子，叫你血都凉，哪怕在那一片鬼哭狼嚎里。等着的时候，我们三个攀谈起来，尽管那一片鼎沸里听不大清。姑娘说，她那匹纯血马是为这场比赛专门操练的。马是肯塔基的，她也是。她说她铁了心要夺得丈夫和六十英亩地。她非得到不可。她没说为什么，我也没问。反正我们都兴奋过了头，说的话不知所云。哦，忘了说。她那身行头，谁见了谁注目，哪怕在那群疯子里。为了减小风的阻力，她收了帆：短裙、黑色紧身裤、一顶瓜皮帽。”
说到这里，一阵刀叉勺落盘的乱响——文纳尔家的太太小姐们震惊得手指发麻，捏不住银器。
“结果，站在前排的我们三个，恰恰都沿着那条老货运道，奔溪边地去。我说：‘抢地的头一个小熊溪的就是我。’那是那一块地上溪的名字。姑娘把帽檐往耳朵上扣紧。‘跟着我，’她笑，‘我给你们带路。’木腿长须老头随即嚎：‘呜——噫！我沿小熊溪挨个儿告诉他们，你们俩来啦。’
“就这样：姑娘在我左，老平原人在我右。十一点四十五。沿线是士兵，一手枪，一手表。最后那五分钟长得像几年；说来怪，人声渐渐静了，一点声息没有。都在听。最后一分钟是一个永恒。十二点。一声吼腾起来，淹没了士兵对空鸣枪的枪声——那是正午与开跑的信号。看得见枪口的烟，听不见响。几千人涌过那道线。像洪水漫过决口的堤坝。抢地（Run）开始了，落后者遭殃。我们扫过草原，卷在一团黑红尘雾里，一转眼脸上手上糊满，个个像地狱里的黑鬼。冲啊，沿那条老货运道——两道车辙，一辙一尺宽，碾进草原的土里。

===Original===

The old man on his pony kept in one rut, the girl on her thoroughbred in the other, and I on my Whitefoot on the raised place in the middle. That first half mile was almost a neck-and-neck race. The old fellow was yelling and waving one arm and hanging on somehow. He was beating his pony with the flask on his flanks. Then he began to drop behind. Next thing I heard a terrible scream and a great shouting behind me. I threw a quick glance over my shoulder. The old plainsman’s pony had stumbled and fallen. His bottle smashed into bits, his six-shooter flew in another direction, and he lay sprawling full length in the rut of the trail. The next instant he was hidden in a welter of pounding hoofs and flying dirt and cinders and wagon wheels.”
A dramatic pause. Black Isaiah was hanging from his perch like a monkey on a branch. His asparagus shoofly was limp. The faces around the table were balloons pulled by a single string. They swung this way and that with Yancey Cravat’s pace as he strode the room, his Prince Albert coat tails billowing. This way﻿—the faces turned toward the sideboard. That way﻿—they turned toward the windows. Yancey held the little moment of silence like a jewel in the circlet of faces. Sabra Cravat’s voice, high and sharp with suspense, cut the stillness.
“What happened? What happened to the old man?”
Yancey’s pliant hands flew up in a gesture of inevitability. “Oh, he was trampled to death in the mad mob that charged over him. Crazy. They couldn’t stop for a one-legged old whiskers with a quart flask.”
Out of the well-bred murmur of horror that now arose about the Venable board there emerged the voice of Felice Venable, sharp-edged with disapproval. “And the girl. The girl with the black﻿—” Unable to say it. Southern.
“The girl and I﻿—funny, I never did learn her name﻿—were in the lead because we had stuck to the old trail, rutted though it was, rather than strike out across the prairie that by this time was beyond the burned area and was covered with a heavy growth of blue stem grass almost six feet high in places. A horse could only be forced through that at slow pace. That jungle of grass kept many a racer from winning his section that day.
“The girl followed close behind me. That thoroughbred she rode was built for speed, not distance. A race horse, blooded. I could hear him blowing. He was trained to short bursts.

===Chinese===

老头同他的小马占一道辙，姑娘和她的纯血马占另一道，我骑白足（Whitefoot）走中间那道棱。头半英里几乎是并驾齐驱。老头嚎着，挥着一只胳膊，也不知怎么扒住的，拿酒瓶拍着小马的腰。他开始落后了。接着我听见身后一声惨叫和一阵大乱。我回头飞快一瞥。老平原人的小马绊倒摔了。酒瓶摔得粉碎，六响枪飞向另一边，他直挺挺摊在车辙里。下一瞬，他就没进一片乱蹄、飞土、火星和车轮里了。”
戏剧性的一顿。黑以赛亚吊在他的高座上，像猴子挂在树枝上。他那把芦笋叶驱蝇扇垂着不动。满桌的脸像被一根线牵着的气球，随着扬西·克拉瓦特大步在屋里来回而这边那边地摆。往这边——脸转向餐具柜。往那边——脸转向窗。扬西把这片刻的寂静，像一颗宝石嵌在那一圈面孔当中。萨布拉·克拉瓦特的声音又高又尖，割开了寂静。
“后来呢？老头后来怎么样了？”
扬西那双柔韧的手往上一扬，做了一个“势所必然”的手势。“哦，他被那股从他身上碾过去的疯潮活活踩死了。疯掉了。谁会为一个独腿长胡子、拎一瓶夸脱威士忌的老头子停蹄呢。”
文纳尔家餐桌上响起一片教养良好的惊呼；菲莉斯·文纳尔的声音从里面冒出来，锋利，满是责难。“那姑娘呢。那个穿黑——”说不出口。南方人的做派。
“那姑娘同我——说来怪，我始终没问到她名字——一路领先，因为我们咬着那条老道走，尽管车辙累累，总强过穿草原——那一片已经过了焚烧带，长着厚厚一丛蓝茎草，有些地方齐六英尺高。马只能慢慢地硬往里趟。那片草的丛林，那一天断送了多少赛手的标地。
“姑娘紧贴在我身后。她那匹纯血马是速度的种，不是耐力的种。赛马，纯血。我听得见它喘。它是按短程冲刺训的。

===Original===

My Indian pony was just getting his second wind as her horse slackened into a trot. We had come nearly sixteen miles. I was well in the lead by that time, with the girl following. She was crouched low over his neck, like a jockey, and I could hear her talking to him, low and sweet and eager, as if he were a human being. We were far in the lead now. We had left the others behind, hundreds going this way, hundreds that, scattering for miles over the prairie. Then I saw that the prairie ahead was afire. The tall grass was blazing. Only the narrow trail down which we were galloping was open. On either side of it was a wall of flame. Some skunk of a Sooner, sneaking in ahead of the Run, had set the blaze to keep the Boomers off, saving the land for himself. The dry grass burned like oiled paper. I turned around. The girl was there, her racer stumbling, breaking and going on, his head lolling now. I saw her motion with her hand. She was coming. I whipped off my hat and clapped it over Whitefoot’s eyes, gave him the spurs, crouched down low and tight, shut my own eyes, and down the trail we went into the furnace. Hot! It was hell! The crackling and snapping on either side was like a fusillade. I could smell the singed hair on the flanks of the mustang. My own hair was singeing. I could feel the flames licking my legs and back. Another hundred yards and neither the horse nor I could have come through it. But we broke out into the open choking and blinded and half suffocated. I looked down the lane of flame. The girl hung on her horse’s neck. Her skullcap was pulled down over her eyes. She was coming through game. I knew that my land﻿—the piece that I had come through hell for﻿—was not more than a mile ahead. I knew that hanging around here would probably get me a shot through the head, for the Sooner that started that fire must be lurking somewhere in the high grass ready to kill anybody that tried to lay claim to his land. I began to wonder, too, if that girl wasn’t headed for the same section that I was bound for. I made up my mind that, woman or no woman, this was a race and devil take the hindmost. My poor little pony was coughing and sneezing and trembling. Her racer must have been ready to drop. I wheeled and went on.

===Chinese===

她那匹马踱成小跑时，我的印第安小马才刚喘匀第二口气。我们已走了将近十六英里。那时我已遥遥领先，姑娘跟着。她伏得低低的，贴着马颈，像赛马骑师，我听得见她在同马说话，低低的、甜甜的、恳恳的，仿佛它是个人。我们此刻远远在前。旁人都落在后面了，几百人奔这边，几百人奔那边，散开几英里，撒满草原。这时我看见前方草原起火了。高草烧得正旺。只有我们正疾驰的那条窄道还开着。道两边是火墙。哪个抢跑客的臭货，抢先溜进来放的火，好把布默们挡在外头，把地留给他自己。干草烧得像油纸。我回头。姑娘在那儿，她的赛马踉跄着，跌而复起，头已经耷拉。我看见她摆手。她跟上来了。我一把抹下帽子扣住白足的眼，给它一记马刺，缩得低低的、紧紧的，闭上自己的眼，顺着道冲进了火炉。热！那是地狱！两边噼啪炸响，像排枪。我闻得着小马腰上燎着的毛味。我自己的头发也在燎。火舌舔着我的腿和背。再有一百码，人马都出不来。可我们冲出来了，哈着、瞎着、半窒息着，扑进开阔地。我顺着火巷回望。姑娘伏在马颈上。瓜皮帽扣到眼上。她硬是要闯过来。我知道，我的地——我下了地狱换来的那块——就在前面不出一英里。我也知道，在这儿流连多半要挨一枪，放火的抢跑客必定伏在高草里，谁想认领他的地就打死谁。我又开始犯嘀咕：那姑娘奔的，会不会同我看中的是同一块。我拿定了主意：管她女人不女人，这是场比赛，落后者遭殃。我可怜的小马又咳又打喷嚏又发抖。她那匹赛马准快散架了。我一拨马，接着赶。

===Original===

I kept thinking how, when I came to Little Bear Creek, I’d bathe my little mustang’s nose and face and his poor heaving flanks, and how I mustn’t let him drink too much, once he got his muzzle in the water.
“Just before I reached the land I was riding for I had to leave the trail and cut across the prairie. I could see a clump of elms ahead. I knew the creek was near by. But just before I got to it I came to one of those deep gullies you find in the plains country. Drought does it﻿—a crack in the dry earth to begin with, widening with every rain until it becomes a small canyon. Almost ten feet across this one was, and deep. No way around it that I could see, and no time to look for one. I put Whitefoot to the leap and, by God, he took it, landing on the other side with hardly an inch to spare. I heard a wild scream behind me. I turned. The girl on her spent racer had tried to make the gulch. He had actually taken it﻿—a thoroughbred and a gentleman, that animal﻿—but he came down on his knees just on the farther edge, rolled, and slid down the gully side into the ditch. The girl had flung herself free. My claim was fifty yards away. So was the girl, with her dying horse. She lay there on the prairie. As I raced toward her﻿—my own poor little mount was nearly gone by this time﻿—she scrambled to her knees. I can see her face now, black with cinders and soot and dirt, her hair all over her shoulders, her cheek bleeding where she had struck a stone in her fall, her black tights torn, her little short skirt sagging. She sort of sat up and looked around her. Then she staggered to her feet before I reached her and stood there swaying, and pushing her hair out of her eyes like someone who’d been asleep. She pointed down the gully. The black of her face was streaked with tears.
“ ‘Shoot him!’ she said. ‘I can’t. His two forelegs are broken. I heard them crack. Shoot him! For God’s sake!’
“So I off my horse and down to the gully’s edge. There the animal lay, his eyes all whites, his poor legs doubled under him, his flanks black and sticky with sweat and dirt. He was done for, all right. I took out my six-shooter and aimed right between his eyes. He kicked once, sort of leaped﻿—or tried to, and then lay still. I stood there a minute, to see if he had to have another. He was so game that, some way, I didn’t want to give him more than he needed.

===Chinese===

我一路盘算：到了小熊溪，要给我这匹小野马洗洗鼻子、脸，洗洗它那可怜的、起伏不停的腰身；还得记住别让它一头扎进水里猛喝。
“眼看要到我奔的那块地，我不得不离开车道，斜插草原。我望得见前方一丛榆树，知道溪就在近旁。可快到时，遇到一道平原地带常见的深沟。旱出来的——起初是干土上一道裂缝，一场场雨水越冲越宽，终成一条小峡谷。这条差不多十英尺宽，深。我望不见绕的路，也没工夫找。我驱白足起跳，天哪，它跳过去了，落地时余量不到一掌。我听见身后一声狂喊。回头。姑娘骑着她那匹力竭的赛马也试了那道沟。它真跳过去了——好一匹纯血马，一位绅士，那牲口——可前膝磕在对面沿上，一滚，顺着沟壁滑进了沟底。姑娘甩脱了身子。我的标地就在五十码外。她也在五十码外，守着她垂死的马。她躺在草原上。等我朝她奔过去——我这匹可怜的小坐骑这会儿也快完了——她挣扎着爬起来跪着。我至今看得见那张脸：黑糊糊的净是灰烬烟垢和泥，头发披散满肩，脸颊磕在石头上淌着血，黑紧身裤撕破，小短裙往下坠。她半坐起来，四下里望。不等我到，她摇晃着站了起来，站在那儿晃，像刚睡醒的人那样把头发从眼睛上拨开。她朝沟底一指。黑脸上淌着一道道泪。
“‘打死它！’她说。‘我下不去手。两条前腿都断了。我听见折的声儿。打死它！看上帝份上！’
“于是我下了马，走到沟沿。那牲口躺在那儿，眼睛翻白，可怜的腿折在身下，腰身汗泥黏黑地糊着。它是完了，没跑。我拔出六响枪，对准它两眼中间。它蹬了一下，像是挣了一挣——或是想挣——随后就不动了。我站了一分钟，看要不要再补一枪。它那样硬气，不知怎么，我不忍多给它一枪。

===Original===

“Then something made me turn around. The girl had mounted my mustang. She was off toward the creek section. Before I had moved ten paces she had reached the very piece I had marked in my mind for my own. She leaped from the horse, ripped off her skirt, tied it to her riding whip that she still held tight in her hand, dug the whip butt into the soil of the prairie﻿—planted her flag﻿—and the land was hers by right of claim.”
Yancey Cravat stopped talking. There was a moment of stricken silence. Sabra Cravat staring, staring at her husband with great round eyes. Lewis Venable, limp, yellow, tremulous. Felice Venable, upright and quivering. It was she who spoke first. And when she did she was every inch the thrifty descendant of French forbears; nothing of the Southern belle about her.
“Yancey Cravat, do you mean that you let her have your quarter section on the creek that you had gone to the Indian Territory for! That you had been gone a month for! That you had left your wife and child for! That﻿—”
“Now, Mamma!” You saw that all the Venable in Sabra was summoned to keep the tears from her eyes, and that thus denied they had crowded themselves into her trembling voice. “Now, Mamma!”
“Don’t you ‘now Mamma’ me! What of the land that you were to have had! It was bad enough to think of your going to that wilderness, but to﻿—” She paused. Her voice took on a new and more sinister note. “I don’t believe a word of it.” She whirled on Yancey, her black eyes blazing. “Why did you let that trollop in the black tights have that land?”
Yancey regarded this question with considerable judicial calm, but Felice, knowing him, might have been warned by the way his great head was lowered like that of a charging bull buffalo.
“If it had been a man I could have shot him. A good many had to, to keep the land they’d run fairly for. But you can’t shoot a woman.”
“Why not?” demanded the erstwhile Southern belle, sharply.
The Venables, as one man, gave a little jump. A nervous sound, that was half gasp and half shocked titter, went round the Venable board. A startled “Felice!” was wrung from Lewis Venable. “Why, Mamma!” said Sabra.
Yancey Cravat, enormously vital, felt rising within him the tide of irritability which this vitiated family always stirred in him. Something now about their shocked and staring faces, their lolling and graceful forms, roused in him an unreasoning rebellion.

===Chinese===

“这时有什么叫我回过头去。姑娘已骑上了我的小马。她朝溪边那块地去了。没等我走出十步，她已经到了我心里标作自己那块的地皮上。她跳下马，撕下裙子，拴在还攥在手中的马鞭上，把鞭杆往草原的土里一插——插下了她的旗——凭占地的权利，那地就是她的了。”
扬西·克拉瓦特不说了。片刻死样的静。萨布拉·克拉瓦特瞪着、瞪着丈夫，眼睛睁得溜圆。刘易斯·文纳尔，瘫软、蜡黄、发颤。菲莉斯·文纳尔，笔直，哆嗦。先开口的是她。一开口，她就是法国先人精明的后代，浑身没有一点南方美人的影子。
“扬西·克拉瓦特，你的意思是，你把那块溪边的四分之一段地，让给了那个穿紧身裤的野丫头！那是你专程去印第安领地要的地！你为它走了一个月！你为它丢下老婆孩子！你——”
“好了，妈妈！”你看得出，萨布拉身上全部的文纳尔血液都被召来，要把眼泪拦在眼眶里；拦不住，便全挤进她发抖的声音里。“好了，妈妈！”
“你别‘好了妈妈’我！说好归你的地呢！你去那片荒野，想想已经够糟了，如今竟然——”她顿住。嗓音换了一个更阴的调子。“我一个字也不信。”她猛转向扬西，黑眼睛冒火。“你为什么把地让给那个穿黑紧身裤的荡妇？”
扬西以相当法官式的镇定对待这个问题；可菲莉斯若知他，本该从他低伏大头如冲阵野牛的样子得到警告。
“那若是个男人，我可以毙了他。不少人只好这么办，才保住自己规规矩矩抢来的地。可你不能毙一个女人。”
“为什么不能？”昔日南方美人的这一问，又快又利。
文纳尔全家像一个人似的，齐齐一跳。一声半是倒气半是惊笑的动静绕着餐桌走了一圈。刘易斯·文纳尔被逼出一声“菲莉斯！”。“哎呀，妈妈！”萨布拉说。
扬西·克拉瓦特，精力弥满，觉得那股躁怒又涨上来——这个被宠坏的家庭总是激起他这股躁怒。此刻，他们那些惊愕瞪视的脸、那些慵懒优雅的身段里的某种东西，在他心里燃起一场无名的反叛。

===Original===

He suddenly hated them. He wanted to be free of them. He wanted to be free of them﻿—of Wichita﻿—of convention﻿—of smooth custom﻿—of﻿—no, not of her. He now smiled his brilliant sweet smile which alone should have warned Felice Venable. But that intrepid matriarch was not one to let a tale go unpointed.
“I’m mighty pleased, for one, that it turned out as it did. Do you suppose I’d have allowed a daughter of mine﻿—a Venable﻿—to go traipsing down into the wilderness to live among drunken one-legged plainsmen, and toothless scrags in calico, and trollops in tights! Never! It’s over now, and a mighty good thing, too. Perhaps now, Yancey, you’ll stop this ramping up and down and be content to run that newspaper of yours and conduct your law practice﻿—such as it is﻿—with no more talk of this Indian Territory. A daughter of mine in boots and calico and sunbonnet, if you please, a-pioneering among savages. Reared as she was! No, indeed.”
Yancey was strangely silent. He was surveying his fine white hands critically, interestedly, as though seeing them in admiration for the first time﻿—another sign that should have warned the brash Felice. When he spoke it was with utter gentleness.
“I’m no farmer. I’m no rancher. I didn’t want a section of farm land, anyway. The town’s where I belong, and I should have made for the town sites. There were towns of ten thousand and over sprung up in a night during the Run. Wagallala﻿—Sperry﻿—Wawhuska﻿—Osage. It’s the last frontier in America, that new country. There isn’t a newspaper in one of those towns﻿—or wasn’t, when I left. I want to go back there and help build a state out of prairie and Indians and scrub oaks and red clay. For it’ll be a state some day﻿—mark my words.”
“That wilderness a state!” sneered Cousin Dabney Venable. “With an Osage buck or a Cherokee chief for governor, I suppose.”
“Why not? What a revenge on a government that has cheated them and driven them like cattle from place to place and broken its treaties with them and robbed them of their land. Look at Georgia! Look at Mississippi! Remember the Trail of Tears!”
“Ho hum,” yawned Cousin Jouett Goforth, and rose, fumblingly. “This has all been very interesting﻿—odd, but interesting. But if you will excuse me now I shall have my little siesta. I am accustomed after dinner﻿ ﻿…”
Lewis Venable, so long silent, now too reached for his cane and prepared to rise.

===Chinese===

他忽然恨起他们来。他要摆脱他们。他要摆脱他们——摆脱威奇托——摆脱规矩——摆脱圆滑的习俗——摆脱——不，不是摆脱她。这时他绽出他那明亮甜美的微笑——单凭这个就够警告菲莉斯·文纳尔了。可这位无畏的老太太不肯放过补上一刀的机会。
“事情这样收场，我可高兴呢，头一个高兴。你当我肯让我女儿——一个文纳尔家的——颠到荒野里去，同一群醉醺醺的独腿平原汉、没牙的印花布瘦婆、穿紧身裤的荡妇住在一块儿！想也别想！如今算完了，完得好。扬西，这下你该收心了吧，别再东奔西颠，安安分分办你那张报、开你那个律师行——也就那么回事——少提什么印第安领地。我的女儿，穿靴子、印花布、戴遮阳帽，像模像样地去野蛮人里拓什么荒！她那样教养出来的姑娘！不，绝不。”
扬西出奇地沉默。他挑剔而饶有兴味地端详自己那双好看的白手，仿佛头一回以欣赏的眼光看见它们——这本该也警告冒失的菲莉斯。他开口时，语气温和到极点。
“我不会种地。我也不放牧。再说，我要的也不是一段农田。城里才是我的去处，我本该直奔城址去。抢地那阵子，一夜之间冒出了上万人的镇子。瓦加拉拉——斯佩里——瓦胡斯卡——奥塞奇。那个新国家，是美洲最后的边疆。那些镇子里一份报都没有——至少我离开时没有。我要回去，帮着把一个州，从草原、印第安人、矮杭和红土里建起来。它总有一天是一个州——记住我这句话。”
“那片荒野成州！”达布尼·文纳尔表哥嗤笑，“州长就派个奥塞奇公鹿还是切罗基酋长吧。”
“有何不可？对那样一个政府，这该是多妙的报复——骗了他们，像赶牲口一样把他们从一地赶到另一地，撕毁同他们的条约，抢走他们的土地。看看佐治亚！看看密西西比！记住‘血泪之路’！”
“呵——欠，”朱厄特·戈福思表哥打着哈欠站起来，摸索着。“这一切都很有意思——怪，但有意思。不过恕我失陪，我要小睡片刻了。我惯于饭后……”
沉默了许久的刘易斯·文纳尔，此刻也去够手杖，准备起身。

===Original===

He was not quick enough. Felice Venable’s hand, thin, febrile, darted out and clutched his coat sleeve﻿—pressed him back so that he became at once prisoner and judge in his chair at the head of the table.
“Lewis Venable, you heard him! Are you going to sit there? He says he’s going back. How about your daughter?” She turned blazing black eyes on her son-in-law. “Do you mean you’re going back to that Indian country? Do you?”
“I’ll be back there in two weeks. And remember, it’s white man’s country now.”
Sabra stood up, the boy Cim grasped about his middle in her arms, so that he began to whimper, dangling there. Her eyes were startled, enormous. “Yancey! Yancey, you’re not leaving me again!”
“Leaving you, my beauty!” He strode over to her. “Not by a long shot. This time you’re going with me.”
“And I say she’s not!” Felice Venable rapped it out. “And neither are you, my fine fellow. You were tricked out of your land by a trollop in tights, and that ends it. You’ll stay here with your wife and child.”
He shook his great head gently. His voice was dulcet.
“I’m going back to the Oklahoma country; and Sabra and Cim with me.”
Felice whirled on her husband. “Lewis! You can sit there and see your daughter dragged off to be scalped among savages!”
The sick man raised his fine white head. The faded blue eyes were turned on the girl. The child, sensing conflict, had buried his head in her shoulder. “You came with me, Felice, more than twenty years ago, and your mother thought you were going to the wilderness, too. You remember? She cried and made mourning for weeks.”
“Sabra’s different. Sabra’s different.”
The reedy voice of the sick man had the ghostly carrying quality of an echo. You heard it above the women’s shrill clamor. “No, she isn’t, Felice. She’s more like you this minute than you are yourself. She favors those pioneer women Yancey was telling about in the old days. Look at her.”
The Venable eye, from one end of the table to the other, turned like a single orb in its socket toward the young woman facing them with defiance in her bearing. Not defiance, perhaps, so much as resolve. Seeing her, head up, standing there beside her husband, one arm about the child, you saw that what her father said was indeed true.

===Chinese===

他不够快。菲莉斯·文纳尔的手，瘦削、暴着青筋，闪电般探出，攥住他的衣袖——把他按回去，使他登时成了上座椅里的囚徒兼法官。
“刘易斯·文纳尔，你听见了！你就坐在那儿不动？他说他要回去。你女儿怎么办？”她把冒火的黑眼睛转向女婿。“你是说你还要回那个印第安人的地方？说，是不是？”
“两星期内我就回去。记住，如今那是白人的国土了。”
萨布拉站起来，怀里拦腰抱着儿子西姆，孩子吊在那儿哼唧起来。她眼睛惊得老大。“扬西！扬西，你不会又要丢下我！”
“丢下你，我的美人！”他大步走到她跟前。“差得远呢。这一回你同我一起去。”
“我说她不去！”菲莉斯·文纳尔一字一顿。“你也别去，我的好家伙。你叫一个穿紧身裤的荡妇把地骗了去，事情就完了。你就待在这儿，守着老婆孩子。”
他轻轻摇了摇那颗大脑袋。嗓音甜润。
“我要回俄克拉何马去；萨布拉和西姆也去。”
菲莉斯猛转向丈夫。“刘易斯！你坐在那儿，眼看女儿被拖去让野人剥头皮！”
病人抬起那颗好看的白头。褪色的蓝眼睛转向姑娘。孩子嗅出了战火，把头埋进她肩窝。“菲莉斯，二十多年前你也是跟我走的，你母亲那时也当你去的是荒野。记得吗？她哭了好几个星期，守丧似的。”
“萨布拉不一样。萨布拉不一样。”
病人细弱的嗓音，带着回声一般的幽幽的穿透力。人声的尖嚣里，你偏偏听得见它。“不，她一样，菲莉斯。这会儿，她比你本人还像你。她像扬西讲的那些拓荒女人。你看看她。”
文纳尔家的独眼——从桌子这头到那头——像眼窝里一整颗眼球似的，齐刷刷转向那个昂然面对他们的少妇。与其说是昂然，不如说是决意。看她昂着头，站在丈夫身边，一只手臂搂着孩子，你就明白她父亲说的千真万确。

===Original===

She was her mother, the Felice Venable of two decades ago; she was the woman in sunbonnet and calico to whom Yancey had given his cup of water; she was the woman jolting endless miles in covered wagons, spinning in log cabins, cooking over crude fires; she was all women who have traveled American prairie and desert and mountain and plain. Here was that inner rectitude, that chastity of lip, that clearness of eye, that refinement of feature, that absence of allure that comes with cold white fire. The pioneer type, as Yancey had said. Potentially a more formidable woman than her mother.
Seeing something of this Felice Venable said again, more loudly, as though to convince herself, “She’s not to go.”
Looking more than ever like her mother, Sabra met this stubbornly. “But I want to go, Mamma.”
“I forbid it. You don’t know what you want. You don’t know what you’re talking about. I say you’ll stay here with your mother and father in decent civilization. I’ve heard enough. I hope this will serve a lesson to you, Yancey.”
“I’m going back to the Nation,” said Yancey, quite pleasantly.
Sabra stiffened. “I’m going with him.” In her new resolve she must have squeezed the hand of the child Cim, for he gave a little yelp. The combined Venables, nerves on edge, leaped in their chairs and then looked at each other with some hostility.
“And I say you’re not.”
“But I want to go.”
“You don’t.”
Perhaps Sabra had not realized until now how terribly she had counted on her husband’s return as marking the time when she would be free to leave the Venable board, to break away from the Venable clan; no more to be handled, talked over, peered at by the Venable eye﻿—and most of all by the maternal Venable eye. Twenty-one, and the yoke of her mother’s dominance was beginning to gall her. Now, at her own inner rage and sickening disappointment, all the iron in her fused and hardened. It had gone less often to the fire than the older woman’s had. For the first time this quality in her met that of her mother, and the metal of the older woman bent.
“I *will* go,” said Sabra Cravat.
If anyone had been looking at Lewis Venable at that moment (which no one ever thought of doing) he could have seen a ghostly smile momentarily irradiating the transparent ivory face. But now it was Yancey Cravat who held their fascinated eye.

===Chinese===

她就是她母亲，二十年前的菲莉斯·文纳尔；她就是那个戴遮阳帽、穿印花布、接过扬西那一勺水的女人；她是坐大篷车颠簸万里、在木屋里纺线、在土灶前做饭的女人；她是所有走过美国草原、荒漠、高山和平原的女人。这里有那种内在的正直、唇上的贞静、眼中的澄澈、五官的清秀，还有那种随冷白火焰而来的、不假诱媚的气度。拓荒型，正如扬西所说。潜在的，一个比她母亲更厉害的女人。
看出了几分，菲莉斯·文纳尔又开口，声音更大，像要说服自己：“不许她去。”
萨布拉越看越像她母亲，倔强地顶住：“可我要去，妈妈。”
“我不准。你不知道你要什么。你不知道自己在说什么。我说了，你就待在这儿，跟你父母一起，待在体面的文明里。我听够了。扬西，望这事给你一个教训。”
“我要回领地去，”扬西说得相当愉快。
萨布拉绷紧了。“我同他一起去。”新下的决心里，她准是攥紧了小西姆的手，孩子嗷了一声。文纳尔全家神经本就绷着，齐齐在椅子里一跳，随即互相敌意地望了望。
“我说不许去。”
“可我要去。”
“你不许。”
萨布拉也许直到此刻才明白，她曾怎样死死指望着丈夫归来——盼的就是那个能离开文纳尔餐桌、挣脱文纳尔家族的日子：再不由文纳尔的眼睛摆布、议论、盯视——尤其不由文纳尔家母亲的那只眼睛。二十一岁了，母亲统治的轭开始磨得她生疼。此刻，怀着满腔的怒火和翻胃的失望，她全身的铁熔成了一块、变硬了。它进火的次数，到底比老太太的那块少。她这股劲头头一回撞上母亲的，弯下去的，是年长者的那块铁。
“我就是要去，”萨布拉·克拉瓦特说。
若有人此刻看着刘易斯·文纳尔（谁也从没想到看他），就能看见一个幽灵似的微笑，在那张透明的象牙脸上亮了一瞬。可此刻攥住全桌目光的，是扬西·克拉瓦特。

===Original===

With a cowboy yip he swung the defiant Sabra and the boy Cim high in the air in his great arms﻿—tossed them up, so that Sabra screamed, and Cim squealed in mingled terror and delight. It was the kind of horseplay (her word) at which Felice Venable always shuddered. Altogether the three seemed suddenly an outrage in that seemly room with its mahogany and its decanters and its circle of staring high-bred faces.
“Week from tomorrow,” announced Yancey, in something like a shout, so exulting it seemed. “We’ll start on a Monday, fresh and fair. Two wagons. One with the printing outfit﻿—you’ll drive that, Sabra﻿—and one with the household goods and bedding and camp stuff and the rest. We ought to make it in nine days.﻿ ﻿… Wichita!” His glance went round the room, and in that glance you saw not only Wichita! but Venables! “I’ve had enough of it. Sabra, my girl, we’ll leave all the goddamned middle-class respectability of Wichita, Kansas, behind us. We’re going out, by God, to a brand-new, two-fisted, rip-snorting country, full of Injuns and rattlesnakes and two-gun toters and gyp water and desper‑*ah*‑dos! *Whoopee!*”
It was too much for black Isaiah in his perilous perch high above the table. He had long ago ceased to wield his asparagus fan. He had been leaning farther and farther forward, the better to hear and see all of the scene that was spread beneath him. Now, at Yancey’s cowboy whoop, he started violently, his slight hold was loosed, and he fell like a great black grape from the vine directly into the midst of one of Felice Venable’s white and virgin frosted silver cakes.
Shouts, screams, upleapings. Isaiah plucked, white-bottomed, out of the center of the vast pastry. The sudden grayish pallor of his face matched the silver tone of his pants’ seat. Felice Venable, nerves strained to breaking, lifted her hand to cuff him smartly. But the black boy was too quick for her. With the swiftness of a wild thing he scuttled across the table to where Yancey Cravat stood with his wife and child, leaped nimbly to the floor, crept between the man’s legs like a whimpering little dog, and lay there, locked in the safety of Yancey’s great knees.

===Chinese===

他牛仔式地欢呼一声，用那双大手臂把倔强的萨布拉和小西姆高高抡到空中——往上抛，抛得萨布拉尖叫，西姆又怕又乐地吱哇乱叫。这一套胡闹（用菲莉斯的话说）向来叫菲莉斯·文纳尔哆嗦。他们三个在那间讲究的屋子里——红木家具、细颈酒瓶、一圈盯着看的高贵面孔——顿时显得像一桩暴行。
“下下个星期一，”扬西宣布，近乎一声呐喊，欢畅得像呐喊。“星期一动身，精神抖擞，天气正好。两辆马车。一辆装印刷机——那辆你赶，萨布拉——一辆装家用什物、铺盖、宿营的家伙和其余的。九天该到了。……威奇托！”他目光扫了一圈屋子，那一眼里你不只见威奇托，还见文纳尔。“我受够了。萨布拉，我的姑娘，咱们把堪萨斯威奇托这该死的中产阶级体面统统甩在身后。咱们这就出去，天哪，去一个崭新的、抡拳头的、翻天响的国家，满地是印第安人、响尾蛇、别双枪的汉子、石膏水，还有亡——命——徒！*Whoopee*（哇嗬）！”
吊在餐桌上空险座上的黑以赛亚，可受不了这个。他早就不摇他那把芦笋扇了。他一直朝前探、朝前探，好把脚底下这场戏看得听得更全。扬西这一声牛仔吼，吓得他一激灵，本就勉强的把持一松，像一颗熟透的黑葡萄离了藤，笔直落进菲莉斯·文纳尔一只雪白未沾的糖霜蛋糕正中央。
喊声，尖叫，纷纷跳起。以赛亚被人从大点心的正中央拈出来，白屁股露着。他脸上突然的灰白，同他裤子后坐的银色正相配。菲莉斯·文纳尔神经绷到断，抬手就要给他狠狠一记耳光。可黑小子比她快。他野物般地一下蹿过桌面，蹿到扬西·克拉瓦特与妻儿站着的地方，灵巧地跳下地，呜呜咽咽像只小狗，钻进那人两腿之间，卧在那儿，锁进扬西那双大膝盖的安全里。

## III / 三
===Original===

Indians were no novelty to the townspeople of Wichita. Sabra had seen them all her life. At the age of three Cim was held up in his father’s arms to watch a great band of them go by on one of their annual pilgrimages. He played Indian, of course, patting his lips to simulate the Indian yodeling yell. He had a war bonnet made of chicken feathers sewed to the edge of a long strip of red calico.
Twice a year, chaperoned by old General “Bull” Plummer, the Indians swept through the streets of Wichita in their visiting regalia﻿—feathers, beads, blankets, chains﻿—a brilliant sight. Ahead of them and behind them was the reassuring blue of United States army uniforms worn by the Kansas regiment from Fort Riley.
