She screamed horribly, stood transfixed. The boy’s face was a mask of fright. Yancey lashed out once with his whip, the thing struck out, he lashed again, again, again, in a kind of fury. She turned away, sickened. The whip kept up its whistle, its snap. The coiled thing lay in ribbons. Isaiah, though ashen with fright, still had to be forcibly restrained from prowling among the mass for the rattlers which, with some combination of sunset and human saliva, were supposed to be a charm against practically every misfortune known to man. Cim had nightmares all that night and awoke screaming.
Once they saw the figure of a solitary horseman against the sunset sky. Inexplicably the figure dismounted, stood a moment, mounted swiftly, and vanished.
“What was that?”
“That was an Indian.”
“How could you tell?”
“He dismounted on the opposite side from a white man.”
That night it was Sabra who did not sleep. She held the boy tight in her arms. Every snap of a twig, every stamp of a horse’s hoof caused her to start up in terror.
Yancey tried in vain to reassure her. “Indian? What of it? Indians aren’t anything to be scared of. Not any more.”
She remembered something that Mother Bridget had said. “They’re no different. They haven’t changed since Joshua.”
“Since what?” He was very sleepy.
“Joshua.”
He could make nothing of this. He was asleep again, heavily, worn out with the day’s journey.
The wind, at certain periods of the year, blows almost without ceasing in Oklahoma. And when it rains the roads become slithering bogs of greased red dough, so that a wagon will sink and slide at the same time. They had two days of rain during which they plodded miserably, inch by inch. Cim squalled, Isaiah became just a shivering black lump of misery, and Sabra thought of her dimity-hung bed back home in Wichita; of the garden in the cool of the evening; of the family gathered in the dining room; of the pleasant food, the easy talk, the luxurious ease. “Lak yo’ breakfus’ in bed, Miss Sabra?

===Chinese===

有一回，他们看见他用长鞭把一条响尾蛇抽死了。他们卸下马来饮水。扬西提着鞭子，牵马下到浊浊的小溪边，西姆在他身边又蹦又喊。他的两支枪连着枪套放在地上，皮带是他刚从腰上解下来的。萨布拉看见那盘粗粗的盘卷，那颗恶毒的头。也许她感应到它了。她可怕地尖叫，钉在那里。孩子的脸是一张惊骇的面具。扬西的鞭子抽出去一记，那东西窜起来，他又抽，一记，一记，一记，近乎狂怒。她转过脸去，想吐。鞭子的啸声、脆响不断。那盘东西成了一条条。以赛亚吓成灰白，还是得死死拦着，不让他往那堆残骸里扒拉响尾蛇——据传拿它配上点日落和人的唾沫，便是人间一切灾殃的万灵符。那一夜西姆尽做噩梦，尖叫着醒来。
有一回，他们看见落日天幕前一个孤零零的骑者身影。不知怎么，那身影下了马，站了一会儿，又飞快上马，不见了。
“那是什么？”
“那是个印第安人。”
“你怎么看得出？”
“他上马、下马，同白人相反的一边。”
那一夜睡不着的是萨布拉。她把孩子紧紧搂在怀里。每一声枯枝断裂、每一声马蹄踏地，都吓得她惊坐起来。
扬西白费口舌地宽她的心。“印第安人？那又怎样？印第安人没什么可怕的。如今不怕了。”
她想起布里吉特嬷嬷说过的一句话。“他们没变。从约书亚那会儿到现在就没变过。”
“从什么时候？”他已经困极了。
“约书亚。”
他没听出个所以然。他又沉沉睡去，被一天的旅程累坏了。
一年里某几个时节，俄克拉何马的风几乎吹个不停。一下雨，路就成了抹了油的红面团的滑泥塘，车又陷又滑。他们赶上了两天雨，一寸一寸凄凄惶惶地挪。西姆嚎，以赛亚成了哆哆嗦嗦一团黑苦肉，萨布拉想起威奇托家中挂着细麻纱帐的床；想起傍晚凉快时的园子；想起一家人聚在饭厅里；想起可口的饭菜、闲适的谈笑、奢侈的安逸。“想不想早饭送到床上吃啊，萨布拉小姐？下着毛毛雨的早晨。”

===Original===

Mizzly mo’nin’.”
At Pawnee Yancey saw fresh deer tracks. He saddled a horse and was off. They had, before this, caught bass in the streams, and Yancey had shot prairie chicken and quail, and Sabra had fried them delicately. But this was their first promise of big game. Sabra felt no fear at being left alone with the two children. It was midafternoon. She was happy, peaceful. There was about this existence a delightful detachment. Her prim girlhood, which, because she had continued to live in her parents’ household, had lasted into her marriage, was now behind her. Ahead of her lay all manner of unknown terrors and strangeness, but here in the wilderness she was secure. She ruled her little world. Her husband was hers, alone. Her child, too. The little black boy Isaiah was as much her slave as though the Emancipation Proclamation had never been. Here, in the wide freedom of the prairie, she was, temporarily at least, suspended out of the reach of human interference.
Now she welcomed this unexpected halt. She and Isaiah carried water from the creek and washed a few bits of clothes and hung them to dry. She bathed Cim. She heated water for herself and bathed gratefully. She set Isaiah to gathering fuel for the evening meal, while Cim played in the shade of the clump of scrub oak. She was quite serene. She listened for the sound of horse’s hoofs that would announce Yancey’s triumphant return. She could hear Cim as he played under the trees, crooning to himself some snatch of song that Yancey had taught him. Vaguely she began to wonder if Yancey should not have returned by now. She brushed her hair thoroughly, enjoying the motion, throwing it over her head and bending far forward in that contortionistic attitude required by her task. After she had braided it she decided to leave it in a long thick plait down her back. Audaciously she tied it with a bright red ribbon, smiling to think of what Yancey would say. She tidied the wagon. She was frankly worried now. Nothing could happen. Of course nothing could happen. And in another part of her mind she thought that any one of a dozen dreadful things could happen. Indians. Why not? Some wild things in the woods. Broken bones. A fall from his horse. He might lose his way. Suppose she had to spend the night alone here on the prairie with the two children. Here was the little clump of scrub oaks.

===Chinese===

在波尼，扬西发现了新鲜鹿踪。他备上马，走了。此前他们在溪里钓过鲈鱼，扬西打过草原松鸡和鹌鹑，萨布拉把它们炸得精精致致。可这是头一回有望打到大猎物。同两个孩子留在原地，萨布拉并不怕。是下午过半。她快活、安宁。这种生存里有一种可喜的超脱。她那规规矩矩的少女时代——因为一直住在父母家，一直延续到婚后——如今落在身后了。前面等着她的是种种未知的恐怖和生疏，可在这荒野里她是安全的。她统治着的小小世界。丈夫是她的，独占的。孩子也是。小黑孩以赛亚也是她的奴隶，仿佛《解放黑奴宣言》从来没颁发过。在这里，在草原的开阔自由里，她至少暂时悬在人间干预够不着的地方。
此刻她乐得有这场意外的歇脚。她同以赛亚从溪里提水，洗了几件小衣裳，晾上。她给西姆洗澡。她烧了水，自己感激地洗了个澡。她派以赛亚去捡晚饭的柴，西姆在矮栎丛的荫凉里玩。她相当安然。她侧耳等着那宣告扬西凯旋的马蹄声。她听得见西姆在树下玩，独自哼着扬西教他的歌子的片段。她开始隐隐觉得，扬西这会儿该回来了吧。她把头发彻底梳了一遍，享受这个动作，把头发整个甩到前面，深深弯下腰——这个活计必需的诀窍姿势。编好之后，她决定就让它编成一条粗长辫垂在背后。她大胆地拿一根鲜红的缎带扎上，想着扬西见了会说什么，自己先笑了。她把车收拾利落。此刻她是真的担心了。不会出事的。当然不会出事。可她心里的另一处又在想：十几桩可怕的事随便哪桩都出得。印第安人。怎么不会？林子里什么野物都有。断骨头。坠马。他也许迷了路。假如她得带着两个孩子独自在草原上过夜呢。这儿是那一小丛矮杭。

===Original===

The land just beyond showed a series of tiny hillocks that rolled gently away toward the horizon﻿—rolled just enough to conceal whatnot of horror! A head perhaps even now peering craftily over the slope’s edge to see what it could see.
In a sudden panic she stepped out of the wagon with the feeling that she must have her own human things near her﻿—Cim, Isaiah﻿—to talk to. Cim was not there playing with his bits of stone and twigs. He had gone off with Isaiah to gather fuel, though she had forbidden it. Isaiah, his long arms full of dead twigs and small branches, was coming toward the wagon now. Cim was not with him.
“Where’s Cim?”
He dropped his load, looked around. “I lef’ him playin’ by hisself right hyah when Ah go fetch de wood. Ain’ he in de wagon?”
“No. No.”
“Might be he crep’ in de print wagon.”
“Wagon?” She ran to the other wagon, peered inside, called. He was not there.
Together they looked under the wagons, behind the trees. Cim! Cim! Cimarron Cravat, if you are hiding I shall punish you if you don’t come out this minute. A shrill note of terror crept into her voice. She began to run up and down, calling him. She began to scream his name, her voice cracking grotesquely. Cim! Cim! She prayed as she ran, mumblingly. O God, help me find him. O God, don’t let anything happen to him. Dear God, help me find him﻿—Cim! Cim! Cim!
She had heard among pioneer stories that of the McAlastair wagon train crossing the continent toward California in ’49. The Benson party had got separated perhaps a half day’s journey from the front section when scouts brought news of Indians on the trail. Immediately they must break camp and hurry on to join the section ahead for mutual protection. In the midst of the bustle and confusion it was discovered that a child﻿—a boy of three﻿—was missing. The whole party searched at first confidently, then frenziedly, then despairingly. The parents of the missing child had three other small children and another on the way. Every second’s delay meant possible death to every other member of the party. They must push on. They appealed to the mother. “I’ll go on,” she said, and the wagon train wound its dusty way across the plains. The woman sat ashen faced, stony, her eyes fixed in a kind of perpetual horror. She never spoke of the child again.
O God! whimpered Sabra, running this way and that. O God! Oh, Cim! Cim!

===Chinese===

再过去的地面是一串小丘，缓缓滚向地平线——滚得刚好能藏住天知道什么样的恐怖！也许一颗脑袋这会儿正狡猾地贴着坡沿窥探，看能看见什么。
她突然慌了，跨出车来，觉得必须把自己的同类拢在身边——西姆、以赛亚——好说话。西姆不在那儿玩他的小石头小枝子了。他跟以赛亚捡柴去了——虽然她禁止过。以赛亚两条长胳膊抱满枯枝细柴，正朝车走来。西姆不在他身边。
“西姆呢？”
他撂下柴，四下张望。“俺去拿柴的时候，他自个儿在这儿玩的呀。他没在车里？”
“没有。没有。”
“兴许爬进印刷车里去了。”
“车里？”她奔向另一辆车，往里看，喊。他不在。
两人一起看车底下、树后头。西姆！西姆！西马龙·克拉瓦特，你要是藏着，再不出来我就罚你。她的嗓音里爬进了尖利的恐惧。她开始跑来跑去地喊。她开始尖叫他的名字，声音怪诞地劈了。西姆！西姆！她边跑边咕哝着祷告。哦上帝，帮我找到他。哦上帝，别让他出事。亲爱的上帝，帮我找到他——西姆！西姆！西姆！
她听拓荒故事里讲过一支四九年横穿大陆去加利福尼亚的麦卡莱斯特车队。本森那一拨同前队拉开了大约半天的路，这时斥候来报，路上有印第安人。必须立刻拔营，赶上前队合力自卫。忙乱之中发现丢了一个孩子——三岁的男孩。全队先是自信地找，继而疯狂地找，最后绝望地找。丢孩子的父母还有三个小的，肚里还怀着一个。多耽搁一秒，全队每个人都可能没命。必须走。他们去求那位母亲。“我走，”她说，车队便拖着滚滚尘土穿过平原去了。那女人面如死灰，石头一样坐着，眼睛凝着一种永恒的恐怖。她再也没有提起过那孩子。
哦上帝！萨布拉呜咽着，东奔西跑。哦上帝！哦，西姆！西姆！

===Original===

She came to a little mound that dipped suddenly and unexpectedly to a draw. And there, in a hollow, she came upon him, seated before a cave in the side of the hill, the front and roof ingeniously timbered to make a log cabin. One might pass within five feet of it and never find it. Four men were seated about the doorstep outside the rude cabin. Cim was perched on the knee of one of them, who was cracking nuts for him. They were laughing and talking and munching nuts and having altogether a delightful time of it. Sabra’s knees suddenly became weak. She was trembling. She stumbled as she ran toward him. Her face worked queerly. The men sprang up, their hands at their hips.
“The man is cracking nuts for me,” remarked Cim, sociably, and not especially glad to see her.
The man on whose knee he sat was a slim young fellow with a sandy mustache and a red handkerchief knotted cowboy fashion around his throat. He put the boy down gently as Sabra came up, and rose with a kind of easy grace.
“You ran away﻿—you﻿—we hunted every﻿—Cim﻿—” she stammered, and burst into tears of mingled anger and relief.
The slim young man seemed the spokesman, though the other three were obviously older than he.
“Why, I’m real sorry you was distressed, ma’am. We was going to bring the boy back safe enough. He wandered down here lookin’ for his pa, he said.” He was standing with one hand resting lightly, tenderly, on Cim’s head, and looking down at Sabra with a smile of utter sweetness. His was the soft-spoken, almost caressing voice of the Southwestern cowman and ranger. At this Sabra’s anger, born of fright, vanished. Besides, he was so young﻿—scarcely more than a boy.
“Well,” she explained, a little sheepishly, “I was worried.﻿ ﻿… My husband went off on the track of a deer﻿ ﻿… hours ago﻿ ﻿… he hasn’t come back﻿ ﻿… then when Cim﻿ ﻿… I came out and he was gone.﻿ ﻿… I was so﻿—so terribly﻿ ﻿…”
She looked very wan and schoolgirlish in her prim gray dress and with her hair in a braid tied with a bright red ribbon, and her tearstained cheeks.
One of the men who had strolled off a little way with the appearance of utmost casualness returned to the group in time to hear this. “He’ll be back any minute now,” he announced. “He didn’t get no deer.”
“But how do you know?”
The soft-spoken young man shot a malignant look at the other, the older man looked suddenly abashed.

===Chinese===

她跑上一座小丘，丘忽然一沉，陷进一道洼沟。就在那儿，一个坑洼里，她撞见了他：坐在山坡一个洞穴前，洞口和洞顶巧妙地架了木头，成了一座木屋。人从五英尺外走过也发现不了。四个男人坐在那粗陋木屋门前的空地上。西姆坐在其中一个的膝头，那人正给他敲坚果吃。他们又说又笑，嚼着坚果，一团和气，其乐融融。萨布拉的双膝忽然软了。她发着抖，朝他奔去时踉跄了一下。她的脸抽得很怪。男人们跳起来，手按在胯上。
“这个人给我敲坚果吃，”西姆好交际地说，见了她并不特别高兴。
他坐的那人是个瘦削的青年，沙色髭，脖子上牛仔式地系一条红手帕。萨布拉走近时他轻轻放下孩子，带着一种从容优雅站起来。
“你跑掉了——你——我们把哪儿都——西姆——”她结结巴巴，迸出又怒又松的一汪眼泪。
那瘦削的年轻人似乎是发言人，尽管另外三个明显比他年长。
“唉，让您受惊了，真过意不去，太太。我们正要把小家伙好好送回去呢。他说他下山坡找他爸，一路溜达到了这儿。”他站着，一只手轻轻的、近乎温存地搭在西姆头上，低头对萨布拉微笑，甜极了。那是西南部牛仔和骑警特有的、软得近乎爱抚的嗓音。这一下，萨布拉那由惊吓生出的气全消了。何况他又那么年轻——几乎还是个孩子。
“这个，”她有点讪讪地解释，“我着急了。……我丈夫追一头鹿去了……几个钟头了……还没回来……偏这时西姆……我出来一看，人没了。……我真吓——吓坏了……”
她穿着规整的灰裙，辫子上扎着鲜红的缎带，满脸泪痕，看上去非常苍白，非常学生气。
先前踱开去、装出万分闲散模样的一人，恰在这时踱回人群，听见了这段话。“他随时就回来，”他宣布，“他没打着鹿。”
“你怎么知道？”
软嗓子的年轻人朝那人射去恶狠狠的一眼，年长的那位忽然讪讪的。

===Original===

Sabra’s question went unanswered. “Won’t you sit and rest yourself, ma’am?” suggested the spokesman. The words were hospitable enough, yet there was that in the boy’s tone which conveyed to Sabra the suggestion that she and Cim had better be gone. She took Cim’s hand. Now that her fright was past she thought she must have looked very silly running down the draw with her tears and her pigtail and her screaming. She thanked them, using a little Southern charm and Southern drawl, which she often legitimately borrowed from the ancestral Venables for special occasions such as this.
“I’m ve’y grateful to you-all,” she now said. “You’ve been mighty kind. If you would just drop around to our camp I’m sure my husband would be delighted to meet you.”
The young man smiled more sweetly than ever, and the others looked at him, an inexplicable glint of humor in their weather-beaten faces.
“I sure thank you, ma’am. We’re movin’ on, my friends here and me. Pronto. Floyd, how about you getting a piece of deer meat for the lady, seeing she’s been cheated of her supper. Now, if you and the little fella don’t mind sittin’ up behind and before, why, I’ll take you back a ways. You probably run fu’ther than you expected, ma’am, scared as you was.” She had, as a matter of fact, in her terror, run almost half a mile from camp.
He mounted first. His method of accomplishing this was something of a miracle. At one moment the horse was standing ready and he was at its side. The next there was a flash, and he was on its back. It was like an optical illusion in which he seemed to have been drawn to the saddle as a needle flies to the magnet. Cim he drew up to the pommel, holding him with one hand; Sabra, perched on the horse’s rump, clung with both arms round the lad’s slim waist. Something of a horsewoman, she noticed his fine Mexican saddle, studded with silver. From the sides of the saddle hung hair-covered pockets whose bulge was the outline of a gun. A slicker such as is carried by those who ride the trails made a compact shipshape roll behind the saddle. The horse had a velvet gait, even with this triple load. Sabra found herself wishing that this exhilarating ride might go on for miles. Suddenly she noticed that the young rider wore gloves. The sight of them made her vaguely uneasy, as though some memory had been stirred. She had never seen a plainsman wearing gloves.

===Chinese===

萨布拉的问题没人答。“您不坐下歇歇吗，太太？”发言人提议。话是够客气的，可这年轻人的语调里有某种东西，向萨布拉暗示：她和西姆还是走的好。她拉起西姆的手。惊恐已过，她想到自己披头散发、拖着辫子、哭着喊着奔下洼沟的样子，一定傻透了。她谢过他们，动用了一点南方的妩媚和南方拖腔——这种场合，她常从文纳尔家祖先那里名正言顺地借来一用。
“真感谢你们几位，”她说，“几位太好了。要是肯赏光到我们营地来坐坐，我丈夫一定高兴认识几位。”
年轻人笑得比先前更甜，旁的人都看他，风霜脸上闪着莫名其妙的诙谐。
“多谢您了，太太。我们几个这就赶路了。*Pronto*（马上）。弗洛伊德，去给这位太太割一块鹿肉来，她的晚饭可是叫人骗掉了。这样吧，您和小家伙要是不嫌，一个坐前头一个坐后头，我送你们一程。您受着吓，怕是跑得比自个儿想的还远呢，太太。”事实是，她在惊恐里确实几乎跑出了离营地半英里。
他先上马。那上法简直是奇迹：这一刻马站好、他立在旁边；下一刻一道闪光，他已在马背上。像一种视错觉，他仿佛被吸上鞍去，如铁屑飞向磁石。他把西姆提到鞍头，一只手揽着；萨布拉跨坐在马臀上，两臂搂住那青年纤细的腰。她也算个女骑手，注意到他那副漂亮的墨西哥鞍，镶着银。鞍两侧挂着毛皮套，鼓起的轮廓是一支枪的形状。一只走长途的人常备的油布雨衣，在鞍后卷成整齐利落的一卷。驮着三个人，那马的步子仍如丝绒。萨布拉不由盼着这趟振奋的骑乘走上几十英里才好。忽然她注意到，年轻的骑手戴着手套。这景象叫她隐隐不安，仿佛勾起了一段记忆。她从没见过平原人戴手套。

===Original===

It was absurd, somehow.
A hundred feet or so from the camp he reined in his horse abruptly, half turned in his saddle, and with his free hand swung Sabra gently to the ground, leaning far from his saddle and keeping a firm hold on Cim and reins as he did so. He placed the child in her upraised arms, wheeled, and was gone before she could open her lips to frame a word of thanks. The piece of deer meat, neatly wrapped, lay on the ground at her feet. She stood staring after the galloping figure, dumbly. She took Cim’s hand. Together they ran toward the camp. Isaiah had a fire going, a pot of coffee bubbling. His greeting to Cim was sternly admonitory. Ten minutes later Yancey galloped in, empty handed.
“What a chase he led me! Twice I thought I had him. I’d have run him into Texas if I hadn’t thought you’d be﻿—”
Sabra, for the first time since her marriage, felt superior to him; was impatient of his tale of prowess. She had her own story to tell, spiced with indignation. She was not interested in his mythical deer. She had an actual piece of fresh deer meat to cook for their supper.
“… and just when I was ready to die with fright, there he was, talking to those four men, and sitting on the knee of one of them as though he’d known him all his life eating nuts.﻿ ﻿… Anything might have happened to him and to me while you were off after your old deer.”
Yancey seemed less interested in the part that she and Cim had played in the adventure than in the appearance and behavior of the four men in the draw, and especially the charming young man who had so gallantly brought them back.
“Thin faced, was he? And a youngster? About nineteen or twenty? What else?”
“Oh, a low voice, and kind of sweet, as though he sang tenor. And his teeth﻿—”
Yancey interrupted. “Long, weren’t they? The two at the side, I mean. Like a wolf’s?”
“Yes. How did you﻿—Do you know him?”
“Sort of,” Yancey answered, thoughtfully.
Sabra was piqued. “It was lucky for us it was someone who knows you, probably. Because you don’t seem to care much about what happened to us﻿—what might have happened.”
“You said you wanted to go a-pioneering.”
“Well?”
“This is it. Stir that fire, Isaiah. Sabra, get that deer meat a-frizzling that your friend gave you. Because we’re moving on.”
“Now? Tonight? But it’s late. I thought we were camping here for the night.”
“We’ll eat and get going. Moonlight tonight.

===Chinese===

不知怎么，太荒唐了。
离营地百来英尺，他猛地收缰，在鞍上半侧过身，腾出一只手把萨布拉轻轻搡下地，一面远远探出鞍外，一只手牢牢抓着西姆和缰绳。他把孩子放进她扬起的双臂，一拨马，不等她开口道谢就没影了。那块鹿肉包得整整齐齐，躺在她脚边的地上。她呆呆望着那疾驰而去的背影。她拉起西姆的手，两人朝营地跑去。以赛亚生好了火，一壶咖啡正冒泡。他见了西姆，迎头就是一通严厉的训诫。十分钟后，扬西空着手驰进营地。
“那家伙带着我兜了个够！两回我都当要得手了。要不是想着你们该——我真会把它撵到得克萨斯去。”
成婚以来头一回，萨布拉觉得自己比他强；他那套骁勇的说辞她听得不耐烦。她有自己的故事要讲，还添着火气。她对他那头神鹿毫无兴趣。她有一块实实在在的新鲜鹿肉要做晚饭。
“……就在我吓得要死的时候，他坐在那儿，同那四个人说话，坐在其中一个的膝盖上，好像认识了一辈子，吃着坚果。……你追你那头老鹿的工夫，他和我什么都可能碰上。”
扬西对她和西姆在这场奇遇里的遭遇倒不甚在意，在意的是洼沟里那四个人的相貌做派，尤其是那位殷勤送他们回来的可爱青年。
“瘦脸，是吗？年纪轻轻？十九二十岁？还有什么？”
“哦，嗓音低低的，还挺甜，像唱男高音的。还有他的牙——”
扬西打断。“很长，是不是？我是说旁边那两颗。像狼？”
“像。你怎么——你认识他？”
“算认识，”扬西若有所思地答。
萨布拉不乐意了。“算我们运气好，碰上的是认识你的人，多半是。因为你好像并不大在乎我们出了什么事——差点出什么事。”
“你说过你要来拓荒。”
“那又怎么样？”
“这就是拓荒。拨拨火，以赛亚。萨布拉，把你朋友送你的鹿肉搁上去煎。因为我们要动身了。”
“现在？今晚？可是天晚了。我以为我们要在这儿过夜呢。”
“吃了就走。今晚有月亮。

===Original===

I don’t just like it here. There’s been a lot of time lost this afternoon. We’ll push on. In another day or so, with luck, we’ll be in Osage, snug and safe.”
They ate hurriedly. Yancey seemed restless, anxious to be off.
They jolted on. Cim slept, a little ball of weariness, in the back of the wagon. Isaiah drowsed beside Sabra, and she herself was half asleep, the reins slack in her hands. The scent of the sun-warmed prairie came up to her, and the pungent smell of the sagebrush. The Indians had swept over this plain in hordes; and buffalo by the millions. She wondered if the early Spaniards, in their lust for gold, had trod this ground﻿—perhaps this very trail. Coronado, De Soto, Narvaez. She had seen pictures of them, these dark-skinned élégantes in their cumbersome trappings of leather and heavy metal, tramping the pitiless plains of this vast Southwest, searching like children for cities of gold.﻿ ﻿… The steady *clop-clop* of the horses’ feet, the rattle of the wagon, the squeak of the wheels, the smell of sunbaked earth﻿ ﻿…
She must have dozed off, for suddenly the sun’s rays were sharply slanted, and she shivered with the cool of the prairie night air. Voices had awakened her. Three horsemen had dashed out of a little copse and stood in the path of Yancey’s lead wagon. They were heavily armed. Their hands rested on their guns. Their faces were grim. They wore the mournful mustaches of the Western plainsman, their eyes were the eyes of men accustomed to great distances; their gaze was searing. All three wore the badge of United States marshals, but there was about them something that announced this even before the eye was caught by their badge of office. The leader addressed Yancey, his voice mild, even gentle.
“Howdy.”
“Howdy.”
“Where you bound for, pardner?”
“Osage.”
The questioner’s hand rested lightly on the butt of the six-shooter at his waist. “What might your name be?”
“Cravat﻿—Yancey Cravat.”
The spokesman’s face lighted up with the slow, incredulous smile of a delighted child. “I’ll be doggoned!” He turned his slow grin on the man at his right, on the man at his left. “Yancey Cravat!” he said again, as though they had not heard. “I sure am pleased to make your acquaintance. Heard about you till I feel like I knew you.”
“Why, thanks,” replied Yancey, unusually modest and laconic. Sabra knew then that Yancey was playing one of his roles.

===Chinese===

我不大喜欢这地方。今天下午耽搁得太多了。我们连夜赶。运气好的话，再有一两天，我们就到奥塞奇了，安安稳稳。”
他们匆匆吃了饭。扬西坐立不安，急着走。
车子一路颠着。西姆在车后睡着，倦成一团小球。以赛亚在萨布拉旁边打盹，她自己也半睡半醒，缰绳松松拖在手里。阳光晒暖的草原的气息涌上来，还有山艾刺鼻的气味。印第安人曾成群结队扫过这片平原；野牛曾以百万计。她不知道当年那些逐金的早期西班牙人，是否踏上过这片土地——也许正是这条道。科罗纳多，德索托，纳尔瓦埃斯。她见过他们的画像：这些黑皮肤的绅士，裹着皮革和重金属的笨重行头，跋涉在这片浩大西南的无情平原上，像孩子一样寻着黄金城。……马蹄规律的嗒、嗒，货车的哐啷，车轴的吱呀，晒透的泥土的气味……
她一定是打了个盹，因为阳光忽然斜得厉害，草原夜气的凉意叫她一哆嗦。人声把她惊醒。三名骑手从小树丛里冲出，拦在扬西头车的道上。他们武装到牙齿。手按着枪。脸色严峻。留着西部平原人那两撇哀愁的髭，眼睛是看惯大距离的人的眼睛；目光灼人。三个人都佩着美国法警的徽章，可不等眼睛扫到徽章，他们周身已先自宣告了这一点。领头的同扬西搭话，嗓音温和，甚至轻柔。
“你好。”
“你好。”
“上哪儿去，伙计？”
“奥塞奇。”
问话的人一只手轻轻搭在腰间六响枪的枪柄上。“请问尊姓大名？”
“克拉瓦特——扬西·克拉瓦特。”
发言人脸上绽出一个孩子得了喜信般的、迟缓的、难以置信的笑。“我的老天！”他把那迟缓的笑转向右边的人，又转向左边的人。“扬西·克拉瓦特！”他又说一遍，像怕他们没听见。“能认识您真是三生有幸。听您的大名听得我就像早就认识您了。”
“多谢，”扬西答，难得的谦虚、简短。萨布拉这才知道，扬西又在扮演他的一个角色。

===Original===

He would talk as they talked. Be one of them.
“Aimin’ to make quite a stay in Osage?”
“Aim to live there.”
“Go on! I’ve a notion to swear you in as Deputy Marshal right now, darned if I ain’t. Citizens like you is what we need, and no mistake. Lawy’in’?”
“I’m planning to take up my law practice in Osage, yes,” Yancey answered, “and start a newspaper as well.”
The three looked a little perturbed at this. They glanced at each other, then at Yancey, then away, uncomfortably. “Oh, newspaper, huh?” There was little enthusiasm in the marshal’s voice. “Well, we did have a newspaper there for a little while in Osage, ’bout a week.”
“A daily?”
“A weekly.”
There was something sinister in this. “What became of it?”
“Well, seems the editor﻿—name of Pegler﻿—died.”
There was a little silence. Sabra gathered up her reins and brought her team alongside Yancey’s, the better to hear. The three mustached ones acknowledged her more formal presence by briefly touching their hat brims with the forefinger of the hand that had rested on their guns.
“Who killed him?”
A little shadow of pained surprise passed over the features of the marshal. “He was just found dead one morning on the banks of the Canadian. Bullet wounds. But bullets is all pretty much alike, out here. He might ’a’ killed himself, plumb discouraged.”
The silence fell again. Yancey broke it. “The first edition of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* will be off the press two weeks from tomorrow.”
He gathered up the reins as though to end this chance meeting, however agreeable. “Well, gentlemen, good evening. Glad to have met you.”
The three did not budge. “What we stopped to ask you,” said the spokesman, in his gentle drawl, “was, did you happen to glimpse four men anywhere on the road? They’re nesting somewhere in here, the Kid and his gang. Stole four horses, robbed the bank at Red Fork, shot the cashier, and lit out for the prairie. Light complected, all of ’em. The Kid is a slim young fella, light hair, red handkerchief, soft spoken, and rides with gloves on. But then you know what he’s like, Cravat, well’s I do.”
Yancey nodded in agreement. “Everybody’s heard of the Kid. No, sir, I haven’t seen him. Haven’t seen anybody the last three days but a Kaw on a pony and a bunch of dirty Cheyennes in a wagon. Funny thing, I never yet knew a bad man who wasn’t light complected﻿—or, anyway, blue or gray eyes.”
“Oh, say, now!”

===Chinese===

人家怎么说话，他就怎么说话。做他们中的一个。
“打算在奥塞奇久住？”
“打算住下。”
“了不得！我真想现在就给你授个副法警当当，天知道我多想。像您这样的公民正是我们需要的，错不了。干律师的？”
“我打算在奥塞奇重操律师业，”扬西答，“另外还要办一份报。”
三人听了有点不安。他们互相看看，又看看扬西，又别开眼，浑身不自在。“哦，报纸，嗯？”法警的嗓音里没多少热情。“这个，奥塞奇倒是有过一份报，办了一阵子——个把星期吧。”
“日报？”
“周报。”
这里头有点不祥。“后来呢？”
“这个，那位编辑——叫佩格勒——死了。”
一时静默。萨布拉收拢缰绳，把车赶到扬西的车旁边，好听得分明。那三位蓄髭的朝她这新增的正式在场略一碰帽檐——用方才按枪的那只手的食指。
“谁杀的他？”
法警的脸上掠过一丝痛心的诧异。“一天早晨，人们发现他死在加拿大河岸边。枪伤。不过在这一带，子弹跟子弹都差不多。他兴许是自尽的，彻底灰了心。”
又是一阵静默。扬西打破它。“《俄克拉何马棚屋报》（*Oklahoma Wigwam*）的创刊号，距明天两周后出机。”
他收起缰绳，像是就此了结这场萍水相逢，无论多么愉快。“那么，先生们，晚上好。幸会。”
三个人没动。“我们拦下您想打听的是，”发言人和和气气地拖长调子说，“路上可曾撞见四个人？‘小子’和他那一伙，就猫在这一带的什么地方。偷了四匹马，抢了红叉的银行，打死了出纳，蹽进了草原。几个人都是浅色皮肤。‘小子’是个瘦高个年轻人，浅色头发，红手帕，说话软声软气，骑马戴手套。不过他什么样，克拉瓦特，您跟我一样清楚。”
扬西点头称是。“谁没听说过‘小子’呢。没有，先生，我没见过。这三天除了一个骑小马的卡乌人、一车脏兮兮的夏延人，谁也没见过。说来怪，我至今没见过一个恶人不是浅色皮肤——至少，不是蓝眼睛灰眼睛。”
“嗳，去你的吧！”

===Original===

protested the marshal, stroking his sandy mustache.
“Fact. You take the Kid, and the James boys, and Tom O’Phalliard, and the whole Mullins gang.”
“How about yourself? You’re pretty good with the gun, from all accounts. And black as a crow.”
Yancey lifted his great head and the heavy lids that usually drooped over the gray eyes and looked at the marshal. “That’s so,” said the other, as though in agreement at the end of an argument. “I reckon it goes fur killers and fur killers of killers.﻿ ﻿… Well, boys, we’ll be lopin’. Good luck to you.”
“Good luck to *you*!” responded Yancey, politely.
The three whirled their steeds spectacularly, raised their right hands in salute; the horses pivoted on their hind legs prettily; Cim crowed with delight. They were off in a cloud of red dust made redder by the last rays of the setting sun.
Yancey gathered up his reins. Sabra stared at him in bewildered indignation. “But the person who shields a criminal is just as bad as the criminal himself, isn’t he?”
Yancey looked back at her around the side of his wagon top. His smile was mischievous, sparkling, irresistible. “Don’t be righteous, Sabra. It’s middle class﻿—and a terrible trait in a woman.”
Late next day, just before sunset, after pushing on relentlessly through the blistering sun of midday, Yancey pointed with his wagon whip to something that looked like a wallow of mud dotted with crazy shanties and tents. Theatrically he picked Cim up in his arms so that the child, too, might see. But he spoke to Sabra.
“There it is,” he said. “That’s our future home.”
Sabra looked. And her brain seemed to have no order or reason about it, for she could think only of the green nun’s veiling trimmed with ruchings of pink which lay so carefully folded, with its modish sleeves all stuffed out with soft paper, in the trunk under the canvas of the wagon.

===Chinese===

法警抗议着，捋他的沙色髭。
“事实。你数数‘小子’，詹姆斯兄弟，汤姆·奥法利亚德，还有整个马林斯帮。”
“那你自己呢？听说你枪法好得很。黑得像乌鸦。”
扬西抬起那颗大脑袋，掀起平日耷拉在灰眼睛上的沉眼皮，看着法警。“倒也是，”对方说，像一场辩论到了收尾时的首肯。“我琢磨，这条理对杀人的管用，对杀杀人犯的也管用。……好了，弟兄们，我们走着。祝你们好运。”
“祝你好运！”扬西客气地回敬。
三人漂亮地拨转马头，举起右手行礼；马儿姿态优美地以后腿为轴一旋；西姆乐得直叫。他们卷进一团红尘里去了，落日最后的余晖把红尘映得更红。
扬西收起缰绳。萨布拉又困惑又气愤地瞪着他。“可包庇罪犯的人，同罪犯本人一样坏，不是吗？”
扬西从车篷边上回头看她。他的笑调皮、闪亮、叫人无法抗拒。“别讲大道理，萨布拉。那是中产阶级的做派——女人身上一种要命的品性。”
第二天近晚，正日落之前，顶着正午灼人的烈日一路狠赶之后，扬西用长鞭一指——指着一片像是泥塘的东西，上面歪歪斜斜地点缀着棚屋和帐篷。他做戏似的把西姆抱起来，好让孩子也看见。话却是对萨布拉说的。
“到了，”他说。“那就是我们未来的家。”
萨布拉望去。她的脑子仿佛全没了章法，因为她只想起那件镶着粉色荷叶边的绿修女纱——叠得整整齐齐，时髦的袖子里塞满软纸，躺在车篷下面那口箱子里。

## VI / 六
===Original===

Long before the end of that first nightmarish day in Osage, Sabra had confronted her husband with blazing eyes. “I won’t bring up my boy in a town like this!”
It had been a night and a day fantastic with untoward happenings. Their wagons had rumbled wearily down the broad main street of the settlement﻿—a raw gash in the prairie. All about, on either side, were wooden shacks, and Indians and dried mud and hitching posts and dogs and crude wagons like their own. It looked like pictures Sabra had seen of California in ’49. They had supped on ham and eggs, fried potatoes, and muddy coffee in a place labeled Ice Cream and Oyster Parlor. They spent that first night in a rooming house above one of the score of saloons that enlivened the main street﻿—Pawhuska Avenue, it was called. It was a longish street, for the Osage town settlers seemed to have felt the need of huddling together for company in this wilderness. The street stopped abruptly at either end and became suddenly prairie.
“Pawhuska Avenue,” said a tipsy sign tacked on the front of a false-front pine shack. Yancey chose this unfortunate time to impart a little Indian lore to Cim, wide eyed on the wagon seat beside his mother.
“That’s Osage,” he shouted to the boy. “*Pawhu*﻿—that means hair. And *scah*, that means white. White Hair. Pawhuska﻿—White Hair﻿—was an old Osage Chief﻿—”
“Yancey Cravat!” Sabra called in a shout that almost equaled his own, and in a tone startlingly like one of Felice Venable’s best (she was, in fact, slightly hysterical, what with weariness and disappointment and fear), “Yancey Cravat, will you stop talking Indian history and find us a place to eat and sleep! Where’s your sense? Can’t you see he’s ready to drop, and so am I?”
The greasy food set before them in the eating house sickened her. She shrank from the slatternly boldfaced girl who slammed the dishes down in front of them on the oilcloth-covered table. At this same table with them﻿—there was only one, a long board accommodating perhaps twenty﻿—sat red-faced men talking in great rough voices, eating with a mechanical and absentminded thoroughness, shoveling potatoes, canned vegetables, pie into their mouths with knives. Cim was terribly wide awake and noisily unruly, excited by the sounds and strangeness about him.
“I’m an Indian!” he would yell, making a great clatter with his spoon on the table. “Ol’ White Hair!

===Chinese===

在奥色治这噩梦般的第一天还没过完，萨布拉早已两眼冒火地质问丈夫：“我不肯把我的儿子养在这样一个镇上！”

那一夜加一天，满是不测，荒唐得像做梦。他们的大车疲惫地辘辘驶下聚居区宽阔的大街——草原上一道新开的生口子。两侧到处是木板棚屋、印第安人、干泥、拴马桩、狗，和同他们自己的一样粗陋的大车。看上去像萨布拉见过的四九年加利福尼亚的图画。他们在一家挂着“冰淇淋牡蛎厅”招牌的铺子里吃了火腿煎蛋、煎土豆和浑咖啡当晚饭。头一夜，他们睡在大街——名叫波哈斯卡大街——上那二十来家使街道热闹起来的酒馆其中一家的楼上寄宿公寓里。街相当长，因为奥色治镇的移民在这片荒野里似乎觉得非挤在一处作伴不可。街两头猛地一断，骤然变成草原。

“波哈斯卡大街，”一块歪歪斜斜的牌子钉在一座假门脸松木棚屋前。扬西偏偏挑了这个不巧的时辰，给坐在车上母亲身边、眼睛瞪得溜圆的西姆上一课印第安掌故。

“那是奥色治话，”他朝孩子喊。"*Pawhu*——是头发。*scah*，是白。白头发。波哈斯卡——白头发——是奥色治人的一位老酋长——”

“扬西·克拉瓦特！”萨布拉喊，声量几乎赶得上他，腔调惊人地像菲莉丝·维纳布尔盛年的一手（她其实有点歇斯底里了，又是累又是失望又是怕），“扬西·克拉瓦特，你能不能别讲你的印第安历史，先给我们找个吃住的地方！你的脑子呢？看不见他快垮了，我也快垮了吗？”

饭馆里摆到他们面前的油腻食物叫她恶心。那个把盘子砰砰掉到油布桌上的邋遢厚脸皮的姑娘，她看一眼都缩。同一张桌上——总共只有这一张，一条长板，坐得下二十来人——坐着红脸膛的汉子，粗声大气地谈着，机械地、心不在焉地、却一扫而光地吃着，拿刀往嘴里扒土豆、罐头菜和馅饼。西姆精神得不得了，吵闹得不象话，四周的声响和生疏把他兴奋坏了。

“我是印第安人！”他挥着勺子在桌上敲得震天响。“老白头发！”

===Original===

*Wa-wa-wa-wa-wa-wa-wa!*” Being reprimanded, and having the spoon forcibly removed from his clutching fingers, he burst into tears and howls.
Sabra had taken him up to the bare and clean enough little room which was to be their shelter for the night. From wide-eyed wakefulness Cim had become suddenly limp with sleep. Yancey had gone out to see to the horses, to get what information he could about renting a house, and a shack for the newspaper. A score of plans were teeming in his mind.
“You’ll be all right,” he had said. “A good night’s sleep and everything’ll look rosy in the morning. Don’t look so down in the mouth, honey. You’re going to like it.”
“It’s horrible! It’s﻿—and those men! Those dreadful men.”
“ ‘For my part, I had rather be the first man among these fellows than the second man in Rome.’ ” Yancey struck an attitude.
Sabra looked at him dully. “Rome?”
“Plutarch, my sweet.” He kissed her; was gone with a great flirt of his coat tails. She heard his light step clattering down the flimsy wooden stairs. She could distinguish his beautiful vibrant voice among the raucous speech of the other men below.
The boy was asleep in a rude box bed drawn up beside theirs. Black Isaiah was bedded down somewhere in a little kennel outside. Sabra sank suspiciously down on the doubtful mattress. The walls of the room were wafer thin; mere pine slats with cracks between. From the street below came women’s shrill laughter, the sound of a piano hammered horribly. Horses clattered by. Voices came up in jocose greeting; there were conversations and arguments excruciatingly prolonged beneath her window.
“I was sellin’ a thousand beef steers one time﻿—holdin’ a herd of about three thousand﻿—and me and my foreman, we was countin’ the cattle as they come between us. Well, the steers was wild long-legged coasters﻿—and run! Say, they come through between us like scairt wolves, and I lost the count﻿ ﻿…”
“Heard where the Mullins gang rode in there this morning and cleaned up the town﻿—both banks﻿—eleven thousand in one and nineteen thousand in the other, and when they come out it looked like the whole county’d rallied against ’em.﻿ ﻿…”
“Say, he’s a bad hombre, that fella. Got a poisoned tongue, like a rattlesnake.﻿ ﻿… Spades trump?”
“No, hearts. Say, I would of known how to handle him. One time we was campin’ on Amarillo Creek﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

"*哇-哇-哇-哇-哇-哇-哇！*“挨了训，勺子又被从攦紧的手指里硬夺下来，他放声大哭大嚎起来。

萨布拉把他抱上楼，带进那间将就过夜的小屋，光秃秃的，倒还干净。西姆从瞪圆了的清醒，忽然睡软了。扬西出去了，去看马，去打听他能打听到的租房消息，和给报纸找间棚屋。他脑子里塞满了成打的计划。

“你会没事的，”他说。“睡个好觉，明早看什么都玫瑰色。别哭丧着脸了，宝贝。你会喜欢这儿的。”

“可怕极了！这儿——还有那些男人！那些可怕的男人。”

“‘就我而言，我宁可在这些家伙里做第一人，不在罗马做第二人。’”扬西摆出一个造型。

萨布拉木然看他。“罗马？”

“普鲁塔克，我的甜心。”他吻了她；一阵衣摆的大翻，人没了。她听见他轻快的脚步磕磕绊绊下了薄木板楼梯。楼下男人们的粗嗓门里，她辨得出他那悦耳嘹亮的嗓音。

孩子在一张粗木箱改的床里睡着，床挨着他们的大床。黑以赛亚在外头一个小窝棚里打了铺。萨布拉满腹狐疑地陷进那张可疑的床垫。屋墙薄得像威化饼干；不过是松木板条，缝里透风。楼下街上传来女人尖利的笑、一架钢琴敲得骇人的声音。马蹄嗒嗒而过。窗子底下，寒暄的哄笑、没完没了折磨人的谈话和抬杠一浪接一浪。

“有一回我卖一千头肉牛——手里压着三千来头的一大群——我跟工头俩，隔着道数牛。嘿，那都是长腿野性的沿海牛——跑得欢！嗐，它们像吓煞的狼一样从我们中间蹿过去，我数都数丢了……”

“听说没有？马林斯那伙匪今儿早上进了城，把全镇洗了——两家银行——一家一万一千，一家一万九——他们出来的时候，看那阵仗，好像全县都围上来了。……”

“我说，那家伙是个狠角色。一条毒舌头，跟响尾蛇似的。……黑桃主打？”

“不，红心。嗐，要是我就会治他。有一回我们在阿马里洛溪边扎营……”

===Original===

A loud knock at the door opposite Sabra’s room. The knock repeated. Then a woman’s voice, metallic, high. “*Quien es? Quien es?*” The impatient rattle of a door knob, and a man’s gruff voice.
A long-drawn wail in the street below, “Oh, Joe! He‑e‑e‑ere’s your mule!” followed by a burst of laughter.
Yet somehow she had fallen asleep in utter exhaustion, only to be awakened by pistol shots, a series of bloodcurdling yells, the crash and tinkle of broken glass. Then came screams of women, the sound of horses galloping. She lay there, cowering. Cim stirred in his bed, sighed deeply, slept again. She was too terrified to go to the window. Her shivering seemed to shake the bed. She wanted to waken the child for comfort, for company. She summoned courage to go to the window; peered fearfully out into the dim street below. Nothing. No one in the street. Yancey’s bleeding body was not lying in the road; no masked men. Nothing again but the clink of glasses and plates; the tinny piano, the slap of cards.
She longed with unutterable longing, not for the sweet security of her bed back in Wichita﻿—that seemed unreal now﻿—but for those nights in the wagon on the prairie with no sound but the rustle of the scrub oaks, the occasional stamp of horses’ hoofs on dry clay, the rippling of a nearby stream. She looked at her little gold watch, all engraved with a bird and a branch and a waterfall and a church spire. It was only nine.
It was midnight when Yancey came in. She sat up in bed in her high-necked, long-sleeved nightgown. Her eyes, in her white face, were two black holes burned in a piece of paper.
“What was it? What was it?”
“What was what? Why aren’t you asleep, sugar?”
“Those shots. And the screaming. And the men hollering.”
“Shots?” He was unstrapping his broad leather belt with its twin six-shooters whose menacing heads peered just above their holsters. He wore it always now. It came, in time, to represent for her a sinister symbol of all the terrors, all the perils that lay waiting for them in this new existence. “Why, sugar, I don’t recollect hearing any﻿—Oh﻿—that!” He threw back his great head and laughed. “That was just a cowboy, feeling high, shooting out the lights over in Strap Turket’s saloon. On his way home and having a little fun with the boys. Scare you, did it?”
He came over to her, put a hand on her shoulder. She shrugged away from him, furious.

===Chinese===

萨布拉房门对面，一阵大声擞门。又擞。随后一个女人的嗓音，金属性，尖利："*Quien es? Quien es?*“门把手不耐烦地哗啦响，一个男人的粗嗓门。

街下一声拖长的哀号：“哦，乔！你——的——骡——子——来——啦！”跟着一片大笑。

不知怎么她竟在彻底的精疲力竭里睡着了，却只落得被枪声惊醒——一连串令人毛骨悚然的呐喊，玻璃碎裂的哗啦和叮当。随后是女人的尖叫，马匹疾驰。她躺在那儿缩成一团。西姆在床上动了动，深深叹口气，又睡了。她怕得不敢到窗前去。她的发抖好像连床都在颤。她想叫醒孩子讨个安慰、讨个作伴。她鼓起勇气走到窗前；战战兢兢朝下面昏暗的街上望去。什么也没有。街上没有人。扬西流着血的身躯没有躺在路中央；没有蒙面人。还是那一切：杯盘的叮当；单薄的琴声，纸牌的啪啪。

她怀着说不出的渴望——渴望的不是威奇塔自己床上那份甜蜜的安稳（那此刻显得不真实了），而是草原上大车里的那些夜晚：除了矮橡树的沙沙、马蹄偶尔踏在干泥上的声响、近旁溪水的淙淙，再没有别的声音。她看看她那块小金表，表壳上雕着一只鸟、一枝树枝、一道瀑布、一座教堂的尖顶。才九点。

扬西回来已是午夜。她穿着高领长袖睡衣在床上坐起。她那张白脸上，两只眼睛像烧穿在纸上的一双黑洞。

“出什么事了？出什么事了？”

“什么出了什么事？怎么还不睡，糖？”

“那些枪声。还有尖叫。还有人喊叫。”

“枪声？”他正解下那条宽皮带，上面别着孪生的六响枪，两颗不祥的头刚好从枪套上探出来。他如今总戴着它。到后来，它在她眼里成了一个新的象征，象征着这种新生活里埋伏着的一切恐怖、一切凶险。“嗐，糖，我不记得听见什么枪——哦——那个！”他后仰起那颗大头，笑了。“不过是个牛仔，兴头上来，在斯特拉普·特基特的酒馆里把灯打灭了。回家的路上同弟兄们寻个开心。吓着你了，是吗？”

他走过来，一只手搭上她的肩。她懎然一耸，挣脱了他。

===Original===

She pressed her hand frantically to her forehead. It was cold and wet. She was panting a little. “I won’t bring my boy up in a town like this. I won’t. I’m going back. I’m going back home, I tell you.”
“Wait till morning, anyhow, won’t you, honey?” he said, and took her in his arms.
Next morning was, somehow, magically, next morning, with the terrors of the night vanished quite. The sun was shining. For a moment Sabra had the illusion that she was again at home in her own bed at Wichita. Then she realized that this was because she had been awakened by a familiar sound. It was the sound of Isaiah’s voice somewhere below in the dusty yard. He was polishing Yancey’s boots, spitting on them industriously and singing as he rubbed. His husky sweet voice came up to her as she lay there.

> “Lis’en to de lambs, all a-cryin’

Lis’en to de lambs, all a-cryin’

Lis’en to de lambs, all a-cryin’

Ah wanta go to heab’n when ah die.

Come on, sister, wid yo’ ups an’ downs,

Wanta go to heab’n when ah die,

De angels waitin’ fo’ to gib yo’ a crown,

Wanta go to heab’n when ah die.”

===Chinese===

她发疯似的把手按在额头上。额头又冷又湿。她微微喘着。“我不肯把我的儿子养在这样一个镇上。我不肯。我要回去。我要回家了，我告诉你。”

“无论如何等到早晨，好不好，宝贝？”他说着把她搂进怀里。

第二天早晨，不知怎么，魔法似的就真的是第二天早晨了，夜里的恐怖无影无踪。太阳照着。有一刻，萨布拉产生了错觉，仿佛又躺在威奇塔自己家里的床上。随后她明白过来：这是因为一声熟悉的响动唤醒了她。是以赛亚的声音，就在下面尘土的院子里。他在擦扬西的靴子，起劲地往上吐唾沫，一边擦一边唱。他沙哑而甜润的嗓音传到她躺着的地方。

> “听哪，羊羔儿都在哭，

听哪，羊羔儿都在哭，

听哪，羊羔儿都在哭，

俺死时要上天国去。

来吧姊妹，带着你的起落，

想死后上天堂去，

天使等着给你加冠呢，

俺死时要上天国去。”

===Original===

Lugubrious though the words were, Sabra knew he was utterly happy.
There was much to be done﻿—a dwelling to be got somehow﻿—a place in which to house the newspaper plant. If necessary, Yancey said, they could live in the rear and set up the printing and law office in the front. Almost everyone who conducted a business in the town did this. “Houses are mighty scarce,” Yancey said, making a great masculine snorting and snuffling at the wash bowl as they dressed. “It’s take what you can get or live in a tent. I heard last night that Doc Nisbett’s got a good house. Five rooms, and he’ll furnish us with water. There’s a dozen families after it, and Doc’s as independent as a hog on ice.”
Sabra rather welcomed this idea of combining office and home. She would be near him all day. As soon as breakfast was over she and Yancey fared forth, leaving Cim in Isaiah’s care (under many and detailed instructions from Sabra). She had put on her black grosgrain silk with the three box pleats on each side, trimmed with the passementerie and jet buttons﻿—somewhat wrinkled from its long stay in the trunk﻿—and her modish hat with the five ostrich plumes and the pink roses that had cost twelve dollars and fifty cents in Wichita, and her best black buttoned kid shoes and her black kid gloves. In the tightly basqued black silk she was nineteen inches round the waist and very proud of it. Her dark eyes, slightly shadowed now, what with weariness, excitement, and loss of sleep, were enormous beneath the brim of the romantic black plumed hat.
Yancey, seeing her thus attired in splendor after almost a fortnight of the gray cheviot, struck an attitude of dazzlement. Blank verse leaped to his ready lips. “ ‘But who is this, what thing of sea or land﻿—female of sex it seems﻿—that so bedeck’d, ornate, and gay, comes this way sailing, like a stately ship of Tarsus, bound for th’ isles of Javan or Gadire, with all her bravery on.﻿ ﻿…’ ”
“Oh, now, Yancey, don’t talk nonsense. It’s only my second-best black grosgrain.”
“You’re right, my darling. Even Milton has no words for such beauty.”
“Do hurry, dear. We’ve so much to do.”

===Chinese===

词句虽凄苦，萨布拉听得出他快活透了。

要办的事多着呢——得设法弄一处住处——还得有个安置报纸家什的地方。扬西说，必要时他们可以住后面，前面办印刷所兼律师事务所。镇上做生意的人家差不多都这样。“房子紧得很，”扬西一边梳洗一边对着洗脸盆大大地喷着鼻子，十足的男人气。“有什么要什么，不然就住帐篷。我昨夜听说尼斯贝特大夫有座好房子。五间，还供水。追着要的一打人家，而那位大夫，独立得像冰上的肥猪。”

这办公兼住家的主意，萨布拉倒相当欢迎。她就整天都在他身边了。早饭一毕，她同扬西就出动了，把西姆留给以赛亚（领了萨布拉又多又细的嘱咐）。她穿上了她那件黑色斜纹绸，两边各三道箱形褶，缀着珠络和黑玉纽扣——在箱子里躺久了，有些皱——戴上那顶时新的帽子，五支鸵鸟翎加粉红玫瑰，在威奇塔花了十二块五，穿上最好的黑扣羔羊皮鞋和黑羔羊皮手套。紧束的黑色绸衣里，她腰围十九英寸，为此十分得意。她的黑眼睛——这两天因为劳累、兴奋和缺觉，微微带了阴影——在那顶浪漫的黑翎帽檐下显得极大。

扬西看她在一身灰色斜纹呢的将近两星期之后忽然这样盛装，摆出一个目眩神摇的姿势。素体诗一跃跳上他那随时待命的嘴：“‘可这是什么，海里还是陆上的什么造物——看去似是女性——这样珠光宝气、花团锦簇，扬帆而来，像塔尔苏斯的一艘楼船，驶往爪哇或加的尔群岛，通体的盛装。……’”

“哦，得了，扬西，别胡说。不过是我第二好的黑斜纹绸。”

“你说得对，亲爱的。这样的美，连弥尔顿也找不到话。”

“快走吧，亲爱的。咱们有那么多事。”

===Original===

With his curling locks, his broad-brimmed white sombrero, his high-heeled boots, his fine white shirt, the ample skirts of his Prince Albert spreading and swooping with the vigor of his movements, Yancey was an equally striking figure, though perhaps not so unusual as she, in this day and place.
The little haphazard town lay broiling in the summer sun. The sky that Sabra was to know so well hung flat and glaring, a gray-blue metal disk, over the prairie.
“Well, Sabra honey, this isn’t so bad!” exclaimed Yancey, and looked about him largely. “ ‘Now Morning saffron-robed arose from the streams of Ocean to bring light to gods and men.’ ”
“Ocean!” echoed Sabra, the literal. “Mighty little water I’ve seen around here﻿—unless you call that desert prairie the ocean.”
“And so it is, my pet. That’s very poetic of you. The prairie’s an ocean of land.” He seemed enormously elated﻿—jubilant, almost. His coat tails switched; he stepped high in his fine Texas star boots. She tucked her hand in her handsome husband’s arm. The air was sweet, and they were young, and it was morning. Perhaps it was not going to be so dreadful, after all.
Somehow, she had yet no feeling that she, Sabra Cravat, was part of this thing. She was an onlooker. The first thing she noticed, as she stepped into the dust of the street in her modish dress and hat, caused her heart to sink. The few women to be seen scuttling about wore sunbonnets and calico﻿—the kind of garments in which Sabra had seen the women back home in Wichita hanging up the Monday wash to dry on the line in the back yard. Here they came out of butcher’s shop or grocery store with the day’s provisions in their arms; a packet of meat, tins of tomatoes or peaches, unwrapped. After sharp furtive glances at Sabra, they vanished into this little pine shack or that. Immediately afterward there was great agitation among the prim coarse window curtains in those dwellings boasting such elegance.
“But the others﻿—the other kind of women﻿—” Sabra faltered.
Yancey misunderstood. “Plenty of the other kind in a town like this, but they aren’t stirring this time of day.”
“Don’t be coarse, Yancey. I mean ladies like myself﻿—that I can talk to﻿—who’ll come calling﻿—that is﻿—”
He waved a hand this way and that. “Why, you just saw some women folks, didn’t you?”
“Those!”

===Chinese===

卷发，白色阔边帽，高跟靴，细白衬衫，普林斯·艾伯特礼服的衣裾随他动作的劲头翻飞张开——在这样的时世和地方，扬西也同样是惹眼的人物，只是也许不如她那样出格。

这座杂乱无章的小镇，趴在夏日的太阳下炙烤。萨布拉日后要熟得不能再熟的那片天空，平平地悬在草原上，刺目地亮，像一面灰蓝色的金属圆盘。

“嗐，萨布拉宝贝，这不算糟嘛！”扬西叫道，大大咧咧地四下张望。“‘现在，披着番红花色长袍的清晨从大洋的溪流中升起，给神和人送来光明。’”

“大洋！”死心眼的萨布拉应声说。“这一带我见着的水可少得很——除非你管那片荒草原叫大洋。”

“正是，我的宝贝。你这话很有点诗意。草原就是一片陆地的大洋。”他显得兴高采烈——简直是欢欣鼓舞。他的衣摆甩着；他穿着那双漂亮的得克萨斯星靴，迈着高步。她把手挽进她英俊丈夫的臂弯。空气是甜的，他们又年轻，又是早晨。也许，到底不会那么可怕。

不知怎么，她还没有自己是这件事的一部分的感觉。她是看客。她一脚踏进街尘里，时新的衣裙和帽子，头一样看见的东西就叫她的心沉了下去。看得见的几个女人都戴着遮阳帽、穿着印花布——就是萨布拉在威奇塔老家见女人们星期一在后院绳上晾晒的那种衣裳。她们这儿从肉铺或杂货店出来，怀里抱着一天的吃食：一包肉，几听番茄或桃子，连纸都不包。朝萨布拉飞快地偷偷一瞥之后，她们便钻进这座或那座小松木棚屋。紧接着，那些有幸挂了窗帘的体面人家，窗帘后便是一阵大骚动。

“可其余的呢——另一种女人——”萨布拉讷讷地说。

扬西会错了意。“这样的镇上另一种有的是，只是这个钟点还不动窝。”

“别粗俗，扬西。我是说同我一样的太太们——能说话的——会来串门的——就是说——”

他朝这头那头挥挥手。“嗐，你刚才不是见了几个娘儿们吗？”

“那些！”

===Original===

“Well, now, honey, you can’t expect those ladies to be wearing their best bib and tucker mornings to do the housework in. Besides, most of the men came without their women folks. They’ll send for them, and then you’ll have plenty of company. It isn’t every woman who’d have the courage you showed, roughing it out here. You’re the stuff that Rachel was made of, and the mother of the Gracchi.”
Rachel was, she knew, out of the Bible; she was a little hazy about the Gracchi, but basked serene in the knowledge that a compliment was intended.
There was the absurdly wide street﻿—surely fifty feet wide﻿—in this little one-street town. Here and there a straggling house or so branched off it. But the life of Osage seemed to be concentrated just here. There were tents still to be seen serving as dwellings. Houses and stores were built of unpainted wood. They looked as if they had been run up overnight, as indeed they had. They stared starkly out into the wide-rutted red clay road, and the muddy road glared back at them, and the brazen sky burned with fierce intensity down on both, with never a tree or bit of green to cheer the spirit or rest the eye. Tied to the crude hitching posts driven well into the ground were all sorts of vehicles: buckboards, crazy carts, dilapidated wagons, mule drawn; here and there a top buggy covered with the dust of the prairie; and everywhere, lording it, those four-footed kings without which life in this remote place could not have been sustained﻿—horses of every size and type and color and degree. Indian ponies, pintos, pack horses, lean long-legged range horses, and occasionally a flashing-eyed creature who spurned the red clay with the disdainful hoof of one whose ancestors have known the mesas of Spain. Direct descendants, these, of the equine patricians who, almost four hundred years before, had been brought across the ocean by Coronado or Moscosco to the land of the Seven Cities of Gold.
There were the sounds of the hammer and the saw, the rattle of chains, the thud of hoofs, all very sharp and distinct, as though this mushroom town were pulling itself out of the red clay of the prairie by its own bootstraps before one’s very eyes. Crude and ugly though the scene was that now spread itself before Sabra and Yancey, it still was not squalid. It had vitality.

===Chinese===

“好了，宝贝，你总不能指望那些太太们做家务也穿最好的行头吧。再说，大多数男人来时没带家眷。回头接了来，你就不愁没伴了。不是每个女人都有你这股勇气，跑到这荒地里来吃苦的。你是有拉结的那个料，有格拉古兄弟的母亲那个料。”

拉结，她知道，是《圣经》里的；格拉古她就有点含糊了，可她安然受用着这份恭维。

就是这么一条宽得离谱的大街——足有五十英尺宽——在这座只有一条街的镇上。四处偶尔有一两座零散的房子岔出去。可奥色治的命脉仿佛就聚在这里。还有当住家用的帐篷。房屋店铺都是没上漆的木头搭的，像是一夜之间仓促支起来的——也确是一夜之间支起来的。它们赤裸裸地瞪着车辙纵横的红土路，泥路也瞪着它们，头顶无耻的天火辣辣地烧着底下这一切，没有一棵树、一点绿，给精神打气、让眼睛歇息。深深钉进地里的粗陋拴马桩上，拴着各式各样的车：四轮轻马车、散了架的马车、破破烂烂的骡车；偶尔一辆带篷的轻便马车，蒙着草原的尘土；而到处趾高气扬的，是那些四条腿的君王——没有它们，这偏远地方的生活就维持不了——各种大小、类型、毛色、档次的马。印第安矮马、花斑马、驮马、精瘦的长腿牧马，偶尔一匹眼睛闪光的尤物，用轻蔑的蹄子刨着红土——它的祖先见过西班牙的高原台地。它们几乎是四百年前由科罗纳多或莫科斯科渡海运到七金城之地的马中贵族的直系后裔。

锤子和锯子的声响，铁链的哗啦，蹄声的嘚嘚，一切都那样尖利、分明，仿佛这座雨后春笋般的镇子正当你着眼睛，揪着自己的靴带从草原的红土里往外拔自己。在萨布拉和扬西眼前展开的这副景象尽管粗陋丑陋，却并不寒酸。它有生气。

===Original===

You sensed that behind those bare boards people were planning and stirring mightily. There was life in the feel of it. The very names tacked up over the store fronts had bite and sting. Sam Pack. Mott Bixler. Strap Buckner. Ike Hawes. Clint Hopper. Jim Click.
Though they had come to town but the night before, it seemed to her that a surprising number of people knew Yancey and greeted him as they passed down the street. “H’are you, Yancey! Howdy, ma’am.” Loungers in doorways stared at them curiously. Cowboys loping by gave her a long hard look that still had in it something of shyness﻿—a boyish look, much like that with which the outlaws had greeted her down in the draw on the prairie when they learned that she was Cim’s mother.
It struck Sabra suddenly with a little shock of discovery that the men really were doing nothing. They lounged in doorways and against hitching posts and talked; you heard their voices in animated conversation within saloon and store and office; they cantered by gracefully, and wheeled and whirled and cantered back again. She was to learn that many of these men were not builders but scavengers. The indomitable old ’49ers were no kin of these. They were, frequently, soft, cruel, furtive, and avaricious. They had gathered here to pick up what they could and move on. Some were cowmen, full of resentment against a government that had taken the free range away from them and given it over to the homesteaders. Deprived of their only occupation, many of these became outlaws. Equipped with six-shooters, a deadly aim, and horsemanship that amounted to the miraculous, they took to the Gyp Hills, or the Osage, swooping down from their hidden haunts to terrorize a town, shoot up a bank, hold up a train, and dash out again, leaving blood behind them. They risked their lives for a few hundred dollars. Here was a vast domain without written laws, without precedent, without the customs of civilization; part of a great country, yet no part of its government. Here a horse was more valuable than a human life. A horse thief, caught, was summarily hanged to the nearest tree; the killer of a man often went free.

===Chinese===

你感觉得到，那些光板子后面，人们在雄心勃勃地打算盘、奔忙。那气色里有生命。钉在店铺门脸上的名字本身就带着刺儿和蜇人的劲。山姆·帕克。莫特·比克斯勒。斯特拉普·巴克纳。艾克·豪斯。克林特·霍珀。吉姆·克利克。

他们前一夜才进的城，可沿街走过去，认识扬西、同他打招呼的人多得叫她惊讶。“好啊，扬西！您好，太太。”门口的闲人好奇地盯着他们。缓驰而过的牛仔把她上上下下打量好一阵，那眼神里却还有几分羞怯——一种少年气的神情，很像草原上沟谷里那些绿林好汉听说她是西姆的母亲时迎接她的那一眼。

萨布拉忽然带着一点发现的小小震动看出：这些男人其实什么正事也没干。他们倚在门口、靠着拴马桩谈天；酒馆、店铺、写字间里都听得见他们高谈阔论；他们优雅地小跑过去，兜个圈，转回来再小跑过去。她日后会明白，这些人里许多不是建设者，是食腐者。不屈不挠的老四九人不属于这一类。这些人往往软弱、残忍、鬼祟、贪财。他们聚到这里，是想捞一把就走。有的是牧牛人，满腔怨气，怨政府夺走了他们的自由牧场，把地划给了宅地农。营生被夺了，其中许多人便做了响马。凭着六响枪、百步穿杨的枪法和神乎其技的骑术，他们上了吉普丘陵，或者奥色治山地，从藏身之处扑下来，吓唬一座镇子，扫射一家银行，劫一列火车，再呼啸而去，身后留下血迹。他们为几百块钱拿命去赌。这是一大片没有成文法、没有先例、没有文明习惯的疆土；属于一个大国的版图，却同它的政府毫无干系。在这里，一匹马比一条人命值钱。偷马贼一旦被抓住，就近吊死在最近的一棵树上；杀人犯倒常常逍遥法外。

===Original===

Down the street these two stepped in their finery, the man swaggering a little as a man should in a white sombrero and with a pretty woman on his arm; the woman looking about her interestedly, terrified at what she saw and determined not to show it. If two can be said to make a procession, then Yancey and Sabra Cravat formed quite a parade as they walked down Pawhuska Avenue in the blaze of the morning sun. Certainly they seemed to be causing a stir. Lean rangers in buckboards turned to stare. Loungers in doorways nudged each other, yawping. Cowboys clattering by whooped a greeting. It was unreal, absurd, grotesque.
“Hi, Yancey! Howdy, ma’am.”
Past the Red Dog Saloon. A group in chairs tilted up against the wall or standing about in high-heeled boots and sombreros greeted Yancey now with a familiarity that astonished Sabra. “Howdy, Cim! Hello, Yancey!”
“He called you Cim!”
He ignored her surprised remark. Narrowly he was watching them as he passed. “Boys are up to something. If they try to get funny while you’re here with me﻿ ﻿…”
Sabra, glancing at the group from beneath her shielding hat brim, did see that they were behaving much like a lot of snickering schoolboys who are preparing to let fly a bombardment of snowballs. There was nudging, there was whispering, an air of secret mischief afoot.
“Why are they﻿—what do you think makes them﻿—” Sabra began, a trifle nervously.
“Oh, they’re probably fixing up a little initiation for me,” Yancey explained, his tone light but his eye wary. “Don’t get nervous. They won’t dare try any monkey-shines while you’re with me.”
“But who are they?” He evaded her question. She persisted. “Who are they?”
“I can’t say for sure. But I suspect they’re the boys that did Pegler dirt.”
“Pegler? Who is﻿—oh, isn’t that the man﻿—the editor﻿—the one who was found dead﻿—shot dead on the banks of the﻿—Yancey! Do you mean they did it!”
“I don’t say they did it﻿—exactly. They know more than is comfortable, even for these parts. I was inquiring around last night, and everybody shut up like a clam. I’m going to find out who killed Pegler and print it in the first number of the *Oklahoma Wigwam*.”
“Oh, Yancey! Yancey, I’m frightened!” She clung tighter to his arm. The grinning mirthless faces of the men on the saloon porch seemed to her like the fanged and snarling muzzles of wolves in a pack.
“Nothing to be frightened of, honey.

===Chinese===

这两个人就这样一身盛装沿街走去，男的微微昂首挺胸——戴白阔边帽、臂弯里挎着漂亮女人的男人理当如此；女的饶有兴味地四下看，看见的一切叫她胆战，又拿定主意不露出来。倘若两个人也可以算一支游行队伍，那么扬西和萨布拉·克拉瓦特在朝阳的烈光里走过波哈斯卡大街，倒真是一场盛大的游行。他们显然引起了轰动。四轮马车上的精瘦牧人回头呆望。门口的闲人互相用胳膊肘捣，哇啦乱叫。嗒嗒而过的牛仔一声呼哨打招呼。这一切不真实、荒唐、怪诞。

“嗨，扬西！您好，太太。”

走过红狗酒馆。一群人歪坐在斜倚墙上的椅子里，或穿着高跟靴、戴着阔边帽站着，此刻招呼扬西的那种熟络，叫萨布拉吃惊。“好啊，西姆！你好，扬西！”

“他管你叫西姆！”

他不理她惊讶的话头。走过时他紧紧地盯着他们。“弟兄们在打什么主意。有你在我身边，他们要是敢耍花招……”

萨布拉从遮掩的帽檐下瞥了那群人一眼，果然看见他们的做派活像一伙憋着坏笑、准备发起一场雪球弹幕的学童。你捣我一肘，我对你耳语，一股暗中使坏的空气。

“他们干嘛——你说他们这是——”萨布拉有点紧张地开口。

“哦，他们多半在给我准备一个下马威，”扬西解释，口气轻巧，眼睛却警觉。“别紧张。有你跟我在一起，他们不敢胡闹。”

“可他们是谁？”他避而不答。她追问。“他们是谁？”

“说不准。可我疑心，就是坑了佩格勒的那伙小子。”

“佩格勒？是谁——哦，那不就是那个人——办报的——被发现死在河岸上的那个——被人开枪打死的——扬西！你是说那是他们干的！”

“我没说一定是他们干的。他们知道的内情太多，多到这一带都嫌烫手。我昨夜四处打听，人人都闭口像蚌。我要查出是谁杀了佩格勒，登在《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的创刊号上。”

“哦，扬西！扬西，我怕！”她把他挽得更紧。酒馆廊子上那一张张咧着嘴、无欢的脸，在她眼里像一群狼龇着牙的口鼻。

“没什么可怕的，宝贝。

===Original===

They know me. I’m no Pegler they can scare. They don’t like my white hat, that’s the truth of it. Dared me last night down at the Sunny Southwest Saloon to wear it this morning. Just to try me out. They won’t have the guts to come out in the open﻿—”
The sentence never was finished. Sabra heard a curious buzzing sound past her ear. Something sang﻿—*zing!* Yancey’s white sombrero went spinning into the dust of the road.
Sabra’s mouth opened as though she were screaming, but the sounds she would have made emerged, feebly, as a croak.
“Stay where you are,” Yancey ordered, his voice low and even. “The dirty dogs.” She stood transfixed. She could not have run if she had wanted to. Her legs seemed suddenly no part of her﻿—remote, melting beneath her, and yet pricked with a thousand pins and needles. Yancey strolled leisurely over to where the white hat lay in the dust. He stooped carelessly, his back to the crowd on the saloon porch, picked up the hat, surveyed it, and reached toward his pocket for his handkerchief. At that movement there was a rush and a scramble on the porch. Tilted chairs leaped forward, heels clattered, a door slammed. The white-aproned proprietor who, tray in hand, had been standing idly in the doorway, vanished as though he had been blotted out by blackness. Of the group only three men remained. One of these leaned insolently against a porch post, a second stood warily behind him, and a third was edging prudently toward the closed door. There was nothing to indicate who had fired the shot that had sent Yancey’s hat spinning.
Yancey, now half turned toward them, had taken his fine white handkerchief from his pocket, had shaken out its ample folds with a gesture of elegant leisure, and, hat in hand, was flicking the dust from his headgear. This done he surveyed the hat critically, seemed to find it little the worse for its experience unless, perhaps, one excepts the two neat round holes that were drilled, back and front, through the peak of its crown. He now placed it on his head again with a gesture almost languid, tossed the fine handkerchief into the road, and with almost the same gesture, or with another so lightning quick that Sabra’s eye never followed it, his hand went to his hip. There was the crack of a shot.

===Chinese===

“他们知道我的斤两。我不是佩格勒，吓不倒的。他们是不喜欢我这顶白帽子，实情就是这样。昨夜在阳光西南酒馆，他们激我今天早上还敢戴。无非试试我。他们没种明着来——”

这句话永远没说完。萨布拉听见耳边一声古怪的嗡。什么东西唱着——*咻*！扬西的白阔边帽打着旋飞进了路尘。

萨布拉嘴张开了，像在尖叫，可她想发出的声音只微弱地出来一丝，像蛙鸣。

“站在原地别动，”扬西命令，声音低而稳。“这群脏狗。”她呆立当场。就是想跑也跑不动了。双腿忽然不像她的一部分——遥远，在她身下融化，却又扎着千百根针。扬西慢悠悠地踱到白帽躺在尘土里的地方。他随意一俯身，背朝着酒馆廊子上的人群，拾起帽子，端详一番，伸手到口袋里去掏手帕。就这一动，廊子上一阵慌乱奔逃。斜倚的椅子跳起来，鞋跟乱响，一扇门砰地关上。那系着白围裙、正端着盘子懒洋洋站在门口的店主，像被一块黑抹掉了。那群人只剩三个。一个无赖地斜倚着廊柱，第二个警惕地躲在他身后，第三个正谨慎地蹭向关着的门。看不出那一枪——把扬西的帽子打飞的那一枪——是谁放的。

扬西此刻半转过身对着他们，已经从口袋里取出那条精美的白手帕，潇洒从容地一抖，抖开它宽大的褶子，一手拿帽，正拂去帽子上的尘土。拂罢，挑剔地端详帽子，似乎觉得它这场遭遇没受多大损失——也许，除非把帽顶正中前后穿过的两个利落圆洞算在内。随后他带着一种近乎慵懒的姿态把帽子重新戴上，把那条好手帕朝路上一抛，而几乎同一个动作——或者说另一个快得萨布拉的眼睛根本追不上的动作——他的手已经到了胯边。一声枪响的脆裂。

===Original===

The man who was edging toward the door clapped his hand to his ear and brought his hand away and looked at it, and it was darkly smeared. Yancey still stood in the road, his hand at his thigh, one slim foot, in its fine high-heeled Texas star boot, advanced carelessly. His great head was lowered menacingly. His eyes, steel gray beneath the brim of the white sombrero, looked as Sabra had never before seen them look. They were terrible eyes, merciless, cold, hypnotic. She could only think of the eyes of the rattler that Yancey had whipped to death with the wagon whip on the trip across the prairie.
“A three-cornered piece, you’ll find it, Lon. The Cravat sheep brand.”
“Can’t you take a joke, Yancey?” whined one of the three, his eyes on Yancey’s gun hand.
“Joke﻿—hell!” snarled the man who had been nicked. His hand was clapped over his ear. “God help you, Cravat.”
“He always has,” replied Yancey, piously.
“If your missus wasn’t with you﻿—” began the man whom Yancey had called Lon. Perhaps the rough joke would have ended grimly enough. But here, suddenly, Sabra herself took a hand in the proceedings. Her fright had vanished. These were no longer men, evil, sinister, to be feared, but mean little boys to be put in their place. She now advanced on them in the majesty of her plumes and her silk, her fine eyes flashing, her gloved forefinger admonishing them as if they were indeed naughty children. She was every inch the descendant of the Marcys of France and the very essence of that iron woman, Felice Venable.
“Don’t you ‘missus’ me! You’re a lot of miserable, good-for-nothing loafers, that’s what you are! Shooting at people in the streets. You leave my husband alone. I declare, I’ve a notion to﻿—”
For one ridiculous dreadful moment it looked as though she meant to slap the leathery bearded cheek of the bad man known as Lon Yountis. Certainly she raised her little hand in its neat black kid. The eyes of the three were popping. Lon Yountis ducked his head exactly like an urchin who is about to be smacked by the schoolmarm. Then, with a yelp of pure terror he fled into the saloon, followed by the other two.
Sabra stood a moment. It really looked as though she might make after them. But she thought better of it and sailed down the steps in triumph to behold a crushed, a despairing Yancey.
“Oh, my God, Sabra! What have you done to me!”
“What’s the matter?”

===Chinese===

那个正蹭向房门的人一手捂住耳朵，拿下来一看，一片黑污。扬西还站在路当中，手垂在腿侧，穿精致高跟得克萨斯星靴的一只秀气的脚随意前伸。他的巨头威慑地低着。白帽檐下那双钢灰的眼睛，是萨布拉从未见过的神色。那是可怕的眼睛，无情，冰冷，催眠。她只能想起路上穿过草原时扬西用马鞭抽死的那条响尾蛇的眼睛。

“三只角的，你找找看，朗。克拉瓦特羊群的品牌。”

“你就开不起玩笑，扬西？”三人中一个哭腔说，眼睛盯着扬西持枪的手。

“玩笑——见鬼！”那个被削了一下的嗥道。他一只手死捂着耳朵。“上帝收拾你，克拉瓦特。”

“祂一直在收拾，”扬西虔诚地答。

“要不是你太太在场——”扬西唤作朗的那个开了腔。这句粗野的玩笑也许会以足够阴惨的方式收场。可就在这时，萨布拉本人亲自插了一手。她的恐惧没了。这些人不再是又坏又阴险、要害怕的男人，而是一帮欠管教的坏小子，该给他们点颜色。此刻她以她翎毛和绸缎的全部威仪朝他们进逼，漂亮的眼睛放光，戴手套的食指点着他们，真把他们当成淘气的孩子。她从头到脚都是法兰西马西家的后代，是菲莉丝·维纳布尔那副铁腕的精魂。

“不许‘太太太太’的！你们就是一群可怜巴巴、一无是处的闲汉，就是这个！在大街上朝人开枪。不许碰我丈夫。哼，我真想——”

有那么可笑又可怕的一瞬，她像是要去掴那个外号朗·扬蒂斯的恶徒那张皮革般的络腮腮帮。她当真抬起了那只戴整洁黑羔羊皮手套的小手。三个人的眼珠都要瞪出来了。朗·扬蒂斯把头一缩，活像要挨女先生巴掌的顽童。随后，一声纯粹的恐怖的尖叫，他逃进了酒馆，另两个跟着。

萨布拉站了一忽儿。看那样子真像要追上去。可她想了想，还是没追，凯旋地下了台阶——迎面撞上一个垂头丧气、绝望透顶的扬西。

“哦，我的天，萨布拉！你把我毁了！”

“怎么了？”

===Original===

“This time tomorrow it’ll be all over the whole Southwest, from Mexico to Arkansas, that Yancey Cravat hid behind a woman’s petticoats.”
“But you didn’t. They can’t say so. You shot him very nicely in the ear, darling.” Thus had a scant eighteen hours in the Oklahoma country twisted her normal viewpoint so askew that she did not even notice the grotesquerie of what she had just said.
“They’re telling it now, in there. My God, a woman’s got no call to interfere when men are having a little dispute.”
“Dispute! Why, Yancey Cravat! He shot your hat right off your head!”
“What of it! Little friendly shooting.”
The enormity of this example of masculine clannishness left her temporarily speechless with indignation. “Let’s be getting on,” Yancey continued, calmly. “If we’re going to look at Doc Nisbett’s house we’d better look at it. There are only two or three to be had in the whole town, and his is the pick of them. It’s central” (Central! she thought, looking about her) “and according to what he said last night there’s a room in the front big enough for getting out the paper. It’ll have to be newspaper and law office in one. Then there are four rooms in the back to live in. Plenty.”
“Oh, plenty,” echoed Sabra, thinking of the nine or ten visiting Venables always comfortably tucked away in the various high-ceilinged bedrooms in the Wichita house.
They resumed their walk. Sabra wondered if she had imagined the shooting outside the Red Dog Saloon.
Doc Nisbett (veterinarian), shirtsleeved, shrewd, with generations of New England ancestry behind him, was seated in a chair tipped up against the front of his coveted property. Nothing of the brilliant Southwest sun had mellowed the vinegar of his chemical makeup. In the rush for Territory town sites at the time of the Opening he had managed to lay his gnarled hands on five choice pieces. On these he erected dwellings, tilted his chair up against each in turn, and took his pick of latecomers frantic for some sort of shelter they could call a home. That perjury, thieving, trickery, gun play, and murder had gone into the acquiring of these﻿—as well as many other﻿—sites was not considered important or, for that matter, especially interesting.
The dwelling itself looked like one of Cim’s childish drawings of a house. The roof was an inverted V; there was a front door, a side door, and a spindling little porch.

===Chinese===

“明天这时，从墨西哥到阿肯色，整个西南都会传遍：扬西·克拉瓦特躲在一个女人的裙子后头。”

“可你没有躲。他们没法这么说。你把他耳朵打得很好呢，亲爱的。”俄克拉何马这地方不过十八个钟头，就把她正常的是非观拧得这样歪，她连自己这句话的荒唐都没有察觉。

“他们这会儿正在里头讲呢。我的天，男人们拌嘴的时候，女人家没有插嘴的份儿。”

“拌嘴！亏你说得出，扬西·克拉瓦特！他一枪把你头上的帽子打飞了！”

“那又怎样！小小的友好的射击。”

这男人抱团逻辑的离谱，气得她一时说不出话。“咱们走吧，”扬西若无其事地接着说。“要看尼斯贝特大夫的房子就去看。全镇统共两三座房子，他那是挑剩的头一份。地段居中”（居中！她想着，四下扫了一眼）“照他昨夜说的，前面有间屋子，出报纸尽够了。报纸和律师事务所就合在一处。后面有四间住人。足够了。”

“哦，足够了，”萨布拉应道，想起威奇塔家里那高顶子的几间卧房里，永远舒舒服服塞着的那八九个来做客的维纳布尔家的人。

他们继续走。萨布拉疑心红狗酒馆外那场枪战是不是自己想象出来的。

尼斯贝特大夫（兽医）挽着衬衫袖子，精明，身后压着好几代新英格兰的家谱，正斜倚在椅子里，椅子翘起来靠在他那座奇货可居的房产前脸上。西南部这灿烂的太阳，一丝也没能把他人造醋酸的本质晒软。开放时抢领地镇址的那阵疯抢里，他成功地把两只疙疙瘩瘩的手按在了五块上好的地皮上。他在这些地皮上盖起房子，把椅子轮流往各家一靠，从那些发疯地想寻个能叫作家的栖身之所的后来者中间挑肥拣瘦。伪誓、偷窃、欺骗、枪战和杀人，都曾掺进这些——还有许多别的——地皮的到手过程里；这被认为无关紧要，甚至谈不上有趣。

房子本身像西姆小孩子画的房子。屋顶一个倒V；有前门、侧门，一道细脚伶仃的小门廊。

===Original===

It was a box, a shelter merely, as angular and unlovely as the man who owned it. The walls were no more than partitions, the floors boards laid on dirt.
Taking her cue from Yancey﻿—“Lovely,” murmured Sabra, agonized. The mantel ornaments that had been Cousin Dabney’s wedding present! The handwoven monogrammed linen! The silver cake dish with the carefree cupids. The dozen solid silver coffee spoons! “Do very nicely. Perfectly comfortable. I see. I see. I see.”
“There you are!” They stood again on the porch, the tour completed. Yancey clapped his hands together gayly, as though by so doing he had summoned a genie who had tossed up the house before their very eyes. In the discussion of monthly rental he had been a child in the hands of this lean and grasping New Englander. “There you are! That’s all settled.” He struck an attitude. “ ‘Survey our empire, and behold our home!’ ”
“Heh, hold on a minute,” rasped Doc Nisbett. “How about water?”
“Sabra, honey, you settle these little matters between you﻿—you and the Doc﻿—will you? I’ve got to run down the street and see Jesse Rickey about putting up the press and setting up the type racks and helping me haul the form tables, and then we’ve got the furniture to buy for the house. Meet you down the street at Hefner’s Furniture Store. Ten minutes.”
He was off, with a flirt of his coat tails. She would have called, “Yancey! Don’t leave me!” but for a prideful reluctance to show fear before this dour-visaged man with the tight lips and the gimlet eyes. From the first he had seemed to regard her with disfavor. She could not imagine why. It was, of course, his Puritan New England revulsion against her plumes, her silks, her faintly Latin beauty.
“Well, now,” repeated Doc Nisbett, nasally, “about water.”
“Water?”
“How much you going to need? Renting this house depends on how much water you think you going to need. How many barrels.”
Sabra had always taken water for granted, like air and sunshine. It was one of the elements. It was simply there. But since leaving Wichita there was always talk of water. Yancey, on the prairie journey, made it the basis of their camping site.
“Oh, barrels,” she now repeated, trying to appear intensely practical. “Well, let﻿—me﻿—see. There’s cooking, of course, and all the cleaning around the house, and drinking, and bathing. I always give Cim his bath in the evening if I can.

===Chinese===

它是一只箱子，仅仅一个遮蔽之所，同它的主人一样见棱见角、其貌不扬。墙不过是一层隔板，地板是搁在泥地上的木板。

萨布拉看着扬西的样子学舌——“可爱极了，”她痛苦地喃喃道。达布尼表哥送的结婚礼物那套壁炉摆设！手织的绣字亚麻布！那个围着无忧无虑小爱神的银蛋糕盘！那一打纯银咖啡匙！“很合用。舒服极了。我明白。我明白。我明白。”

“这就成了！”他们重又站到门廊上，参观完毕。扬西快活地拍起手来，仿佛这么一拍，就唤出了一个精灵，在他们眼皮底下变出了这座房子。谈到月租时，他完全是这个精瘦贪财的新英格兰人手里的孩子。“这就成了！都定了。”他摆出一个造型。“‘巡视我们的帝国，看我们的家！’”

“欸，且慢，”尼斯贝特大夫哑着嗓子说。“水呢？”

“萨布拉，宝贝，这些小事你同大夫之间料理料理——你和大夫——好不好？我得跑一趟街那头，找杰西·里基搭机器、立字架、帮我搬版台，然后家里还得买家具。街那头赫夫纳家具店见。十分钟。”

他衣摆一甩，走了。她本想喊“扬西！别丢下我！”却碍于一份骄傲，不愿在这张紧闭着嘴唇、长着锥子眼的阴沉面孔前露怯。从一见面，他就似乎对她没什么好脸色。她不明白为什么。当然，那是他清教徒的新英格兰脾气，见不得她的翎毛、她的绸缎、她那微带拉丁的风致。

“那么，”尼斯贝特大夫又哼着鼻子，“说水。”

“水？”

“你们要用多少？租这房子，就看你们觉得要用多少水。几桶。”

萨布拉一向把水当作空气和阳光一样理所当然。它是元素。它本来就在那儿。可自打离开威奇塔，话题里总有水。草原路上，扬西拿它定扎营的地点。

“哦，桶，”她重复，竭力显得精明能干。“好，让——我——想想。当然有做饭，屋里屋外的打扫，喝的，洗的。我总要给西姆洗澡，能洗的话总在晚上。”

===Original===

You wouldn’t believe how dirty that child gets by the end of the day. His knees﻿—oh, yes﻿—well, I should think ten barrels a day would be enough.”
“Ten barrels,” said Doc Nesbitt, in a flat voice utterly devoid of expression, “a day.”
“I should think that would be ample,” Sabra repeated, judiciously.
Doc Nisbett now regarded Sabra with a look of active dislike. Then he did a strange thing. He walked across the little porch, shut the front door, locked it, put the key in his pocket, seated himself in the chair and tilted it up against the wall at exactly the angle at which they had come upon him.
Sabra stood there. Seeing her, it would have been almost impossible to believe that anyone so bravely decked out in silk and plumes and pink roses could present a figure so bewildered, so disconsolate, so defeated. Literally, she did not know what to do. She had met and surmounted many strange experiences in these last ten days. But she had been born of generations of women to whom men had paid homage. Perhaps in all her life she had never encountered the slightest discourtesy in a man, much less this abysmal boorishness.
She looked at him, her face white, shocked. She looked up, in embarrassment, at the glaring steel sky; she looked down at the blinding red dust, she looked helplessly in the direction that Yancey had so blithely taken. She glanced again at Doc Nisbett, propped so woodenly against the wall of his hateful house. His eye was as cold, as glassy, as unseeing as the eye of a dead fish.
She should, of course, have gone straight up to him and said, “Do you mean that ten barrels are too much? I didn’t know. I am new to all this. Whatever you say.”
But she was young, and inexperienced, and full of pride, and terribly offended. So without another word she turned and marched down the dusty street. Her head in its plumed hat was high. On either cheek burned a scarlet patch. Her eyes, in her effort to keep back the hot tears, were blazing, liquid, enormous. She saw nothing. From the saloons that lined the street there came, even at this hour of the morning, yelps and the sound of music.
And then a fearful thing happened to Sabra Cravat.
Down the street toward her came a galloping cowboy in sombrero and chaps and six-shooters. Sabra was used to such as he. Full of her troubles, she was scarcely aware that she had glanced at him.

===Chinese===

“你不会相信那孩子一天下来能有多脏。他的膝盖——哦，是——唔，我想一天十桶总够了吧。”

“十桶，”尼斯贝特大夫说，声音平平，毫无表情，“一天。”

“我想那该绰绰有余了，”萨布拉斟酌着重复。

尼斯贝特大夫此刻看萨布拉的眼神，已是活生生的嫌恶。随后他做了一件怪事。他穿过小门廊，关上前门，上了锁，钥匙揣进兜里，在椅子里坐下，把椅子翘起来靠在墙上——翘的角度，同他们初见他时分毫不差。

萨布拉站在那里。任谁见了，也几乎不能相信：一个这样绸缎、翎毛、粉红玫瑰披挂齐整的人，竟能摆出这样一副茫然、这样凄惶、这样一败涂地的姿态。她是真的不知道该怎么办。这十来天她遭遇过、也应付过许多奇事。可她生在一个世系的女儿群里——那些世系的女人，男人是向来敬奉的。她一生中恐怕从没在男人那里受过一点点怠慢，更别提这种无底的无礼。

她望着他，脸色发白，震骇。她窘迫地抬头望那刺目的钢色天空；低头望那晃眼的红尘；又无可奈何地朝扬西欢天喜地离去的方向望。她又看了一眼尼斯贝特大夫，木头似的靠在他那讨厌的房子墙上。他的眼睛像死鱼的眼，又冷，又亮，又无神。

她本该径直走上前说：“您是说十桶太多了？我不知道。这些我都是新学的。您说怎样就怎样。”

可她年轻，没阅历，一身傲气，又委屈极了。于是一言不发，她转身在尘土的街道上昂然而去。插翎毛的帽子昂着头。两颊各烧着一块猩红。为了憋住热泪，她的眼睛烧得亮亮的、水汪汪的、老大老大。她什么也看不见。沿街的酒馆里，哪怕在这样的清晨，也传出嚎叫和乐声。

接着，一桩可怕的事落到了萨布拉·克拉瓦特头上。

街那头，一个戴阔边帽、穿皮护腿、挎六响枪的牛仔朝她纵马奔来。这一路人萨布拉见惯了。她满腹心事，几乎不曾觉得自己看了他一眼。

===Original===

How could she know that he was just up from the plains of Texas, that this raw town represented for him the height of effete civilization, that he was, in celebration of his arrival, already howling drunk as befits a cowboy just off the range, and that never before in his life (he was barely twenty-three) had he seen a creature so gorgeous as this which now came toward him, all silk, plumes, roses, jet, scarlet cheeks, and great liquid eyes. Up he galloped; stared, wheeled, flung himself off his horse, ran toward her in his high-heeled cowboy boots (strangely enough all that Sabra could recall about him afterward were those boots as he came toward her. The gay tops were of shiny leather, and alternating around them was the figure of a dancing girl with flaring skirts, and a poker hand of cards which later she learned was a royal flush, all handsomely embossed on the patent leather cuffs of the boots). She realized, in a flash of pure terror, that he was making straight for her. She stood, petrified. He came nearer, he stood before her, he threw his arms like steel bands about her, he kissed her full on the lips, released her, leaped on his horse, and was off with a bloodcurdling yelp and a clatter and a whirl of dust.
She thought that she was going to be sick, there, in the road. Then she began to run, fleetly but awkwardly, in her flounced and bustled silken skirts. Hefner’s Furniture Store. Hefner’s Furniture Store. Hefner’s Furniture Store. She saw it at last. Hefner’s Furniture and Undertaking Parlors. A crude wooden shack, like the rest. She ran in. Yancey. Yancey! Everything looked dim to her bewildered and sun-blinded eyes. Someone came toward her. A large moist man, in shirtsleeves. Hefner, probably. My husband. My husband, Yancey Cravat. No. Sorry, ma’am. Ain’t been in, I know of. Anything I can do for you, ma’am?
She blurted it, hysterically. “A man﻿—a cowboy﻿—I was walking along﻿—he jumped off his horse﻿—he﻿—I never saw him b﻿—he kissed me﻿—there on the street in broad daylight﻿—a cowboy﻿—he kissed﻿—”
“Why, ma’am, don’t take on so. Young fella off the range, prob’ly. Up from Texas, more’n likely, and never did see a gorgeous critter like yourself, if you’ll pardon my mentioning it.”
Her voice rose in her hysteria. “You don’t understand! He kissed me. He k-k-k-k﻿—” racking sobs.
“Now, now, lady. He was drunk, and you kind of went to his head.

===Chinese===

她怎么知道，他刚从得克萨斯的平原上来，这座生坯子小城在他眼里已是骄奢文明的极顶；她怎么知道，他为了庆祝自己进城，已经喝得鬼哭狼嚎——一个刚下牧场的牛仔正该如此；她怎么知道，他生平（他才二十三岁）从没见过这样一位华彩照人的造物：绸缎、翎毛、玫瑰、黑玉、绯红的双颊、水汪汪的大眼睛，此刻正朝他走来。他纵马而上；瞪着，一兜，翻身下马，穿着高跟牛仔靴朝她奔来（说来奇怪，事后萨布拉关于他记得的，只有他跑过来时那双靴子。花哨的靴口是亮面皮，一圈间隔着印着舞女的图案——裙裾翻飞——和一手扑克牌，后来她才知道那是一手同花大顺，全都堂皇地凸印在靴口的漆皮翻边上）。她在纯粹的恐怖的一闪里明白过来：他直冲她来了。她呆立着。他近了，站到她面前，双臂像钢箍一样抱住她，对着她的嘴唇结结实实亲了一口，放开她，跃上马，一声令人胆裂的呼哨，一溜尘土，没影了。

她以为自己当场就要吐在路上了。随后她跑起来，飞快而笨拙，拖着镶褶、垫臀的绸裙。赫夫纳家具店。赫夫纳家具店。赫夫纳家具店。终于望见了。赫夫纳家具与殡仪馆。一座粗陋的木棚，同别家一样。她冲进去。扬西。扬西！她那惶乱、被太阳晃花的眼里，一切都模模糊糊。有人朝她走来。一个大块头、汗津津的男人，挽着衬衫袖子。多半就是赫夫纳。我丈夫。我丈夫，扬西·克拉瓦特。没有，太太。不巧，就我所知没来过。有什么我能效劳的吗，太太？

她歇斯底里地脱口而出。“一个人——一个牛仔——我正在街上走——他跳下马——他——我从来没见过他——他亲了我——光天化日，就在街上——一个牛仔——他亲了——”

“嗐，太太，别这么难受。多半是牧场上下来的小伙子。十有八九是打得克萨斯上来的，从来没见过您这样标致的人儿，恕我多嘴。”

她的嗓音随着歇斯底里拔高。“你不明白！他亲了我。他亲、亲、亲——”撕心的抽噎。

“好了，好了，太太。他喝醉了，你嘛，有点叫他冲昏了头。

===Original===

He’ll ride back to Texas, and you’ll be none the worse for it.”
At this calloused viewpoint of a tragedy she broke down completely and buried her head on her folded arms atop the object nearest at hand. Her slim body shook with her sobs. Her tears flowed. She cried aloud like a child.
But at that a plaintive but firm note of protest entered Mr. Hefner’s voice.
“Excuse me, ma’am, but that’s velvet you’re crying on, and water spots velvet something terrible. If you’d just lean on something else﻿ ﻿…”
She raised herself from the object on which she had collapsed, weeping, and looked at it with brimming eyes that widened in horror as she realized that she had showered her tears on that pride of Hefner’s Furniture and Undertaking establishment, the newly arrived white velvet coffin (child’s size) intended for show window purposes alone.

===Chinese===

“他骑马回得克萨斯去，你一点亏也没吃。”

听到这番对一场悲剧的铁石心肠的看法，她彻底垮了，把头埋进交叠的胳膊里，伏在手边最近的物件上。她纤细的身子随着抽噎发抖。泪水横流。她像个孩子放声哭了。

可这一来，赫夫纳先生的声音里升起一声委婉却坚决的抗议。

“对不起，太太，您哭的这可是丝绒，水点子糟蹋丝绒糟蹋得凶。您换个别的东西靠靠，成不成……”

她从她伏在上面痛哭的那件东西上撑起身，噙满泪水的眼睛看着它，惊恐地越睁越大——她这才明白，她的眼泪，全洒在了赫夫纳家具与殡仪馆的镇馆之宝上：那具新到的白丝绒棺材（儿童尺寸），本打算只作橱窗陈列之用。

## VII / 七
===Original===

From Doc Nisbett, Yancey received laconic information to the effect that the house had been rented by a family whose aquatic demands were more modest than Sabra’s. Sabra was inconsolable, but Yancey did not once reproach her for her mistake. It was characteristic of him that he was most charming and considerate in crises which might have been expected to infuriate him. “Never mind, sugar. Don’t take on like that. We’ll find a house. And, anyway, we’re here. That’s the main thing. God, when I think of those years in Wichita!”
“Why, Yancey! I thought you were happy there.”
“ ‘A prison’d soul, lapped in Elysium.’ Almost five years in one place﻿—that’s the longest stretch I’ve ever done, honey. Five years, back and forth like a trail horse; walking down to the *Wigwam* office in the morning, setting up personal and local items and writing editorials for a smug citizenry interested in nothing but the new waterworks. Walking back to dinner at noon, sitting on the veranda evenings, looking at the vegetables in the garden or the Venables in the house until I couldn’t tell vegetables from Venables and began to think, by God, that I was turning into one or the other myself.”
He groaned with relief, stretched his mighty arms, shook himself like a great shaggy lion. In all this welter of red clay and Indians and shirtsleeves and tobacco juice and drought he seemed to find a beauty and an exhilaration that eluded Sabra quite. But then Sabra, after those first two days, had ceased to search for a reason for anything. She met and accepted the most grotesque, the most fantastic happenings. When she looked back on the things she had done and the things she had said in the first few hours of her Oklahoma experience it was as though she were tolerantly regarding the naivetes of a child. Ten barrels of water a day! She knew now that water, in this burning land, was a precious thing to be measured out like wine. Life here was an anachronism, a great crude joke. It was hard to realize that while the rest of the United States, in this year of 1889, was living a conventionally civilized and primly Victorian existence, in which plumbing, gaslight, trees, gardens, books, laws, millinery, Sunday churchgoing, were taken for granted, here in this Oklahoma country life had been set back according to the frontier standards of half a century earlier.

===Chinese===

从多克·尼斯贝特那里，扬西得到一句简短的消息：那房子租给了一户人家，人家的用水需求没有萨布拉的大。萨布拉伤心得没法安慰，可扬西一句也没有责怪她这个错。这正是他的本色：在那些换个人要气炸的危机里，他偏偏最迷人、最体贴。“不要紧，甜心。别这样难过。我们会找到房子的。再说，我们到了。这才是主要的。老天，一想起威奇托那些年！”
“咦，扬西！我还以为你在那儿很快活呢。”
“‘一个囚禁的灵魂，裹在极乐里。’在一个地方一待几乎五年——那是我蹲过的最长的一班，亲爱的。五年，像匹拉道的老马来来回回；早晨走到《棚屋报》编辑部，排一点人事和本地消息，给一群只关心新自来水工程的沾沾自喜的市民写社论。中午走回家吃午饭，傍晚坐在游廊上，看园子里的蔬菜或宅子里的文纳尔们，直到我分不清哪是蔬菜哪是文纳尔，开始觉得，天哪，我自己也要变成这两样中的一样了。”
他庆幸地哼了一声，舒展那双伟力的臂膀，像一头鬃毛蓬蓬的大狮子抖了抖身子。在这红土、印第安人、衬衫袖子、烟草汁和旱灾的一片混沌里，他仿佛找到了一种美和一种振奋，萨布拉却全然寻不着。不过也是，头两天过后，萨布拉就不再为任何事寻找缘由了。最古怪、最荒唐的事，她碰上便受下。回想她俄克拉何马头几个钟头里说过的话、做过的事，就像宽容地看一个孩子的幼稚。一天十桶水！她如今知道，在这片火燎的土地上，水是件宝贝，得像酒一样论份量给。这里的生活是个时代错位，一个粗大的玩笑。很难意识到：正当合众国的其余部分，在这 1889 年，过着常规的文明日子——维多利亚式的规行矩步，水管、煤气灯、树木、花园、书籍、法律、女帽、礼拜天上教堂，一概视为当然——俄克拉何马这里的生活却倒退了半个世纪，退回边疆的标准。

===Original===

Literally she was pioneering in a wilderness surrounded but untouched by civilization.
Yancey had reverted. Always﻿—even in his staidest Wichita incarnation﻿—a somewhat incredibly romantic figure, he now was remarkable even in this town of fantastic humans gathered from every corner of the brilliantly picturesque Southwest. His towering form, his curling locks, his massive head, his vibrant voice, his dashing dress, his florid speech, his magnetic personality drew attention wherever he went. On the day following their arrival Yancey had taken from his trunk a pair of silver-mounted ivory-handled six-shooters and a belt and holster studded with silver. She had never before seen them. She had not known that he possessed these grim and gaudy trappings. His white sombrero he had banded with a rattlesnake skin of gold and silver, with glass eyes, a treasure also produced from the secret trunk, as well as a pair of gold-mounted spurs which further enhanced the Texas star boots. Thus bedecked for his legal and editorial pursuits he was by far the best dressed and most spectacular male in all the cycloramic Oklahoma country. He had always patronized a good tailor, and because the local talent was still so limited in this new community he later sent as far as San Antonio, Texas, when his wardrobe needed replenishing.
Sabra learned many astounding things in these first few days, and among the most terrifying were the things she learned about the husband to whom she had been happily married for more than five years. She learned, for example, that this Yancey Cravat was famed as the deadliest shot in all the deadly shooting Southwest. He had the gift of being able to point his six-shooters without sighting, as one would point with a finger. It was a direction-born gift in him and an enviable one in this community. He was one of the few who could draw and fire two six-shooters at once with equal speed and accuracy. His hands would go to his hips with a lightning gesture that yet was so smooth, so economical that the onlooker’s eye scarcely followed it. He could hit his mark as he walked, as he ran, as he rode his horse. He practised a great deal. From the back door of their cabin Sabra and Cim and rolling-eyed Isaiah used to stand watching him. He sometimes talked of wind and trajectory.
