字面意义上，她就是在荒野里拓荒，文明四面环绕，却一指未沾。
扬西返本归原了。他向来——即便在威奇托最古板的化身里——已是一个浪漫得难以置信的人物，如今在这座从绚丽如画的大西南每个角落汇聚而来的奇人城里，他依然出挑。高大的身形，鬈曲的头发，硕大的头颅，震颤的嗓音，招摇的衣装，华美的辞令，磁石般的个性，走到哪儿都引人注目。到达后的第二天，扬西从他的箱子里取出一对银饰的白象牙柄六响枪，还有一条镶银的枪带和枪套。她从没见过这些。她不知道他拥有这些森然而花哨的行头。他那顶白阔边帽，他给缠上一条金银色的响尾蛇皮，嵌着玻璃眼；这也是从那只秘密箱子里变出来的宝贝；另有一副金装马刺，越发衬得那双得克萨斯星靴神气。就这样披挂齐整去从事他的法律与编辑事业，他是这全景画卷般的俄克拉何马全境衣着最讲究、最壮观的男性。他向来光顾好裁缝，而且因为这新市镇里的本地手艺实在有限，后来衣橱需要添置时，他一直写信远至得克萨斯州的圣安东尼奥去订。
头几天里萨布拉知道了许许多多骇人的事，而其中最可怕的，是她了解到这个她幸福地嫁了五年多的丈夫。譬如她了解到：这位扬西·克拉瓦特，在整个以枪弹闻名的西南部，是以最致命的枪手著称的。他有凭手指般随指随中的本事，六响枪不用瞄准。这是他与生俱来的天赋，在这一带人人艳羡。他是寥寥几个能同时拔出两把六响枪、速度与准头分毫不差的人之一。他双手向腰间一探，那手势闪电一般，却又那么圆滑、那么省净，旁观者的眼睛几乎跟不上。他走路能中的，奔跑能中的，骑在马上也能中的。他练得很多。他们小屋的后门外，萨布拉和西姆还有眼睛滴溜转的以赛亚，常站着看他。他有时谈风，谈弹道。

===Original===

You had to make allowance mathematically, he said, for this ever-blowing Oklahoma wind. Sabra was vaguely uneasy. Wichita had not been exactly effete, and Dodge City, Kansas, was notoriously a gunplay town. But here no man walked without his six-shooters strapped to his body. On the very day of her harrowing encounter with Doc Nisbett and the cowboy, Sabra, her composure regained, had gone with Yancey to see still another house owner about the possible renting of his treasure. The man was found in his crude one-room shack which he used as a combination dwelling and land office. He and Yancey seemed to know each other. Sabra was no longer astonished to find that Yancey, twenty-four hours after his arrival, appeared to be acquainted with everyone in the town. The man glanced up at them from the rough pine table at which he was writing.
“Howdy, Yancey!”
“Howdy, Cass!”
Yancey, all grace, performed an introduction. The lean, leather-skinned house owner wiped his palm on his pants’ seat in courtly fashion and, thus purified, extended a hospitable hand to Sabra. Yancey revealed to him their plight.
“Well, now, say, that’s plumb terr’ble, that is. Might be I can help you out﻿—you and your good lady here. But say, Yancey, just let me step out, will you, to the corner, and mail this here letter. The bag’s goin’ any minute now.”
He licked and stamped the envelope, rose, and took from the table beside him his broad leather belt with its pair of holstered six-shooters, evidently temporarily laid aside for comfort while writing. This he now strapped quickly about his waist with the same unconcern that another man would use in slipping into his coat. He merely was donning conventional street attire for the well-dressed man of the locality. He picked up his sheaf of envelopes and stepped out. In three minutes he was back, and affably ready to talk terms with them.
It was, perhaps, this simple and sinister act, more than anything she had hitherto witnessed, that impressed Sabra with the utter lawlessness of this new land to which her husband had brought her.
This house, so dearly held by the man called Cass, turned out to be a four-room dwelling inadequate to their needs, and they were in despair at the thought of being obliged to wait until a house could be built. Then Yancey had a brilliant idea. He found a two-room cabin made of rough boards.

他说，这永远在刮的俄克拉何马的风，你得拿数学去折算。萨布拉隐隐不安。威奇托谈不上文弱，堪萨斯的道奇城更是有名的枪镇。可这里，没有一个人出门不把六响枪挎在身上。就在她同多克·尼斯贝特和那牛仔道遇的当天，萨布拉惊魂甫定，又同扬西去见另一位房主，谈他那宝贝的出租可能。那人在他粗陋的单间棚屋里被找到了——那屋子住宅兼地产办公。他和扬西像是旧识。到镇上才二十四小时，扬西就仿佛全镇人人认识，这已不再使萨布拉惊奇了。那人从粗糙的松木写字台上抬起头来看他们。
“你好啊，扬西！”
“你好，卡斯！”
扬西优雅周到地作了介绍。瘦削的、皮革皮肤的房子主人彬彬有礼地在裤腿上擦了擦手心，这才伸出一只好客的手给萨布拉。扬西向他和盘托出他们的窘境。
“咦，这个，说真的，太糟糕了，可不是。兴许我能帮你们一把——帮你和你这位好太太。不过这样，扬西，你容我出去一步，到街角把这封信寄了。邮袋随时要走。”
他舐了信封贴上邮票，起身，从手边的桌上拿起他那条宽皮带和插在上面的两把六响枪——显然是写字时为舒服暂时摘下的。此刻他把皮带麻利地系上腰，那不在意劲儿，同另一个人穿外衣一样。他不过是穿戴本地体面人的常规街装。他拈起一沓信封出去了。三分钟后回来，和和气气，准备同他们谈价钱。
大约正是这个简单而凶险的动作，胜过她此前见过的一切，叫萨布拉深深领教了丈夫带她来的这片新土地那种彻头彻尾的无法无天。
这位卡斯先生视为至宝的房子，原来只有四个房间，不够他们用；一想到非得等一幢房子盖起来不可，他们陷入了绝望。这时扬西想出一个妙主意。他寻到一座粗木板搭的两间小屋。

===Original===

This was hauled to the site of the main house, plastered, and﻿—added to it﻿—provided them with a six-room combination dwelling, newspaper plant, and law office. There was all the splendor of sitting room, dining room, bedroom, and kitchen to live in. One room of the small attached cabin was a combination law and newspaper office. The other served as composing room and print shop. The Hefner Furniture and Undertaking Parlors provided them with furniture﻿—a large wooden bedstead to fit Sabra’s mattress and spring; a small bed for Cim; tables, chairs﻿—the plainest of everything. The few bits of furnishing and ornament that Sabra had brought with her from Wichita were fortunately﻿—or unfortunately﻿—possessed of the enduring beauty of objects which have been carefully made by hands exquisitely aware of line, texture, color, and further enhanced by the rich mellow patina that comes with the years. Her pieces of silver, of china, of fine linen were as out of place in this roughly furnished cabin of unpainted lumber as a court lady in a peasant’s hovel. In two days Sabra was a housewife established in her routine as though she had been at it for years. A pan of biscuits in the oven of the wood-burning kitchen stove; a dress pattern of calico, cut out and ready for basting, on the table in the sitting room.
Setting up the newspaper plant and law office was not so simple. Yancey, for example, was inclined to write his first editorial entitled “Whither Oklahoma?” before the hand press had been put together. He was more absorbed in the effect of the sign tacked up over the front of the shop than he was in the proper mechanical arrangement of the necessary appliances inside. **The Oklahoma Wigwam**, read the sign in block letters two feet high, so that the little cabin itself was almost obscured. Then, beneath, in letters scarcely less impressive: **Yancey Cravat, Prop. and Editor. Attorney at Law. Notary**.
The placing of this sign took the better part of a day, during which time all other work was suspended. While the operation was in progress Yancey crossed the road fifty times, ostensibly to direct matters from a proper vantage point of criticism, but really to bask in the dazzling effect of the bold fat black letters. As always in the course of such proceedings on the part of the laboring male there was much hoarse shouting, gesticulation, and general rumpus.

===Chinese===

把它拖到正屋的地基上，抹上泥，一并接上去——他们便有了一套六间的居所兼报馆兼律师楼。客厅、饭厅、卧室、厨房，住得堂堂皇皇。接出来的小屋里，一间是律师兼报务办公室，另一间作排字间和印刷间。赫夫纳家具殡仪馆供给了他们家具——一张大木床配萨布拉的床垫和弹簧；西姆的一张小床；桌子，椅子——一切从简。萨布拉从威奇托带来的那几件摆设和饰物，幸而——或不幸——却具有那种经久的美：出自一双对线条、质感、色彩极有感觉的巧手精心制就，又添了岁月的醇厚包浆。她的银器、瓷器、细麻布，在这间不上漆的粗木小屋里，就像宫廷贵妇落在了农户的茅棚里。两天之内，萨布拉成了位按部就班立住门户的主妇，仿佛已经干了多年。烧柴的灶膛里一锅饼干；客厅桌上，一份裁好待绲的印花布衣裳样子。
布置报馆和律师楼就不这么简单了。譬如扬西，手摇印刷机还没组装起来，就想先写他的第一篇社论，题目是《俄克拉何马向何处去？》。招牌钉在铺面正上方之后的效果，比屋里那些必要器械的合理机械布置更叫他神往。招牌上是两英尺高的方块大字：**俄克拉何马棚屋报**，几乎把小屋本身都遮住了。底下，是几乎同样气派的稍小一点的字：**扬西·克拉瓦特，发行人兼主编。律师。公证人**。
钉这块招牌花去大半天，其间一切活计全部停工。施工期间，扬西横穿马路五十来趟，名义上是为了站到合适的角度监督，实际上是为了在那粗黑大字的炫目效果里晒一晒。正如劳动者男性进行这类工程时的一贯情形，吼声、比划和总骚乱少不了。

===Original===

To Sabra, coming to the door from time to time, dish towel or ladle in hand, the clamor seemed out of all proportion to the results achieved. She thought (privately) that two women could have finished the job in half the time with one tenth the fuss. She still was far too feminine, tactful, and in love with her husband to say so. Cim enjoyed the whole thing enormously, as did his black satellite, bodyguard, and playmate, Isaiah. They capered, shouted, whooped, and added much to the din.
Yancey from across the road﻿—“Lift her up a little higher that end!”
“What say?” from the perspiring Jesse Rickey, his assistant.
“That end﻿—up! **No! Up!** I said, **Up!**”
“Well, which end, f’r Chris’ sakes, right or left?”
“Right! **Right!** God Almighty, man, don’t you know your right from your left?”
“Easy now. E‑e‑e‑esy! Over now. Over! There! That’s﻿—no﻿—yeh﻿—now head her a little this way.﻿ ﻿…”
“How’s that?”
“Oh, my land’s sakes alive!” thought Sabra, going back to her orderly kitchen. “Men make such a lot of work of nothing.”
It was her first admission that the male of the species might be fallible. A product of Southern training, even though a daily witness, during her girlhood, to the dominance of her matriarchal mother over her weak and war-shattered father, she had been bred to the tradition that the male was always right, always to be deferred to. Yancey, still her passionate lover, had always treated her tenderly, as a charming little fool, and this role she had meekly﻿—even gratefully﻿—accepted. But now suspicion began to rear its ugly head. These last three weeks had shown her that the male was often mistaken, as a sex, and that Yancey was almost always wrong as an individual. But these frightening discoveries she would not yet admit even to herself. Also that he was enthralled by the dramatics of any plan he might conceive, but that he often was too impatient of its mechanics to carry it through to completion.
“Yancey, this case of type’s badly pied.” Jesse Rickey, journeyman printer and periodic drunkard, was responsible for this misfortune, having dropped a case, face down, in the dust of the road while assisting Yancey in the moving. “It’ll have to be sorted before you can get out a paper.”
“Oh, Rickey’ll tend to that. I’ve got a lot of important work to do.

===Chinese===

萨布拉不时到门口来，手里或抹布或勺子，觉得那喧腾同成果完全不成比例。她（私下）想，两个女人干这活，一半时间、十分之一的动静就完了。她还太女性、太乖巧、也太爱丈夫，没说出口。西姆对这一切喜欢得不得了，他的黑伙伴、护卫兼玩伴以赛亚也是。他们蹦着、喊着、嗬嗬叫着，添了许多响动。
马路对面的扬西喊——“那一头，再抬高一点儿！”
“什么？”汗津津的助手杰西·里基问。
“那一头——上！**不，上！**我说，**上！**”
“到底哪头啊，老天，左边还是右边？”
“右边！**右边！**我的老天爷，伙计，你左右都分不清吗？”
“稳住。稳——稳——住！过去。过去！好！这就——不——对——再朝这边挪一点。……”
“这下如何？”
“哦，我的老天爷！”萨布拉想着，回到她井井有条的厨房。“男人真是没事找事。”
这是她头一回承认，这一物种的男性也可能出错。她是南方教养的产物；尽管少女时代日日目睹她那家长制的母亲对她那懦弱而战争摧残的父亲的统治，她受的教养却是：男性永远是对的，永远该顺从。扬西，至今仍是她热烈的情人，向来温存地待她，当她是可爱的小傻瓜；这角色她也温顺地——甚至感激地——领受了。可如今怀疑开始昂起它丑陋的头。这三个星期让她看到：男性作为一种性别常会弄错，而扬西作为个人几乎总是错的。可这些可怕的发现，她连对自己还不肯认。也看到：凡他心中筹想的计划，凡有戏剧性，他便痴恋，可对其中的机械细节，他常嫌麻烦，办不到底。
“扬西，这几盘铅字全打散了。”杰西·里基——熟练排字工兼周期性酒鬼——该对这桩不幸负责：帮扬西搬家时，他把一盘铅字面朝下摔在了路尘里。“得先拣字，才出得了报。”
“哦，里基会料理的。我有一大堆要紧事要办。

===Original===

Editorials to write, news to get, lot of real estate transfers﻿—and I’m going to find out who killed Pegler and print it in the first issue if it takes the last drop of blood in me.”
“Oh, please don’t. What does it matter! He’s dead. Maybe he did shoot himself. And besides, you’ve got Cim and me to think of. You can’t let anything happen to you.”
“Let that Yountis gang get away with a thing like that and anything *is* likely to happen to me; the same thing that happened to him. No, sir! I’ll show them, first crack, that the *Oklahoma Wigwam* prints all the news, all the time, knowing no law but the Law of God and the government of these United States! Say, that’s a pretty good slogan. Top of the page, just above the editorial column.”
In the end it was she who sorted the case of pied type. The five years of Yancey’s newspaper ownership in Wichita had familiarized her, almost unconsciously, with many of the mechanical aspects of a newspaper printing shop. She even liked the smell of printer’s ink, of the metal type, of the paper wet from the hand press. She found that the brass and copper thin spaces, used for setting up ads, had no proper container, and at a loss to find one she hit upon the idea of using a muffin tin until a proper receptacle could be found. It never was found, and the muffin tin still served after a quarter of a century had gone by. She was, by that time, sentimental about it, and superstitious.
The hand press was finally set up, and the little job press, and the case rack containing the type. The rollers were in place, and their little stock of paper. Curiously enough, though neither Yancey nor Sabra was conscious of it, it was she who had directed most of this manual work and who had indeed actually performed much of it, with Isaiah and Jesse Rickey to help her. Yancey was off and up the street every ten minutes. Returning, he would lose himself in the placing of his law library, his books of reference, and his favorite volumes, for which he contended there was not enough shelf room in the house proper. He had brought along boxes of books stowed away in the covered wagons.

===Chinese===

社论要写，新闻要采，还有好些地产过户——还有，我要查明是谁杀了佩格勒，把它登在创刊号上，哪怕榨干我最后一滴血。”
“哦，求你别。有什么要紧！人已经死了。兴许真是自尽。再说，你还有我和西姆要考虑。你不能出事。”
“让尤恩蒂斯那帮家伙逍了遥，我倒真可能出事；出他那一样的事。不，先生！第一炮我就要让他们知道，《俄克拉何马棚屋报》刊一切新闻，无时无刻，不认别的法，只认上帝的法和美利坚合众国的政府！咦，这口号不错。放版头上，社论栏正上方。”
到头来，拣那几盘散字的是她。扬西在威奇托办报的五年，使她几乎不知不觉熟悉了报馆排印的许多机械门道。她甚至喜欢印刷油墨、金属铅字、手摇机上取下的微湿纸张的气味。她发现排广告用的黄铜和紫铜薄隔空没有个正经家什盛，一时又寻不着，便想到拿一只烤玛芬的锡盘凑合，等找到正经器皿再说。正经器皿一直没找到，那只玛芬盘便一直用下去，用了四分之一世纪。到那时，她对它已经有了感情，还带着迷信。
手摇机终于装好了，小活件机也装好了，还有装铅字的字架。墨辊就位，那一小批纸张也安顿了。说来奇怪，扬西和萨布拉谁也没意识到：指挥这大部分手工活计的正是她，而且其中许多实际是她亲手做的，帮手是以赛亚和杰西·里基。扬西呢，每十分钟就溜上街去一趟。回来，又一头扎进他法律书、工具书和心爱书籍的摆放里——他一口咬定正屋里没有足够的书架。他随篷车带来了整箱整箱的书。

===Original===

If the combined book wealth contained in all the houses, offices, and shops of the entire Oklahoma country so newly settled could have been gathered in one spot it probably would have been found to number less than this preposterous library of the paradoxical Yancey Cravat. Glib and showy though he was with his book knowledge Yancey still had in these volumes of his the absorption of the true book lover. He gave more attention to the carpenter who put up these crude bookshelves than he had bestowed upon the actual coupling of the two cabins when first they had moved in. The books he insisted on placing himself, picking them up, one by one, and losing himself now in this page, now in that, so that at the end of the long hot afternoon he had accomplished nothing. Blackstone and Kent (ineffectual enough in this lawless land) were shocked to find themselves hobnobbing side by side with *Childe Harold* and the *Decameron*. Culpepper’s *Torts* nestled cosily between the shameless tale of the sprightly Wife of Bath and Yancey’s new and joyously discovered copy of FitzGerald’s *Omar Khayyám*.
Lost to all else he would call happily in to Sabra as she bent over the case rack, her cheek streaked with ink, her fingers stained, her head close to Jesse Rickey’s bleary-eyed one as she sorted type or filled the muffin tin with the metal thin spaces: “Sabe! Oh, Sabe﻿—listen to this.” He would clear his throat. “ ‘Son of Nestor, delight of my heart, mark the flashing of bronze through the echoing halls, and the flashing of gold and of amber and of silver and of ivory. Suchlike, methinks, is the court of Olympian Zeus within, for the world of things that are here; wonder comes over me as I look thereon.’﻿ ﻿… God, Sabra, it’s as fine as the Old Testament. Finer!”
“ ‘The world of things that are here,’ ” echoed Sabra, not bitterly, but with grave common sense. “Perhaps if you’d pay more attention to those, and less to your nonsense in books about gold and silver and ivory, we might get settled.”
But he was ready with a honeyed reply culled from the same book so dear to his heart and his grandiloquent tongue. “ ‘Be not wroth with me hereat, goddess and queen.’ ”
The goddess and queen pushed her hair back from her forehead with a sooty hand, leaving still another smudge of printer’s ink upon that worried surface.

===Chinese===

把整个新定居的俄克拉何马全境所有人家、办公室和铺子里的藏书凑到一处，恐怕也及不上这位自相矛盾的扬西·克拉瓦特这座荒唐藏书楼。尽管他卖弄书本知识时伶俐浮华，对这些书，他却有真正爱书人那种沉迷。他盯木匠搭这些粗陋书架的那份上心，胜过初搬来时对接两间小屋那桩正事。书他坚持亲手上架，一本一本捧起，一会儿耽进这页，一会儿耽进那页，于是一个漫长炎热的下午过去，一事无成。布莱克斯通和肯特（在这片无王法的地方其实顶不了什么用）惊愕地发现，自己同《恰尔德·哈罗尔德》和《十日谈》肩并肩厮混。卡尔佩珀的《侵权法》舒舒服服地夹在巴斯妇人的无耻故事和扬西新近欢天喜地淘到的菲茨杰拉德译《鲁拜集》之间。
他把一切忘个干净，只快活地朝萨布拉喊——她正弯腰在字架前，腮上一道墨，手指乌黑，脑袋凑着杰西·里基那双惺松的醉眼拣字，或往玛芬盘里装薄隔空：“萨比！哦，萨比——听这段。”他清清嗓子。“‘涅斯托尔之子，我心中的欢喜，看那铜光闪烁，穿过回响的大厅；看那金光、琥珀光、银光、象牙光。我猜想，这便是奥林波斯山上宙斯的官廷，里头尽是人间万物；我望着，只有惊奇。’……老天，萨布拉，这不输《旧约》。还强！”
“‘里头尽是人间万物，’”萨布拉应道，不带刻薄，倒带着庄重的常识。“你要是多照看照看这些，少耽你那些金银象牙的胡说八道，我们兴许就安顿好了。”
可他早已从那本他心爱的书里摘好了一句蜜答。“‘女神，王后，切勿为此嗔怪我。’”
女神兼王后用一只乌黑的手把头发从前额撩开，在那操劳的额面上又添了一抹印刷油泥。

===Original===

Jesse Rickey, the printer (known, naturally, to his familiars as “Gin” Rickey, owing to his periods of intemperance), and black Isaiah were, next to Sabra, most responsible for the astounding fact that the Cravat family finally was settled in house and office. The front door, which was the office entrance, faced the wide wallow of the main street. The back and the side doors of the dwelling looked out on a stretch of Oklahoma red clay, littered with the empty tin cans that mark any new American settlement, and especially one whose drought is relieved by the thirst-quenching coolness of tinned tomatoes and peaches. Perhaps the canned tomato, as much as anything, made possible the settling of the vast West and Southwest. In the midst of this clay and refuse, in a sort of shed-kennel, lived little Isaiah; rather, he slept there, like a faithful dog, for all day long he was about the house and the printing office, tireless, willing, invaluable. He belonged to Sabra, body and soul, as completely as though the Civil War had never been. A little servant of twelve, born to labor, he became as dear to Sabra, as accustomed, as one of her own children, despite her Southern training and his black skin. He dried the dishes, a towel tied around his neck; he laid the table; he was playmate and nursemaid for Cim; he ran errands, a swift and splayfooted Mercury; he was a born reporter, and in the course of his day’s scurrying about the town on this errand or that brought into Sabra’s kitchen more items of news and gossip (which were later transferred to the newspaper office) than a whole staff of trained newspaper men could have done. He was so little, so black, so lithe, so harmless looking, that his presence was, more often than not, completely overlooked. The saloon loungers, cowboys, rangers, and homesteaders in and about the town alternately spoiled and plagued him. One minute they were throwing him dimes in the dust for his rendition of his favorite song:

> “King Jesus come a-ridin’ on a milk-white steed,

Wid a rainbow on his shoulder.”

===Chinese===

杰西·里基，那位排字工（熟人自然管他叫“金汤力”里基，因为他一阵一阵贪杯），还有黑以赛亚，是继萨布拉之后功劳最大的人——克拉瓦特一家居然安顿进了住宅和办公室，这桩奇事多半归他们。前门即办公门，面朝主街那片宽阔的泥塘。住宅的后门和侧门外，是一带俄克拉何马红土，散乱着空铁皮罐——凡美国新定居点都点缀着这种罐头，尤其旱灾靠罐头番茄和罐头桃子的清凉解渴来缓解的地方。也许，正是罐头番茄同任何东西一样，成就了辽阔西部和西南部的定居。在这片红土和垃圾中间，一间棚屋狗窝里，住着小以赛亚；说住，不如说睡在那儿，像条忠犬，因为他整日在宅子和报馆里转，不知疲倦，百依百顺，无可替代。他属于萨布拉，身心都属，彻底得仿佛南北战争从来没打过。一个十二岁的小仆人，生来劳碌，渐渐成了萨布拉的贴心人，习惯了得就像她自己的孩子，顾不得她的南方教养和他的黑皮肤。他擦碗，脖子上系条毛巾；他摆餐桌；他是西姆的玩伴和保姆；他跑腿，一只撒开脚丫的飞毛腿墨丘利；他还是天生的记者，一日里为这差那差满镇乱窜，带回萨布拉厨房的新闻和闲话（后来都转到报馆去），比整整一班科班报人还多。他那么小，那么黑，那么灵巧，那么无害，以致人们在场往往完全看不见他。镇上和镇外的酒馆闲人、牛仔、骑警和垦荒客，一时把他惯坏，一时又作践他。这一刻他们往尘土里丢给他一毛钱一枚，听他唱他那支心爱的歌：

> “主耶稣骑着一匹乳白的骏马，

> 肩上肩上一道彩虹。”

===Original===

The next moment they were making his splayfeet dance frenziedly as the bullets from their six-shooters plopped playfully all about him and his kinky hair seemed to grow straight and dank with terror.
Sabra, in time, taught him to read, write, and figure. He was quick to learn, industrious, lovable. He thought he actually belonged to her. Cim was beginning to learn the alphabet, and as Sabra bent over the child, Isaiah, too, would bring his little stool out of its corner. Perched on it like an intelligent monkey he mastered the curlicues in their proper sequence. He cleared the unsightly back yard of its litter of tin cans and refuse. Together he and Sabra even tried to plant a little garden in this barren sanguine clay. More than anything else, Sabra missed the trees and flowers. In the whole town of almost ten thousand inhabitants there were two trees: stunted jack oaks. Sometimes she dreamed of lilies of the valley﻿—the translucent, almost liquid green of their stems and leaves, the perfumed purity of their white bells.
All this, however, came later. These first few days were filled to overflowing with the labor of making the house habitable and the office and plant fit for Yancey’s professional pursuits. Already his talents as a silver-tongue were being sought in defense of murderers, horse thieves, land grabbers, and more civil offenders in all the surrounding towns and counties. It was known that the average jury was wax in his hands. Once started on his plea it was as though he were painting the emotions that succeeded each other across the faces of the twelve (or less, depending on the number available in the community) good men (or good enough) and true. A tremolo tone﻿—their eyes began to moisten, their mouth muscles to sag with sympathy; a wave of the hand, a lilt of the golden voice﻿—they guffawed with mirth. Even a horse thief, that blackest of criminals in this country, was said to have a bare chance for his life if Yancey Cravat could be induced to plead for him﻿—and provided always, of course, that the posse had not dealt with the offender first.
Yancey, from the time he rose in the morning until he went to bed late at night, was always a little overstimulated by the whisky he drank.

===Chinese===

下一刻，六响枪的子弹戏谑地在他四周噼啵乱蹦，逼得他两只撇脚疯狂地跳，那头鬈发吓得直发直、发潮。
萨布拉后来教会了他读书、写字、算术。他学得快，勤勉，可爱。他以为自己真的就是她的。西姆开始学字母了，萨布拉俯身教孩子时，以赛亚也把他的小凳从角落里搬出来，蹲在上面，像只聪明的猴子，把那些花体字母按顺序一个个拿下。他把后院那一地丑陋的罐头盒和垃圾清理干净。他还同萨布拉一起，试着在这片荒瘠的血红黏土上种一个小园子。萨布拉最念想不过的，就是树和花。全镇近万人口，一共两棵树：两株发育不良的矮栎。有时她梦见铃兰——梦见那茎叶半透明、几乎是液态的绿，那白钟小花的香洁。
不过这些都是后来的事。头几天里，日子满得溢出来：把房子弄得能住人，把办公室和厂子弄得配得上扬西的职业。他那张能把死人说活的嘴，已经忙起来——四乡八镇的杀人犯、盗马贼、抢地客和更文的官司，都来请他辩护。谁都知道，陪审团在他手里就是蜡。他一开陈述，仿佛就是在那十二个（或更少，视镇上凑得到的人数而定）良善（或够得上良善）诚实的男人脸上，一笔一笔画着接连变换的情绪。颤音一起——他们眼睛发潮，嘴部肌肉同情地垂落；手一挥，金嗓子一扬——他们哄堂大笑。据说，连盗马贼——这一带最黑的罪犯——若能请动扬西·克拉瓦特为他陈情，也有一线生机——当然，还得先有个前提：民团没有先把那罪犯办了。
扬西从早晨起床到深夜就寝，总是让威士忌微醺着。

===Original===

This, together with a natural fearlessness, an enormous vitality, and a devouring interest in everybody and everything in this fantastic Oklahoma country, gained him friends and enemies in almost equal proportion.
In the ten days following their arrival in Osage, his one interest seemed to be the tracing of the Pegler murder﻿—for he scoffed at the idea that his predecessor’s death was due to any other cause.
He asked his question everywhere, even in the most foolhardy circumstances, and watched the effect of his question. Pegler had been a Denver newspaper man; known, respected, decent. Yancey had sworn to bring his murderers to justice.
Sabra argued with him, almost hysterically, but in vain. “You didn’t do anything about helping them catch the Kid, out there on the prairie, when they were looking for him, and you knew where he was﻿—or just about﻿—and he had killed a man, too, and robbed a bank, and I don’t know what all.”
“That was different. The Kid’s different,” Yancey answered, unreasonably and infuriatingly.
“Different! How different? What’s this Pegler to you! They’ll kill you, too﻿—they’ll shoot you down﻿—and then what shall I do?﻿—Cim﻿—Cim﻿—and I here, alone﻿—Yancey, darling﻿—I love you so﻿—if anything should happen to you﻿—” She waxed incoherent.
“Listen, honey. Hush your crying and listen. Try to understand. The Kid’s a terror. He’s a bad one. But it isn’t his fault. The government at Washington made him an outlaw.”
“Why, Yancey Cravat, what are you talking about? Don’t you ever say a thing like that before Cim.”
“The Kid’s father rode the range before there were fences or railroads in Kansas, and when this part of the country was running wild with longhorn cattle that had descended straight from the animals that the Spaniards had brought over four centuries ago. The railroads began coming in. The settlers came with it, from the Gulf Coast, up across Texas, through the Indian Territory to the end of steel at Abilene, Kansas. The Kid was brought up to all that. Freighters, bull whackers, mule skinners, hunters, and cowboys﻿—that’s all he knew. Into Dodge City, with perhaps nine months’ pay jingling in his pocket. I’ll bet neither the Kid nor his father before him ever saw a nickel or a dime. They wouldn’t have bothered with such chicken feed. Silver dollars were the smallest coin they knew. They worked for it, too.

===Chinese===

这，加上天生的无畏、旺盛得吓人的精力，和对这片光怪陆离的俄克拉何马的人人事事的那种贪婪兴味，给他招来的朋友和仇人几乎一样多。
到达奥塞奇后的十来天里，他唯一的兴味仿佛就是追查佩格勒被杀一案——因为他嗤笑前任的死另有原因的一切说法。
他到处发问，最鲁莽的场合也发，一面问，一面盯着他的问题起的作用。佩格勒是丹佛的报人；有名望，受敬重，正派。扬西发过誓，要把凶手绳之以法。
萨布拉同他吵，近乎歇斯底里，白费口舌。“那天在大草原上，他们找‘小子’的时候，你倒什么忙也不帮，你明知道他在哪儿——差不多知道——他也杀过人，也抢过银行，还有天知道多少桩。”
“那不一样。‘小子’不一样，”扬西答，毫无道理，气死人。
“不一样！怎么不一样！这佩格勒于你算什么！他们连你也会杀——把你一枪撂倒——那我怎么办？——西姆——西姆——我孤零零在这儿——扬西，亲爱的——我这样爱你——你要有个三长两短——”她语无伦次起来。
“听着，甜心。别哭了，听着。试着明白。‘小子’是个魔星。是个坏人。可那不是他的错。是华盛顿政府把他造成了亡命徒。”
“亏你说得出口，扬西·克拉瓦特！这种话以后别当着西姆说。”
“‘小子’的父亲，在堪萨斯还没有栅栏、没有铁路的年代就跑牧场，那时这片地方还满是长角牛野跑——那牛是四百年前西班牙人带来的牲口嫡传的种。铁路进来了。移民跟着来，从墨西哥湾沿岸，北上穿过得克萨斯，穿过印第安领地，到堪萨斯阿比林的铁轨尽头。‘小子’就是被这一切养大的。赶货运车的、抡牛鞭的、骡夫、猎人和牛仔——他就懂这些。进道奇城，兜里叮当响着大概九个月的工钱。我敢说，‘小子’也好他爹也好，银角子铜角子一类的从没见过。那点鸡食他们懒得弯腰。银元是他们认得的最小的钱。那也是他们挣来的。

===Original===

I’ve seen seventy-five thousand cattle at a time waiting shipment to the East, with lads like the Kid in charge. The Kid’s grandfather was a buffalo hunter. The range was the only life they wanted. Along comes the government. What happens?”
“What?” breathed Sabra, as always enthralled by one of Yancey’s arguments, forgetting quite that she must oppose this very plea.
“They take the range away from the cattle men and cowboys﻿—the free range that never belonged to them really, but that they had come to think of as theirs through right of use. Squatters come in, Sooners, too, and Nesters, and then the whole rush of the Opening. The range is cut up into town sites, and the town into lots, before their very eyes. Why, it must have sickened them﻿—killed them almost﻿—to see it.”
“But that’s progress, Yancey. The country’s got to be settled.”
“This was different. There’s never been anything like this. Settling a great section of a country always has been a matter of years﻿—decades﻿—centuries, even. But here they swept over it in a day. You know that as well as I do. Wilderness one day; town sites the next. And the cowboys and rangers having no more chance than chips in a flood. Can’t you see it? Shanties where the horizon used to be; grocery stores on the old buffalo trails. They went plumb locoed, I tell you. They couldn’t fight progress, but they could get revenge on the people who had taken their world away from them and cut it into little strips and dirtied it.”
“You’re taking the part of criminals, of murderers, of bad men! I’m ashamed of you! I’m afraid of you! You’re as bad as they are.”
“Now, now, Sabra. No dramatics. Leave that for me. I’m better at it. The Kid’s bad, yes. They don’t come worse than he. And they’ll get him, eventually. But he never kills unless he has to. When he robs a bank or holds up a train it’s in broad daylight, by God, with a hundred guns against him. He runs a risk. He doesn’t shoot in the dark. The other fellow always has a chance. It’s three or four, usually, against fifty. He was brought up a reckless, lawless, unschooled youngster. He’s a killer now, and he’ll die by the gun, with his boots on. But the man who fathered him needn’t be ashamed of him. There’s no yellow in the Kid.”
For one dreadful sickening second something closed with iron fingers around Sabra Cravat’s heart and squeezed it, and it ceased to beat.

===Chinese===

我见过一次七万五千头牛等着装车东运，看管的就是‘小子’这样的小伙子。‘小子’的祖父是猎野牛的。牧场是他们唯一想要的生活。政府来了。怎么样？”
“怎么样？”萨布拉屏着气问，一如既往被扬西的雄辩迷住，全忘了自己原该驳他这一套。
“他们把牧场从牧场主和牛仔手里夺走——那自由牧场其实从来不属于他们，可用惯了，他们已当作自己的了。占地客进来，抢跑客也进来，垦荒户也进来，接着就是开放的大洪流。牧场切成一块块城址，城切成一块块宅基，当着他们的眼。唉，看那光景，准得把他们心呕出来——几乎要他们的命。”
“可那是进步啊，扬西。国家总得有人定居。”
“这个不一样。天底下没有这样的。开发一大片国土，从来是几年——几十年——甚至几百年的事。可这儿，一天之内横扫而过。这你知道得同我一样清楚。昨天荒野，今天城址。牛仔和骑警呢，比洪水里的木片还无路可走。你看不见吗？地平线上原是苍穹的地方，搭起了棚屋；野牛的古道上开起了杂货铺。他们全疯了，我告诉你。他们斗不过进步，可他们能向那些夺走他们的世界、把它切成小条、把它弄脏的人复仇。”
“你在替罪犯、杀人犯、恶棍说话！我替你害羞！我怕你！你同他们一样坏。”
“好了，好了，萨布拉。别演了。演留给我。我更在行。‘小子’坏，对。没有比他更坏的了。他们迟早逮住他。可他从不滥杀。他抢银行、劫火车，都是青天白日，天哪，一百条枪对着他。他冒险。他不放黑枪。对方总有活路。常常是三四个对五十个。他从小就是个横冲直撞、无法无天、没人教的野孩子。如今他是杀手了，他会死在枪下，穿着靴子死。可生他的那位父亲不必为他羞愧。‘小子’身上没有懦种的黄。”
有那么可怕的一秒钟，一只铁手攥住萨布拉·克拉瓦特的心，一捏，心跳停了。

===Original===

White faced, her dark eyes searched her husband’s face. Wichita whispers. Kansas slander. But that face was all exaltation, like the face of an evangelist, and as pure. His eyes were glowing. The iron fingers relaxed.
“But Pegler. The men who killed Pegler. Why are they so much worse﻿—”
“Skunks. Dirty jackals hired by white-livered politicians.”
“But why? Why?”
“Because Pegler had the same idea I have﻿—that here’s a chance to start clean, right from scratch. Live and let live. Clean politics instead of the skulduggery all around; a new way of living and of thinking, because we’ve had a chance to see how rotten and narrow and bigoted the other way has been. Here everything’s fresh. It’s all to do, and we can do it. There’s never been a chance like it in the world. We can make a model empire out of this Oklahoma country, with all the mistakes of the other pioneers to profit by. New England, and California, and the settlers of the Middle West﻿—it got away from them, and they fell into the rut. Ugly politics, ugly towns, ugly buildings, ugly minds.” He was off again. Sabra, all impatience, stopped him.
“But Pegler. What’s that got to do with Pegler?” She hated the name. She hated the dead man who was stalking their new life and threatening to destroy it.
“I saw that one copy of his paper. He called it the *New Day*﻿—poor devil. And in it he named names, and he outlined a policy and a belief something like﻿—well﻿—along the lines I’ve tried to explain to you. He accused the government of robbing the Indians. He accused the settlers of cheating them. He told just how they got their whisky, in spite of its being forbidden, and how their monthly allotment was pinched out of their foolish fingers﻿—”
“Oh, my heavens, Yancey! Indians! You and your miserable dirty Indians! You’re always going on about them as if they mattered! The sooner they’re all dead the better. What good are they? Filthy, thieving, lazy things. They won’t work. You’ve said so yourself. They just squat there, rotting.”
“I’ve tried to explain to you,” Yancey began, gently. “White men can’t do those things to a helpless﻿—”
“And so they killed him!” Sabra cried, irrelevantly. “And they’ll kill you, too. Oh, Yancey﻿—please﻿—please﻿—I don’t want to be a pioneer woman. I thought I did, but I don’t. I can’t make things different. I liked them as they were. Comfortable and safe. Let them alone.

===Chinese===

她脸色发白，黑眼睛搜索丈夫的脸。威奇托的私语。堪萨斯的流言。可那张脸全是昂扬，像传道者的脸，一样纯洁。他的眼睛放着光。铁指松开了。
“可佩格勒。杀佩格勒的人。为什么他们坏得多——”
“臭鼬。脏豺狼。胆小政客雇的。”
“可为什么？为什么？”
“因为佩格勒同我想的一样——这儿有个从头干起的机会。干干净净，白手起家。你活也让别人活。要干净的政治，不要四下的卑鄙伎俩；一种新的活法、新的想法，因为旧的那种有多烂、多窄、多偏，我们已经看够了。这儿一切都是新的。什么都没做，什么都做得成。世上从没有过这样的机会。我们能把这俄克拉何马建成一个模范的帝国，前头拓荒人犯过的错都是我们的垫脚石。新英格兰，加利福尼亚，中西部的垦殖者——机会从他们手里溜走了，他们跌进了辙里。丑陋的政治，丑陋的城镇，丑陋的建筑，丑陋的头脑。”他又上天了。萨布拉满心不耐，拦住他。
“可佩格勒。这同佩格勒什么相干？”她恨这个名字。她恨那个死人，他阴魂不散地追着他们的新生活，威胁要毁掉它。
“我见过他那份报的唯一一份。报名叫《新日》——可怜鬼。那上面他指名道姓，提出了一套方针和信念，有点像——呃——同我给你讲的这些一条路子。他控告政府掠夺印第安人。他控告垦荒客欺骗他们。他揭他们怎么弄到威士忌——明令禁绝也拦不住——每月的配给金怎么从他们傻乎乎的手指头里抠走——”
“哦，我的天，扬西！印第安人！你和你那倒霉的脏印第安人！你老念叨他们，倒好像他们了不得！他们全死光了才好。他们有什么用？肮脏、偷东西、懒骨头。他们不肯干活。你自己说过的。他们就那么蹲着，烂着。”
“我一直想跟你解释，”扬西温和地开口，“白人不能对一个无助的——”
“所以他们杀了他！”萨布拉喊，牛头不对马嘴。“他们也会杀你。哦，扬西——求你——求你——我不想当拓荒女人。我以为我想，可我不。我没本事把世道换个样。我喜欢原来那样。又舒服又安稳。随它去吧。

===Original===

I don’t want to live in a model empire. Darling! Darling! Let’s just make it a town like Wichita﻿ ﻿… with trees﻿ ﻿… and people being sociable﻿ ﻿… not killing each other all the time﻿ ﻿… church on Sunday﻿ ﻿… a school for Cim.﻿ ﻿…”
The face she adored was a mask. The ocean-gray eyes were slate-gray now, with the look she had seen and dreaded﻿—cold, determined, relentless.
“All right. Go back there. Go back to your trees and your churches and your sidewalks and your Sunday roast beef and your whole goddamned, smug, dead-alive family. But not me! Me, I’m staying here. And when I find the man who killed Pegler I’ll face him with it, and I’ll publish his name, and if he’s alive by then I’ll bring him to justice and I’ll see him strung up on a tree. If I don’t it’ll be because I’m not alive myself.”
“Oh, God!” whimpered Sabra, and sank, a limp bundle of misery, into his arms. But those arms were, suddenly, no haven, no shelter. He put her from him, gently, but with iron firmness, and walked out of the house, through the newspaper office, down the broad and sinister red road.

===Chinese===

我不想住在什么模范帝国里。亲爱的！亲爱的！咱们就把它建成一个威奇托那样的镇子吧……有树……人们客客气气……不是动不动就互相开枪……礼拜天上教堂……给西姆一所学校。……”
她深爱的那张脸成了一副面具。海灰色的大眼睛此刻是石板灰，带着她见过而害怕的那种神情——冷，决绝，不留情。
“好啊。你回那儿去。回你的树、你的教堂、你的石板人行道、你的礼拜天烤牛肉、你那整个该死的沾沾自喜的死活人家庭去。我不去！我，我留在这儿。等我找到杀佩格勒的人，我要当面揭他，把他的名字登出去，那时他若还活着，我把他送上法庭，亲眼看他被吊上树。我若没办到，那是因为我自己已经不活了。”
“哦，上帝！”萨布拉呜咽着，瘫成一团软软的悲苦，倒进他怀里。可那双臂膀突然不再是港，不再是檐。他把她轻轻推开——轻，却铁一般坚定——走出宅子，穿过报馆，走上那条宽阔而狰狞的红土路。

## VIII / 八
===Original===

Yancey put his question wherever he came upon a little group of three or four lounging on saloon or store porch or street corner. “How did Pegler come to die?” The effect of the question always was the same. One minute they were standing sociably, gossiping, rolling cigarettes; citizenry at ease in their shirtsleeves. Yancey would stroll up with his light, graceful step, his white sombrero with the two bullet holes in its crown, his Prince Albert, his fine high-heeled boots. He would ask his question. As though by magic the group dispersed, faded, vanished.
He visited Coroner Hefner, of Hefner’s Furniture Store and Undertaking Parlor. That gentleman was seated, idle for the moment, in his combination office and laboratory. “Listen, Louie. How did Pegler come to die?”
Hefner’s sun-kissed and whisky-rouged countenance became noticeably less roseate. His pale blue pop-eyes stared at Yancey in dismay. “Are you going around town askin’ that there question, or just me?”
“Oh﻿—around.”
Hefner leaned forward. He looked about him furtively. He lowered his voice. “Yancey, you and your missus, you bought your furniture and so on here in my place, and what’s more, you paid cash for it. I want you as a customer, see, but not in the other branch of my business. Don’t go round askin’ that there question.”
“Think I’d better not, h’m?”
“I know you better not.”
“Why not?”
The versatile Hefner made a little gesture of despair, rose, vanished by way of his own back door, and did not return.
Yancey strolled out into the glaring sunshine of Pawhuska Avenue. Indians, Mexicans, cowboys, solid citizens lounged in whatever of shade could be found in the hot, dry, dusty street. On the corner stood Pete Pitchlyn talking to the Spaniard, Estevan Miro. They were the gossips of the town, these two. This Yancey knew. News not only of the town, but of the Territory﻿—not alone of the Territory but of the whole brilliant burning Southwest, from Texas through New Mexico into Arizona, sieved through this pair. Miro not only knew; he sold his knowledge. The Spaniard made a gay splash of color in the drab prairie street. He wore a sash of purple wound round his middle in place of a belt and his neckerchief was of scarlet. His face was tiny, like the face of a child, and pointed; his hair was thick, blue-black, and lay in definite strands, coarse and glossy, like fine wire.

===Chinese===

扬西逢着三三两两倚在酒馆门廊、铺子门廊或街角闲荡的人，就把他的问题放过去。“佩格勒是怎么死的？”这问题的效力回回一样。前一分钟他们还立在交际着、闲磕牙着、卷着烟，一群衬衫袖子的安闲良民。扬西迈着轻快优雅的步子踱过去，头戴顶上两个弹孔的白阔边帽，身穿长礼服，脚蹬精致的高跟马靴。他发问。人群像着了魔法，散了，淡了，没了。
他拜访了验尸官赫夫纳，就是开赫夫纳家具店殡仪馆的那位。那位先生正闲着，坐在他办公兼实验的屋里。“听着，路易。佩格勒是怎么死的？”
赫夫纳那张日晒威士忌双染的红脸，明显地退了几分红。淡蓝的眼睛凸着，惊惶地瞪扬西。“你是打算满镇去问那个问题呢，还是只问我？”
“哦——满镇。”
赫夫纳前倾身子，鬼祟地四下看了看，压低嗓音。“扬西，你和你太太，家具什么的是在我这儿买的，而且你们给的是现钱。我想要你这样的主顾，明白，可不要我另一行生意的主顾。别满镇去问那个问题。”
“你觉得我不问为妙，嗯？”
“我知道你不问为妙。”
“为什么？”
多才多艺的赫夫纳做了个绝望的小手势，起身，从自家后门遁出去，再没回来。
扬西踱进波哈斯卡大街白晃晃的日头里。印第安人、墨西哥人、牛仔、殷实的市民，在又热又干又尘的街上随便什么荫凉里倚着。街角上，皮特·皮奇林正同西班牙人埃斯特万·米罗说话。这两人是镇上的包打听，这个扬西知道。不单镇上的新闻，连领地的——不单领地的，连整个燎亮的大西南的，从得克萨斯经新墨西哥到亚利桑那——都要从这一对的眼里筛过。米罗不但知道，还卖他的知道。这西班牙人是灰扑扑草原街上的一抹花哨颜色：腰间缠一条紫腰带代替皮带，脖子上一条猩红领巾。他的脸小小的，像孩子的脸，尖尖的；头发又厚又蓝黑，一绺一绺清清楚楚，粗而亮，像细铜丝。

===Original===

His two upper incisor teeth were separated by, perhaps, the width of an eighth of an inch. He was very quiet, and his movements appeared slow because of their feline grace. Eternally he rolled cigarettes in the cowboy fashion, with exquisite deftness, manipulating the tobacco and brown paper magically between the thumb and two fingers of his right hand. The smoke of these he inhaled, consuming a cigarette in three voracious pulls. The street corner on which he lounged was ringed with limp butts.
Pete Pitchlyn, famous Indian scout of a bygone day, has grown potbellied and flabby, now that the Indians were rotting on their reservations and there was no more work for him to do. He was a vast fellow, his height of six feet three now balanced by his bulk. His wife, a full-blood Cherokee squaw, squatted on the ground in the shade of a nearby frame shack about ten feet away, as befits a wife whose husband is conversing with another male. On the ground all around her, like a litter of puppies tumbling about a bitch, were their half-breed children. Late in his hazardous career as a scout on the plains Pitchlyn had been shot in the left heel by a poisoned Indian arrow. It was thought he would surely die. This failing, it was then thought he would lose that leg. But a combination of unlimited whisky, a constitution made up of chilled steel, and a determination that those varmints should never kill him, somehow caused him not only to live but to keep the poison-ravaged leg clinging to his carcase. Stubbornly he had refused to have it amputated, and by a miracle it had failed to send its poison through the rest of that iron frame. But the leg had withered and shrunk until now it was fully twelve inches shorter than the sound limb. He refused to use crutches or the clumsy mechanical devices of the day, and got about with astonishing speed and agility. When he stood on the sound leg he was, with his magnificent breadth of shoulders, a giant of six feet three. But occasionally the sound leg tired, and he would rest it by slumping for a moment on the other. He then became a runt five feet high.

===Chinese===

两颗上门牙中间隔着大约八分之一英寸的缝。他非常安静，动作看着慢，那是猫科的优雅使然。他没完没了地按牛仔的路数卷烟，卷得精巧绝伦，右手拇指加两指之间，烟丝和棕纸像变戏法。烟他往肺里吸，三口狼吞就烧掉一支。他倚的那个街角，围了一圈软塌塌的烟蒂。
皮特·皮奇林，当年名震一方的印第安斥候，如今印第安人在保留地上烂着，他没了用武之地，肚子也大了，肉也松了。他块头极大，六英尺三的个子如今叫腰围匀住了。他的老婆，一个全血切罗基妇人，遵着老婆的本分——丈夫同另一个男人说话呢——蹲在十来英尺外一间木棚的荫凉里。她四下的地上，像一窝小狗围着母狗打滚，是他们那群混血孩子。皮奇林在平原上做斥候的凶险生涯晚期，左脚跟中了毒箭。人人都当他必死。没死成，又都当他那条腿必丢。可无量的威士忌、一副冰钢打就的体格、外加一股“那帮畜生休想弄死我”的狠劲，不知怎么竟叫他不但活了，还叫那条毒蚀的腿继续挂在身躯上。他拗着不肯锯，奇迹般地，那条腿也没把毒送进那副铁架的其余部分。可腿枯了缩了，如今比好腿整整短了十二英寸。他拄拐也不拄，也不用那时笨拙的机械义肢，走起来快得惊人。用好腿站着，凭那副壮丽的宽肩，他是个六英尺三的巨人。可好腿偶尔也乏，他就往那条病腿上矗一矗歇一歇，于是变成了一个五英尺高的矬子。

===Original===

The story was told of him that when he first came to Osage in the rush of the Run he, with hundreds of others, sought the refreshment of the Montezuma Saloon, which hospice﻿—a mere tent﻿—had opened its bar and stood ready for business as the earliest homesteader drew his red-eyed sweating horse up before the first town site to which claim was laid in the settlement of Osage (at that time﻿—fully a month before﻿—a piece of prairie as bare and flat as the palm of your hand). The crowd around the rough pine slab of the hastily improvised bar was parched, wild eyed, clamorous. The bartenders, hardened importations though they were, were soon ready to drop with fatigue. Even in this milling mob the towering figure of Pete Pitchlyn was one to command attention. Above the clamor he ordered his drink﻿—three fingers of whisky. It was a long time coming. He had had a hard day. He leaned one elbow on the bar, while shouts emerged as croaks from parched throats, and glasses and bottles whirled all about him. Dead tired, he shifted his weight from the sound right leg to the withered left, and conversed halfheartedly with the thirsty ones on this side and that. The harried bartender poured Pitchlyn’s whisky, shoved it toward him, saw in his place only a wearily pensive little man whose head barely showed above the bar, and, outraged, his patience tried beyond endurance, yelled:
“Hey, you runt! Get out of there! Where’s the son of a bitch who ordered this whisky?”
Like a python Pete Pitchlyn uncoiled to his full height and glared down on the bewildered bartender.
Crowded though it was, the drinks were on the house.
These two specimens of the Southwest it was that Yancey now approached, his step a saunter, his manner carefree, even bland. Almost imperceptibly the two seemed to stiffen, as though bracing themselves for action. In the old scout it evidenced itself in his sudden emergence from lounging cripple to statuesque giant. In the Spaniard you sensed, rather than saw, only a curiously rippling motion of the muscles beneath the smooth tawny skin, like a snake that glides before it really moves to go.
“Howdy, Pete!”
“Howdy, Yancey!”
He looked at the Spaniard. Miro eyed him innocently. “*Qué tal?*”
“*Bien. Y tú?*”
They stood, the three, wary, silent. Yancey balanced gayly from shining boot toe to high heel and back again.

===Chinese===

关于他有个故事。抢地那阵他头一回到奥塞奇，同几百号人一起寻蒙提祖马酒馆的清凉。那好客之所——不过一顶帐篷——早已开了柜台，只等头一个垦荒客把那匹红眼汗马拴在奥塞奇第一块城址前（那时——足一个月前——那儿还是一片光板草原，平得像你的手心）。临时搭的松木板柜台四周围满了人，喉咙冒烟，眼里放光，吵成一片。酒保虽是外头调来的老油子，不多时也累得要倒。即便在这乱作一团的人堆里，皮特·皮奇林那高大的身形也惹眼。他隔着喧哗要他的酒——三指宽的威士忌。酒迟迟不来。他这一天够受的。他一肘支在柜上，四周冒烟的喉咙里干喊着，杯瓶乱飞。他乏透了，把重心从好的右腿挪到枯的左腿上，有一搭没一搭地同两边渴汉搭话。忙昏头的酒保斟好皮奇林的威士忌，推过去，一看那位置上只立着一个倦倦出神的小个子，脑袋刚刚露出柜台，登时火了，忍到头的耐心炸了：
“嘿，矬子！一边去！订威士忌的那个狗娘养的哪儿去了？”
皮特·皮奇林像一条蟒蛇展开来，直起全身，俯视那发愣的酒保。
店堂虽挤，这一轮酒全算店里的。
扬西此刻走近的，正是大西南的这两件标本。他步子款款，神情闲闲，简直和颜悦色。两人几乎不可察觉地绷紧了，像在运气待发。老斥候那一绷，表现为从倚着的瘫子骤然立成一尊巨像；西班牙人那一绷，你与其说看见，不如说感觉到——光洁的黄褐色皮下，肌腱奇异地一波一波，像蛇真正动身前的那一滑。
“你好，皮特！”
“你好，扬西！”
他看那西班牙人。米罗无辜地打量他。“*Qué tal?*（你好？）”
“*Bien. Y tú?*（好。你呢？）”
三人站着，戒备着，沉默着。扬西从锃亮的靴尖摆到高跟又摆回来，摆得欢快。

===Original===

The Cherokee woman kept her sloe eyes on her man, as though, having received one signal, she were holding herself in readiness for another.
Yancey put the eternal question of the inquiring reporter. “Well, boys, what do you know?”
The two were braced for a query less airy. Their faces relaxed in an expression resembling disappointment. It was as when gunfire fails to explode. The Spaniard shrugged his shoulders, a protean gesture intended on this occasion to convey to the utter innocence and uneventfulness of the daily existence led by Estevan Miro. Pete Pitchlyn’s eyes, in that ravaged face, were coals in an ash heap. It was not for him to be seen talking on the street corner with the man who was asking a fatal question﻿—fatal not only to the asker but to the one who should be foolhardy enough to answer it. He knew Yancey, admired him, wished him well. Yet there was little he dared say now before the reptilian Miro. Yancey continued, conversationally:
“I understand there’s an element rarin’ around town bragging that they’re going to make Osage the terror of the Southwest, like Abilene and Dodge City in the old days; and the Cimarron.” The jaws of Pete Pitchlyn worked rhythmically on the form of nicotine to which he was addicted. Estevan Miro inhaled a deep draught of his brand of poison and sent forth its wraith, a pale gray jet, through his nostrils. Thus each maintained an air of nonchalance to hide his nervousness. “I’m interviewing citizens of note,” continued Yancey, blandly, “on whether they think this town ought to be run on that principle or on a Socratic one that the more modern element has in mind.” He lifted his great head and turned his rare gaze full on the little Spaniard. His gray eyes, quizzical, mocking, met the black eyes, and the darker ones shifted. “Are you at all familiar with the works of Socrates﻿—‘Socrates﻿ ﻿… whom well inspir’d the oracle pronounced wisest of men’?”
Again Estevan Miro shrugged. This time the gesture was exquisitely complicated in its meaning, even for a low-class Spaniard. Slight embarrassment was in it, some bewilderment, and a grain﻿—the merest fleck﻿—of something as nearly approaching contempt as was possible in him for a man whom he feared.
“Yancey,” said Pete Pitchlyn, deliberately, “stick to your lawy’in’.”
“Why?”
“Anybody’s got the gift of gab like you have is wastin’ their time doin’ anything else.”

===Chinese===

那切罗基妇人一双乌黑的眼睛不离她的男人，仿佛接到了一个信号，正等着下一个。
扬西提出了探员记者那个永恒的问题。“怎么样，伙计们，有什么新闻？”
两人原本防的是一记不那么轻飘的问话。脸上的绷劲儿一松，露出近乎失望的表情，像点了火的炮没响。西班牙人耸耸肩——这一个千变万化的手势，这回的意思是：埃斯特万·米罗的日常生活清白无邪、平安无事。皮特·皮奇林那双眼睛，在那张毁烂的脸上，是灰堆里的两块炭。让人看见他在街角同那个提着致命问题的人说话——那问题不光要提问人的命，也要敢答的人的命——这可不行。他识得扬西，敬他，盼他好。可当着米罗那条爬虫，他此刻什么也不敢说。扬西闲谈似的接着说：
“我听说镇上有那么一路角色，四处扬言要把奥塞奇办成西南一霸，办成当年阿比林、道奇城那样子，还有锡马龙。”皮特·皮奇林的牙床有节奏地嚼着他赖以为生的烟屎。埃斯特万·米罗深深吸进一口他那牌子的毒，从鼻孔里送出它的魂——一道灰白细流。两人就这样各摆各的漫不经心，遮各的紧张。“我正在采访知名公民，”扬西和和气气地接着说，“问他们这镇子该按那一套办呢，还是按新派人物心里的那套苏格拉底原则办。”他抬起那颗大脑袋，把那双难得一展的目光整个罩住小个子西班牙人。灰眼睛里的揶揄和嘲弄迎上黑眼睛，黑的那双先移开了。“苏格拉底的著作您可熟？——‘苏格拉底……神谕称他为最有智慧的人。’”
埃斯特万·米罗又耸了耸肩。这回那手势的含义精妙复杂，即使对一个下等西班牙人也够瞧的。里头有几分窘，几分惶，还有一粒——顶针尖大的一点——近乎轻蔑的东西：他对他所怕的人，能轻蔑到头的也就这么多了。
“扬西，”皮特·皮奇林字斟句酌地说，“守着你的律师行吧。”
“为什么？”
“有你这样一张铁嘴的人，干别的都是糟蹋。”

===Original===

“Oh, I wouldn’t say that,” Yancey replied, all modesty. “Running a newspaper keeps me in touch with folks. I like it. Besides, the law isn’t very remunerative in these parts. Running a newspaper’s my way of earning a living. Of course,” he continued brightly, as an afterthought, “there have been times when running a newspaper has saved the editor the trouble of ever again having to earn a living.” The faces of the two were blank as a sponged slate. Suddenly﻿—“Come on, boys. Who killed Pegler?”
Pete Pitchlyn, his Cherokee squaw, and the litter of babies dispersed. It was magic. They faded, vanished. It was as though the woman had tossed her young into a pouch, like a kangaroo. As for the cripple, he might have been a centipede. Yancey and the Spaniard were left alone on the sunny street corner. The face of Miro now became strangely pinched. The eyes were inky slits. He was summoning all his little bravado, pulling it out of his inmost depths.
“I know something. I have that to tell you,” he said in Spanish, his lips barely moving.
Yancey replied in the same tongue, “Out with it.”
The Spaniard did not speak. The slits looked at Yancey. Yancey knew that already he must have been well paid by someone to show such temerity when his very vitals were gripped with fear. “You know something, h’m? Well, Miro, *mas vale saber que haber*.” With which bit of philosophy he showed Miro what a Westerner can do in the way of a shrug; and sauntered off.
Miro leaped after him in one noiseless bound, like a cat. He seemed now to be more afraid of not revealing that which he had been paid to say than of saying it. He spoke rapidly, in Spanish. His hard *r* sounds drummed like hail on a tin roof. “I say only that which was told to me. The words are not mine. They say, ‘Are you a friend of Yancey Cravat?’ I say, ‘Yes.’ They say then, ‘Tell your friend Yancey Cravat that wisdom is better than wealth. If he does not keep his damn mouth shut he will die.’ The words are not mine.”
“Thanks,” replied Yancey, thoughtfully, speaking in English now. Then with one fine white hand he reached out swiftly and gave Miro’s scarlet neckerchief a quick strong jerk and twist. The gesture was at once an insult and a threat. “Tell them﻿—” Suddenly Yancey stopped. He opened his mouth, and there issued from it a sound so dreadful, so unearthly as to freeze the blood of any within hearing.

===Chinese===

“哦，那倒不见得，”扬西答，一派谦逊。“办报叫我不脱离民众。我喜欢。再说这一带打官司不大来钱。办报是我的饭碗。当然，”他又轻快地补一句，像忽然想起，“历史上也有几回，办报省了编辑再度挣饭碗的麻烦。”那两张脸干净得像擦过的石板。忽然——“说吧，伙计们。谁杀了佩格勒？”
皮特·皮奇林、他的切罗基老婆和那一窝娃娃，散了。简直是魔法。他们淡了，没了。那女人仿佛像袋鼠一样把崽子全收进了育儿袋。至于那瘫子，他简直像条百足虫。阳光灿烂的街角只剩扬西和西班牙人。米罗的脸这下奇怪地抽紧了。眼睛成了两道墨缝。他在调动他全部那点虚胆，从心底往外拽。
“我知道点事。我有话告诉您。”他用西班牙语说，嘴唇几乎不动。
扬西用同一种话答：“讲。”
西班牙人不作声。两道缝看着扬西。扬西明白：他五脏早叫恐惧攥住了，还敢露这胆色，必是已叫人出了大价钱。“你知道点什么，嗯？好啊，米罗，*mas vale saber que haber*（知道胜过拥有）。”借这句哲理，他让米罗见识了一个西部人耸肩的功力；随即踱开。
米罗无声地一纵，像猫一样追上他。此刻他仿佛更怕那拿钱要说的话说不出，倒不怕说了。他用西班牙语飞快地讲。那一条条硬卷舌音，像冰雱打铁皮顶。“我只说人家告诉我的。话不是我的。他们问：‘你是扬西·克拉瓦特的朋友吗？’我说：‘是。’他们便说：‘告诉你的朋友扬西·克拉瓦特，智慧胜过财富。他要是不闭上他的臭嘴，他就得死。’话不是我的。”
“多谢，”扬西若有所思地答，改回英语。随后他伸出一只好看的白手，飞快地一探，把米罗那条猩红领巾狠狠一拽一拧。这手势既是侮辱又是威胁。“告诉他们——”扬西突然刹住。他张开嘴，喉咙里发出的声音可怕到、非人间到，冻结每一个听见的人的血。

===Original===

It was a sound between the gobble of an angry turkey cock and the howl of a coyote. Throughout the Southwest it was known that this terrible sound, famed as the gobble, was Cherokee in origin and a death cry among the Territory Indians. It was known, too, that when an Indian gobbled it meant sudden destruction to any or all in his path.
The Spaniard’s face went a curious dough gray. With a whimper he ran, a streak of purple and scarlet and brown, round the corner of the nearest shack, and vanished.
Unfortunately, Yancey could not resist the temptation of dilating to Sabra on this dramatic triumph. The story was, furthermore, told in the presence of Cim and Isaiah, and illustrated﻿—before Sabra could prevent it﻿—with a magnificent rendering the bloodcurdling gobble. They were seated at noonday dinner, with Isaiah slapping briskly back and forth between stove and table. Sabra’s fork, halfway to her mouth, fell clattering on her plate. Her face blanched. Her appetite was gone. Cim, tutored by that natural Thespian and mimic, black Isaiah, spent the afternoon attempting faithfully to reproduce the hideous sound, to the disastrous end that Sabra, nerves torn to shreds, spanked him soundly and administered a smart cuff to Isaiah for good measure. Luckily, the full import of the sinister Indian gobble was lost on her, else she might have taken even stronger measures.
It was all like a nightmarish game, she thought. The shooting, the carousing, the brawls and high altercations; the sounds of laughter and ribaldry and drinking and song that issued from the flimsy cardboard false-front shacks that lined the preposterous street. Steadfastly she refused to believe that this was to be the accepted order of their existence. Yancey was always talking of a new code, a new day; live and let live. He was full of wisdom culled from the Old Testament, with which he pointed his remarks. “ ‘The fathers have eaten sour grapes, and the children’s teeth are set on edge,’ ” when Sabra reminded him of this or that pleasant Wichita custom. But Sabra prepared herself with a retort, and was able, after some quiet research, to refute this with:
“ ‘Stand ye in the ways, and see, and ask for the old paths, where is the good way, and walk therein.’ There! Now perhaps you’ll stop quoting the Bible at me every time you want an excuse for something you do.”

===Chinese===

那声音介于发怒的公火鸡的咯咯和郊狼的嚎叫之间。整个西南部都知道，这可怕的声音——鼎鼎大名的“咯咯”——源自切罗基，是领地印第安人的死亡之喊。大家还知道：印第安人一“咯咯”，他路上的任何人任何物，顷刻毁灭。
西班牙人的脸泛出一种古怪的面团灰。他呜咽一声，撒腿就跑——一道紫、猩红和棕色的残影——绕过最近一间棚屋的墙角，没了。
不幸的是，扬西忍不住要向萨布拉炫耀这一场戏剧性的胜利。而且这故事是当着西姆和以赛亚讲的，还没等萨布拉拦住，就配上了一段血凝级的“咯咯”示范。他们正坐着吃午饭，以赛亚在灶台和餐桌间风快地来回。萨布拉的叉子举到半路，哐啷掉在盘上。她的脸刷白。胃口没了。西姆呢，受那位天生的戏子兼摹仿家黑以赛亚的调教，花了一下午工夫忠实地复现那可怖的声响，直到萨布拉神经撕成了条，结结实实揍了他一顿屁股，顺手又赏了以赛亚一记响亮的脖儿拐。所幸那印第安“咯咯”的全部凶险含义她没听懂，不然措施兴许更严厉。
她想，这一切像一场噩梦般的游戏。枪击，酗酒，斗殴和高声的争吵；从那些沿着这条荒唐街道排开的纸板假门脸的破棚里，涌出来的笑声、粗话、酒气和歌唱。她固执地不肯相信：这就是他们生活的既定秩序。扬西张口闭口新规矩、新日子；你活也让别人活。他满腹《旧约》里摘来的智慧，拿来给他的话上刀口。“‘父亲吃了酸葡萄，儿子的牙就酸倒了，’”每当萨布拉向他提起威奇托这样那样的好规矩，他便这么引一句。可萨布拉也备下了反制，安静研究一番之后，能用这句回敬：
“‘你们当站在路上察看，访问古道，哪是善道，便行在其间。’瞧！这下你该少拿圣经堵我，每回你要给自己的所作所为找借口就引一句。”

===Original===

“The devil,” retorted Yancey, “can cite Scripture for his purpose.” But later she wondered whether by this he had intended a rather ungallant fling at her own quotation or a sheepish excuse for his own.
She refused to believe, too, that this business of the Pegler shooting was as serious as Yancey made it out to be. It was just one of his whims. He would, she told herself, publish something or other about it in the first edition of the *Oklahoma Wigwam*. Yancey stoutly maintained it was due off the press on Thursday. Privately, Sabra thought that this would have to be accomplished by a miracle. This was Friday. A fortnight had gone by. Nothing had been done. Perhaps he was exaggerating the danger as well as the importance of all this Pegler business. Something else would come up to attract his interest, arouse his indignation, or outrage his sense of justice.
She was overjoyed when, that same day, a solemn deputation of citizens, three in number, de rigueur in sombreros and six-shooters, called on Yancey in his office (where, by some chance, he happened momentarily to be) with the amazing request that he conduct divine service the following Sunday morning. Osage was over a month old. The women folks, they said, in effect, thought it high time that some contact be established between the little town sprawled on the prairie and the Power supposedly gazing down upon it from beyond the brilliant steel-blue dome suspended over it. Beneath the calico and sunbonnets despised of Sabra on that first day of her coming to Osage there apparently glowed the same urge for convention, discipline, and the old order that so fired her to revolt. She warmed toward them. She made up her mind that, once the paper had gone to press, she would don the black silk and the hat with the plumes and go calling on such of the wooden shacks as she knew had fostered this meeting. Then she recollected her mother’s training and the stern commands of fashion. The sunbonnets had been residents of Osage before she had arrived. They would have to call first. She pictured, mentally, a group of Mother Hubbards balanced stylishly on the edge of her parlor chairs, making small talk in this welter of Southwestern barbarism.
She got out a plaid silk tie for Cim. “Church meeting!” she exclaimed, joyously.

===Chinese===

“魔鬼，”扬西回敬，“也会引经据典达己私欲。”可过后她犯嘀咕：他这话是顺带讥她引的那句不雅呢，还是给自己那句找的讪讪的台阶。
她也不肯相信，佩格勒枪击这桩事有扬西讲的那么严重。那不过是他的一阵心血来潮。她对自己说，他会在《俄克拉何马棚屋报》创刊号上就这事登个三言两语。扬西咬定报纸星期四准出机。私下里，萨布拉觉得这非得出现奇迹。今天是星期五。两个星期过去了。什么也没干成。兴许这佩格勒事件的危险和重要，都是他夸大了的。自会有别的事跳出来，攥住他的兴味，点起他的义愤，或者刺犯他的正义感。
同一天，一队神情庄重的市民——三位，照例阔边帽加六响枪——到办公空拜访扬西（碰巧他一时在场），提出一个惊人的请求：请他下个礼拜天上午主持礼拜。奥塞奇满月了。他们的意思大致是：镇上的女眷们认为，这座瘫在草原上的小镇，同那位想必正从头顶钢蓝的穹顶外俯视着它的至高者之间，早该建立一点联系了。在萨布拉初到奥塞奇那天被她看不起的印花布和遮阳帽底下，显然也燃着同一种对规矩、管教和旧秩序的渴望——正是这渴望烧得她反叛。她对他们生出暖意。她打定主意：报纸一付印，就穿上黑绸裙、插羽毛的帽子，挨家去拜这些促成了礼拜会的木棚。随即她想起母亲的教导和时尚的严令。遮阳帽们比她先来奥塞奇，得她们先来拜她。她在脑海里描绘出一群家居宽松袍，时髦地撵在她客厅椅边上，在这西南蛮荒的汪洋里寒暄的景象。
她给西姆翻出一条格纹绸领带。“教堂聚会！”她欢喜地喊。

===Original===

Here, at last, was something familiar; something on which she could get a firm foothold in this quagmire. Yancey temporarily abandoned his journalistic mission in order to make proper arrangements for Sunday’s meeting. There was, certainly, no building large enough to hold the thousands who, surprisingly enough, made up this settlement spawned overnight on the prairie. Yancey, born entrepreneur, took hold with the enthusiasm that he always displayed in the first spurt of a new enterprise. Already news of the prospective meeting had spread by the mysterious means common to isolated settlements. Nesters, homesteaders, rangers, cowboys for miles around somehow got wind of it. Saddles were polished, harnesses shined, calicoes washed and ironed, faces scrubbed. Church meeting.
Yancey turned quite naturally to the one shelter in the town adequate to the size of the crowd expected. It was the gambling tent that stood at the far north end of Pawhuska Avenue, flags waving gayly from its top in the brisk Oklahoma wind. For the men it was the social center of Osage. Faro, stud poker, chuckaluck diverted their minds from the stern business of citizenship and saved them the trouble of counting their ready cash on Saturday night. Sunday was, of course, the great day in the gambling tent. Rangers, cowboys, a generous sprinkling of professional bad men from the nearby hills and plains, and all the town women who were not respectable flocked to the tent on Sunday for recreation, society, and excitement. Shouts, the tinkle of glass, the sound of a tubercular piano playing Champagne Charley assailed the ears of the passersby. The great canvas dome, measuring ninety by one hundred and fifty feet, was decorated with flags and bunting; cheerful, bright, gay.
It was a question whether the owner and dealer would be willing to sacrifice any portion of Sunday’s brisk trade for the furtherance of the Lord’s business, even though the goodwill of the townspeople were to be gained thereby. After all, he might argue, it was not this element that kept a faro game going.
Yancey, because of his professional position and his well-known power to charm, was delegated to confer with that citizen *du monde*, Mr. Grat Gotch, better known as Arkansas Grat, proprietor and dealer of the gambling tent. Mr. Gotch was in.

===Chinese===

到底有件熟悉的东西了；在这片泥潭里，她总算有一处踩得稳的立足点。扬西暂时撂下他的新闻使命，着手妥善安排礼拜天的聚会。镇上当然没有一座房子装得下这几千人——这座一夜之间在草原上生出来的居民点，人数竟有这么可观。扬西天生的经纪才，一上来就拿出他每逢新事业头一股热乎劲儿的那种热乎。聚会将办的消息，已经循着隔绝居民点特有的神秘途径传开了。方圆几英里的垦荒户、占地客、骑警、牛仔，不知怎么全得了信。鞍子擦亮了，挽具上光，印花布洗净熨平，脸也刮了。教堂聚会。
扬西自然而然地转向镇上唯一容得下预期人群的荫凉：那顶赌棚，立在波哈斯卡大街最北头，俄克拉何马劲风里棚顶旗帜招展。对男人们，那是奥塞奇的社交中心。法罗、五张牌、骰子摊，替他们解闷，省得他们星期六晚上数现钱的麻烦，公民义务那么 的事也就搁下了。礼拜天，当然是赌棚的大日子。骑警、牛仔、附近山里平原上来的职业恶棍济济一堂，再加上全镇不体面的女人们，礼拜天都涌向赌棚，找乐子、找社交、找刺激。喊声，玻璃叮当，一架痨病腔的钢琴弹着《香槟查利》，直往过路人耳朵里灌。那顶大帆布穹顶，九十英尺宽一百五十英尺长，张灯结彩，喜气、鲜亮、欢快。
赌棚的老板兼荷官肯不肯为上帝的事业牺牲一点礼拜天的红火生意，换全镇的好感，是个问题。他尽可以辩：撑着法罗赌局运转的，本就不是那一路人。
扬西，凭他的职业地位和众所周知的魅力，被派去同那位风月场中的公民——格拉特·戈奇先生，诨名阿肯色格拉特，赌棚的老板兼荷官——交涉。戈奇先生在。

===Original===

Not only that, it being midafternoon and a slack hour for business, he was superintending the placing of a work of art recently purchased by him and just arrived via the Missouri, Kansas & Texas Railroad, familiarly known throughout the Territory, by a natural process of elision, as the Katy. The newly acquired treasure was a picture, done in oils, of a robust and very pink lady of full habit who, apparently having expended all her energy upon the arrangement of her elaborate and highly modern coiffure, was temporarily unable to proceed further with her toilette until fortified by refreshment and repose. To this end she had flung herself in a complete state of nature (barring the hairpins) down on a convenient couch where she lolled at ease, her lips parted to receive a pair of ripe red cherries which she held dangling between thumb and forefinger of a hand whose little finger was elegantly crooked. Her eyes were not on the cherries but on the beholder, of whom she was, plainly, all unaware.
As a tent naturally boasts no walls, it was impossible properly to hang this objet d’art, and it was being suspended by guy ropes from the tent top so that it dangled just in front of the bar, as it properly should, flanked by mirrors. Arkansas Grat had pursued his profession in the bonanza days of Denver, San Francisco, White Oaks, and Dodge City. In these precocious cities his artistic tastes had been developed. He knew that the eye, as well as the gullet, must have refreshment in hours of ease. A little plump man, Grat, with a round and smiling countenance, strangely unlined. He looked like an old baby.
He now, at Yancey’s entrance, called his attention to the newly acquired treasure, expressing at the same time his 叹赏 for it.
“Ain’t she,” he demanded, “a lalapaloosa!”
Yancey surveyed the bright pink lady. He had come to ask a favor of Grat, but he would not sell his artistic soul for this mess of pottage.
“It’s a calumny,” he announced, with some vehemence, “on nature’s fairest achievement.”
The word was not contained in Mr. Gotch’s vocabulary. He mistook Yancey’s warmth of tone for enthusiasm. “That’s right,” he agreed, in triumphant satisfaction. “I was sayin’ to the boys only this morning when she come.”
Yancey ordered his drink and invited Gotch to have one with him.

===Chinese===

不但在于，而且正值下午过半、生意清淡，他正指挥悬挂他新近购得、刚由密苏里—堪萨斯—得克萨斯铁路（领地里人人顺口缩略，叫它凯蒂）运抵的一件艺术品。新得的宝贝是一幅油画：一位丰満结实的粉红女人，显然是把全部气力都花在了那头精工的现代发式上，一时无力于梳妆的其余部分，须待茶点与歇息回力。为此她把自己一整副天生模样（发卡除外）掼在一张现成的躺椅上，恹恹地歪着，樱唇微启，等着接一对熟透的红樱桃——樱桃悬在她拇指食指之间，小指雅致地翘着。她的眼睛不看樱桃，看的是看画的人，而且显然浑然不觉有人在看。
帐篷天生的没有墙，这件艺术品没法正经悬挂，此刻正用张索从棚顶吊下来，恰悬在柜台正前——正得其所——两侧衬着镜子。阿肯色格拉特跑他的行当，跑的是丹佛、旧金山、怀特奥克斯和道奇城的黄金年月。在那几座早熟的城市里，他的艺术趣味养成了。他知道，闲时 需要润的不仅是嗓子，还有眼睛。格拉特是个圆滚滚的小个子男人，一张圆脸带笑，奇异地没有皱纹，像个老婴儿。
扬西一进来，他便唤他注意新得的宝贝，一并表达他的 叹赏。
“她棒不棒，”他问，“一个顶天！”
扬西端详那位鲜亮的粉红女人。他是有求于格拉特来的，可他不为一碗红豆汤出卖艺术灵魂。
“这是诽谤，”他断然说，颇为激烈，“诽谤造化最美好的成就。”
这个字眼不在戈奇先生的词库里。他把扬西的语气当成同好了。“可不是，”他得意地附和，“今儿早她刚到，我还跟弟兄们这么说呢。”
扬西要了他的酒，邀戈奇同饮。

===Original===

Arkansas Grat was not one of those abstemious characters frequently found in fiction who, being dispensers of alcoholic refreshment, never sample their own wares. Over the whisky Yancey put his case.
“Listen, Grat. The women folks have got it into their heads that there ought to be a church service Sunday, now that Osage is over a month old, with ten thousand inhabitants, and probably the metropolis of the great Southwest in another ten years. They want the thing done right. I’m chosen to conduct the meeting. There’s no building in town big enough to hold the crowd. What I want to know is, can we have the loan of your tent here for about an hour Sunday morning for the purpose of divine worship?”
Arkansas Grat set down his glass, made a sweeping gesture with his right hand that included faro tables, lolling cherry eater, bar, piano, and all else that the tent contained.
“Divine worship! Why, hell, yes, Yancey,” he replied, graciously.
They went to work early Sunday. So as not to mar the numbers they covered the faro and roulette tables with twenty-two-foot boards. Such of the prospective congregation as came early would use these for seats. There were, too, a few rude benches on which the players usually sat. The remainder must stand. The meeting was to be from eleven to twelve. As early as nine o’clock they began to arrive. They seemed to spring out of the earth. The horizon spewed up little hurrying figures, black against the brilliant Oklahoma sky. They came from lonely cabins, dugouts, tents. Ox carts, wagons, buggies, horsemen, mule teams. They were starving for company. It wasn’t religion they sought; it was the stimulation that comes of meeting their kind in the mass. They brought picnic baskets and boxes prepared for a holiday. The cowboys were gorgeous. They wore their pink and purple shirts, their five-gallon hats, their gayest neckerchiefs, their most ornate high-heeled boots. They rode up and down before the big tent, their horses curveting and stepping high. “Whoa there! Don’t crowd the cattle!﻿ ﻿… You figgerin’ on gettin’ saved, Quince?﻿ ﻿… Yessir, I’m here for the circus and I’m stayin’ for the concert and grand olio besides.﻿ ﻿… Say, you’re too late, son. Good whisky and bad women has ruined you.”
The town seemed alive with blanketed Indians.
They squatted in the shade of the wooden shacks.

===Chinese===

阿肯色格拉特可不是小说里常见的那种洁身自好的酒保——自己卖酒从不沾唇。就着威士忌，扬西说明来意。
“听着，格拉特。镇上的女眷们认准了：礼拜天该做一场礼拜。奥塞奇满月了，上万人口，再过十年多半是这大西南的头号都会。她们要把这事办得像样。大家推我主持。镇上没有一座房子装得下。我要问的是：礼拜天上午，能不能借你这棚子个把钟头，作敬拜之用？”
阿肯色格拉特放下杯子，右手豪迈地一挥，把法罗台、歪着的吃樱桃美人、柜台、钢琴和棚里的一切全挥了进去。
“敬拜！嗐，行啊，扬西，”他答，雍容大度。
星期天他们一早上就动手。为不伤了牌桌上的点数，法罗台和轮盘台都蒙上了二十二英尺的长木板，早到的会众多半就坐在木板上。还有几条平日赌客坐的粗板凳。其余的只好站着。聚会定在十一点到十二点。才九点钟，人就到了。他们像从地里冒出来。地平线上吐出一个个匆匆的小黑影，衬着俄克拉何马明晃晃的天。他们从孤零零的小屋、地窖房、帐篷里来。牛车、大车、马车、骑马的、骡队。他们饿的是人群。他们寻的不是宗教；是同族成群相见而来的那种亢奋。他们带着备下过节野餐的篮子和食盒。牛仔们漂亮极了：粉红紫红的衬衫，五加仑大帽，最鲜亮的领巾，最花哨的高跟靴。他们骑马在大棚前来回遛，马儿腾跃扬蹄。“吁！别挤！“……昆斯，你算计着得救呢？……是嘞，马戏我来，音乐会加压轴大杂烩我也不走。……嗐，你晚了，小子。好威士忌加坏女人把你毁啦。”
满镇仿佛都是毯子裹身的印第安人。
他们蹲在木棚的荫凉里。

===Original===

They walked in from their nearby reservations, or rode their mangy horses, or brought in their entire families﻿—squaw, papoose, two or three children of assorted sizes, dogs. The family rarely was a large one. Sabra had once remarked this.
“They don’t have big families, do they? Two or three children. You’d think savages like that﻿—I mean﻿—”
Yancey explained. “The Indian is a cold race﻿—passionless, or almost. I don’t know whether it’s the food they eat﻿—their diet﻿—or the vigorous outdoor life they’ve lived for centuries, or whether they’re a naturally sterile race. Funny. No hair on their faces﻿—no beards. Did you ever see an Indian festival dance?”
“Oh, no! I’ve heard they﻿—”
“They work themselves up, you know, at those dances. Insidious music, mutilations, hysteria﻿—all kinds of orgies to get themselves up to pitch.”
Sabra had shuddered with disgust.
This Sunday morning they flocked in by the dozens, with their sorry nags and their scabrous dogs. The men were decked in all their beads and chains with metal plaques. They camped outside the town, at the end of the street.
Sabra, seeing them, told herself sternly that she must remember to have a Christian spirit, and they were all God’s children; that these red men had been converted. She didn’t believe a word of it. “They’re just where they were before Joshua,” Mother Bridget had said.
Rangers, storekeepers, settlers. Lean squatters with their bony wives and their bare-legged, rickety children, as untamed as little wolves.
Sabra superintended the toilettes of her men folk from Yancey to Isaiah. She herself had stayed up the night before to iron his finest shirt. Isaiah had polished his boots until they glittered. Sabra sprinkled a drop of her own cherished cologne on his handkerchief. It was as though they were making ready a bridegroom.
He chided her, laughing, “My good woman, do you realize that this is no way to titivate for the work of delivering the Word of God? Sackcloth and ashes is, I believe, the prescribed costume.” He poured and drank down three fingers of whisky, the third since breakfast.
Cim cavorted excitedly in his best suit, with the bright plaid silk tie and the buttoned shoes, tasseled at the top. The boy, Sabra thought as she dressed him, grew more and more like Yancey, except that he seemed to lack his father’s driving force, his ebullience.

他们从近处的保留地走来，或骑他们的癞马，或携全家——婆娘、襁褓、两三个高矮不齐的孩子、狗。印第安人的家眷很少一大群。萨布拉有一回就说过这个。
“他们家孩子不多，是吧？两三个。照说那样的野——我是说——”
扬西解释。“印第安人是个冷淡的种族——无欲，或近乎无欲。说不清是因为他们吃的东西——食谱——还是他们几百年户外漂泊的剽悍，还是天生不育。有意思。脸上没毛——不长胡子。你看过印第安人的节庆舞吗？”
“哦，没有！听说他们——”
“他们在那种舞会上把自己激动起来，你知道的。煽情的音乐，自残，癔狂——各式狂欢，把自己催到火候。”
萨布拉厌恶地打过寒噤。
这个礼拜天的早晨，他们几十几十地涌来，带着他们可怜的驽马和癞皮狗。男人们浑身挂满了珠串和金属牌链。他们在镇外、大街尽头扎营。
萨布拉望见他们，正告自己必须存一点基督的心肠：他们都是上帝的孩子，这些红人已经皈依。一个字她也不信。“他们还停在约书亚以前呢，”布里吉特嬷嬷说过。
骑警、店主、定居户。精瘦的垦荒客带着精瘦的老婆和光腿的、佝偻的、小狼一样野的孩子。
萨布拉督率她家男众的梳妆，从扬西到以赛亚。她头天夜里熬着夜，熨了他最好的衬衫。以赛亚把他的靴子擦得锃亮。萨布拉在自己珍藏的科隆香水里蘸了一滴，点上他的手帕。那架势像在装扮新郎。
他笑着嗔她：“我的好太太，您不晓得么，为传上帝之道打扮，岂是这般涂脂抹粉的？我记得，规定的行头是麻衣灰尘。”他斟下三指威士忌，一饮而尽——早饭以来的第三杯了。
西姆穿着他的礼服乱蹦，系着鲜亮的格纹绸领带，蹬着高帮纽扣鞋，鞋口带穗。给孩子穿衣时萨布拉想，这孩子越长越像扬西，只是似乎缺了他爹那股子驱劲，那股子沸腾。

But he was high spirited enough now, so that she had difficulty in dressing him.
“I’m going to church!” he shouted, his voice shrill. “Hi, Isaiah! Blessed be the name of the Lawd Amen hall’ujah glory be oh my fren’s come and be save hell fire and brimstone﻿—”
“Cimarron Cravat, stop that this minute or you’ll have to stay home.” Evidently he and Isaiah, full of the Sunday meeting, had been playing church on Saturday afternoon. This was the result of their rehearsal.
Yancey’s sure dramatic instinct bade him delay until he could make an effective entrance. A dozen times Sabra called to him, as he sat in the front office busy with paper and pencil. This was, she decided, his sole preparation for the sermon he would be bound to deliver within the next hour. Later she found in the pocket of his sweeping Prince Albert the piece of paper on which he had made these notes. The paper was filled with those cabalistic whorls, crisscrosses, parallel lines and skulls with which the hand unconsciously gives relief to the troubled or restless mind. One word he had written on it, and then disguised it with meaningless marks﻿—but not quite. Sabra, studying the paper after the events of the morning, made out the word “Yountis.”
At last he was ready. As they stepped into the road they saw that stragglers were still hurrying toward the tent. Sabra had put on, not her second-best black grosgrain, but her best, and the hat with the plumes, none of which splendor she had worn since that eventful first day. She and Yancey stepped sedately down the street, with Cim’s warm wriggling fingers in her own clasp. Sabra was a slimly elegant little figure in her modish black; Yancey, as always, a dashing one; Cim’s clothes were identical with those being worn, perhaps, by a million little boys all over the United States, now on their unwilling way to church. Isaiah, on being summoned from his little kennel in the back yard, had announced that his churchgoing toilette was not quite completed, urged them to proceed without him, and promised to catch up with them before they should have gone a hundred feet.
They went on their way.

===Chinese===

可他此刻精神头十足，穿戴都难。
“我要上教堂喽！”他尖着嗓子喊，“嗨，以赛亚！赞美主的名，阿们，哈利路亚，荣耀归主，哦我的朋友们来受拯救，地狱的火与硫磺——”
“西马龙·克拉瓦特，马上给我停，不然留你在家。”看得出，他和以赛亚满脑子礼拜聚会，星期六下午玩了一下午教堂。这就是他们排演的成果。
扬西万无一失的戏剧本能命他压着，等着一个有效的入场。萨布拉喊了他十几遍，他只坐在前头办公空里忙着纸笔。她想，这就是他对一小时内必讲的道的全部准备。后来她在那件大摆尾的长礼服口袋里，找到了他涂写的这张纸。纸上满是那些玄虚的旋涡、交叉线、平行线和骷髅——不安的手不自觉给不安的心找的排遣。纸上他写了一个词，又用无意义的涂划盖住——却没盖严。早晨的事过完，萨布拉细看那张纸，认出了那个词：“尤恩蒂斯”。
他终于备好了。他们跨上路时，还望得见零星的人匆匆赶往大棚。萨布拉穿的不是第二好的黑罗缎，是最好的那件，还有插羽毛的帽子——自那多事的第一天之后，这身华彩再没上过身。她和扬西端庄地沿街走去，西姆温热乱动的小手指头攢在她手里。萨布拉一身时髦的黑，是个苗条雅致的小身影；扬西，一如既往，招摇的一个；西姆那身衣裳，同此刻全美利坚也许一百万个不情愿上教堂的小男孩身上的，一模一样。以赛亚呢，从后院小窝里叫出来，宣布他上教堂的梳妆尚未竣工，请他们先行，保证他们走不出一百英尺他就赶上。
他们上路了。

===Original===

It occurred neither to Sabra nor to Yancey that there was anything bizarre or even unusual in their thus proceeding, three well-dressed and reasonably conventional figures, toward a gambling tent and saloon which, packed to suffocation with the worst and the best that a frontier town has to offer, was for one short hour to become a House of God.
“Are you nervous, Yancey dear?”
“No, sugar. Though I will say I’d fifty times rather plead with a jury of Texas Panhandle cattlemen for the life of a professional horse thief than stand up to preach before this gang of﻿—” He broke off abruptly. “What’s everybody laughing at and pointing to?” Certainly passersby were acting strangely. Instinctively Sabra and Yancey turned to look behind them. Down the street, perhaps fifty paces behind them, came Isaiah. He was strutting in an absurd and yet unmistakably recognizable imitation of Yancey’s stride and swing. Around his waist was wound a red calico sash, and over that hung a holstered leather belt so large for his small waist that it hung to his knees and bumped against them at every step. Protruding from the holsters one saw the ugly heads of what seemed at first glance to be two six-shooters, but which turned out, on investigation by the infuriated Mrs. Cravat, to be the household monkey wrench and a bar of ink-soaked iron which went to make up one of the printing shop metal forms. On his head was a battered﻿—an unspeakable﻿—sombrero which he must have salvaged from the backyard debris. But this was not, after all, the high point of his sartorial triumph. He had found somewhere a pair of Yancey’s discarded boots. They were high heeled, slim, star trimmed. Even in their final degradation they still had something of the elegance of cut and material that Yancey’s footgear always bore. Into these wrecks of splendor Isaiah had thrust, as far as possible, his own great bare splay feet. The high heels toppled. The arched insteps split under the pressure. Isaiah teetered, wobbled, walked now on his ankles as the treacherous heel betrayed him; now on his toes. Yet he managed, by the very power of his dramatic gift, to give to the appreciative onlooker a complete picture of Yancey Cravat in ludicrous﻿—in grotesque miniature.
He advanced toward them, in spite of his pedestrian handicaps, with an appalling imitation of Yancey’s stride.

===Chinese===

萨布拉和扬西谁也不觉得这样子有什么怪诞，甚至不觉得有什么不寻常：三个衣冠楚楚、大体规整的身影，走向一顶赌棚酒馆——里头塞得透不过气，塞满了边疆小镇所能提供的最好的和最坏的人——而它将有短短一个钟头，成为上帝的殿堂。
“你紧张吗，亲爱的扬西？”
“不，甜心。不过说真的，替得克萨斯潘汉德尔牧场一个职业盗马贼向陪审团求一条命，也比站到这帮人面前讲道强五十倍——”他猛地刹住。“大伙儿笑什么、指什么？”行人果真行止古怪。萨布拉和扬西本能地回头。街那头，落后他们五十来步，以赛亚来了。他大摇大摆，滑稽而绝无错认地摹仿着扬西的步伐与摆幅。他腰上缠一条红印花布腰带，腰带上又挎一条枪带，枪带对他的小腰太大，直垂到膝盖，一步一磕。枪套里探出两个丑家伙，乍看是两把六响枪，经怒火中烧的克拉瓦特太太查证，原来是家里的活扳手和一根浸透油墨的铁条——印刷间一副铅版上的零件。他头上扣着一顶破得——没法说——的阔边帽，必是从后院垃圾里捡的。可这还不是他服饰成就的顶点。他不知从哪儿翻出一双扬西扔掉的靴子。高跟，窄瘦，缀星。纵然沦落到这步田地，那裁剪和料子里还留着扬西靴子一贯的气派。以赛亚把他那双光脚的大撇脚，能塞多深塞多深，插进了这对辉煌的残骸。高跟东倒西歪，弓起的靴面被撑裂。以赛亚摇摇晃晃，一步一闪，脚跟叛他时便踩着脚踝走，踮脚尖走。可他凭他那戏剧天才的神力，硬是给会心的看客一幅完整的扬西·克拉瓦特像——一幅滑稽的、漫画的小像。
他不顾自己步行的种种障碍，朝他们走来，把扬西的步伐摹仿得骇人。

===Original===

Sabra’s face went curiously sallow, so that she was, suddenly, Felice Venable, enraged. Yancey gave a great roar of laughter, and at that Sabra’s blazing eyes turned from the ludicrous figure of the black boy to her husband. She was literally panting with fury. Her idol, her god, was being mocked.
“You﻿—laugh!﻿ ﻿… Stop.﻿ ﻿…”
She went in a kind of swoop of rage toward the now halting figure of Isaiah. Though Cim’s hand was still tightly clutched by her own she had quite forgotten that he was there so that, as she flew toward the small mimic, Cim was yanked along as a cyclone carries small objects in its trail by the very force of its own velocity. She reached him. The black face, all eyes now (and those all whites), looked up at her, startled, terrorized. She raised her hand in its neat black kid glove to cuff him smartly. But Yancey was too quick for her. Swiftly as she had swooped upon Isaiah, Yancey’s leap had been quicker. He caught her hand halfway in its descent. His fingers closed round her wrist in an iron grip.
“Let me go!” For that instant she hated him.
“If you touch him I swear before God I’ll not set foot inside the tent. Look at him!”
The black face gazed up at him. In it was worship, utter devotion. Yancey, himself a born actor, knew that in Isaiah’s grotesque costume, in his struttings and swaggerings, there had been only that sincerest of flattery, imitation of that which was adored. The eyes were those of a dog, faithful, hurt, bewildered.
Yancey released Sabra’s wrist. He turned his brilliant winning smile on Isaiah. He put out his hand, removed the mangy sombrero from the child’s head, and let his fine white hand rest a moment on the woolly poll.
Isaiah began to blubber, his fright giving way to injury. “Ah didn’t go fo’ to fret nobody. You-all was dress up fine fo’ chu’ch meetin’ so I crave to dress myself up Sunday style﻿—”
“That’s right, Isaiah. You look finer than any of us. Now listen to me. Do you want a real suit of Sunday clothes?”
The white teeth now vied with the rolling eyes. “Sunday suit fo’ me to wear! Fo’ true!”
“Listen close, Isaiah. I want you to do something for me. Something big. I don’t want you to go to the church meeting.” Then, as the black boy’s expressive face, all smiles the instant before, became suddenly doleful: “Isaiah, listen hard. This is something important. Everybody in town’s at the church meeting.

===Chinese===

萨布拉的脸奇怪地蜡黄起来，一时间，她成了怒不可遏的菲莉斯·文纳尔。扬西爆出一声大笑，萨布拉冒火的眼睛随即从黑孩子那滑稽的身影转向丈夫。她真真气得直喘。她的偶像，她的神，正被人取笑。
“你——还笑！……住手。……”
她挟着一阵狂怒扑向此刻站住不敢动的以赛亚。西姆的手还被她紧紧攢着，可她全忘了他在，于是她朝那小摹仿家飞去时，西姆被拽得一路踉跄，像龙卷风凭自身的速度拖起的小零碎。她到了他跟前。那张黑脸，此刻全是眼睛（而且全是眼白），仰望着她，吓呆了。她举起戴着工整黑羊皮手套的手，要给他响亮的一记。可扬西比她更快。她扑向以赛亚有多快，扬西的跨越比她更快。他半途截住她下落的手，铁钳般扣住她的手腕。
“放开！”那一瞬间她恨他。
“你敢碰他，我对天发誓，我这只脚不进那棚子。看看他！”
那张黑脸仰望他。脸上是崇拜，是彻底的奉戴。扬西自己是个天生的演员，他知道：以赛亚那身怪装、那副招摇，里头只有最真诚的奉承——摹仿他所热爱的。那双眼睛是狗的眼睛，忠诚，受伤，惶惑。
扬西松开萨布拉的手腕。他把那明亮的、讨人欢心的微笑转向以赛亚。伸出手，摘掉孩子头上那顶癞痢阔边帽，让那只好看的白手在毛茸茸的头顶上停了停。
以赛亚哭起来，惊吓让位给了委屈。“俺没想着惹谁。你们都穿得漂漂亮亮去教堂聚会，俺也想把自个儿拾掇成主日样——”
“这就对了，以赛亚。你比我们谁都精神。听着。你想要一身真正的主日衣裳吗？”
这回白牙同滴溜转的眼睛争辉。“给俺穿的主日套装！真的！”
“仔细听，以赛亚。我要你替我办件事。大事。我不要你去教堂聚会。”见黑孩子那张一刻前还堆笑的脸霎时蔫了，他又说：“以赛亚，使劲听。这事要紧。全镇人都在教堂聚会。

===Original===

Jesse Rickey’s drunk. The house and the newspaper office are left alone. There are people in town who’d sooner set fire to the newspaper plant and the house than see the paper come out on Thursday. I want you to go back to the house and into the kitchen, where you can see the back yard and the side entrance, too. Patrol duty, that’s what I’m putting you on.”
“Yes, *suh*, Mr. Yancey!” agreed Isaiah. “Patrol.” His dejected frame now underwent a transformation as it stiffened to fit the new martial role.
“Now listen close. If anybody comes up to the house﻿—they won’t come the front way, but at the back, probably, or the side﻿—you take this﻿—and shoot.” He took from beneath the Prince Albert a gun which, well on the left, under the coat, was not visible as were the two six-shooters that he always carried at his belt. It was a six-shooter of the kind known as the single action. The trigger was dead. It had been put out of commission. The dog﻿—that part of the mechanism by which the hammer was held cocked and which was released at the pulling of the trigger﻿—had been filed off. It was the deadliest of Southwestern weapons, a six-shooter whose hammer, when pulled back by the thumb, would fall again as soon as released. No need for Isaiah’s small forefinger to wrestle with the trigger.
“Oh, Yancey!” breathed Sabra, in horror. She made as though to put Cim behind her﻿—to shield him with her best black grosgrain silk from sight of this latest horror of pioneer existence. “Yancey! He’s a child!” Now it was she who was protecting the black boy from Yancey. Yancey ignored her.
“You remember what I told you last week,” he went on, equably. “When we were shooting at the tin can on the fence post in the yard. Do it just as you did then﻿—draw, aim, and shoot with the one motion.”
“Yes, *suh*, Mr. Yancey! I kill ’em daid.”
“You’ll have a brand-new suit of Sunday clothes next week, remember, and boots to go with it. Now, scoot!”
Isaiah turned on the crazy high-heeled boots. “Take them off!” screamed Sabra. “You’ll kill yourself. The gun. You’ll stumble!”
But he flashed a brilliant, a glorified smile at her over his shoulder and was off, a ludicrous black Don Quixote miraculously keeping his balance; the boots slapping the deep dust of the road now this way, now that.
All Sabra’s pleasurable anticipation in the church meeting had fled.

杰西·里基醉了。住宅和报馆空着。镇上有人巴不得烧了报馆和宅子，也不愿看报纸星期四出来。我要你回宅子去，进厨房守着，那儿后院侧门都望得见。巡逻勤务，我派你这个。”
“是，老爷！”以赛亚应道，“巡逻。”他那蔫了的骨架随即一变，挺得笔直，配上了这个新的军职。
“听仔细。要是有人上宅子来——他们不会走前门，多半在后头，或者旁边——你就拿这个——开枪。”他从长礼服底下摸出一支枪。那枪很靠左，藏在礼服下，不像他常年挎在腰带上的那两把六响枪那样外露。是那种叫作单动的六响枪。扳机是死的，早废了。击发卡铁——就是扳起机锤、扣扳机时释放它的那个机件——锉掉了。这是西南部最致命的家伙：机锤拇指一扳，一撒手就落。以赛亚的小食指不必同扳机搏斗。
“哦，扬西！”萨布拉惊恐地喘。她作势要把西姆藏到身后——拿她最好的黑罗缎裙挡住他，不叫这拓荒生涯最新一桩恐怖入眼。“扬西！他还是个孩子！”这回轮到她保护黑孩子了，防的是扬西。扬西不理她。
“你记得上礼拜教你的，”他不动声色地接着说，“咱在院里打栅栏柱上那个铁皮罐。就像那回一样——拔枪、瞄准、击发，一个动作。”
“是，老爷！俺把他们打得死死的。”
“下礼拜给你一身崭新的主日衣裳，记住，配靴子。去！”
以赛亚在那双要命的高跟靴上打转。“脱下来！”萨布拉尖叫，“你要摔死的。还有枪。你要绊倒的！”
可他回头朝她一闪，一个灿烂的、光荣的笑，去了：一个滑稽的黑人堂吉诃德，奇迹般保住平衡；那双靴子在深尘里东拍一记西拍一记。
萨布拉对教堂聚会的全部欢喜，飞了。

===Original===

“How could you give a gun to a child like that! You’ll be giving one to Cim, here, next. Alone in the house, with a gun.”
“It isn’t loaded. Come on, honey. We’re late.”
For the first time in their married life she doubted his word absolutely. He strode along toward the tent. She hurried at his side. Cim trotted to keep up with her, his hand in hers.
“What did you mean when you said there were people who would set fire to the house? I never heard of such﻿ ﻿… Did you really mean that someone﻿ ﻿… or was it an excuse to send Isaiah back because of the way he looked?”
“That was it.”
For the second time she doubted him. “I don’t believe you. There’s something going on﻿—something you haven’t told me. Yancey, tell me.”
“I haven’t time now. Don’t be foolish. I just don’t like the complexion of﻿—I just thought that maybe this meeting was the idea of somebody who isn’t altogether inspired by a desire for a closer communion with God. Just occurred to me. I don’t know why. Good joke on me, if it’s true.”
“I’m not going to the meeting. I’m going back to the house.” She was desperate. Her house was burning up, Isaiah was being murdered. Her linen, the silver in the DeGrasse pattern, the cake dish, the green nun’s veiling.
“You’re coming with me.” He rarely used this tone toward her.
“Yancey! Yancey, I’m afraid to have you stand up there, before all those people. I’m afraid. Let’s go back. Tell them you’re sick. Tell them I’m sick. Tell them﻿—”
They had reached the tent. The flap was open. A roar of talk came to them from within. The entrance was packed with lean figures smoking and spitting. “Hi, Yancey! How’s the preacher? Where’s your Bible, Yancey?”
“Right here, boys.” And Yancey reached into the capacious skirt of his Prince Albert to produce in triumph the Word of God. “Come in or stay out, boys. No loafing in the doorway.” With Sabra on his arm he marched through the close-packed tent. “They’ve saved two seats for you and Cim down front﻿—or should have. Yes, there they are.”
Sabra felt faint. She had seen the foxlike face of Lon Yountis in the doorway. “That man,” she whispered to Yancey. “He was there. He looked at you as you passed by﻿—he looked at you so﻿—”
“That’s fine, honey. Better than I hoped for. Nothing I like better than to have members of my flock right under my eye.”
