Thus for a good half column.
Sabra, reading the damp galley proof, was murmurous with admiration. “It’s just wonderful! But, Yancey, don’t you think we ought to have more news items? Gossip, sort of. I don’t mean gossip, really, but about people, and what they’re doing, and so on. Those are the things I like to read in a newspaper. Of course men like editorials and important things like that. But women﻿—”
“That’s right, too,” agreed Jesse Rickey, looking up, ink smeared, from his case. “Get the women folks to reading the paper.”
Sabra was emerging slowly from her role of charming little fool. By degrees she was to take more and more of a hand in the assembling of the paper’s intimate weekly items, while Yancey was concerned with cosmic affairs. Indeed, had it not been for Sabra and Jesse Rickey that first issue of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* might never have appeared, for the front office of the little wooden shack that served as newspaper plant was crowded, following that eventful Sunday, with congratulatory committees, so that it seemed stuffed to suffocation with sombreros, six-shooters, boots, tobacco, and repetitious talk.
“Yessir, Yancey, that was one of the quickest draws I ever see.﻿ ﻿… And you was on to him all the time, huh? Sa‑a‑ay, you’re a slick one, all right. They don’t come no slicker.﻿ ﻿… The rest of the gang has took to the Hills, I understand. That shows they’re scairt, because they got a feud with the Kid and his outfit, and the Kid sees ’em he’ll drop ’em like a row of gobblers at a turkey shootin’. Yancey, you’re the kind of stuff this country needs out here. First thing you know you’ll be Governor of the Territory. How’s that, boys! Come on out and have a drink to the future new Governor, the Honorable Yancey Cravat!”
The group moved in a body across the dusty street into the Sunny Southwest Saloon, from whence came further and more emphatic sounds of approbation.
Sabra, in her checked gingham kitchen apron, was selecting fascinating facts from the stock of ready-print brought with them from Wichita, fresh supplies of which they would receive spasmodically by mail or express via the Katy or the Santa Fe.

> Girls inhabiting the Island of Himla, near Rhodes, are not allowed to marry until they have brought up a specified number of sponges, each taken from a certain depth. The people of the Island earn their living by the sponge fishery.

===Chinese===

就这样写足了半栏。

萨布拉读着湿漉漉的长条校样，赞叹得喃喃出声。“太棒了！可是，扬西，你不觉得咱们该多来点新闻？闲话之类。倒不是真要闲话，是关于人的，他们在干什么，等等。这些才是我爱在报上看的东西。男人当然喜欢社论和那些大事情。可女人——”

“这话也在理，”杰西·里基从字架上抬起头来说，满脸油墨。“让娘儿们也读起报来。”

萨布拉正慢慢地从她那“迷人小傻妞”的角色里走出来。她将一点一点地越来越多地经手报纸每周的私密小消息，而扬西操心宇宙大事。的确，若不是萨布拉和杰西·里基，《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的创刊号也许永远出不来：那个多事的礼拜天之后，充当报馆的那座小木棚的前屋，挤满了一拨又一拨道贺的代表团，阔边帽、六响枪、靴子、烟叶和车轱辘话，塞得人透不过气。

“是这么着，扬西，那是我见过的拔枪最快的一回。……你从头到尾都盯着他呢，啊？嗐——你真是个滑家伙，没错。再滑没有了。……余党听说都上山了。这说明他们怕了，因为他们同小子那伙人有仇，小子撞见他们，会像打火鸡似的把他们一排掉倒。扬西，这种料，正是这地方需要的。一晃眼你就要做领地总督了。怎么样，弟兄们！来，出去喝一杯，敬未来的新总督，尊敬的扬西·克拉瓦特！”

一伙人成群结队横过尘土的街道进了阳光西南酒馆，从那里又传来更进一步、更热络的赞许之声。

萨布拉系着方格条布围裙，正从他们从威奇塔带来的现成印刷品里拣选有趣的掌故；新的补给，将由凯蒂线或圣塔菲线邮寄快递，断断续续地送到。

> 罗得斯附近希姆拉岛的姑娘们，须捞起规定数目的海绵——每只取自一定的深度——方准出嫁。岛上居民以采海绵为生。

===Original===

> The thumb is stronger than all the other fingers together.

> During the last ten years a great many mines have replaced animal haulage with compressed air motors.

===Chinese===

> 拇指比其余四指加在一起还有力。

> 近十年来，许多矿山已用压缩空气马达取代了畜力牵引。

===Original===

As the printing plant boasted only a little hand press, the two six-column forms had to be inked with a hand roller. Over this was placed the damp piece of white print paper. Each sheet was done by hand. The first issue of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* numbered four hundred and fifty copies, and before it was run off, Yancey, Jesse Rickey, Sabra, Isaiah﻿—every member of the household except little Cim﻿—had taken a turn at the roller. Sabra’s back and arm muscles ached for a week.
Yancey made vigorous protest. “What! Ink on the white wonder of dear Juliet’s hand! Out, damned spot! See here, honey. This will never do. My sweet Southern jasmine working over a miserable roller! I’d rather never get out a paper, I tell you.”
“It looks as if you never would, anyway.” The sweet Southern jasmine did not mean to be acid; but the events of the past two or three weeks were beginning to tell on her nerves. The ready-print contained the opening chapters of a novel by Bertha M. Clay in which beauty and virtue triumphed over evil. An instalment of this would appear weekly. The second half of it was missing. But Sabra sagely decided that this fragment, for a time at least, would compensate the feminine readers of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* for the preponderance of civic and political matter and the scarcity of social and personal items. She made up her mind that she would conquer her shyness and become better acquainted with some of those cheviots and straw bonnets seen at the Sunday church meeting.
Yancey and Jesse Rickey seemed to have some joke between them. Sabra, in her kitchen, could hear them snickering like a couple of schoolgirls. They were up to some mischief. Yancey was possessed of the rough and childlike notion of humor that was of the day and place.
“What are you boys up to?” she asked him at dinner.
He was all innocence. “Nothing. Not a thing! What a suspicious little puss you’re getting to be.”
The paper came out on Thursday afternoon, as scheduled. Sabra was astonished and a little terrified to see the occasion treated as an event, with a crowd of cowboys and local citizens in front of the house, pistols fired, whoops and yells; and Yancey himself, aided by Jesse Rickey, handing out copies as if they had cost nothing to print.

===Chinese===

印刷所只有一台小小的手摇印刷机，两块六栏的版得用手辊上墨。上面覆一张微湿的白报纸。每一张都靠手工完成。《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的创刊号印了四百五十份；还没印完，扬西、杰西·里基、萨布拉、以赛亚——全家除了小西姆——都轮过辊子。萨布拉的后背和胳膊酸痛了一个星期。

扬西提出激烈抗议。“什么！墨迹沾上亲爱的朱丽叶那只白的奇迹的手！出去，该死的污点！听着，宝贝。这万万使不得。我的南方甜茉莉，来伺候一根破辊子！我宁可一辈子不出报，我告诉你。”

“看这样子，你本来就一辈子出不来。”南方甜茉莉本不打算说刻薄话；可这两三个星期的事，开始磨损她的神经了。现成印刷品里有一部伯莎·M·克莱的小说的开头几章，讲美与德战胜邪恶。每星期登一段。后半部缺着。可萨布拉精明地断定，这一星半点，至少暂时能补偿《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》女读者们的委屈——市政政治消息太多，社交私人消息太少。她打定主意，要克服羞怯，同礼拜天教会聚会上见过的那些斜纹呢和草帽们当中几位处得熟些。

扬西和杰西·里基之间好像憨着什么玩笑。萨布拉在厨房里就听得见他们两个像女学生似的吃吃笑。他们在捣什么鬼。扬西满脑子那种属于当时当地的、粗鲁而孩子气的幽默。

“你们俩捣什么鬼？”吃饭时她问他。

他无辜得很。“没干什么。什么也没干！你这小猫咪，疑心越来越重了。”

报纸如期在星期四下午出来了。萨布拉惊愕地、还有点害怕地看到，这件事被当成一桩大事来办：房前聚了一群牛仔和镇民，放枪，呼哨，喊叫；扬西亲自——由杰西·里基帮着——把报纸一份份发出去，仿佛印刷不要钱。

===Original===

Perhaps twenty-five of these were distributed, opened eagerly, perused by citizens leaning against the porch posts, and by cowboys on horseback, before Sabra, peeking out of the office window, saw an unmistakable look of surprise﻿—even of shock﻿—on their faces and heard Cass Bixby drawl, “Say, Yancey, that’s a hell of a name for a newspaper.”
She sent Isaiah out to get hold of a copy. He came back with it, grinning. It was a single sheet. The *Oklahoma Galoot*. Motto: Take It or Leave It. Beneath this a hastily assembled and somewhat pied collection of very personal items, calculated to reveal the weakness and foibles of certain prominent citizens now engaged in perusing the false sheet.
The practical joke being revealed and the bona fide paper issued, this was considered a superb triumph for Yancey, and he was again borne away to receive the congratulatory toasts of his somewhat sheepish associates.
It was a man’s town. The men enjoyed it. They rode, gambled, swore, fought, fished, hunted, drank. The antics of many of them seemed like those of little boys playing robber’s cave under the porch. The saloon was their club, the brothel their social rendezvous, the town women their sweethearts. Literally there were no other young girls of marriageable age; for the men and women who had come out here were, like Sabra and Yancey, married couples whose ages ranged between twenty and forty. It was no place for the very young, the very old, or even the middle-aged. Through it all wove the Indians, making a sad yet colorful pattern. The Osage reservation was that nearest the town of Osage. There now was some talk of changing the name of the town because of this, but it never was done. It had been named in the rush of the Run. The Osages, unlike many of the other Territory Plains tribes, were a handsome people﻿—tall, broad-shouldered, proud. The women carried themselves well, head up, shoulders firm, their step leisurely and light. Their garments were mean enough, but over them they wore the striped blanket of the tribe, orange and purple and scarlet and blue, dyed with the same brilliant lasting dyes that Mother Bridget had used in Sabra’s coverlet. They came in from the Reservation on foot; sometimes a family rattled along the red clay road that led into town, huddled in a wagon, rickety, mud spattered.

===Chinese===

发出去的也许有二十五份，人们急急展开，倚着门廊柱子读，或骑在马上读；萨布拉从办公室窗口偷看出去，只见他们脸上分明露出惊讶——甚至是震动——又听见卡斯·比克斯比拖长了腔说：“我说，扬西，这报名可真够呛。”

她打发以赛亚出去弄一份来。他拿着回来，咧着嘴笑。那是一张单页。《俄克拉何马笨蛋报》。格言：爱看不看。下面是一堆仓促凑起、颇见散乱的私人小消息，字字算计着揭正在读这份假报的某些头面人士的短处和癖好。

恶作剧揭了底、真报发了出来，这被公认为扬西的一大辉煌胜利，他又被簇拥而去，接受他那伙有点训训的伙伴的祝贺和敬酒。

这是一座男人的镇子。男人们乐在其中。他们骑马、赌钱、骂人、打架、钓鱼、打猎、喝酒。他们中许多人的行径，活像躲在门廊底下玩强盗窟的小男孩。酒馆是他们的俱乐部，窑子是他们的社交场，镇上的女人们是他们的心上人。名副其实地，再没有其他待嫁之龄的年轻姑娘；因为到这儿来的男男女女，都同萨布拉和扬西一样，是二十到四十岁之间的已婚夫妇。这地方容不得太小、太老、甚至中年的人。贯穿这一切的，是印第安人，织出一幅凄凉而斑斓的图案。奥色治保留地是离奥色治镇最近的。当时镇上有人议论为此改镇名，可始终没改成。它是在大竞跑的疯狂里起的名。奥色治人同领地其他许多平原部落不同，是个英俊的民族——高大，宽肩，骄傲。女人们仪态很好，头昂扬，肩端正，步子从容轻快。她们衣裳寒酸，可外面披着部落的条纹毯，橙、紫、猩红、蓝，用的正是布丽奇特嬷嬚给萨布拉织盖被用的那种鲜亮持久的染料。他们从保留地步行进城；有时一家人挤在一辆摇摇晃晃、溅满泥的大车里，沿着通镇的红土路哐当哐当过来。

===Original===

Sometimes a buck rode a scrofulous horse, his lean legs hugging its sorry flanks. The town treated them with less consideration than the mongrel curs that sunned themselves in the road. They bought their meager supplies with the stipend that the government allowed them; the men bought, stole, or begged whisky when they could, though fire water was strictly forbidden them, and to sell or give it to an Indian was a criminal offense. They lolled or squatted in the sun. They would not work. They raised a little corn which, mixed with lye, they called *soffica*. This mess, hot or cold, was eaten with a spoon made from the horn of a cow. Sabra hated them, even feared them, though Yancey laughed at her for this. Cim was forbidden by her to talk to them. This after she discovered that Yancey had taken him out to visit the Reservation one afternoon. Here, then, was the monstrous society in which Sabra Cravat now found herself. For her, and the other respectable women of the town, there was nothing but their housework, their children, their memories of the homes they had left.
And so the woman who was, after all, the most intelligent among them, set about creating some sort of social order for the good wives of the community. All her life Sabra had been accustomed to the openhanded hospitality of the South. The Venable household in Wichita had been as nearly as possible a duplicate of the Mississippi mansion which had housed generations of Sabra’s luxury-loving and openhanded ancestors. Hordes of relatives came and went. Food and drink were constantly being passed in abundance. White muslin dresses and blue sashes whirled at the least provocative tinkle of the handsome old square piano with its great blobs of grapevine carving. Friends drove up for midday dinner and stayed a week. Felice Venable’s musical drawl was always tempting the sated guest to further excesses. “I declare, Cousin Flora May, you haven’t eaten enough to keep a bird alive. Angie’ll think you don’t fancy her cooking.﻿ ﻿… Lacy, just another quail. They’re only a mouthful.﻿ ﻿… Mittie, pass the currant jell.”
Grimly Sabra (and, in time, the other virtuous women of the community) set about making this new frontier town like the old as speedily as possible. Yancey, almost single handed, tried to make the new as unlike the old as possible. He fought a losing fight from the first.

===Chinese===

有时一个汉子骑着一匹瘰疠疤的马，两条瘦腿夹着那可怜的马胁。镇上人对他们的礼遇，还不如对在路上晒太阳的杂种狗。他们用政府发给的津贴买少得可怜的口粮；男人们碰到机会就买、偷、讨威士忌——虽然“火水”对他们严令禁止，卖酒给印第安人或送他喝都是刑事罪。他们或在太阳下横躺，或蹲踞。他们不肯干活。他们种一点玉米，掺上碱水，管它叫 *soffica*。这糊糊，热的凉的，都用牛角削的勺子吃。萨布拉恨他们，甚至怕他们，虽然扬西为此笑她。她禁止西姆同他们说话——这是在她发现扬西有天下午带他去保留地做客之后。这便是萨布拉·克拉瓦特如今置身其中的畸形社会。对她，对镇上其他体面女人来说，只剩下家务、孩子、和对离开的家园的回忆。

于是，她们中间毕竟最聪明的这个女人，着手为全镇的好太太们创造一种社会秩序。萨布拉一生过惯了南方敞开手的好客。威奇塔的维纳布尔家宅，几乎是那座密西西比宅邸的翻版——那宅邸里住过好几代萨布拉那爱好奢华、出手大方的祖先。成群的亲戚来了又去。吃的喝的不停地大量传递。老式大方钢琴上葡萄藤雕花的大疙瘯一响，白纱裙和蓝腰带就旋起来。朋友赶车来吃顿午饭，一住一星期。菲莉丝·维纳布尔那音乐般的拖腔，总在诱撑饱的客人再多吃一点。“我说真的，弗洛拉·梅表妹，你吃的还不够喂活一只鸟呢。安琪要以为你不喜欢她的手艺了。……蕾西，再来只鹌鹑。不过一口的量。……米蒂，把醋栗冻递过来。”

萨布拉铁了心（到后来，全镇的贤德太太们也一样），着手尽快把这座新的边疆镇子变得同老家一样。扬西呢，几乎是单枪匹马，尽力叫新的不像旧的那样。他从第一天起就打一场输定的仗。

===Original===

He was muddled; frequently insincere; a brilliant swaggerer. He himself was not very clear as to what he wanted, or how to go about getting it. He only knew that he was impatient of things as they were; that greed, injustice, and dishonesty in office were everywhere; that here, in this wild and virgin land, was a chance for a Utopian plan. But he had no plan. He was sentimental about the underdog; overgallant to women; emotional, quick-tempered, impulsive, dramatic, idealistic. And idealism does not flourish in a frontier settlement. Yancey Cravat, with his unformed dreams﻿—much less the roistering play boys of saloon and plain and gambling house﻿—never had a chance against the indomitable materialism of the women.
Like Sabra, most of the women had brought with them from their homes in Nebraska, in Arkansas, in Missouri, in Kansas, some household treasure that in their eyes represented elegance or which was meant to mark them as possessed of taste and background. A chair, a bed, a piece of silver, a vase, a set of linen. It was the period of the horrible gimcrack. Women all over the country were covering wire bread toasters with red plush, embroidering sulphurous yellow chenille roses on this, tying the whole with satin ribbons and hanging it on the wall to represent a paper rack (to be used on pain of death). They painted the backsides of frying pans with gold leaf and daisies, enhanced the handles of these, too, with bows of gay ribbon and, the utilitarian duckling thus turned into a swan, hung it on the wall opposite the toaster. Rolling pins were gilded or sheathed in velvet. Coal scuttles and tin shovels were surprised to find themselves elevated from the kitchen to the parlor, having first been subjected to the new beautifying process. Sabra’s house became a sort of social center following the discovery that she received copies of *Harper’s Bazaar* with fair regularity. Felice Venable sometimes sent it to her, prompted, no doubt, by Sabra’s rather guarded account of the lack of style hints for the person or for the home in this new community. Sabra’s social triumph was complete when she displayed her new draped jars, done by her after minute instructions found in the latest copy of *Harper’s*.

===Chinese===

他自己就是糊涂的；时常不真诚；一个神气活现的奇才。他自己也说不清要什么、怎么去要。他只知道，他对现状不耐烦；知道贪婪、不公、官场的无耻处处皆是；知道这里，在这片荒凉的处女地上，有一次实现乌托邦计划的机会。可他没有计划。他同情弱者；对女人过分殷勤；易动感情，火爆性子，冲动，戏剧化，理想主义。而理想主义在边疆聚落里活不成。扬西·克拉瓦特，连同他那些未成形的梦——更不必说酒馆里、草原上、赌场里那些吃喝玩乐的顽童——在同女人们那种不屈不挠的物质主义的对垒中，从来就没有过机会。

同萨布拉一样，女人们多半从内布拉斯加、阿肯色、密苏里、堪萨斯的家里带来一件什么传家的宝贝，在她们眼里代表雅致，或者标明她们有品位、有来历。一把椅子，一张床，一件银器，一只花瓶，一套亚麻布。那是一个俗气小玩意横行的年代。全国的女人都在给铁丝面包烤架罩红长毛绒，在上面绣出硫磺黄的绳绒玫瑰，再整件系上缎带，挂到墙上充报纸架（用了就得挨骂）。她们给煎锅的背面涂金箔、画雏菊，锅柄也系上花哨的缎带蝴蝶结——实用的丑小鸭就此变作天鹅——挂在烤架对面的墙上。擀面杖镀金的镀金，套丝绒的套丝绒。煤斗和铁皮铲子惊愕地发现自己先被过了一遍新的美化工序，随即从厨房攫升到了客厅。自打发现萨布拉相当定期地收到《哈泼斯时尚》，她家就成了某种社交中心。那有时是菲莉丝·维纳布尔寄来的，无疑是因为萨布拉在信里相当含蓄地提过：在这个新社会，人和家的时新指点都无处可寻。等萨布拉照着最新一期《哈泼斯》上的细致指点，把她新罩的罐子亮出来，她的社交凯旋达到了顶点。

===Original===

She then graciously printed these instructions in the *Oklahoma Wigwam*, causing a flurry of excitement in a hundred homes and mystifying the local storekeepers by the sudden demand for jars.

> “As everything [the fashion note announced, haughtily] is now draped, we give an illustration [Sabra did not﻿—at least in the limited columns of the *Wigwam*] of a china or glass jar draped with India silk and trimmed with lace and ribbon, the decoration entirely concealing any native hideousness in the shape or ornamentation of the jar. Perfectly plain jars can also be draped with a pretty piece of silk and tied with ribbon bows or ornamented with an odd fragment of lace and thereby makes a pretty ornament at little or no cost.”

===Chinese===

随后她大方地把这些指点评登在《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》上，惹得一百户人家一阵兴奋，又让本地店主对着突然涌来的罐子需求莫名其妙。

> “如今样样东西[时装简讯傲然宣布]都要罩起来，兹示一例[萨布拉没有插图——至少在《维格沃姆报》有限的版面上没有]：以印度绸罩瓷罐或玻璃罐，饰以花边和缎带，装饰可完全掩去罐子本身形状或纹样之任何原生丑陋。素罐亦可以一段漂亮绸子罩之，系上缎带结，或缀以一角古怪的花边，即成一件漂亮的摆设，花费甚少或竟无。”

===Original===

Certainly the last four words of the hint were true.
With elegancies such as these the womenfolk of Osage tried to disguise the crudeness and bareness of their glaring wooden shacks. Usually, there was as well a plush chair which had survived the wagon journey; a tortured whatnot on which reposed painted seashells and the objets d’art above described; or, on the wall, a crayon portrait or even an oil painting of some stern and bewhiskered or black-silk and fichued parent looking down in surprised disapproval upon the ructions that comprised the daily activities of this town. From stark ugliness the house interiors were thus transformed into grotesque ugliness, but the Victorian sense of beauty was satisfied. The fact was that these women were hungry for the feel of soft silken things; their eyes, smarting with the glare, the wind, the dust, ached to rest on that which was rich and soothing; their hands, roughened by alkali water, and red dust, and burning sun and wind, dwelt lovingly on these absurd scraps of silk and velvet, snipped from an old wedding dress, from a bonnet, from finery that had found its way to the scrap bag.
Aside from the wedding silver and linen that she had brought with her, the loveliest thing that Sabra possessed was the handwoven blue coverlet that Mother Bridget had given her. It made a true and brilliant spot of color in the sitting room, where it lay neatly folded at the foot of the sofa, partly masking the ugliness of that utilitarian piece of furniture. This Sabra did not know. As silk patchwork quilts, made in wheel and fan patterns, and embroidered in spider webs of bright-colored threads were quite the fashion, the blue coverlet was looked on with considerable disrespect. Thirty years later, its color undimmed, Sabra contributed it temporarily to an exhibition of early American handiwork held in the Venetian room of the Savoy-Bixby Hotel, and it was cooed and ah’d over by all the members of Osage’s smart set. They said it was quaint and authentic and very native and a fine example of pioneer handicraft and Sabra said yes indeed, and told them of Mother Bridget. They said she must have been quaint, too. Sabra said she was.
Slowly, in Sabra’s eyes, the other women of the town began to emerge from a mist of drabness into distinct personalities. There was one who had been a school teacher in Cairo, Illinois.

===Chinese===

当然，这条指点最后四个字是真的。

靠了这诸般风雅，奥色治的女人们努力掩盖她们那些晃眼的木棚内部的粗陋和空旷。通常还有一把熬过了大车长途的长毛绒椅子；一个备受折磨的摆设架，架上是彩绘贝壳和上文所述的种种艺术品；或者墙上，一幅蜡笔肖像，甚至油画，画着某位严峻的络腮胡父亲或披黑绸戴三角巾的母亲，惊愕而不以为然地俯视着这座城镇日常营生的鸡飞狗跳。室内于是从赤裸裸的丑，化装成了怪诞的丑，可维多利亚式的美感满足了。事实是，这些女人渴求柔软丝滑之物的触感；她们的眼睛被强光、风和尘刺得生疼，渴望歇在华丽抚慰的东西上；她们那被碱水、红尘、烈日和风磨粗的手，爱怜地摩挲着这些可笑的绸缎零头——从一件旧嫁衣、一顶帽子、一路辗转进了碎布袋的盛装上剪下来的。

除了随身带来的嫁妆银器和亚麻布，萨布拉拥有的最可爱的东西，就是布丽奇特嬷嬚送的那床手织蓝盖被。它在起居室里是一块真实而鲜艳的色斑，整整齐齐折在沙发脚头，半掩着那件实用家具的丑。这一点萨布拉并不知道。那时轮形和扇形图案、用彩色绒线绣出蛛网的花绸拼被正大行其道，这床蓝盖被颇受轻视。三十年后，它的颜色毫未黯淡，萨布拉把它暂借给萨伏伊-比克斯比饭店威尼斯厅举办的一场美国早期手工展览，奥色治上流社会的全体成员对着它啧啧称赞。他们说它古雅、地道、非常本土，是拓荒手工的绝佳典范；萨布拉说是啊是啊，又给他们讲起布丽奇特嬷嬚。他们说她一定也古雅得很。萨布拉说是的。

渐渐地，在萨布拉眼里，镇上别的女人开始从一片灰蒙蒙的雾里显出各自的面目。有一位，从前在伊利诺伊州开罗当过教员。

===Original===

Her husband, Tracy Wyatt, ran the spasmodic bus and dray line between Wahoo and Osage. They had no children. She was a sparse and simpering woman of thirty-nine, who talked a good deal of former trips to Chicago during which she had reveled in the culture of that effete city. Yancey was heard learnedly discoursing to her on the subject of Etruscan pottery, of which he knew nothing. The ex-school teacher rolled her eyes and tossed her head a good deal.
“You don’t know what a privilege it is, Mr. Cravat, to find myself talking to someone whose mind can soar above the sordid life of this horrible town.”
Yancey’s ardent eyes took on their most melting look. “Madam, it is you who have carried me with you to your heights. ‘In youth and beauty wisdom is but rare!’ ” It was simply his way. He could not help it.
“Ah, Shakespeare!” breathed Mrs. Wyatt, bridling.
“Shakespeare﻿—hell!” said Yancey to Sabra, later. “She doesn’t know Pope when she hears him. No woman ought to pretend to be intelligent. And if she is she ought to have the intelligence to pretend she isn’t. And this one looks like Cornelia Blimber, to boot.”
“Cornelia?﻿ ﻿…”
“A schoolmarm in Dickens’s *Dombey and Son*. A magnificent book, honey. I want you to read it. I want Cim to read it by the time he’s twelve. I’ve got it somewhere here on the shelves.” He was searching among the jumble of books. Five minutes later he was deep in a copy of Plutarch which he had bewailed as lost.
Sabra persisted. “But why did you make her think she was so smart and attractive when you were talking to her?”
“Because she is so plain, darling.”
“It’s just that you can’t bear not to have everybody think you’re fascinating.”
She never read *Dombey and Son*, after all. She decided that she preferred exchanging recipes and discussing the rearing of children with the other women to the more intellectual conversation of Mrs. Wyatt.
---
It was Sabra who started the Philomathean Club. The other women clutched at the idea. It was part of their defense against these wilds. After all, a town that boasted a culture club could not be altogether lost. Sabra had had no experience with this phase of social activity. The languorous yet acid Felice Venable had always scorned to take part in any civic social life that Wichita knew. Kansas, even then, had had its women’s clubs, though they were not known by this title.

===Chinese===

她丈夫特雷西·怀亚特经营着瓦胡与奥色治之间那趟时有时无的公共马车和货运线。他们没有孩子。她三十九岁，瘦削，爱抿嘴笑，满口从前去芝加哥的旅行，说她怎样在那个文弱之都的文化里陶冶过。有人听见扬西就伊特鲁里亚陶器这个题目对她高谈阔论——他对此一无所知。这位前教员眼睛乱转，头乱扬。

“您不知道这是多大的荣幸，克拉瓦特先生，能同一位头脑能飞离这个可怕镇子之肮脏生活的人说话。”

扬西热切的眼睛露出最融化人的神色。“夫人，是您把我带到了您的高处。‘青春与美貌之中，智慧何其稀少！’”这不过是他的做派。他改不了。

“啊，莎士比亚！”怀亚特太太含羞带娇地叹道。

“莎士比亚——见鬼！”过后扬西对萨布拉说。“她听见蒲柏都认不得。女人不该装聪明。真聪明的，就该聪明到懂得装不聪明。何况这一位，活脱是科尼莉亚·布利姆伯。”

“科尼莉亚？……”

“狄更斯《董贝父子》里的女塾师。一部了不起的书，宝贝。我要你读。我要西姆十二岁前读。就搁在这几架上什么地方。”他在那一堆乱书里翻。五分钟后，他已埋首于那本他方才还在哀叹丢失的普鲁塔克里了。

萨布拉不依。“可你同她说话时，干嘛要叫她觉得自己又聪明又迷人？”

“因为她长得那么平常，亲爱的。”

“你就是受不了任何一个人不觉得你迷人。”

《董贝父子》她到底也没有读。她认定，比起怀亚特太太更有学问的谈话，她还是宁可同别的女人交换菜谱、讨论养孩子。

---

爱学会是萨布拉创办的。别的女人一把抓住了这个主意。这是她们对抗这片荒野的防御的一部分。毕竟，一座自夸有文化社团的镇子，总不至于全然没救。这一类社会活动萨布拉没有经验。慵懒而刻薄的菲莉丝·维纳布尔向来不屑参加威奇塔的任何市民社交。堪萨斯那时就已经有妇女社团了，虽然还不叫这个名字。

===Original===

The Ladies’ Sewing Circle, one was called; the Twentieth Century Culture Society; the Hypatias.
Felice Venable, approached as a prospective member, had refused languidly.
“I just naturally hate sewing,” she had drawled, looking up from the novel she was reading. “And as for culture! Why, the Venables and the Marcys have had it in this country for three hundred years, not to speak of England and France, where they practically started it going. Besides, I don’t believe in women running around to club meetings. They’ll be going into politics next.”
Sabra timidly approached Mrs. Wyatt with her plan to form a woman’s club, and Mrs. Wyatt snatched at it with such ferocity as almost to make it appear her own idea. Each was to invite four women of the town’s elite. Ten, they decided, would be enough as charter members.
“I,” began Mrs. Wyatt promptly, “am going to ask Mrs. Louie Hefner, Mrs. Doc Nisbett﻿—”
“Her husband’s horrid! I hate him. I don’t want her in my club.” The ten barrels of water still rankled.
“We’re not asking husbands, my dear Mrs. Cravat. This is a ladies’ club.”
“Well, I don’t think the wife of any such man could be a lady.”
“Mrs. Nisbett,” retorted Mrs. Wyatt, introducing snobbery into that welter of mud, Indians, pine shacks, drought, and semi-barbarism known as Osage, Indian Territory, “was a Krumpf of Ouachita, Arkansas.”
Sabra, descendant of the Marcys and the Venables, lifted her handsome black eyebrows. Privately, she decided to select her four from among the less vertebrate and more ebullient of Osage’s matrons. Culture was all very well, but the thought of mingling once every fortnight with nine versions of the bony Mrs. Wyatt or the pedigreed Mrs. Nisbett (née Krumpf) was depressing. She made up her mind that next day, after the housework was done, she would call on her candidates, beginning with that pretty and stylish Mrs. Evergreen Waltz. Sabra had inherited a strain of frivolity from Felice Venable. At supper that evening she told Yancey of her plans.
“We’re going to take up literature, you know. And maybe early American history.”
“Why, honey, don’t you know you’re making it?”
This she did not take seriously. “And then current events, too.”
“Well, the events in this town are current enough. I’ll say that for them. The trick is to catch them as they go by. You girls’ll have to be quick.” She told him of her four prospective members.

===Chinese===

一个叫女红会；一个叫二十世纪文化会；还有希帕提娅会。

有人去找菲莉丝·维纳布尔入会，她懒洋洋地拒绝了。

“我天生就恨针线，”她从正读着的小说上抬起眼，拖着腔说。“至于文化！嗐，维纳布尔家和马西家在这个国家有三百年文化了，更不必提英格兰和法兰西——那儿的文化简直是他们发起的。再说，我不赞成女人到处跑着开什么会。下一步就要进政界了。”

萨布拉怯怯地拿着成立妇女社团的计划去见怀亚特太太，怀亚特太太一把抢过去，那股凶劲儿，几乎让它成了她自己的主意。每人邀请镇上精英妇女四位。她们商定，十位做发起会员足够了。

“我，”怀亚特太太立刻开列，“要请路易·赫夫纳太太、尼斯贝特大夫太太——”

“她丈夫可恶！我恨他。我不要她进我的社。”十桶水的仇还记着。

“我们请的不是丈夫，亲爱的克拉瓦特太太。这是太太们的社团。”

“哼，这样的男人的老婆，也算不上太太。”

“尼斯贝特太太，”怀亚特太太回敬，把势利引进了那片泥泞、印第安人、松木棚、干旱和半野蛮的混沌——即所谓印第安领地奥色治，“可是阿肯色州沃希托的克伦普夫家的小姐。”

马西家和维纳布尔家的后裔萨布拉，扬起她那两道漂亮的黑眉毛。私下里，她打定主意，她的四位要从奥色治太太们里骨头软些、热闹些的人里挑。文化固然好，可一想到每两个星期要同九个怀亚特太太那样的瘦骨头或尼斯贝特太太（娘家克伦普夫）那样的名门过一回，就提不起劲。她拿定主意，第二天家务做完就去拜会她的候选人，从那位漂亮时新的常青·华尔兹太太开头。萨布拉从菲莉丝·维纳布尔身上继承了一脉轻浮。那天晚饭时，她把计划讲给扬西听。

“我们要研读文学，你知道。也许还有美国早期史。”

“嗐，宝贝，你还不知道吗？你们正在创造历史呢。”

这话她没当真。“然后还有时事。”

“唔，这镇上的时事倒真够‘时’的。这一点我得替它们说句公道话。诀窍是在它们路过时逮住。你们这些姑娘得手快。”她把四位候选会员讲给他听。

===Original===

“Waltz’s wife!” Surprise and amusement, too, were in his voice, but she was too full of her plans to notice. Besides, Yancey often was mystifyingly amused at things that seemed to Sabra quite serious. “Why, that’s fine, Sabra. That’s fine! That’s the spirit!”
“I noticed her at church meeting last Sunday. She’s so pretty, it rests me to look at her, after all these﻿—not that they’re﻿—I don’t mean they’re not very nice ladies. But after all, even if it’s a culture club, someone nearer my own age would be much more fun.”
“Oh, much,” Yancey agreed, still smiling. “That’s what a town like this should be. No class distinctions, no snobbery, no highfalutin notions.”
“I saw her washing hanging on the line. Just by accident. You can tell she’s a lady. Such pretty underthings all trimmed with embroidery, and there were two embroidery petticoats all flounced and every bit as nice as the ones Cousin Belle French Vian made for me by hand, for my trousseau.”
“I’m not surprised.” Yancey was less loquacious than usual. But then, men were not interested in women’s clothes.
“She looks kind of babyish and lonely, sitting there by the window sewing all day. And her husband’s so much older, and a cripple, too, or almost. I noticed he limps quite badly. What’s his trouble?”
“Shot in the leg.”
“Oh.” She had already learned to accept this form of injury as a matter of course. “I thought I’d ask her to prepare a paper for the third meeting on Mrs. Browning’s *Aurora Leigh*. I could lend her yours to read up on, if you don’t mind, just in case she hasn’t got it.”
Yancey thought it unlikely.
Mrs. Wyatt’s house was one of the few in Osage which were used for dwelling purposes alone. No store or office occupied the front of it. Tracy Wyatt’s bus and dray line certainly could not be contained in a pine shack intended for family use. Mrs. Wyatt had five rooms. She was annoyingly proud of this, and referred to it on all possible occasions.
“The first meeting,” she said, “will be held at my house, of course. It will be so much nicer.”
She did not say nicer than what, but Sabra’s face set itself in a sort of mask of icy stubbornness. “The first meeting of the Philomathean Society will be held at the home of the Founder.” After all, Mrs. Wyatt’s house could not boast a screen door, as Sabra’s could. It was the only house in Osage that had one. Yancey had had Hefner order it from Kansas City.

===Chinese===

“华尔兹的老婆！”他的声音里有惊讶，也有好玩，可她满脑子计划，没有留意。再说，扬西时常对萨布拉觉得顶正经的事情莫名其妙地觉得好玩。“嗐，好极了，萨布拉。好极了！这就对啦！”

“礼拜天教会聚会上我留意到她了。她那么漂亮，看着她就歇乏，看惯了这些——并不是说她们——我不是说她们不是很好的太太。可到底，就算是文化社团，有个年纪同我相近的，也要有趣得多。”

“哦，有趣得多，”扬西还是笑着说。“这样的镇子就该这样。没有阶级之分，没有势利眼，没有自命不凡。”

“我看见她晾在绳上的衣裳。纯属偶然。一看就知道是位太太。那么多绣花边的漂亮小衣裳，还有两条绣花衬裙，层层褶边，同贝尔·法兰奇·维安表姐亲手给我绣的嫁妆衬裙一样漂亮。”

“我不奇怪。”扬西比平常话少。不过，男人对女人的衣裳本来就不感兴趣。

“她坐在窗前整天做针线，样子有点孩子气，有点孤单。她丈夫年纪大那么多，还是个瘸子——差不多算瘸子。我看他瘸得挺厉害。他是什么毛病？”

“腿上挨过一枪。”

“哦。”她已经学会把这一类伤病当作家常便饭。“我想请她为第三次会议准备一篇关于勃朗宁夫人《奥萝拉·利》的论文。要是她没有那本书——希望你不介意——把你的借她先读读。”

扬西觉得她多半不会有。

怀亚特太太的房子是奥色治寥寥几座纯粹住家的房子之一。前面没有店铺或写字间。特雷西·怀亚特的马车货运线，自然塞不进一座居家用的松木棚。怀亚特太太有五个房间。她为此烦人地骄傲，逢着机会就提。

“第一次聚会，”她说，“当然在我家举行。那样体面得多。”

她没说比什么体面，可萨布拉的脸凝成一副冰冷的倔强的面具。“爱学会的第一次聚会，将在创办人家里举行。”到底，怀亚特太太家连一扇纱门都没有，萨布拉家却有。那是奥色治唯一有纱门的住家。扬西托赫夫纳从堪萨斯城订来的。

===Original===

The wind and the flies seemed to torture Sabra. It was so unusual a luxury that frequently strangers came to the door by mistake, thinking that here was the butcher shop, which boasted the only other screen door in the town.
“I’ll serve coffee and doughnuts,” Sabra added, graciously. “And I’ll move to elect you president. I”﻿—this not without a flick of malice﻿—“am too busy with my household and my child and the newspaper﻿—I often assist my husband editorially﻿—to take up with any more work.”
The paper on Mrs. Browning’s *Aurora Leigh* never was written by the pretty Mrs. Evergreen Waltz. Three days later Sabra, chancing to glance out of her sitting-room window, saw the crippled and middle-aged gambler passing her house, and in spite of his infirmity he was walking with great speed﻿—running, almost. In his hand was a piece of white paper﻿—a letter, Sabra thought. She hoped it was not bad news. He had looked, she thought, sort of odd and wild.
Evergreen Waltz, after weeks of tireless waiting and watching, had at last intercepted a letter from his young wife’s lover. As he now came panting up the street the girl sat at the window, sewing. The single shot went just through the center of the wide white space between her great babyish blue eyes. They found her with the gold initialed thimble on her finger, and the bit of work on which she had been sewing, now brightly spotted with crimson, in her lap.
“Why didn’t you tell me that when she married him she was a girl out of a﻿—out of a﻿—house!” Sabra demanded, between horror and wrath.
“I thought you knew. Women are supposed to have intuition, or whatever they call it, aren’t they? All those embroidered underthings on the line in a town where water’s scarce as champagne﻿—scarcer. And then *Aurora Leigh*.”
She was thoroughly enraged by now. “What, for pity’s sake, has *Aurora Leigh* got to do with her!”
He got down the volume. “I thought you’d been reading it yourself, perhaps.” He opened it. “ ‘Dreams of doing good for good-for-nothing people.’ ”

===Chinese===

风和苍蝇似乎都在折磨萨布拉。这一样奢侈品如此稀罕，常有生人错走到门口，以为这里是肉铺——全镇只有肉铺才有另一扇纱门。

“我供应咖啡和炸面圈，”萨布拉大方地补充，“并且我要提名选你做会长。我嘛”——这话不无一丝恶意——“家务、孩子，还有报纸，忙不过来——我常在编辑上协助我丈夫——再担不起别的职务了。”

那篇关于勃朗宁夫人《奥萝拉·利》的论文，漂亮的常青·华尔兹太太终究没有写成。三天后，萨布拉恰巧从起居室窗口望出去，看见那个瘸腿的中年赌徒经过她家，不顾腿疾，走得飞快——简直是跑。手里捏着一张白纸——一封信，萨布拉想。她但愿不是坏消息。他看上去，她想，有点古怪，有点发狂。

常青·华尔兹经过几个星期不知疲倦的守候监视，终于截住了他年轻妻子情人的一封信。此刻他喘着气奔上街来，姑娘正坐在窗前做针线。那一枪不偏不倚，正打在她两只婴孩般的蓝眼睛之间那一大块空白的正中央。人们发现她时，手指上还戴着金缩写的顶针，膝上放着那件她正缝着的活计，如今鲜明地点着殷红。

“她嫁给他的时候，是个从那种——从那种——窑子里出来的姑娘，你怎么不告诉我！”萨布拉一半惊骇一半愤怒地质问。

“我以为你知道。女人不是有直觉，还是管它什么，吗？水比香槟还金贵的镇子上，晾衣绳上那些绣花小衣裳——比香槟还缺。再说还有《奥萝拉·利》。”

她此刻怒透了。“看在老天份上，《奥萝拉·利》跟她有什么相干！”

他把那本书取下来。“我还当你自己一直在读呢。”他翻开。“‘梦想为不中用的人行善。’”

## XI / 十一
===Original===

Sabra’s second child, a girl, was born in June, a little more than a year after their coming to Osage. It was not as dreadful an ordeal there in those crude surroundings as one might have thought. She refused to send for her mother; indeed, Sabra insisted that Felice Venable be told nothing of the event until after her granddaughter had wailed her way into the Red Man’s country. Yancey had been relieved at Sabra’s decision. The thought of his luxury-loving and formidable mother-in-law with her flounced dimities and her high-heeled slippers in the midst of this Western wallow to which he had brought her daughter was a thing from which even the redoubtable Yancey shrank. Curiously enough, it was not the pain, the heat, nor the inexpert attention she received that most distressed Sabra. It was the wind. The Oklahoma wind tortured her. It rattled the doors and windows; it whirled the red dust through the house; its hot breath was on her agonized face as she lay there; if allowed its own way it leaped through the rooms, snatching the cloth off the table, the sheets off the bed, the dishes off the shelves.
“The wind!” Sabra moaned. “The wind! The wind! Make it stop.” She was a little delirious. “Yancey! With your gun. Shoot it. Seven notches. I don’t care. Only stop it.”
She was tended, during her accouchement, by the best doctor in the county and certainly the most picturesque man of medicine in the whole Southwest, Dr. Don Valliant. Like thousands of others living in this new country, his past was his own secret. He rode to his calls on horseback, in a black velveteen coat and velveteen trousers tucked into fancy leather boots. His soft black hat, rivaling Yancey’s white one, intensified the black of his eyes and hair. It was known that he often vanished for days, leaving the sick to get on as best they could. He would reappear as inexplicably as he had vanished; and it was noticed then that he was worn looking and his horse was jaded. It was no secret that he was often called to attend the bandits when one of their number, wounded in some outlaw raid, had taken to their hiding place in the Hills. He was tender and deft with Sabra, though between them he and Yancey consumed an incredible quantity of whisky during the racking hours of her confinement.

===Chinese===

萨布拉的第二个孩子，一个女孩，生在六月——他们到奥塞奇后一年多一点。在那样的粗陋环境里，生产并不像人们想的那么可怕。她不肯叫人去接母亲；的确，萨布拉坚持等女儿嚎着降生到红人的国度之后，才能让菲莉斯·文纳尔知道这件事。萨布拉的决定让扬西松了口气。一想到他那位爱奢侈、难对付的丈母娘，带着镶荷叶边的细麻纱裙和高跟拖鞋，驾临他拽她女儿来蹚的这潭西部泥潭——连天不怕地不怕的扬西也发悚。说来奇怪，最折磨萨布拉的，不是疼痛，不是暑热，也不是她所受的生手照应，而是风。俄克拉何马的风拷问她。它摇得门窗咔咔响；它把红尘卷得满屋都是；她躺在那儿，它灼热的呼吸扑在她痛楚的脸上；一不拦它，它就窜遍各屋，掳走桌上的桌布、床上的床单、架子上的碗碟。
“风！”萨布拉呻吟。“风！风！叫它停。”她有点儿说胡话了。“扬西！拿你的枪。崩了它。七个缺口都记上了。不管。只要它停。”
产褥间照看她的，是全县最好的医生，也是全西南最富有画风的名医：唐·瓦利安特大夫妻。同这个新国家里的千百人一样，他的过去是他自己的秘密。他出诊骑马，穿黑天鹅绒上衣和天鹅绒裤，裤脚塞在花哨的皮靴里。他那顶黑色软帽同扬西的白帽不相上下，衬得他的眼睛和头发愈发黑。谁都知道他常常一连几天失踪，把病人撂给天意。他又会莫名其妙地回来；那时人们注意到他面带倦容，马也垮了。他常被叫去照看匪徒，也不是秘密——他们中间哪一个在外劫中挂了花，藏进山里的巢穴时，便请他去。他对萨布拉温柔而轻巧；只是在她难产的煎熬时辰里，他和扬西两个人喝掉的威士忌数量惊人。

===Original===

At the end he held up a caterwauling morsel of flesh torn from Sabra’s flesh﻿—a thing perfect of its kind, with an astonishing mop of black hair.
“This is a Spanish beauty you have for a daughter, Yancey. I present to you Señorita Doña Cravat.”
And Donna Cravat she remained. The town, somewhat scandalized, thought she had been named after Dr. Don himself. Besides, they did not consider Donna a name at all. The other women of the community fed their hunger for romance by endowing their girl children with such florid names as they could conjure up out of their imagination or from the novels they read between dish washings. The result was likely to range from the pathetic to the ridiculous. Czarina McKee; Emmeretta Folsom; Gazelle Slaughter; Maurine Turket; Cassandra Sipes; Jewel Riggs.
The neighborhood wives showered the Cravat household with the customary cakes, pies, meat loaves, and bowls of broth. Black Isaiah was touching, was wonderful. He washed dishes, he mopped floors, he actually cooked as though he had inherited the art from Angie, his vast black mother, left behind in Wichita. One of Sabra’s gingham kitchen aprons, checked blue and white, was always hitched up under his arms, and beneath this utilitarian yet coquettish garment his great bare feet slapped in and out as he did the work of the household. He was utterly fascinated by the new baby. “Looka dat! She know me! Hi, who yo’ rollin’ yo’ eyes at, makin’ faces!” He danced for her, he sang Negro songs to her, he rocked her to sleep. He was, as Donna grew older, her nursemaid, pushing her baby buggy up and down the dusty street, and later still her playmate as well as Cim’s.
When Sabra Cravat arose from that bed something in her had crystallized. Perhaps it was that, for the first time in a year, she had had hours in which to rest her tired limbs; perhaps the ordeal itself worked a psychic as well as a physical change in her; it might have been that she realized she must cut a new pattern in this Oklahoma life of theirs. The boy Cim might surmount it; the girl Donna never. During the hours through which she had lain in her bed in the stifling wooden shack, mists seemed to have rolled away from before her eyes. She saw clearly.

===Chinese===

末了，他举起一小块嗷嗷叫唤的肉——从萨布拉的肉上撕下来的肉——一件同类中的完美之作，顶着一头惊人的黑发。
“这是个西班牙美人儿呢，扬西，你添的女儿。我向您呈上：克拉瓦特大小姐（Señorita Doña Cravat）。”
唐娜·克拉瓦特（Donna Cravat）这名字就跟定了她。镇上人不无非议，以为她是照着唐大夫本人的名字取的。况且他们压根不觉得“唐娜”算个名字。镇上旁的女人们，把对浪漫的饥饿，倾泻在给女儿起的花哨名字里——名字全凭想象，或凭洗碗间隙读的小说里变出来。结果也就从可怜滑稽到荒唐可笑不等。沙皇皇后麦基；埃默雷塔·福尔索姆；瞪羚斯劳特；莫琳图尔克特；卡珊德拉赛普斯；珠宝里格斯。
街坊太太们照例送来蛋糕、派、肉糕和一碗碗肉汤，把克拉瓦特家埋了。黑以赛亚感人极了，了不起极了。他洗碗，他拖地，他居然还会做饭，仿佛这门手艺是从他那留在威奇托的高大的黑妈妈安琪那儿继承的。萨布拉的一条厨房围裙——蓝白格子布——总系在他胳肢窝下，在这件实用而又风骚的衣裳底下，他那双光脚板啪哒啪哒出出进进，包办全家的活计。他让新生的小娃娃迷住了。“瞧嘿！她认得俺！嘿，你冲谁滚眼珠呢，做鬼脸！”他为她跳舞，给她唱黑人歌，摇她入睡。唐娜大些了，他做她的保姆，推着她的婴儿车在尘土街上走来走去；再后来，又做她的玩伴，同西姆的玩伴一样。
萨布拉·克拉瓦特从那张床上起来时，她身上的某样东西结晶了。也许是因为一年来头一回，她那困乏的四肢得了几个钟头的歇；也许是那场磨难本身，在她身上起了身心两面的变化；也可能是她明白了：他们的俄克拉何马日子，她得裁一个新样子出来。男孩西姆也许拗得过它；女孩唐娜永远不能。她在那间塞气的木棚里躺着的那些时辰里，眼前的雾仿佛一层层撤开了。她看得清楚了。

===Original===

She felt light and terribly capable﻿—so much so that she made the mistake of getting up, dizzily donning slippers and wrapper, and tottering into the newspaper office where Yancey was writing an editorial and shouting choice passages of it into the inattentive ear of Jesse Rickey, who was setting type in the printing shop.
“… the most stupendous farce ever conceived by the mind of man in a civilized country.﻿ ﻿…”
He looked up to see in the doorway a wraith, all eyes and long black braids. “Why, sugar! What’s this? You can’t get up!”
She smiled rather feebly. “I’m up. I felt so light, so﻿—”
“I should think you would. All that physic.”
“I feel so strong. I’m going to do so many things. You’ll see. I’m going to paper the whole house. Rosebuds in the bedroom. I’m going to plant two trees in the front. I’m going to start another club﻿—not like the Philomathean﻿—I think that’s silly now﻿—but one to make this town﻿ ﻿… no saloons﻿ ﻿… women like that Dixie Lee﻿ ﻿… going to have a real hired girl as soon as the newspaper begins to﻿ ﻿… feel so queer﻿ ﻿… Yancey﻿ ﻿…”
As she began to topple, Yancey caught the Osage Joan of Arc in his arms.
Incredibly enough, she actually did paper the entire house, aided by Isaiah and Jesse Rickey. Isaiah’s ebony countenance splashed with the white paste mixture made a bizarre effect, a trifle startling to anyone coming upon the scene unawares. Also Jesse Rickey’s inebriate eye, which so often resulted in many grotesque pied print lines appearing in unexpected and inconvenient places in the *Oklahoma Wigwam* columns, was none too dependable in the matching of rosebud patterns. The result, in spots, was Burbankian, with roses grafted on leaves and tendrils emerging from petals. Still, the effect was gay, even luxurious. The Philomathean Club, as one woman, fell upon wallpaper and paste pot, as they had upon the covered jars in Sabra’s earlier effort at decoration. Within a month Louie Hefner was compelled to install a full line of wallpaper to satisfy the local demand.
Slowly, slowly, the life of the community, in the beginning so wild, so unrelated in its parts, began to weave in and out, warp and woof, to make a pattern. It was at first faint, almost undiscernible. But presently the eye could trace here a motif, there a figure, here a motif, there a figure. The shuttle swept back, forward, back, forward.

===Chinese===

她觉得轻盈，觉得能干得吓人——能干到犯了错：下了床，晕晕地趿上拖鞋、披上晨衣，一步一步挪进报馆；扬西正在那儿写社论，还把精采段落朗声念给无心听的杰西·里基——他在印刷间排字。
“……文明国度里人类心智所构想过的最浩大的闹剧。……”
他抬头，看见门口一个游魂，全是眼睛和一条黑长辫。“咦，甜心！这是怎么？你不能起来！”
她虚弱地笑了笑。“我起来了。我觉得那么轻，那么——”
“难怪。泻药吃的。”
“我觉得那么有力气。我要做那么多事。你瞧着。我要给全屋裱糊。卧室里糊玫瑰苞。我要在前头种两棵树。我还要另办一个俱乐部——不要菲洛马西安那样的——现在想来那真傻——要办一个把这座镇子……不许酒馆……不许狄克茜·李那样的女人……等报纸一——雇一个真正花钱雇的女仆……觉得好晕……扬西……”
她开始摇晃，扬西一把把这位奥塞奇的贞德搂进怀里。
难以置信，她当真把全家裱糊了一遍，帮手是以赛亚和杰西·里基。以赛亚那张黑脸溅满白色裱糊浆，那景象古怪得很，撞见的人少不得吓一跳。而杰西·里基那只醉眼——《棚屋报》版面上那些莫名其妙出现在不该出现的地方的歪斜错行，多半是它的功劳——拿它来对玫瑰花纹，也不大可靠。结果，有几处简直是伦德·伯班克再世：玫瑰嫁在叶子上，卷须从花瓣里钻出来。不过效果仍旧喜气，甚至华丽。菲洛马西安俱乐部全体女性，扑向了墙纸和浆糊盆，一如当年扑向萨布拉装潢尝试中的那些带盖坛子。不出一个月，路易·赫夫纳被迫进了一整堂墙纸，供应本地需求。
慢慢地，慢慢地，这地方的生活——起初那样狂野、那样零散不相连——开始穿来穿去，经线纬线，织出纹样来。起初纹样很淡，几乎辨不出。可不一会儿，眼睛就能在这儿认出一个母题，那儿认出一个图形；这儿一个母题，那儿一个图形。梭子来回、来回地穿。

===Original===

“It’s almost time for the Jew,” Sabra would say, looking up from her sewing. “I need some number forty sewing-machine needles.”
And then perhaps next day, or the day after, Cim, playing in the yard, would see a familiar figure, bent almost double, gnomelike and grotesque, against the western sky. It was Sol Levy, the peddler, the Alsatian Jew. Cim would come running into the house, Donna, perhaps, trotting at his heels. “Mom, here comes the Jew!”
Sabra would fold up her work, brush the threads from her apron; or if her hands were in the dough she would hastily mold and crimp her pie crust so as to be ready for his visit.
Sol Levy had come over an immigrant in the noisome bowels of some dreadful ship. His hair was blue-black and very thick, and his face was white in spite of the burning Southwest sun. A black stubble of beard intensified this pallor. He had delicate blue-veined hands and narrow arched feet. His face was delicate, too, and narrow, and his eyes slanted ever so little at the outer corners, so that he had the faintly Oriental look sometimes seen in the student type of his race. He belonged in crowded places, in populous places, in the color and glow and swift drama of the bazaars. God knows how he had found his way to this vast wilderness. Perhaps in Chicago, or in Kansas City, or Omaha he had heard of this new country and the rush of thousands for its land. And he had bummed his way on foot. He had started to peddle with an oilcloth-covered pack on his back. Through the little hot Western towns in summer. Through the bitter cold Western towns in winter. They turned the dogs on him. The children cried, “Jew! Jew!” He was only a boy, disguised with that stubble of beard. He would enter the yard of a farmhouse or a dwelling in a town such as Osage. A wary eye on the dog. Nice Fido. Nice doggie. Down, down! Pins, sewing-machine needles, rolls of gingham and calico, and last, craftily, his Hamburg lace. Hamburg lace for the little girls’ petticoats, for the aprons of the lady of the house; the white muslin apron edged with Hamburg lace, to be donned after the midday dinner dishes were done, the house set to rights, her hair tidied with a wet comb, the basket of mending got out, or the roll of strips for the rag rug, to be plaited in the precious hours between three and five. He brought news, too.
“The bridge is out below Gray Horse.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“犹太人该来了，”萨布拉做针线时抬眼说。“我要几根四十号缝纫机针。”
然后也许第二天，或第三天，在院里玩的西姆就会望见一个熟悉的身影，对着西天，弯得几乎对折，侏儒似的，怪模怪样。那是索尔·利维，货郎，阿尔萨斯的犹太人。西姆会跑进屋来，唐娜也许颠颠地跟在脚后。“妈，犹太人来了！”
萨布拉就叠起活计，拂去围裙上的线头；手若正在面里，就赶忙把派皮捏好捏出边，准备迎他。
索尔·利维是移民过来的，挤在某条可怕轮船的恶臭肚腹里。他的头发蓝黑、厚密，任凭西南的烈日晒，脸依旧白。一部黑色的须茬把这白衬得更白。他有一双青筋细绕的手，一双窄而弓的脚。脸也细，也窄，外眼角微微上挑，使他带上一点东方的神情——他那族人中的学生型有时可见。他本该属于拥挤的地方，人烟稠密的地方，属于集市那样的色彩、光焰和疾速的悲欢。天知道他怎么流落到了这片茫茫荒野。也许在芝加哥，或堪萨斯城，或奥马哈，他听说了这个新国家、听说了几千人抢地的洪流。他是靠两条腿白扒路来的。起初背着一个油布包背货。夏天穿这些火炉般的小镇，冬天穿这些冰窖般的小镇。人们放狗咬他。孩子们喊：“犹太佬！犹太佬！”他不过是个少年，靠那部须茬充老。他会走进一家农舍的院子，或奥塞奇这样镇子上一户人家的院子。眼角防着狗。好菲多。乖狗狗。下去，下去！别针，缝纫机针，一卷卷格子布印花布，最后，机巧地，他的汉堡花边。汉堡花边，给小姑娘的衬裙，给主妇的围裙；那条镶汉堡花边的白纱围裙，等午饭碗碟洗完、屋子归置停当、头发蘸水梳光、补衣篮端出来——或者布条卷拿出来，准备在三点到五点那金贵的时辰里编碎布毯——那时再系上。他还捎来新闻。
“灰马下头的桥断了。……

===Original===

The Osages are having a powwow at Hominy. All night they kept me awake with their drums, those savages.﻿ ﻿… The Kid and his gang held up the Santa Fe near Wetoka and got thirty-five thousand dollars; but one of them will never hold up a train again. A shot in the head. Verdigris Bob, they call him. A name! They say the posse almost caught the Kid himself because this Verdigris Bob when he finds he is dying he begs the others to leave him and go on, but first they must stop to take his boots off. His boots he wants to have off, that murderer, to die a respectable man! The Kid stops to oblige him, and the posse in ten more minutes would have caught him, too. A feather in that sheriff’s cap, to catch the Kid!﻿ ﻿… A country! My forefathers should have lived to see me here!”
His beautiful, civilized face, mobile as an actor’s, was at once expressive of despair and bitter amusement. His long slender hands were spread in a gesture of wondering resignation.
Later he bought a horse﻿—a quadruped possessed unbelievably of the power of locomotion﻿—a thing rheumy-eyed, cadaverous, high rumped, like a cloth horse in a pantomime. Sol Levy was always a little afraid of it; timorous of those great square white teeth, like gravestones. He came of a race of scholars and traders. Horses had been no part of their experience. He had to nerve himself to wait on it, to give it the feed bag, an occasional apple or lump of sugar. With the horse and rickety wagon he now added kitchenware to his stock, coarse china, too; bolts of woollen cloth; and, slyly, bright colored silks and muslin flowers and ribbons. Dixie Lee and her girls fell upon these with feverish fingers and shrill cries, like children. He spread his wares for them silently. Sometimes they teased him, those pretty morons; they hung on his meager shoulders, stroked his beard. He regarded them remotely, almost sadly.
“Come on, Solly!” they said. “Why don’t you smile? Don’t you never have no fun? I bet you’re rich. Jews is all rich. Ain’t that the truth, Maude?”
His deep-sunk eyes looked at them. Shiksas. They grew uncomfortable under his gaze, then sullen, then angry. “Go on, get the hell out of here! You got your money, ain’t you? Get, sheeny!”
He sometimes talked to Dixie Lee. There existed between these two a strange relation of understanding and something resembling respect.

===Chinese===

奥塞奇人在霍米尼跳鬼舞。敲了一夜的鼓，闹得我睡不着，那帮野人。……‘小子’和他的帮伙在威托卡附近劫了圣菲的车，得了三万五千块；可他们中间有一个再也劫不了车了。头上吃了一枪。人叫他铜绿鲍勃。这算什么名字！他们还说，民团差一点儿逮住‘小子’本人——就因为那个铜绿鲍勃，他发现自己要死，求大伙丢下他快走，可走之前得停下来给他脱靴子。他要脱了靴子死，那个杀人犯，死个体面人！‘小子’停下来成全他，再过十分钟民团就把他也抓住了。逮住‘小子’，那警官帽子上可就添彩了！……好一个国度！我的祖先们真该活着看看我流落到这儿！”
他那张漂亮的、文明的脸，灵机得像演员的脸，一时表着绝望，一时表着苦涩的莞尔。他细长的双手摊开，作一个愕然听天的手势。
后来他买了一匹马——一头竟然具备移动能力的四足兽——泪眼、骷髅相、高屁股，像哑剧里的布马。索尔·利维一直有点怕它；怕那口方方正正的白牙，像墓碑。他出自一个学者与商人的种族。马从来不在他们的经验里。他得给自己打气才敢伺候它，给它套料袋，偶尔赏一只苹果或一块糖。有了马和那辆摇摇晃晃的货车，他的货里如今添了厨具，还有粗瓷；整匹的呢料；还有——偷偷地——鲜亮的绸子、纱做的假花和缎带。狄克茜·李和她的姑娘们扑在这些东西上，手指发烧，叫声尖利，像一群孩子。他默默地为她们摆开货。有时她们逗他，那些漂亮的白痴；她们吊在他瘦削的肩上，捋他的胡子。他远远地看着她们，几乎是忧伤地。
“来嘛，索利！”她们说。“你怎么不笑？你就从来不痛快痛快？我敢说你准有钱。犹太人个个有钱。是不是，莫德？”
他那双深陷的眼睛看着她们。非我族女。她们在他的注视下不自在起来，继而恼羞，继而撒泼。“去你的，滚出去！钱不是给你了吗？滚，犹太鬼！”
他有时同狄克茜·李说话。这两人之间有一种奇特的谅解，和一种近乎敬意的东西。

===Original===

Outcasts, both of them, he because of his race, she because of her calling. “A smart girl like you, what do you want in such a business?”
“I’ve got to live, Solly. God knows why!”
“You come from a good family. You are young yet, you are smart. There are other ways.”
“Ye‑e‑e‑s? I guess I’ll take up school teaching. Tell a lot of snotty-nosed brats that two and two make four and get handed eleven dollars at the end of the month for it. I tried a couple of things. Nix, nix!”
In a year or two he opened a little store in Osage. It was, at first, only a wooden shack containing two or three rough pine tables on which his wares were spread. He was the town Jew. He was a person apart. Sometimes the cowboys deviled him; or the saloon loungers and professional bad men. They looked upon him as fair game. He thought of them as savages. Yancey came to his rescue one day in the spectacular fashion he enjoyed. Seated at his desk in the *Wigwam* office Yancey heard hoots, howls, catcalls, and then the *crack* and *rat-a-tat-tat* of a fusillade. The porch of the Sunny Southwest Saloon was filled with grinning faces beneath sombreros. In the middle of the dusty road, his back against a Howe scale, stood Sol Levy. They had tried to force him to drink a great glass of whisky straight. He had struggled, coughed, sputtered; had succeeded in spitting out the burning stuff. They had got another. They were holding it up from their vantage point on the porch. Their six-shooters were in their hands. And they were shooting at him﻿—at his feet, at his head, at his hands, expertly, devilishly, miraculously, never hitting him but always careful to come within a fraction of an inch. He had no weapon. He would not have known how to use it if he had possessed one. He was not of a race of fighters.
“Drink it!” the yells were high and less than human. “You’re a dead Jew if you don’t. Dance, gol darn you! Dance for your drink!”
The bullets spat all about him, sang past his ears, whipped up the dust about his feet. He did not run. He stood there, facing them, frozen with fear. His arms hung at his sides. His face was deathly white. They had shot off his hat. He was bareheaded. His eyes were sunken, suffering, stricken. His head lolled a little on one side. His thick black locks hung dank on his forehead.

===Chinese===

两个弃儿，他因为他的种族，她因为她的行当。“像你这样伶俐的姑娘，干这行干什么？”
“我得活呀，索利。天知道为什么！”
“你出身好人家。你还年轻，你伶俐。还有别的路。”
“是——吗？我看我去教书吧。教一群鼻涕娃娃二加二等于四，月底倒找我十一块钱。我试过几样了。不灵，不灵！”
一两年后，他在奥塞奇开了一家小店。起初不过一座木棚，摆两三张粗松木桌，货摊在上面。他是镇上的犹太人。他是个特殊的人。牛仔们有时作践他；酒馆闲人和职业恶棍也作践。他们拿他当正当的猎物。他把他们看作野人。一天，扬西以他钟爱的那种惊人方式替他解了围。扬西正坐在《棚屋报》办公室的桌前，听见一片嚎叫、喝倒彩、猫叫，跟着是一阵排枪的脆响和嗒嗒嗒。阳光西南酒馆的门廊上挤满了阔边帽底下咧嘴的脸。尘土的大路中央，索尔·利维背抵着一台豪厄台秤站着。他们逼他灌一大杯纯威士忌。他挣扎、咳嗽、喷溅，总算把那烧刀子吐了出来。他们又斟了一杯。他们从门廊那居高临下的位置举着它。六响枪在他们手里。他们朝他射击——朝他的脚，他的头，他的手，打得准，打得刁，打得神，从没碰着他，却回回小心地擦着毫厘之差。他没有武器。有也不会使。他不是一个战士的种族。
“喝！”嚎声高得没了人味。“不喝你就是个死犹太人。跳啊，该死的！为你的酒跳！”
子弹在他四周噼啪，擦着耳朵唱，把他脚边的尘土打得直冒。他不跑。他站在那儿，面对他们，吓僵了。两臂垂在身侧。脸白得像死人。帽子已叫他们打飞了。他光着头。眼睛深陷，痛楚，呆滞。头微微歪向一边。浓黑的发绺湿漉漉贴在额上。

===Original===

At that first instant of seeing him as he rushed out of his office, Yancey thought, subconsciously, “He looks like﻿—like﻿—” But the resemblance eluded him then. It was only later, after the sickening incident had ended, that he realized of Whom it was that the Jew had reminded him as he stood there, crucified against the scale.
Yancey ran into the road. It is impossible to say how he escaped being killed by one of the bullets. He seemed to leap into the thick of them like a charmed thing. As he ran he whipped out his ivory-handled guns, and at that half the crowd on the saloon porch made a dash for the door and were caught in it and fell sprawling, and picked themselves up, and crawled or ran again until they were inside. Yancey stood beside Sol Levy, the terrible look in his eyes, the great head thrust forward and down, like a buffalo charging. Here was a scene to his liking.
“I’ll drill the first son of a bitch that fires another shot. I will, so help me God! Go on, fire now, you dirty dogs. You filthy loafers. You stinking spawn of a rattlesnake!”
He was, by now, a person in the community﻿—he was, in fact, the person in the town. The porch loafers looked sheepish. They sheathed their weapons, or twirled them, sulkily.
“Aw, Yancey, we was foolin’!”
“We was only kiddin’ the Jew.﻿ ﻿… Lookit him, the white-livered son of a gun. Lookit﻿—Holy Doggie, look at him! He’s floppin’.”
With a little sigh Sol Levy slid to the dust of the road and lay in a crumpled heap at the foot of the Howe scale. It was at that moment, so curiously does the human mind work, that Yancey caught that elusive resemblance. Now he picked the man up and flung him over his great shoulder as he would a sack of meal.
“Yah!” hooted the jokesters, perhaps a little shamefaced now.
Yancey, on his way to his own house so near by, made first a small detour that brought him to the foot of the tobacco-stained saloon porch steps. His eyes were like two sword blades flashing in the sun.
“Greasers! Scum of the Run! Monkey skulls!”
His limp burden dangling over his shoulder, he now strode through the *Wigwam* office, into the house, and laid him gently down on the sitting-room couch. Revived, Sol Levy stopped to midday dinner with the Cravats. He sat, very white, very still, in his chair and made delicate pretense of eating.

===Chinese===

冲出办公室、第一眼望见他的那一瞬，扬西下意识想：“他像——像——”那相似当时没捉住。直到这场叫人作呕的事收了场，他才想起：那犹太人背抵着秤杆立在那儿的样子，让他想起的是谁。
扬西冲进大路。没法说他怎么没叫子弹撂倒。他像一件有神护的东西，一头扎进弹雨中央。边跑他边拔出那对象牙柄的枪，门廊上的人一见，一半抢门而入，堵在门口，摔作一团，爬起来，再爬再跑，直到进了屋。扬西站在索尔·利维身边，眼里那副可怕的神色，那颗大脑袋前倾下压，像一头冲阵的野牛。这正是他中意的场面。
“再开一枪的狗娘养的，我给他钻个眼儿。上帝作证！开啊，脏狗。臭懒汉。响尾蛇下的崽子！”
他如今在这地方是个人物——事实上，是这镇上头一号人物。门廊上的闲汉们讪讪的。他们收了枪，或悻悻地转着枪。
“哎，扬西，我们闹着玩呢！”
“我们不过逗逗犹太佬。……瞧他那样，没卵子的家伙。瞧——我的老天，瞧他！他倒了。”
索尔·利维轻轻一声叹息，滑倒在路尘里，瘫作一堆，倒在豪厄台秤脚边。恰在那一刻——人心就是这样奇怪——扬西捉住了那个捉摸不定的相似。此刻他把那人拎起，往自己那副大肩膀上一撂，像撂一袋粗粉。
“呀！”玩笑精们起哄，这会儿多少有点惭愧。
扬西要去的自家近在咫尺，却先拐了个小弯，绕到那被烟草汁浸透的酒馆门廊台阶下。他的眼睛像两把在太阳底下闪的剑。
“下等人！抢地场上的渣滓！猴脑壳！”
软塌塌的负担在他肩上晃着，他大步穿过《棚屋报》办公室，走进住宅，把他轻轻安置在客厅的躺椅上。缓过来之后，索尔·利维留下来同克拉瓦特一家吃午饭。他坐在椅子里，很白，很静，细巧地装着吃。

===Original===

Sabra, because Yancey asked her to, though she was mystified, had got out her DeGrasse silver and a set of her linen. His long meager fingers dwelt lingeringly on the fine hand-wrought stuff. His deep-sunk haunting eyes went from Sabra’s clear-cut features, with the bold determined brows, to Yancey’s massive head, then to the dazzling freshness of the children’s artless countenances.
“This is the first time that I have sat at such a table in two years. My mother’s table was like this, in the old country. My father﻿—peace to his soul!﻿—lighted the candles. My mother﻿—sainted﻿—spread the table with her linen and her precious thin silver. Here in this country I eat as we would not have allowed a beggar to eat that came to the door for charity.”
“This Oklahoma country’s no place for you, Sol. It’s too rough, too hard. You come of a race of dreamers.”
The melancholy eyes took on a remote﻿—a prophetic look. There was, suddenly, a slight cast in them, as though he were turning his vision toward something the others could not see. “It will not always be like this. Wait. Those savages today will be myths, like the pictures of monsters you see in books of prehistoric days.”
“Don’t worry about those dirty skunks, Sol. I’ll see that they leave you alone from now on.”
Sol Levy smiled a little bitter smile. His thin shoulders lifted in a weary shrug. “Those barbarians! My ancestors were studying the Talmud and writing the laws the civilized world now lives by when theirs were swinging from tree to tree.”

===Chinese===

萨布拉虽然纳罕，还是照扬西的嘱托，摆出了她德格拉斯花样的银器和一套亚麻布。他细长的手指在那精致的手织物上流连。他那双萦怀的深陷的眼睛，从萨布拉那轮廓分明、眉峰果敢的脸，移到扬西的巨头，再移到孩子们那两张天真脸庞的耀眼新鲜。
“两年来，我头一回坐这样的餐桌。在故国，我母亲的餐桌就是这样。我父亲——愿他灵魂安息——点起蜡烛。我母亲——已列入圣品——铺开她的亚麻布和她的贵重薄银器。而在这个国度，我吃的这饭，从前有叫花子上门讨饭，我们都不给他吃这样的。”
“这俄克拉何马不是你待的地方，索尔。太粗，太硬。你出自一个梦想家的种族。”
那双忧郁的眼睛里泛起一种遥远的——预言者的神色。忽然，那双眼睛微微内斜，仿佛他把目光转向了旁人看不见的什么东西。“不会永远这样的。等着。今天这些野人，来日就是神话，像史前书里画的怪兽。”
“别担心那些臭鼬，索尔。我保证，往后他们不敢惹你。”
索尔·利维淡淡一笑，笑里带苦。瘦削的双肩做一个疲倦的耸。“那些野蛮人！我的祖先在研读《塔木德》、写下今日文明世界赖以生活的律法的时候，他们的祖先还在树上荡来荡去呢。”

## XII / 十二
===Original===

In the three and a half years of her residence in Osage Sabra had yielded hardly an inch. It was amazing. It was heroic. She had set herself certain standards, and those she had maintained in spite of almost overwhelming opposition. She had been bred on tradition. If she had yielded at all it was in minor matters and because to do so was expedient. True, she could be seen of a morning on her way to the butcher’s or the grocer’s shielded from the sun by one of the gingham sunbonnets which in the beginning she had despised. Certainly one could not don a straw bonnet, velvet or flower trimmed, to dart out in a calico house dress for the purchase of a pound and a half of round steak, ten cents worth of onions, and a yeast cake.
Once only in those three years had she gone back to Wichita. At the prospect of the journey she had been in a fever of anticipation for days. She had taken with her Cim and Donna. She was so proud of them, so intent on outfitting them with a wardrobe sufficiently splendid to set off their charms, that she neglected the matter of her own costuming and found herself arriving in Wichita with a trunk containing the very clothes with which she had departed from it almost four years earlier. Prominent among these was the green nun’s veiling with the pink ruchings. She had had little enough use for it in these past years or for the wine-colored silk-warp henrietta.
“Your skin!” Felice Venable had exclaimed at sight of her daughter. “Your hands! Your hair! As dry as a bone! You look a million. What have you done to yourself?”
Sabra remembered something that Yancey once had said about Texas. Mischievously she paraphrased it in order to shock her tactless mother. “Oklahoma is fine for men and horses, but it’s hell on women and oxen.”
The visit was not a success. The very things she had expected to enjoy fell, somehow, flat. She missed the pace, the exhilarating uncertainty of the Oklahoma life. The teacup conversation of her girlhood friends seemed to lack tang and meaning. Their existence was orderly, calm, accepted. For herself and the other women of Osage there was everything still to do. There lay a city, a county, a whole vast Territory to be swept and garnished by an army of sunbonnets. Paradoxically enough, she was trying to implant in the red clay of Osage the very forms and institutions that now bored her in Wichita.

===Chinese===

在奥色治住了三年半，萨布拉几乎没有退让过一寸。这了不得。这是英勇的。她给自己立下了某些标准，不顾几乎压倒一切的阻力，守住了它们。她是传统喂大的。要说退让，也只是些小事，且因为退让方便。不错，如今早晨也能看见她去肉铺或杂货铺，戴着一顶她当初鄙夷的条布遮阳帽遮太阳。穿着印花布家常裙子出门买一磅半牛后腿肉、一毛钱的洋葱和一块酵母饼，总不能戴一顶丝绒或缀花的草帽吧。

这三年里她只回过威奇塔一次。行期一定，她兴奋得像发了几天的烧。她带上了西姆和唐娜。她为他们那样骄傲，一心要给他们置办一套足衬其可爱的行头，反倒疏忽了自己的装束，等到了威奇塔才发现，箱子里装的原封是四年前离开时的那些衣裳。其中显眼的是那件缀粉红褶边的绿修女薄呢。这几年来，它也好，那件酒红丝经亨丽埃塔也好，几乎没派上过用场。

“你的皮肤！”菲莉丝·维纳布尔一见女儿就叫。“你的手！你的头发！干得像骨头！你可真‘精神’。你把自己怎么了？”

萨布拉想起扬西从前说过得克萨斯的一句话。她淘气地改了改，好刺一刺她那没心没肺的母亲。“俄克拉何马养男人和马，精蹋女人和牛。”

这次探亲不算成功。她原指望享受的那些东西，不知怎么都淡了。她惦记俄克拉何马生活的步子和那种提神的不可测。少女时代女友们的茶杯闲谈，似乎没了滋味和意思。她们的日子是有条不紊、安安静静、照单全收的。而她自己和奥色治的女人们，一切都还等着动手。那里有一座城、一个县、一整片广大的领地，等着一支遮阳帽大军去打扫、去装点。矛盾得很：她正往奥色治的红土里栽的，恰恰是如今在威奇塔叫她厌倦的那些形式和建制。

===Original===

Yet it was, perhaps, a very human trait. It was illustrated literally by the fact that she was, on her return, more thrilled to find that the scrawny elm, no larger than a baby’s arm, which she had planted outside the doorway in Osage, actually had found some moisture for its thirsty roots, and was now feebly vernal, than she had been at sight of the cool glossy canopy of cedar, arbor vitae, sweet locust, and crêpe myrtle that shaded the Kansas garden. She took a perverse delight in bringing the shocked look to the faces of her Wichita friends, and to all the horde of Venables and Marcys and Vians that swarmed up from the South to greet the pioneer. Curiously enough, it was not the shooting affrays and Indian yarns that ruffled them so much as her stories of the town’s social life.
“… rubber boots to parties, often, because when it rains we wade up to our ankles in mud. We carry lanterns when we go to the church sociables.﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Buckner’s sister came to visit her from St. Joseph, Missouri, and she remarked that she had noticed that the one pattern of table silver seemed to be such a favorite. She had seen it at all the little tea parties that had been given for her during her visit. Of course it was my set that had been the rounds. Everybody borrowed it. We borrow each other’s lamps, too, and china, and even linen.”
At this the Venables and Marcys and Vians and Goforths looked not only shocked but stricken. Chests of lavender-scented linen, sideboards flashing with stately silver, had always been part of the Venable and Marcy tradition.
Then the children. The visiting Venables insisted on calling Cim by his full name﻿—Cimarron. Sabra had heard it so rarely since the day of his birth that she now realized, for the first time, how foolish she had been to yield to Yancey’s whim in the naming of the boy. Cimarron. Spanish: wild, or unruly. The boy had made such an obstreperous entrance into the world, and Yancey had shouted, in delight, “Look at him! See him kick with his feet and strike out with his fists! He’s a wild one. Heh, Cimarron! *Peceno Gitano.*”
Cousin Jouett Goforth or Cousin Dabney Venable said, pompously, “And now, Cimarron, my little man, tell us about the big red Indians. Did you ever fight Indians, eh, Cimarron?” The boy surveyed them from beneath his long lashes, his head lowered, looking for all the world like his father.

===Chinese===

不过，这或许正是很人性的一点。有桩事实可为明证：回程时，她发现自己种在奥色治门口的那棵瘦榆树——细得不过婴儿胳膊——当真给干渴的根找到了一点水汽，如今怯怯地透出春意，她见了它，竟比看见堪萨斯园里那清凉油亮的雪松、崖柏、皂荚和紫薇的华盖还要欢喜。她怀着一种别扭的快乐，去把威奇塔的朋友、还有从南方涌来看望拓荒者的维纳布尔、马西、维安浩浩荡荡一族人的脸，一张张弄成惊愕。说来奇怪，叫他们皱眉的，倒不是枪战和印第安奇谈，而是她讲的镇上社交生活。

“……去赴会常常穿胶靴，因为一下雨，我们得蹚过脚踝深的泥。去教会联谊，我们提灯笼。……巴克尔太太的妹妹从密苏里州圣约瑟来看她，说她注意到，好像全镇就流行一种银餐具。她来做客期间，人家为她办的那些小茶会上，回回见到同一套。当然，那是我的那套，转了一圈又一圈。人人都借。我们还互借灯，借瓷器，连亚麻桌布都借。”

听到这里，维纳布尔、马西、维安、戈福思们不光惊愕，简直震动了。一柜柜薰衣草香的亚麻布，一副副银光闪闪的餐具，向来是维纳布尔和马西家传统的一部分。

然后是孩子。来做客的维纳布尔们坚持叫西姆的全名——西马龙。自他出生那天起，萨布拉极少听到这名字，此刻才头一回明白，当初顺着扬西的性子给孩子起名，自己有多傻。西马龙。西班牙语：野，或不驯。这孩子出生时来势那样凶猛，扬西欢喜得直喊：“瞧他！看他蹬脚、挥拳！是个野的。嘿，西马龙！*Peceno Gitano*。”

朱厄特·戈福思表哥或者达布尼·维纳布尔表哥煞有介事地说：“那么，西马龙，小家伙，给我们讲讲大红印第安人。你跟印第安人打过仗吗，啊，西马龙？”孩子垂着头，从长长的睫毛底下打量他们，活脱是他父亲。

===Original===

Cimarron was almost eight now. If it is possible for a boy of eight to be romantic in aspect, Cimarron Cravat was that. His head was not large, like Yancey’s, but long and fine, like Sabra’s﻿—a Venable head. His eyes were Sabra’s, too, dark and large, but they had the ardent look of Yancey’s gray ones, and he had Yancey’s absurdly long and curling lashes, like a beautiful girl’s. His mannerisms﻿—the head held down, the rare upward glance that cut you like a sword thrust when he turned it full on you﻿—the swing of his walk, the way he gestured with his delicate hands﻿—all these were Yancey in startling miniature.
His speech was strangely adult. This, perhaps, because of his close association with his elders in those first formative years in Osage. Yancey had delighted in talking to the boy; in taking him on rides and drives about the broad burning countryside. His skin was bronzed the color of his father’s. He looked like a little patrician Spaniard or perhaps (the Venables thought privately) part Indian. Then, too, there had been few children of his age in the town’s beginning. Sabra had been, at first, too suspicious of such as there were. He would, probably, have seemed a rather unpleasant and priggish little boy if his voice and manner had not been endowed miraculously with all the charm and magnetism that his father possessed in such disarming degree.
He now surveyed his middle-aged cousins with the concentrated and disconcerting gaze of the precocious child.
“Indians,” he answered, with great distinctness, “don’t fight white men any more. They can’t. Their﻿—uh﻿—spirit is broken.” Cousin Dabney Venable, who still affected black stocks (modified), now looked slightly apoplectic. “They only fought in the first place because the white men took their buff’loes away from them that they lived on and ate and traded the skins and that was all they had; and their land away from them.”
“Well,” exclaimed Cousin Jouett Goforth, of the Louisiana Goforths, “this is quite a little Redskin you have here, Cousin Sabra.”
“And,” continued Cimarron, warming to his subject, “look at the Osage Indians where my father took me to visit the reservation near where we live.

===Chinese===

西马龙如今快八岁了。倘若一个八岁的男孩也可能相貌浪漫，西马龙·克拉瓦特就是了。他的头不像扬西的那样大，而是像萨布拉的一样，长而秀气——一颗维纳布尔的头。眼睛也是萨布拉的，又黑又大，却带着扬西那双灰眼睛的炽烈；他又生了扬西那副长得荒唐、卷得漂亮的睫毛，像漂亮姑娘的。他的举止——垂着头，难得抬眼一瞥，可一旦正对你，那一瞥利得像剑——走路的摆荡，那双秀气的手比画的姿势——这一切都是惊人缩影的扬西。

他谈吐出奇地老成。这多半因为在奥色治那最初的、塑形的几年里，他整天同大人厮混。扬西顶喜欢同这孩子说话；喜欢带他骑马驾车，逛那辽阔灼热的乡野。他的皮肤晒成了父亲那样的古铜。他看上去像个小小的西班牙贵族，或者——维纳布尔们私下想——有一半印第安血。再说，建镇之初，镇上同龄的孩子本来就少。起初，萨布拉对那几个也信不过。若不是他的嗓音和风度奇迹般地继承了他父亲那种令人解除武装的魅力和磁力，他多半会显得是个相当讨厌、相当迂腐的小男孩。

此刻，他用早熟孩子那种聚精会神、叫人发窘的注视，打量着他这些中年的表亲。

“印第安人，”他一字一顿地答，“不再同白人打仗了。他们打不了。他们的——呃——气断了。”还系着（改良过的）黑领带的达布尼·维纳布尔表哥，这会儿脸色有点发紫。“他们当初打仗，不过是因为白人夺走了他们赖以为生的野牛——他们吃它，拿皮子做买卖，那是他们的全部家当；还夺走了他们的土地。”

“嗐哟，”路易斯安那戈福思家的朱厄特·戈福思表哥叫道，“萨布拉表妹，你这儿可是养了个小红番。”

“还有，”西马龙越讲越来劲，“瞧瞧奥色治印第安人，我父亲带我去过我们家附近他们的保留地。”

===Original===

The white people made them move out of Missouri to Kansas because they wanted their land, and from there to another place﻿—I forget﻿—and then they wanted that, too, and they said, ‘Look, you go and live in the Indian Territory where we tell you,’ and it’s all bare there, and nothing grows in that place﻿—it’s called the Bad Lands﻿—unless you work and slave and the Osages they were used to hunting and fishing not farming, so they are just starving to death and my father says some day they will get their revenge on the white﻿—”
Felice Venable turned her flashing dark gaze on her daughter.
“Aha!” said Cousin Jouett Goforth.
Cousin Dabney Venable, still the disgruntled suitor, brought malicious eyes to bear on Sabra. “Well, well, Cousin Sabra! Look out that you don’t have a Pocahontas for a daughter-in-law some fine day.”
Sabra was furious, though she tried in her pride to conceal it. “Oh, Cim has just heard the talk of the men around the newspaper office﻿—the Indian agent, Mr. Heeney, sometimes drops in on his trips to Osage﻿—they’re talking now of having the Indian Agent’s office transferred to Osage, though Oklahoma City is fighting for it﻿—Yancey has always been very much stirred by the wretched Indians﻿—Cim has heard him talking.”
Cim sensed that he had not made his desired effect on his listeners. “My father says,” he announced, suddenly, striding up and down the room in absurd and unconscious imitation of his idol﻿—one could almost see the Prince Albert coat tails switching﻿—“my father says that some day an Indian will be President of the United States, and then you bet you’ll all be sorry you were such dirty skunks to ’em.”
The eyes of the visiting Venables swung, as one orb, from the truculent figure of the boy to the agitated face of the mother.
“My poor child!” came from Felice Venable in accents of rage rather than pity. She was addressing Sabra.
Sabra took refuge in hauteur. “You wouldn’t understand. Our life there is so different from yours here. Yancey’s Indian editorials in the *Wigwam* have made a sensation. They were spoken of in the Senate at Washington.” Felice dismissed all Yancey’s written works with a wave of her hand.

===Chinese===

“白人逼他们从密苏里搬到堪萨斯，因为白人要他们的地；又从那儿赶到另一个地方——我忘了——后来那儿白人也要了，他们说：‘瞧着，你们搬到印第安领地去，住我们指定的地方，’那地方光秃秃的，什么也不长——那叫荒地——除非你苦干死干；可奥色治人过惯了打猎捕鱼，不会种地，所以他们正活活饿死；我父亲说，总有一天他们要找白人报仇——”

菲莉丝·维纳布尔把她炯炯的黑目光转到女儿身上。

“啊哈！”朱厄特·戈福思表哥说。

达布尼·维纳布尔表哥——至今还是那个没死心的求婚者——把恶意的目光对准萨布拉。“嗐嗐，萨布拉表妹！当心哪天讨个波卡洪塔斯做儿媳妇。”

萨布拉气坏了，虽然碍于骄傲竭力不显。“哦，西姆不过是听多了报馆里男人们的闲谈——印第安事务官希尼先生有时去奥色治顺路进来坐坐——他们如今正商量把印第安事务官署迁到奥色治，虽然俄克拉何马城也在抢——扬西一向为可怜的印第安人懤懤不平——西姆听了他的话。”

西姆觉出自己没在听者身上收到预期的效果。“我父亲说，”他忽然宣布，一边在屋里踱来踱去，对他崇拜的偶像那副做派荒唐地、全然不自觉地模仿着——几乎看得见普林斯·艾伯特礼服的衣摆在甩——“我父亲说，总有一天，一个印第安人会做合众国总统，到那时你们准会后悔，后悔自己对他们这样卑鄙无耻。”

来做客的维纳布尔们的眼睛，像一只眼球似的齐刷刷从孩子好斗的身影，转到母亲慌乱的脸上。

“我可怜的孩子！”菲莉丝·维纳布尔的语气是愤怒多过怜悯。她说的是萨布拉。

萨布拉躲进高傲里。“你们不会懂的。我们在那边的生活同你们这里两样。扬西在《维格沃姆报》上那些印第安社论轰动一时。华盛顿的参议院都提到过。”菲莉丝把手一挥，勾销了扬西的全部文字。

===Original===

“In fact,” Sabra went on﻿—she who hated Indians and all their ways﻿—“in fact, his editorials on the subject have been so fearless and free that he has been in danger of his life from the people who have been cheating the Indians. It has been even more dangerous than when he tracked down the murderer of Pegler.”
“Pegler,” repeated the Venables, disdainfully, and without the slightest curiosity in their voices.
Sabra gave it up. “You don’t understand. The only thing you care about is whether the duck runs red or not.”
Even little Donna was not much of a success. The baby was an eerie little elf, as plain as the boy was handsome. She resembled her grandmother, Felice Venable, without a trace of that redoubtable matron’s former beauty. But she had that almost indefinable thing known as style. At the age of two she wore with undeniable chic the rather clumsy little garments that Sabra had so painstakingly made for her; and when she was dressed, for the first time, in one of the exquisitely hemstitched, tucked and embroidered white frocks that her grandmother had wrought for her, that gifted though reluctant needlewomen said, tartly, “Thank God, she’s got style, at least. She’ll have to make out with that.”
All in all, Sabra found herself joyously returning to the barren burning country to which, four years earlier, she had gone in such dread and terror. She resented her mother’s do-this, do-that. She saw Felice Venable now, no longer as a power, an authority in all matters of importance, but as a sallow old lady who tottered on heels that were too high and who, as she sat talking, pleated and unpleated with tremulous fingers the many ruffles of her white dimity wrapper. The matriarch had lost her crown. Sabra was matriarch now of her own little kingdom; and already she was planning to extend that realm beyond and beyond its present confines into who knows what vastness of demesne.
She decided that she must take the children more than ever in hand. No more of this talk of Indians, of freedom, or equality of man. She did not realize (it being long before the day of psychology as applied so glibly to the training of children) that she was, so far as Cim was concerned, years too late. At eight his character was formed.

===Chinese===

“事实上，”萨布拉接着说——她这个恨印第安人、恨他们一切做派的人——“事实上，他这个题目的社论写得那样无畏、那样自由，那些坑骗印第安人的人已经要他的命了。这比他追查佩格勒凶手那阵还危险。”

“佩格勒，”维纳布尔们轻蔑地重复，语气里没有一丝好奇。

萨布拉放弃了。“你们不懂。你们唯一关心的，是鸭肉煎出来红不红。”

连小唐娜也不大成气候。这婴儿是个精灵古怪的小东西，男孩子多俊，她就多平常。她像祖母菲莉丝·维纳布尔，却半点没有那位可怕主妇当年的美貌。可她有那样一样近乎说不清的东西，叫作风。两岁上，她就把萨布拉辛辛苦苦给她做的那些相当笨拙的小衣裳，穿出了不可否认的俏皮劲儿；等她头一回穿上祖母给她缝的、精细地缝了边上褶、抽了细褶、绣了花的那件白裙子，那位天才而不情愿的女红师傅尖刻地说：“谢天谢地，她好歹有风。她就指着这个过吧。”

总而言之，萨布拉发觉自己欢欢喜喜地回那片贫瘠灼热的地方去了——四年前她是怀着何等的恐惧去的。她烦母亲的指手画脚。此刻她看菲莉丝·维纳布尔，再不是一个权力、一个诸事权威，而是一个踩着过高的鞋跟摇摇晃晃的蜡黄老太，坐着说话时，用发抖的手指把白色条布晨衣的一道道褶边捏了又放。大家长丢了她的王冠。萨布拉如今是她自己那个小小王国的家长了；而且她已经在计划把疆域越拓越远，拓出如今的边界，拓进天知道多么广大的领地。

她拿定主意，要加倍把孩子抓在手里。不许再讲印第安人、讲自由、讲人人平等。她没有意识到（那还是心理学被轻巧地套用到育儿上的久远之前的时代），就西姆而言，她已经晚了多年。八岁上，他的性格已经成形。

===Original===

She had taught him the things that Felice Venable had taught her﻿—stand up straight; eat your bread and butter; wash your hands; say how do you do to the lady; one and one are two; somebody has been eating *my* porridge, said the little wee bear. But Yancey had taught him poetry far beyond his years, and accustomed his ears to the superb cadences of the Bible; Yancey had told him, bit by bit, and all unconsciously, the saga of the settling of the great Southwest.
“Cowboys wear big sombreros to shield their faces from the rain and the sun when they’re riding the range, and the snow from dripping down their backs. He wears a handkerchief knotted at the back of the neck and hanging down in front so that he can wipe the sweat and dust from his face with it, and then there it is, open, drying in the wind; and in a dust storm he pulls it over his mouth and nose, and in a blizzard it keeps his nose and chin from freezing. He wears chaps, with the hairy side out, to keep his legs warm in winter and to protect them from being torn by chaparral and cactus thorns in summer. His boots are high heeled to keep his feet from slipping in the stirrups when he has to work standing in the saddle, and because he can sink them in the sod when he’s off his horse and roping a plunging bronc. He totes a six-shooter to keep the other fellow from shooting.”
The child’s eyes were enormous, glowing, enthralled. Yancey told him the story of the buffalo; he talked endlessly of the Indians. He even taught him some words of Comanche, which is the court language of the Indian. He put him on a horse at the age of six. A sentimentalist and a romantic, he talked to the boy of the sunset; of Spanish gold; of the wild days of the Cimarron and the empire so nearly founded there. The boy loved his mother dutifully, and as a matter of course, as a child loves the fount of food, of tender care, of shelter. But his father he worshiped, he adored.
Sabra’s leave-taking held one regret, one pain. Mother Bridget had died two weeks before Sabra arrived in Wichita. It was not until she learned this sad news that Sabra realized how tremendously she had counted on telling her tale of Osage to the nun. She would have understood.

===Chinese===

她教他的，是菲莉丝·维纳布尔当年教她的——站直；把面包黄油吃完；洗手；向夫人问好；一加一等于二；“有人吃过*我的*粥，小不点熊说”。可扬西教了他远超年龄的诗，让他的耳朵习惯了《圣经》那雄浑的节律；扬西一点一点、又全然无意地，把大西南定居的英雄史诗讲给了他。

“牛仔戴大阔边帽，是为了在牧场上骑马时给脸挡雨挡太阳，免得雪水顺脊背往下淌。他脖子上系一方手绢，后头打结，前头垂着，好擦脸上的汗和土，擦完一松手，敞着，让风吹干；起沙暴的时候拉起来罩住口鼻，刮白毛风的时候，它护住他的鼻子和下巴不冻掉。他穿皮护腿，毛朝外，冬天暖腿，夏天防丛林和仙人掌的刺撕破。他的靴子高跟，是为了在马上站着干活的当口脚不滑出马镫，也为了下马套一匹暴跳的野马时，靴跟能踩进草皮里。他挂六响枪，是为了叫对方不敢开枪。”

孩子的眼睛瞪得老大，放光，入了迷。扬西给他讲野牛的故事；没完没了地讲印第安人。甚至教了他几句科曼切话——印第安人的宫廷语言。他六岁就把他扶上了马背。他这个感伤主义者和浪漫派，对男孩讲落日；讲西班牙黄金；讲西马龙的那些野日子，和差点在那里建起来的帝国。孩子敬爱母亲，出于本分，理所当然，像孩子爱那食物、温存和荫庇的源头。可对父亲，他是崇拜，是倾倒。

萨布拉的辞行里有一桩遗憾，一桩痛。她到威奇塔前两星期，布丽奇特嬷嬷去世了。直到听见这个噩耗，萨布拉才明白，她原是那样一心一意，打算把奥色治的故事讲给嬷嬷听。嬷嬚会懂的。

===Original===

She would have laughed at the story of the ten barrels of water; of the wild cowboy’s kiss in the road; she would have sympathized with Sabra’s terror during that Sunday church meeting. She had known that very life a half century ago, there in Kansas. Sabra, during her visit, did not go to the Mission School. She could not.
She had meant, at the last, to find occasion to inform her mother and the minor Venables that it was she who ironed Yancey’s fine white linen shirts. But she was not a spiteful woman. And she reflected that this might be construed as a criticism of her husband.
So, gladly, eagerly, Sabra went back to the wilds she once had despised.

===Chinese===

十桶水的故事，路上野牛仔那一吻，嬷嬚听了都会笑；那个礼拜天教会聚会时萨布拉的恐惧，嬷嬚也会体恤。五十年前，就在堪萨斯，嬷嬚过的正是那种日子。探亲期间，萨布拉没有去传教学校。她去不了。

她原打算临走找个机会，告诉母亲和维纳布尔的列位旁支：扬西那些细白麻布衬衫，是他——萨布拉——熨的。可她不是个刻薄的女人。再说她想了想，这话未免会被听成对丈夫的贬损。

于是，萨布拉欢欢喜喜、急急切切地回了她一度鄙视的荒野。

## XIII / 十三
===Original===

Before the Katy pulled in at the Osage station (the railroad actually had been extended, true to Dixie Lee’s prediction, from Wahoo to Osage and beyond) Sabra’s eyes were searching the glaring wooden platform. Len Orson, the chatty and accommodating conductor, took Donna in his arms and stood with her at the foot of the car steps. His heavy gold plated watch chain, as broad as a cable, with its concomitant Masonic charm, elk’s tooth, gold pencil, and peach pit carved in the likeness of an ape, still held Donna enthralled, though she had snatched at it whenever he passed their seat or stood to relate the gossip of the Territory to Sabra. She was hungry for news, and Len was a notorious fishwife. Now, as she stepped off the train, Sabra’s face wore that look of radiant expectancy characteristic of the returned traveler, confident of a welcome.
“Well, I guess I know somebody’ll be pretty sorry to see you,” Len said, archly. He looked about for powerful waiting arms in which to deposit Donna. The engine bell clanged, the whistle tooted. His kindly and inquisitive blue eyes swept the station platform. He plumped Donna, perforce, into Sabra’s strangely slack arms, and planted one foot, in its square-toed easy black shoe, onto the car step in the nick of time, the other leg swinging out behind him as the train moved on.
Yancey was not there. The stark red-painted wooden station sat blistering in the sun. Yancey simply was not there. Not only that, the station platform, usually graced by a score of vacuous faces and limp figures gathered to witness the exciting event of the Katy’s daily arrival and departure, was bare. Even the familiar figure of Pat Leary, the station agent, who always ran out in his shirtsleeves to wrestle such freight or express as was left on the Osage platform, could not be seen. From within the ticket office came the sound of his telegraph instrument. Its click was busy; was frantic. It chattered unceasingly in the hot afternoon stillness.
Sabra felt sick and weak. Something was wrong. She left her boxes and bags and parcels on the platform where Len Orsen had obligingly dumped them.

===Chinese===

凯蒂线还没在奥色治站停稳（铁路果真如迪克茜·李所料，从瓦胡修到了奥色治，还往前延伸），萨布拉的眼睛已经在那晃眼的木头站台上搜寻了。健谈而热心的列车员伦·奥森抱着唐娜，站在车踏板脚下。他那根粗得像缆绳的镀金表链，连着共济会徽记、麋鹿牙、金铅笔和一颗刻成猿猴模样的桃核，仍旧迷得唐娜出神——一路上每逢他从她们的座位边经过，或站着给萨布拉讲领地上的闲话，她都要伸手去抓。她正饿着要新闻，而伦是出了名的长舌妇。此刻她跨下火车，脸上带着归来的旅人那种容光焕发的期待，笃定有人欢迎。

“我看哪，有人见着你要乐坏了。”伦狡黠地说。他四下张望，想找一双有力的、候着的臂膀把唐娜递过去。车头的钟当当响，汽笛呜了一声。他那双善良而好奇的蓝眼睛扫过站台上上下下。他只好把唐娜塞进萨布拉出奇松软的臂弯，一只脚——穿着方头黑便鞋——赶在最后关头踩上车梯，另一条腿随着开动的火车甩在身后。

扬西不在。那座红漆剥落的木头小车站，在太阳下冒着泡。扬西就是不在。不但如此，平日总有二十来张呆脸和懒洋洋的身影聚在站台上，观赏凯蒂线每日到发这件大事，此刻却空空荡荡。连站长帕特·利里那熟悉的身影也看不见——他总是挽着衬衫袖子跑出来，收拾撂在奥色治站台上的行李包裹。售票室里传出他的电报机的声音。滴答声很忙；忙得发疯。在炎午的寂静里，它喋喋不休。

萨布拉觉得又恶心又发虚。出事了。她把箱子提包包裹撂在站台上——伦·奥森好心替她卸下的——

===Original===

Half an hour before their arrival in Osage she had entrusted the children to the care of a fellow passenger while she had gone to the washroom to put on one of the new dresses made in Wichita and bearing the style cachet of Kansas City: green, with cream colored ruchings at the throat and wrists, and a leghorn hat with pink roses. She had anticipated the look in Yancey’s gray eyes at sight of it. She had made the children spotless and threatened them with dire things if they sullied their splendor before their father should see them.
And now he was not there.
With Donna in her arms and Cim at her heels she hurried toward the sound of the clicking. And as she went her eyes still scanned the dusty red road that led to the station, for sight of a great figure in a white sombrero, its coat tails swooping as it came.
She peered in at the station window. Pat Leary was bent over his telegraph key. A smart tight little Irishman who had come to the Territory with the railroad section crew when the Katy was being built. Station agent now, and studying law at night.
“Mr. Leary! Mr. Leary! Have you seen Yancey?” He looked up at her absently, his hand still on the key. *Click﻿ ﻿… click﻿ ﻿… clickclickclickety﻿—clicketyclickclick.*
“Wha’ say?”
“I’m Mrs. Cravat. I just got off the Katy. Where’s my husband? Where’s Yancey?”
He clicked on a moment longer; then wiped his wet forehead with his forearm protected by the black sateen sleevelet. “Ain’t you heard?”
“No,” whispered Sabra, with stiff lips that seemed no part of her. Then, in a voice rising to a scream, “No! No! No! What? Is he dead?”
The Irishman came over to her then, as she crouched at the window. “Oh, no, ma’am. Yancey’s all right. He ain’t hurt to speak of. Just a nick in the arm﻿—and left arm at that.”
“Oh, my God!”
“Don’t take on. You goin’ to faint or﻿—?”
“No. Tell me.”
“I been so busy.﻿ ﻿… Yancey got the Kid, you know. Killed him. The whole town’s gone crazy. Pitched battle right there on Pawhuska Avenue in front of the bank, and bodies layin’ around like a battlefield. I’m sending it out. I ain’t got much time, but I’ll give you an idea. Biggest thing that’s happened in the history of the Territory﻿—or the whole Southwest, for that matter. Shouldn’t wonder if they’d make Yancey President. Governor, anyway. Seems Yancey was out hunting up in the Hills last Thursday﻿—”
“Thursday!
