===Original===

Let’s get our hundred and sixty acre allotment of Cherokee Strip land and start a ranch﻿—raise cattle﻿—live in the open﻿—ride﻿—this town life is no good﻿—it’s hideous.”
Her arms fell, leaden, to her side. “Ranch? Where?”
“You’re not listening. There’s to be a new Run. The Cherokee Strip Opening. You know. You wrote news stories about it only last week, before the opening date had been announced. Let’s go, Sabra. It’s the biggest thing yet. The 1889 Run was nothing compared to it. Sell the *Wigwam*, take the children, make the Run, get our hundred and sixty, start a ranch, stock up with cattle and horses, build a ranch house and patio; in the saddle all day﻿—”
“Never!” screamed Sabra. Her face was distorted. Her hands were clutching the air, as though she would tear to bits this plan of his for the future. “I won’t. I won’t go. I’d rather die first. You can’t make me.”
He came to her, tried to take her in his arms, to pacify her. “Sugar, you won’t understand. It’s the chance of a lifetime. It’s the biggest thing in the history of Oklahoma. When the Territory’s a state we’ll own forever one hundred and sixty acres of the finest land anywhere. I know the section I want.”
“Yes. You know. You know. You knew the last time, too. You let that slut﻿—that hussy﻿—take it away from you﻿—or you gave it to her. Go and take her with you. You’ll never make me go. I’ll stay here with my children and run the paper. Mother! Cim! Donna!”
She had a rare and violent fit of hysterics, after which Yancey, aided clumsily by Arita Red Feather, divested her of the new finery, quieted the now screaming children, and finally restored to a semblance of suppertime order the household into which he had hurled such a bomb. Felice Venable herself, in her heyday, could not have given a finer exhibition of Marcy temperament. It was intended, as are all hysterics (no one ever has hysterics in private), to intimidate the beholder and fill him with remorse. Yancey was properly solicitous, tender, charming, as only he could be. From the shelter of her husband’s arms Sabra looked about the cosy room, smiled wanly upon her children, bade Arita Red Feather bring on the belated supper. “That,” thought Sabra to herself, bathing her eyes, smoothing her hair, and coming pale and wistful to the table, her lip quivering with a final effective sigh, “settles that.”
But it did not.

===Chinese===

咱们去领咱们那一百六十英亩切罗基长条地，办一座牧场——养牛——住露天——骑马——这镇子生活不是东西——丑透了。”
她的手臂铅一样垂回身侧。“牧场？在哪儿？”
“你没在听。要再抢一回地。切罗基长条地开放。你知道的。上礼拜开放日期还没公布，你还写过它的新闻呢。走吧，萨布拉。这是到如今最大的一桩。一八八九那回同它一比什么都不是。卖掉《棚屋报》，带上孩子，去抢地，领咱们那一百六十英亩，办牧场，进牛进马，盖牧场宅子和院子；成天在鞍上——”
“绝不！”萨布拉尖叫。她的脸都扭了。她的双手在空中乱抓，仿佛要把他这份关于未来的计划撕成碎片。“我不。我不去。我宁可先死。你逼不动我。”
他走到她跟前，想搂她、安抚她。“甜心，你就是不明白。这是一生一次的机会。是俄克拉何马历史上最大的一件事。等领地成了州，咱们就永远拥有一百六十英亩头一份的好地。我看中的那块我有数。”
“是。你有数。你有数。上一回你也有数。你让那个荡妇——那个野丫头——把它从你手里拿了去——兴许还是你送给她的。带她去呀。你永远别想叫我走。我带着孩子守在这儿办报。妈妈！西姆！唐娜！”
她发了一场上等的、暴烈的歇斯底里。过后，扬西在阿丽塔·红羽笨手笨脚的帮助下，剥下她那身新鲜华丽，哄住这时已嚎成一团的孩子，总算把这被他投了炸弹的家，恢复到一份晚饭前的秩序。菲莉斯·文纳尔本人全盛时，也演不出一场比这更精的马西式脾气。这一场，正如一切歇斯底里（没人私下里发作歇斯底里），本意就在吓住看客，叫他们愧悔。扬西的关切、温存、迷人，恰到好处，非他不能。萨布拉偎在丈夫臂弯的掩体里，环顾这间温暖的小屋，对孩子们惨然一笑，吩咐阿丽塔·红羽上那顿迟了的晚饭。“这下，”萨布拉心想——洗着眼，抿着发，苍白而幽怨地移到桌前，嘴唇还配合着最后一声有效的一声长叹——“这下摆平了。”
可没有。

===Original===

September actually saw Yancey making ready to go. Nothing that Sabra could say, nothing that she could do, served to stop him. She even negotiated for a little strip of farm land outside the town of Osage and managed to get Yancey to make a payment on it, in the hope that this would keep him from the Run. “If it’s land you want you can stay here and farm the piece at Tuskamingo. You can raise cattle on it. You can breed horses on it.”
Yancey shook his head. He took no interest in the farm. It was Sabra who saw to the erection of a crude little farmhouse, arranged for the planting of such crops as it was thought that land would yield. It was very near the Osage Reservation land and turned out, surprisingly enough, doubtless owing to some mineral or geological reason (they knew why, later), to be fertile, though the Osage land so near by was barren and flinty.
“Farm! That’s no farm. It’s a garden patch. D’you think I’m settling down to be a potato digger and chicken feeder, in a hayseed hat and manure on my boots!”
September, the month of the opening of the vast Cherokee Strip, saw him well on his way. Cim howled to be taken along, and would not be consoled for days.
Sabra’s farewell was intended to be cold. Her heart, she told herself, was breaking. The change that these last four years had made in her never was more apparent than now.
“You felt the same way when I went off to the first Run,” Yancey reminded her. “Remember? You carried on just one degree less than your mother. And if I hadn’t gone you’d still be living in the house in Wichita, with your family smothering you in Southern fried chicken and advice.” There was much truth in this, she had to admit. She melted; clung to him.
“Yancey! Yancey!”
“Smile, sugar. Wait till you see Cim and Donna, five years from now, riding the Cravat acres.”
After all, a hundred other men in Osage were going to make the Cherokee Strip Run. The town﻿—the whole Territory﻿—had talked of nothing else for months.
She dried her eyes. She even managed a watery smile. He was making the Run on a brilliant, wild-eyed mare named Cimarron, with a strain of Spanish in her for speed and grace, and a strain of American mustang for endurance.

===Chinese===

九月里，扬西竟然真备起了行装。萨布拉说什么、做什么，都拦不住。她甚至谈下了奥塞奇镇外一小条农田，还设法叫扬西付了一笔款子，指望这样拴住他，不去抢地。“你若要的是地，你可以留下来，种塔斯卡明戈那一块。你可以在上头养牛。可以在上头育马。”
扬西摇头。他对那田毫无兴味。盖那座粗陋的小农舍、安排下地试种的庄稼，都是萨布拉张罗的。那地离奥塞奇保留地很近，结果——说来出奇，多半因为什么矿质或地质的缘故（缘由他们后来知道了）——竟出奇地肥，虽然近旁的奥塞奇地又瘠又硬。
“农庄！那算什么农庄。一块菜园子。你当我打算定居下来，当个刨土豆、喂鸡的，戴顶草帽、靴子上踩着粪！”
九月，那浩大切罗基长条地开放的月份，眼看他上了路。西姆嚎着要跟去，好几天劝不转。
萨布拉的送别是存心冷的。她的心，她对自己说，正在碎。这四年在她身上造成的变化，从没有像此刻这样分明。
“我头一回抢地去的时候，你也是这副样子，”扬西提醒她。“记得吗？你那闹腾，只比你母亲低一度。要不是我去了，你这会儿还住在威奇托的宅子里，让你一家人用南方炸鸡和人生教导把你闷死呢。”这话里的实话不少，她不得不认。她软了；偎住他。
“扬西！扬西！”
“笑一笑，甜心。等着瞧吧，五年后的今天，西姆和唐娜骑在克拉瓦特家的地里。”
再说，奥塞奇还有上百号男人要去抢切罗基长条地。全镇——全领地——几个月来说的没别的。
她擦干眼睛。她甚至挤出一个水汪汪的笑。他骑的那匹马叫锡马龙，一匹亮丽的、眼睛野野的母马：血里一股西班牙种的速度和身段，一股美国野马的耐力。

===Original===

He had decided to make the trip from Osage to the Cherokee Outlet on horseback by easy stages so as to keep the animal in condition, though the Santa Fe and the Rock Island roads were to run trains into the Strip. He made a dashing, a magnificent figure as he sat the strong, graceful animal that now was pawing and pirouetting to be off. Though a score of others were starting with him, it was Yancey that the town turned out to see. He rode in his white sombrero, his fine white shirt, his suit with the Prince Albert coat, his glittering high-heeled Texas star boots with the gold-plated spurs. The start was made shortly after sunrise so as to make progress before the heat of the day. But a cavalcade awoke them before dawn with a *rat-a-tat-tat* of six-shooters and a bloodcurdling series of cowboy yips. The escort rode with Yancey and the others for a distance out on the Plains. Sabra, at the last minute, had the family horse hitched to the buggy, bundled Cim and Donna in with her, and﻿—Isaiah hanging on behind, somehow﻿—the prim little vehicle bumped and reeled its way over the prairie road in the wake of the departing adventurers.
At the last Sabra threw the reins to Isaiah, sprang from the buggy, ran to Yancey as he pulled up his horse. He bent far over in his saddle, picked her up in one great arm, held her close while he kissed her long and hard.
“Sabra, come with me. Let’s get clear away from this.”
“You’ve gone crazy! The children!”
“The children, too. All of us. Come on. Now.” His eyes were blazing. She saw that he actually meant it. A sudden premonition shook her.
“Where are you going? Where are you going?”
He set her down gently and was off, turned halfway in his saddle to face her, his white sombrero held aloft in his hand, his curling black locks tossing in the Oklahoma breeze.
Five years passed before she saw him again.

===Chinese===

他决定从奥塞奇骑马、分程缓行到切罗基出口地，好保住马力，虽然圣菲和石岛两条铁路都要放车进长条地。他跨在那匹正刨着蹄、转着圈急着要走的壮健身姿的马背上，是一个招摇的、堂皇的形象。虽然同行的还有二十来人，全镇倾城出来看的，是扬西。他穿白阔边帽、精致的白衬衫、长礼服那一套，蹬着锃亮的高跟得克萨斯星靴，配镀金马刺。为了赶在白天的暑热前赶路，日出不久就动身。可天没亮，一队人马就用六响枪的嗒嗒嗒和一串寒毛直竖的牛仔吼，把他们叫醒了。护送的人陪扬西一行走出一程，上了平原。萨布拉临了把家里那匹马套上轻马车，把西姆和唐娜塞进车里——以赛亚不知怎么吊在车后——这辆规整的小车便一路颠着、晃着，沿草原路追着已去的冒险家们的背影。
到末了，萨布拉把缰绳扔给以赛亚，跳下车，奔到勒住马的扬西跟前。他从鞍上深深俯下，一条大臂把她捞起来，紧紧搂着，久久地、重重地吻她。
“萨布拉，跟我走。咱们远远离开这个。”
“你疯了！孩子们！”
“孩子们也去。咱们全家。来。就现在。”他眼睛烧着。她看出他是当真的。一阵预感攥住她。
“你要去哪儿？你要去哪儿？”
他轻轻把她放下，去了，半个身子拧在鞍上朝着她，白阔边帽高举在手里，黑色鬈发在俄克拉何马的劲风里翻飞。
五年过去，她才又见到他。

## XIX / 十九
===Original===

Cim was nineteen, Donna fifteen. And now Sabra lived quite alone in the new house on Kihekah Street, except for a colored woman servant sent from Kansas. She ran the paper alone, as she wished it run. She ordered the house as she wished it. She very nearly ran the town of Osage. She was a power in the Territory. And Yancey was gone, Cim was gone, Donna was gone. Sabra had refused to compromise with life, and life had take matters out of her hands.
Donna was away at an Eastern finishing school﻿—Miss Dignum’s on the Hudson. Yancey had opposed that, of course. It had been Sabra’s idea to send Donna east to school.
“East?” Yancey had said. “Kansas City?”
“Certainly not.”
“Oh﻿—Chicago.”
“I mean New York.”
“You’re crazy.”
“I didn’t expect you to approve. I suppose you’d like her to go to an Indian school. Donna’s an unusual girl. She’s not a beauty and never will be, but she’s brilliant, that’s what she is. Brilliant. I don’t mean intellectual. You needn’t smile. I mean that she’s got the ambition and the insight and the foresight, too, of a woman of twice her age.”
“I’m sorry to hear that.”
“I’m not. She’s like Mamma in many ways, only she’s got intelligence and drive. She doesn’t get along with the girls here﻿—Maurine Turket and Gazelle Slaughter and Jewel Riggs and Czarina McKee, and those. She’s different. They go switching up and down Pawhuska Avenue. They’ll marry one of these tobacco-chewing loafers and settle down like vegetables. Well, she won’t. I’ll see to that.”
“Going to marry her off to an Eastern potentate﻿—at fifteen?”
“You wait. You’ll see. She knows what she wants. She’ll get it, too.”
“Sure it isn’t you who know what you want her to want?”
But Sabra had sent her off to Miss Dignum’s on a diet of prunes and prisms that even her high-and-mighty old grandmother Felice Venable approved.
Cim, walking the prairies beyond Osage with that peculiar light step of his, his eyes cast down; prowling the draws and sprawling upon the clay banks of the rivers that ran so red through the Red Man’s Territory, said that he wanted to be a geologist. He spoke of the Colorado School of Mines. He worked in the *Wigwam* office and hated it. He could pi a case of type more quickly and completely than a drunken tramp printer. The familiar “shrdlu etaoin” was likely to appear in any column in which he had a hand.

===Chinese===

西姆十九岁，唐娜十五岁。如今萨布拉独自住在基赫卡街（Kihekah Street）的新房子里，身边只有一个从堪萨斯来的黑人女仆。报纸她一个人办，照她的意思办。家务她一个人做主，照她的意思做。奥塞奇镇也差不多是她说了算。她是这片领地里一个有分量的人物。而扬西走了，西姆走了，唐娜也走了。萨布拉不肯跟生活妥协，生活便索性把事情从她手里夺了过去。
唐娜远在东部一所女子精修学校念书——哈得孙河畔的迪格纳姆小姐学校。扬西自然反对这件事。把唐娜送到东部去上学，是萨布拉的主意。
“东部？”扬西当时说。“堪萨斯城？”
“当然不是。”
“哦——芝加哥。”
“我指的是纽约。”
“你疯了。”
“我没指望你赞成。我看你想让她上印第安人学校。唐娜这孩子不寻常。她不是美人，永远也成不了美人，可她聪明——就是这么回事。聪明。我不是说书念得好那种聪明。你不用笑。我是说，她有抱负，有眼光，还有远见，是年纪比她大一倍的女人才有的。”
“听你这么说我真难过。”
“我可不难过。她很多地方像妈妈，只是她有头脑、有闯劲。她跟这儿的姑娘们合不来——莫琳·特基特啦、加泽尔·斯劳特啦、朱厄尔·里格斯啦、查琳娜·麦基啦，那一群。她跟她们不一样。她们成天扭着腰肢在波哈斯卡大街上晃来晃去，将来嫁给哪个嚼烟叶的二流子，然后像棵蔬菜似的扎下根来。哼，她不会。这点我自有办法。”
“打算把她许给东部的什么大人物——她才十五岁？”
“你等着瞧吧。她知道自己要什么。她也拿得着。”
“敢说不是你替她想好了她该要什么？”
可萨布拉还是把她送进了迪格纳姆小姐学校，吃那套规行矩步的严苛管教——连她那位高高在上的老外婆费利斯·维纳布尔（Felice Venable）都点头称是。
西姆呢，常以他那特有的轻步子、垂着眼在奥塞奇镇外的草原上走；在洼地里逡巡，在那些流经红种人领地、红得发亮的河的泥岸上摊手摊脚地躺着。他说他想当个地质学家。他提到过科罗拉多矿业学院。他在《维格沃姆报》馆里做事，恨透了这份差事。他弄乱一整箱活字，比一个喝醉的流浪排字工还快、还彻底。凡是他经手的版面，随时可能冒出排字工都认得的“shrdlu etaoin”那路倒版乱字。

===Original===

Even Jesse Rickey, his mournful mustaches more drooping than ever, protested to Yancey.
“She can’t make a newspaper man out of that kid,” he said. “Not in a million years. Newspaper men are born, not made. Cim, he just naturally hates news, let alone a newspaper office. He was born without a nose for news, like a fellow that’s born without an arm, or something. You can’t grow it if you haven’t got it.”
“I know it,” said Yancey, wearily. “He’ll find a way out.”
For the first time a rival newspaper flourished in the town of Osage. The town was scarcely large enough to support two daily papers, but Yancey’s political attitude so often was at variance with the feeling of the Territory politicians that the new daily, slipshod and dishonest though it was, and owned body and soul by Territorial interests, achieved a degree of popularity.
Sabra, unable to dictate the policy of the *Wigwam* with Yancey at its head, had to content herself with the management of its mechanical workings and with its increasingly important social and club columns. Osage swarmed with meetings, committees, lodges, Knights of This and Sisters of That. The Philomathean and the Twentieth Century clubs began to go in for Civic Betterment, and no Osage merchant or professional man was safe from cajoling and unattractive females in shirtwaists and skirts and eyeglasses demanding his name signed to this or that petition (with a contribution. Whatever you feel that you can give, Mr. Hefner. Of course, as a leading business man﻿ ﻿…).
They planted shrubs about the cinder-strewn environs of the Santa Fe and the Katy depots. They agitated for the immediate paving of Pawhuska Avenue (it wasn’t done). The Ladies of the Eastern Star. The Venus Lodge. Sisters of Rebekah. Daughters of the Southwest. They came into the *Wigwam* office with notices to be printed about lodge suppers and church sociables. Strangely enough, they were likely to stay longer and to chat more freely if Yancey and not Sabra were there to receive them. Sabra was polite but businesslike to her own sex encountered in office hours. But Yancey made himself utterly charming. He could no more help it than he could help breathing. It was almost functional with him. He made the stout, commonplace, middle-aged women feel that they were royal﻿—and seductive.

===Chinese===

连杰西·里基（Jesse Rickey）都跟扬西诉起苦来，他那两撇愁苦的胡子耷拉得比先前更厉害了。
“那孩子当不了报人，”他说。“一百万年也当不了。报人是天生生成的，不是造就出来的。西姆这孩子，天生就讨厌新闻，更别说报馆了。他生下来就没有新闻鼻子，就像有人生下来缺条胳膊什么的。没有的东西，长不出来。”
“我知道，”扬西疲惫地说。“他自己会找出路的。”
奥塞奇镇头一回有了一家兴旺的对头报纸。这么个镇子，养活两份日报本来勉强，可是扬西的政治立场常常跟领地政客们的心思拧着，于是这份新日报——虽说办得潦草、来路不正，又让领地的利益集团买了身家性命——居然颇得了些人心。
扬西当着家，萨布拉左右不了《维格沃姆报》的方针，只好退而经管它的排印事务，兼管那份越来越有分量的社交与社团栏目。奥塞奇镇上到处是开会、委员会、联谊会，什么什么骑士团、什么什么姐妹会。菲洛马西安读书会和二十世纪俱乐部开始搞起“市政改良”来，镇上没有一个商人或专业人士躲得开那些好纠缠、又不讨人喜欢的女人——衬衫式上衣、长裙、架着眼镜，逼着人在这样那样的请愿书上签名（外加捐款。您量力而行，赫夫纳先生。当然啦，您是有头有脸的生意人……）。
她们在圣菲车站和凯蒂车站周围煤渣遍地的近旁种上灌木。她们鼓动立刻给波哈斯卡大街铺路面（没铺成）。东方之星妇女会。维纳斯分会。丽贝卡姐妹会。西南部女儿会。她们拿着要付印的会餐启事、教堂联谊会启事，走进《维格沃姆报》馆。奇怪的是，若接待她们的是扬西而不是萨布拉，她们往往坐得更久、聊得更放。萨布拉对办公时间遇上自己的同性，客气归客气，公事公办。扬西却把自己弄得魅力十足。这跟呼吸一样，由不得他。在他几乎是生理本能。他让那些粗壮、平庸的中年妇女觉得自己高贵无比——而且风情万种。

===Original===

He flattered them with his fine eyes; he bowed them to the door; their eyeglasses quivered. He was likely, on their departure, to crumple their carefully worded notice and throw it on the floor. Sabra, though she made short work of the visiting Venuses and Rebekahs, ran their notice and, if necessary, carefully rewrote it.
“God A’mighty!” he would groan at noonday dinner. “The office was full of Wenuses this morning. Like a swarm of overstuffed locusts.”
Sabra was at the head of many of these Betterment movements. Also if there could be said to be anything so formal as society in Osage, Sabra Cravat was the leader of it. She was the first to electrify the ladies of the Twentieth Century Culture Club by serving them Waldorf salad﻿—that abominable mixture of apple cubes, chopped nuts, whipped cream, and mayonnaise. The club fell upon it with little cries and murmurs. Thereafter it was served at club meetings until Osage husbands, returning home to supper after a day’s work, and being offered this salvage from the feast, would push it aside with masculine contempt for its contents and roar, “I can’t eat this stuff. Fix me some bacon and eggs.”
From this culinary and social triumph Sabra proceeded to pineapple and marshmallow salad, the recipe for which had been sent her by Donna in the East. Its indirect effects were fatal.
When it again became her turn to act as hostess to the members of the club she made her preparations for the afternoon meeting, held at the grisly hour of half-past two. Refreshments were invariably served at four. With all arrangements made, she was confronted by Ruby Big Elk with the astounding statement that this was a great Indian Festival day (September, and the corn dances were on) and that she must go to the Reservation in time for the Mescal Ceremony.
“You can’t go,” said Sabra, flatly. Midday dinner was over. Yancey had returned to the office. Cim was lounging in the hammock on the porch. For answer Ruby turned and walked with her stately, irritating step into her own room just off the kitchen and closed the door.
“Well,” shouted Sabra in the tones of Felice Venable herself, “if you do go you needn’t come back.” She marched out to the front porch, where the sight of the lounging Cim only aggravated her annoyance.
“This ends it. That girl has got to leave.”
“What girl?”
“Ruby.

===Chinese===

他用那双俊眼奉承她们；一路鞠着躬把她们送出门；她们的眼镜直颤。她们一走，他多半会随手把那份字斟句酌的启事揉成一团，扔在地上。萨布拉呢，虽然三言两语打发掉来访的维纳斯们和丽贝卡们，启事却照登不误，必要时还细心改写一遍。
“老天爷！”吃午饭时他会哀叹。“今天上午报馆里全是维纳斯。像一群撑肥了的蝗虫。”
这些改良运动，多半由萨布拉领头。再说，奥塞奇若真称得上有所谓的上流社会，那领头的也是萨布拉·克拉瓦特。她头一个端出华尔道夫沙拉，叫二十世纪文化俱乐部的太太们惊为奇珍——那种苹果丁、碎果仁、打发的奶油和蛋黄酱的可怕混合物。俱乐部的太太们扑上去，啧啧地小声惊叹。从此每次开会必上这道菜，直到奥塞奇的丈夫们劳碌一天回家吃晚饭，被让以这道宴席残羹，都会带着男人对这类东西的轻蔑把它推到一边，吼道：“这玩意儿我没法吃。给我煎点咸肉鸡蛋。”
这一烹调与社交上的胜利之后，萨布拉进而做起了菠萝棉花糖沙拉，方子是唐娜从东部寄来的。它的间接后果是致命的。
轮到她再次做东招待俱乐部会员的时候，她为下午的聚会做好了一切准备——聚会在可怕的两点半举行。茶点照例四点上。万事俱备，鲁比·大鹿（Ruby Big Elk）却迎面给了她一个惊人的声明：今天是印第安人的大节日（九月了，玉米舞正跳着），她必须赶去保留地参加麦斯卡尔仪式。
“你不能去。”萨布拉斩钉截铁地说。午饭已过。扬西回报馆去了。西姆在门廊的吊床上晃悠。鲁比只回了她一个转身，迈着那庄重而气人的步子走进紧挨厨房的自己的房间，关上了门。
“好啊，”萨布拉用十足费利斯·维纳布尔的腔调嚷道，“你要去就别回来了。”她大步走到前廊，一眼看见那个晃晃悠悠的西姆，火气更大了。
“到此为止了。那丫头非走不可。”
“哪个丫头？”
“鲁比。”

===Original===

Twenty women this afternoon, and she says she’s going to the Reservation. They’ll be here at half-past two.” It was rather incoherent, but Cim, surprisingly enough, seemed to understand.
“But she told you a month ago.”
“Told me what? How do you know?”
“Because she told me she told you, ever so long ago.”
“Maybe she did. She never mentioned it again. I can’t be expected to remember every time the Indians have one of their powwows. I told her she couldn’t go. She’s in there getting ready. Well, this ends it. She needn’t come back.”
She flounced into the kitchen. There stood a mild-mannered young Indian girl unknown to her.
“What do you want?”
“I am here,” the girl answered, composedly, “to take Ruby Big Elk’s place this afternoon. I am Cherokee. She told me to come.” She plucked Ruby’s blue and white checked gingham apron off the hook behind the door and tied it around her waist.
“Well!” gasped Sabra, relieved, but still angry. Through the kitchen window she saw Cim hitching up the two pintos to the racy little yellow phaeton that Yancey had bought. She must run out and tell him before he left. He had seemed disturbed. She was glad he was clearing out. She liked having the men folks out of the way when afternoon company was due.
Ruby’s door opened. The girl came out. Her appearance was amazing. She wore a dress of white doeskin hanging straight from shoulders to ankles, and as soft and pliable as velvet. The hem was fringed. Front, sleeves, collar were finely beaded in an intricate pattern that was more like embroidery than beading. On her feet were moccasins in ivory white and as exquisitely beaded as the dress. It was the robe of a princess. Her dark Indian eyes were alive. Her skin seemed to glow in contrast with the garment. The girl was, for the moment, almost beautiful.
“Hello, Theresa Jump.﻿ ﻿… This is Theresa Jump. She will do my work this day. I have told her. She knows about the pineapple and marshmallow salad.” For a moment it seemed to Sabra that just the faintest shadow of amusement flitted over Ruby’s face as she said this. But then, Sabra never had pretended to understand these Indians. “I will be back tomorrow morning.”
She walked slowly out of the house by way of the kitchen door, across the yard with her slow insolent dragging step. A stab of suspicion cut Sabra. She flew to the back porch, stood there a moment.

===Chinese===

“今天下午二十个客人，她倒说她要去保留地。她们两点半就到。”话没头没脑，可西姆居然像是听懂了。
“可她一个月前就跟你说了。”
“跟我说什么？你怎么知道？”
“因为她跟我说过她早就跟你说过，好久了。”
“也许说过吧。她再没提过。印第安人每回开他们的帕瓦，总不能指望我回回都记住。我告诉她不能去。她正在屋里收拾呢。好，到此为止。她不用回来了。”
她气冲冲地闯进厨房。那儿站着一个陌生的、举止文静的印第安年轻姑娘。
“你要什么？”
“我是来，”姑娘从容不迫地答道，“今天下午替鲁比·大鹿的班的。我是切罗基人。是她叫我来的。”她从门后的挂钩上取下鲁比那件蓝白格布围裙，系在腰上。
“哎呀！”萨布拉舒了口气，可气还没消。透过厨房的窗子，她看见西姆正把两匹花斑马套上扬西买的那辆时髦的黄色小敞篷快马车。得赶在他走之前跑出去告诉他。他方才像是心绪不宁。她巴不得他走开。下午有客的时候，她喜欢把家里的男人们打发得远远的。
鲁比的房门开了。姑娘走了出来。她的模样叫人吃惊。她穿一件白色鹿皮裙，从肩直垂到脚踝，柔软得像丝绒。裙摆缀着流苏。前襟、袖子、领口都细密地缀着珠子，纹样繁复，与其说是缀珠，不如说是刺绣。脚上一双象牙白的鹿皮鞋，缀珠精巧得同那裙子一样。这是一身公主的衣裳。她那双印第安人的黑眼睛亮了起来。衬着这身衣裳，她的皮肤仿佛在发光。这姑娘此刻，几乎是个美人。
“你好，特里萨·江普。……这是特里萨·江普（Theresa Jump）。今天的活她替我干。我都交代过了。菠萝棉花糖沙拉她也知道怎么做。”说这话时，萨布拉仿佛看见鲁比脸上掠过一丝极淡的笑意。不过话说回来，这些印第安人，萨布拉本来就从不假装看得懂。“我明早回来。”
她慢吞吞地穿过厨房门走出屋子，迈着那迟缓、傲慢、拖沓的步子穿过院子。一阵疑心的刺痛扎了萨布拉一下。她飞奔到后廊，在那里站了片刻。

===Original===

Ruby Big Elk walked slowly toward the barn. Cim drove out with the phaeton and pintos. He saw the Indian woman in her white doeskin dress. His eyes shone enormous. He lifted his head as though to breathe deeply. At that look in his face Sabra ran across the yard. One hand was at her breast, as though an Indian arrow had pierced her. Ruby had set one foot in its cream white moccasin on the buggy step. Cim held out his free hand.
Sabra reached them, panting. “Where are you going?”
“I’m driving Ruby out to the Reservation.”
“No, you’re not. No, you’re not.” She put one hand in a futile gesture on the buggy wheel, as though to stop them by main force. She knew she must not lose her dignity before this Indian woman﻿—before her son. Yet this thing was, to her way of thinking, monstrous.
Cim gathered up the reins, his eyes on the restive ponies. “I may stay to see some of the dancing and the Mescal Ceremony. Father says it’s very interesting. Big Elk has invited me.”
“Your father knows you’re going? Like this?”
“Oh, yes.” He cast a slight, an oblique glance at her hand on the wheel. Her hand dropped heavily to her side. He spoke to the horses. They were off. Ruby Big Elk looked straight ahead. She had uttered no word. Sabra turned and walked back to the house. The hot tears blinded her. She was choking. But her pride spoke, even then. You must not go the kitchen way. That Indian girl will see you. They are all alike. You must go around by the front way. Pretend it is nothing. Oh, God, what shall I do! All those women this afternoon. Perhaps I am making a fuss over nothing. Why shouldn’t he take the Indian girl out to the Reservation and stop an hour or two to see the dances and the rites?﻿ ﻿… His face! His face when he saw her in that dress.
She bathed her eyes, powdered her nose, changed her dress, came into the kitchen, smiling. “… the pineapple cut into chunks about like this. Then you snip the marshmallow into it with the scissors. Mix whipped cream into your mayonnaise﻿ ﻿… a cherry on top﻿ ﻿… little thin sandwiches﻿ ﻿… damp napkin﻿ ﻿…” She went into the sitting room, adjusted a shade, plumped a pillow. The door bell rang. “Howdy-do, Mrs. Nisbett.﻿ ﻿… No, you’re not. You’re just on time. It’s everybody else who’s late.” She thought, “Women are wonderful. No man could do what I am doing. Smiling and chatting when I am almost crazy.”

===Chinese===

鲁比·大鹿朝牲口棚慢慢走去。西姆驾着那辆敞篷快马车、带着两匹花斑马驶出来。他看见了那穿白鹿皮裙的印第安女人。他的眼睛霎时亮得吓人。他仰起头，仿佛要深深吸一口气。一看见他脸上的那种神情，萨布拉就跑着穿过了院子。一只手按在胸前，仿佛一支印第安箭射穿了她。鲁比一只穿象牙白鹿皮鞋的脚已踏上马车踏板。西姆腾出一只手伸了过去。
萨布拉喘着气赶到跟前。“你们去哪儿？”
“我送鲁比去保留地。”
“不行，你不能去。你不能去。”她一只手虚虚地按在车轮上，那手势毫无用处，仿佛想凭蛮力拦住他们。她知道，在这个印第安女人面前——在自己儿子面前——绝不能失了体面。可是在她看来，这件事是骇人听闻的。
西姆收拢缰绳，眼睛盯着躁动的两匹小马。“我可能留下看看跳舞和麦斯卡尔仪式。爸爸说很有意思。大鹿（Big Elk）请了我。”
“你爸爸知道你去？就这么去？”
“哦，知道。”他斜斜地、淡淡地瞥了一眼她按在车轮上的那只手。那只手沉沉地垂回身侧。他吆喝一声马。车走了。鲁比·大鹿直视着前方。她一个字也没说。萨布拉转身走回屋去。热泪糊住了她的眼。她喘不上气来。可即便那时，她的自尊还在说话。不能走厨房那边。那个印第安丫头会看见的。她们全是一路货。得绕前面走。装作什么事都没有。哦，上帝，我可怎么办！今天下午还有那么多女人。也许我是小题大做了。他送那印第安姑娘去保留地，待一两个钟头看看舞蹈和仪式，又有什么不行？……他的脸！他看见她穿那身衣裳时的那张脸！
她洗了眼睛，扑了鼻粉，换了衣裳，笑着走进厨房。“……菠萝切成差不多这么大的块儿。再用剪子把棉花糖剪进去。蛋黄酱里拌上打发的奶油……顶上搁一颗樱桃……小小的薄片三明治……餐巾要垫湿的……”她走进客厅，正正灯罩，拍松靠垫。门铃响了。“你好啊，尼斯比特太太。……哪儿的话，你没迟到。你来得不早不晚，是别人都迟到了。”她想：“女人真了不起。我做的这些事，没有哪个男人做得来。人都快疯了，还能笑着聊天。”

===Original===

Her fine dark eyes were luminous. Her clear ivory skin was tinged now with a spot of red on either cheek. She looked very handsome.
Theresa Jump proved clumsy and unteachable. Sabra herself mixed and served the pineapple and marshmallow salad, and though this novelty proved a great success, the triumph of serving it was spoiled for Sabra. She bundled the girl off at six, after the dishes were done. Wearily she began to set the house to rights, but Yancey came home to a confusion of chairs and squashed pillows, a mingled odor of perfumery and coffee; a litter of cake crumbs, bits of embroidery silk, and crumpled tea napkins. His huge frame moving about the cluttered sitting room made these feminine remnants seem ridiculous. The disorder of the household irked him. Worst of all, Sabra, relieved now of her guests, was free to pour out upon him all the pent-up wrath, anxiety, and shock of the past few hours. Ruby. Cim. Theresa Jump. Peyote. Osages. If his own father allows such things﻿—what will people say﻿—no use trying to make something of yourself.
Yancey, usually so glib with quotations from this or that sonorous passage of poetry, said little. He did not even try to cajole her into a better humor with his flattery, his charm, his tenderness. His eyes were bloodshot, his hand more unsteady than usual. He had been drinking even more than was his wont, she knew that at once. By no means drunk (she had never seen him really drunk﻿—no one had﻿—he was seemingly incapable of reaching a visible state of drunkenness), he was in one of his fits of moody depression. The great shoulders sagged. The splendid head lolled on his breast. He seemed sunk in gloomy thought. She felt that he hardly heard what she was saying. She herself could eat nothing. She set a place for him at the dining-room table and plumped down before him a dish of the absurd salad, a cup of coffee, some cake, a plate of the leftover sandwiches, their edges curled dismally.
“What’s this?” he said.
“Pineapple and marshmallow salad. With Ruby gone and all, I didn’t get anything for your supper﻿—I was so upset﻿—all those women﻿ ﻿…”
He sat looking down at the slippery mass on his plate. His great arms were spread out on the table before him. The beautiful hands were opening and closing convulsively. So a mastodon might have looked at a worm.

===Chinese===

她那双漂亮的黑眼睛莹莹发亮。光洁的象牙色皮肤上，此刻两颊各透出一块红晕。她看起来非常动人。
特里萨·江普又笨手笨脚，又教不会。菠萝棉花糖沙拉只得萨布拉自己拌、自己端，这道新鲜菜虽然大获成功，出风头的乐趣对萨布拉却全毁了。六点钟，碗碟收拾停当，她便打发那姑娘走了。她疲惫地开始收拾屋子，而扬西回到的，是一屋子东倒西歪的椅子和压塌的靠垫，一股香水和咖啡混合的气味；满地蛋糕渣、绣花丝线头和揉皱的茶点餐巾。他高大的身躯在凌乱的客厅里走动，把这些女人的残余衬得愈发可笑。家里的乱糟糟使他烦躁。最糟的是，萨布拉送走了客人，正可以把她几个钟头里积压的怒火、焦虑和惊骇，一股脑儿倒在他身上。鲁比。西姆。特里萨·江普。佩奥特。奥塞奇人。连他亲爹都容许这种事——人家会怎么说——再想出人头地也是白搭。
扬西素来惯于随口引用这段那段铿锵的诗句，此刻却很少说话。他甚至没有试图用奉承、魅力和温存哄她消气。他两眼通红，手比平常抖得更厉害。他喝得比平日更多，这一点她一眼就看出来了。绝没有醉（她从没见他真醉过——谁也没见过——他像是根本到不了让人看得出来的醉态），他正陷在他那阵阴郁的消沉里。宽阔的肩膀塌着。那颗气派的脑袋垂在胸前。他像是沉在阴郁的思绪里。她觉得他几乎没听她在说什么。她自己什么也吃不下。她在餐桌上给他摆好位置，往他面前咚地放下一盘那种荒唐的沙拉、一杯咖啡、几块蛋糕、一碟剩下的三明治，边角凄凄地卷着。
“这是什么？”他说。
“菠萝棉花糖沙拉。鲁比一走，乱成一团，你的晚饭我没来得及弄——我心里乱得很——那么些个女人……”
他坐着，低头看盘子里那摊滑腻腻的东西。两条大臂摊开在面前的桌上。那双漂亮的手痉挛着一开一合。一头乳齿象看着一条虫子，大概也就是这样。

===Original===

“Pineapple and marshmallow salad,” he repeated, thoughtfully, almost wonderingly. Suddenly he threw back the magnificent head and began to laugh. Peal after peal of Herculean laughter. “Pineapple and marsh﻿—” choking, the tears running down his cheeks. Sabra was angry, then frightened. For as suddenly as he had begun to laugh he became serious. He stood up, one hand on the table. Then he seemed to pull his whole body together like a tiger who is about to spring. He stood thus a moment, swaying a little. “ ‘*Actum est de republica.*’ ”
“What?” said Sabra, sharply.
“Latin, Latin, my love. Pineapple and marshmallow salad! ‘It is all over with the Republic.’ ” She shrugged her shoulders impatiently. Yancey turned, stiffly, like a soldier, walked out of the room, flicked his white sombrero off the hall rack and put it on at the usual jaunty angle, went down the porch stair with his light, graceful step, to the sidewalk and up the street, the great head lowered, the arms swinging despondently at his sides.
Sabra went on with her work of tidying up the house. Her eyes burned, her throat was constricted. Men! Men! Cim off with that squaw. Yancey angry because she had given him this very feminine dish of leftovers. What was the use of working, what was the use of pride, what was the use of ambition for your children, your home, your town if this was all it amounted to? Her work done, she allowed herself the luxury of a deliberate and cleansing storm of tears.
Eight o’clock. She heated some of the afternoon coffee and drank it sitting at the kitchen table. She went out on the front porch. Darkness had come on. A hot September evening. The crickets squeaked and ground away in the weeds. She was conscious of an aching weariness in all her body, but she could not sleep. Her eyes felt as though they were being pulled apart by invisible fingers. She put her palms over them, to shut them, to cool them. Nine. Ten. Eleven. Twelve. She undressed, unpinned the braids of her thick hair, brushed it, plaited it for the night. All the time she was listening. Listening. One.
Suddenly she began to dress again with icy fumbling fingers. She did up her hair, put on her hat and a jacket. She closed the door behind her, locked it, slipped the key into the mail box. The *Wigwam* office. Yancey was not there. The office was dark.

===Chinese===

“菠萝棉花糖沙拉，”他若有所思地、几乎带着惊奇地重复了一遍。突然，那颗气派的头向后一仰，他大笑起来。一阵接一阵大力神般的狂笑。“菠萝和棉——”笑得直呛，眼泪顺着腮帮子往下淌。萨布拉先是生气，继而害怕。因为他笑得突然，变得也突然——他一下严肃起来。他站起身，一只手撑在桌上。随即他仿佛把整个身子收紧，像一头就要扑出去的老虎。他那样立了片刻，微微摇晃着。“‘共和国这下完了。’”
“什么？”萨布拉厉声问。
“拉丁文，拉丁文，我的爱人。菠萝棉花糖沙拉！‘共和国这回是全完了。’”她不耐烦地耸耸肩。扬西僵直地转个身，像个士兵，走出房间，从门厅衣帽架上拂下他那顶白阔边帽，照老样子歪歪地戴上，迈着轻快优雅的步子下了门廊台阶，走上人行道，沿街去了，那颗大脑袋低着，两条手臂垂在身侧颓然摆动。
萨布拉接着收拾屋子。她两眼发烧，喉咙发紧。男人！男人！西姆跟那个印第安婆娘跑了。扬西呢，为了一盘女人气的残羹剩菜就动了气。要是到头来不过如此，那么操劳有什么用，体面有什么用，为儿女、为家、为镇子抱的那些指望又有什么用？活干完了，她放任自己奢侈地、痛痛快快地哭了一场，哭得畅快淋漓。
八点钟。她把下午剩下的咖啡热了热，坐在厨房桌边喝了。她走到前廊。天黑下来了。一个燠热的九月黄昏。蟋蟀在草棵子里吱吱磨着。她觉出浑身一种酸痛的疲乏，却睡不着。两眼像是被看不见的手指往外掰着。她把手掌捂上去，想合上它们，凉凉它们。九点。十点。十一点。十二点。她脱了衣裳，解开浓发的发辫，梳过，编成睡辫。这工夫她一直在听。听着。一点了。
突然，她用冰凉的、不听使唤的手指重新穿起衣裳。挽好头发，戴上帽子，披了件短外套。她随手带上门，锁好，把钥匙塞进信箱。《维格沃姆报》馆。扬西不在。报馆黑着。

===Original===

She shook the door, rattled the knob, peered in, unlocked it with the key in her handbag. Her heart was pounding, but she was not afraid of the darkness. A cat’s eyes gleamed at her from the printing shop. She struck a light. No one. No one. The linotype machine grinned at her with its white teeth. Its iron arm and hand shook tauntingly at her in the wavering light. With a sudden premonition she ran to Yancey’s desk, opened the drawer in which he kept his holster and six-shooters, now that Osage had become so effete as to make them an unessential article of dress. They were not there. She knew then that Yancey had gone.
Doc Valliant. She closed and locked the door after her, stepped out into the quiet blackness of Pawhuska Avenue. Doc Valliant. He would go with her. He would drive her out there. But his office and the room at the rear, which was his dwelling, gave forth no response. Gone out somewhere﻿—a case. Down the rickety wooden steps of the two-story brick building. She stood a moment in the street, looking this way and that. She struck her palms together in a kind of agony of futility. She would go alone if she had a horse and buggy. She could rent one at the livery stable. But what would they think﻿—those men at the livery stable? They were the gossips of the town. It would be all over Osage, all over the county. Sabra Cravat driving out into the prairie alone in the middle of the night. Something up. Well, she couldn’t help that. She had to go. She had to get him.
Toward the livery stable, past the Bixby House. A quiet little figure rose from the blackness of the porch where all through the day the traveling men and loafers sat with their chairs tilted back against the wall. The red coal of his cigar was an eye in the darkness.
“Sabra! What is this! What are you doing running around at this hour of the night?”
Sol Levy, sitting there in the Oklahoma night, a lonely little figure, sleepless, brooding. He had never before called her Sabra.
“Sol! Sol! Cim’s out at the Reservation. Something’s happened. I know. I feel it.”
He did not scoff at this, as most men would. He seemed to understand her fear, her premonition, and to accept it with Oriental fatalism.
“What do you want to do?”
“Take me out there. Hitch up and drive me out there. Cim’s got the buggy. He went out with her.”
He did not ask where Yancey was. He asked nothing. “Go home,” he said.

===Chinese===

她摇门，咔啦咔啦扭门把手，往里张望，用手袋里的钥匙开了锁。她心口怦怦直跳，却不怕黑。印刷间里有猫眼睛朝她幽幽发亮。她划亮一根火柴。没有人。没有人。莱诺整行铸排机冲她龇着白牙。铁臂铁手在摇曳的光里朝她挑衅地晃。一阵不祥的预感，她奔到扬西的桌前，拉开那个抽屉——奥塞奇如今娇气得把枪套和六响枪当作不必要的行头了，他就把家伙收在那儿。枪不在。她于是明白，扬西走了。
瓦利安特大夫。她回身锁好门，走进波哈斯卡大街安静的黑暗里。瓦利安特大夫。他会陪她去。他会赶车送她去。可他的诊所以及后面他住的那间屋子，都没有动静。出门了——出诊去了。走下那座两层砖楼摇摇欲坠的木台阶。她在街上站了片刻，东张西望。她痛苦而又无措地合掌拍了一下。要是有一匹马一辆车，她就自己去。车马行里能租。可是那些人会怎么想——车马行里那些人？他们全是镇上的长舌。到明天就会传遍奥塞奇，传遍全县。萨布拉·克拉瓦特半夜三更一个人赶车出了草原。出事了。唉，管不了这些了。她必须去。她必须把他找回来。
朝车马行去，路过比克斯比旅馆。门廊的黑影里立起一个安静的小个子——白天那门廊上，跑买卖的和闲汉们总把椅子仰靠着墙坐在那儿。他雪茄上那点红火炭，是黑暗中的一只眼睛。
“萨布拉！这是怎么了！深更半夜的你跑出来做什么？”
索尔·利维，坐在俄克拉何马的夜色里，一个孤单的小个子，无眠，出神。他从前从没叫过她萨布拉。
“索尔！索尔！西姆在保留地上。出事了。我知道。我感觉到了。”
换作大多数男人都会笑话这话，他没有。他像是懂得她的恐惧、她的预感，并以一种东方人的宿命论接受了它。
“你想怎么办？”
“带我出去。套上车，赶车送我去。家里那辆车西姆赶走了。他跟她一起去的。”
他没有问扬西在哪儿。他什么也没问。“回家去，”他说。

===Original===

“Wait on your porch. I will get my rig and come for you. They shouldn’t see you. Do you want me to go home with you first?”
“No, no. I’m not afraid. I’m not afraid of anything.”
Sol Levy had two very fine horses; really good animals. They won the races regularly at the local fairs. The little light rig with its smart rubber tires whirled behind them over the red dusty Oklahoma prairie roads. His slim hands were not expert with horses. He was a nervous, jerky driver. They left the town behind them, were swallowed up by the prairie. The Reservation was a full two hours distant. Sabra took off her hat. The night air rushed against her face, cooling it. A half hour.
“Let me drive, will you, Sol?”
Without a word he entrusted the reins to her strong, accustomed hands; the hands of one who had come of generations of horse lovers. The animals sensed the change. They leaped ahead in the darkness. The light buggy rocked and bounced over the rutted roads. Sol asked her nothing. They drove in silence. Presently she began to talk, disjointedly. Yet, surprisingly enough, he seemed intuitively to understand﻿—to fill in the gaps with his own instinct and imagination. What she said sounded absurd; he knew it for tragedy.
“… pineapple and marshmallow salad﻿ ﻿… hates that kind of thing﻿ ﻿… queer for a long time﻿ ﻿… moody﻿ ﻿… drinking﻿ ﻿… Ruby Big Elk﻿ ﻿… Cim﻿ ﻿… his face﻿ ﻿… peyote﻿ ﻿… Mescal Ceremony﻿ ﻿… Osage﻿ ﻿… white doeskin dress﻿ ﻿… Theresa Jump﻿ ﻿…”
“I see,” said Sol Levy, soothingly. “Sure. Well, sure. The boy will be all right. The boy will be all right. Well, Yancey﻿—you know how he is﻿—Yancey. Do you think he has gone away again? I mean﻿—gone?”
“I don’t know.” Then, “Yes.”
Three o’clock and after. They came in sight of the Osage Reservation, a scattered settlement of sterile farms and wooden shanties sprawled on the bare unlovely prairie.
Darkness. The utter darkness that precedes the dawn. Stillness, except for the thud of their horses’ flying hoofs and the whir and bump of the buggy wheels. Then, as Sabra slowed them down, uncertainly, undecided as to what they might best do, they heard it﻿—the weird wavering cadences of the Mescal song, the hail-like clatter of the gourd rattle shaken vigorously and monotonously; and beneath and above and around it all, reverberating, haunting, ominous, the beat of the buckskin drum.

===Chinese===

“在你家廊子上等着。我去套车来接你。别让人看见你。要不要我先陪你回家？”
“不，不用。我不怕。我什么都不怕。”
索尔·利维有两匹极好的马，真正的好牲口，本地集会上的赛马照例是它们赢。那辆小巧轻便、橡胶轮胎崭新漂亮的马车跟在它们后面，在俄克拉何马红尘滚滚的草原路上飞驰。他那双瘦手使不来马。他赶起车来又紧张又生硬地一抽一顿。他们把镇子抛在身后，被草原吞没。保留地足有两个钟头的路。萨布拉摘下帽子。夜风扑在脸上，把它吹凉。半个钟头。
“让我来赶，行吗，索尔？”
他一言不发，把缰绳交到她那双有力而熟练的手里；那是爱马人家世世代代传下来的手。牲口觉出了换人。它们在黑暗里撒开脚步。轻车在辙痕纵横的路上颠得直摇晃。索尔什么也没问她。他们默默赶路。过了一会儿她开口说话，语无伦次。然而——真叫人意外——他仿佛凭直觉就懂了——用他自己的本能和想象把缺口补上。她说出来的话听着荒唐；他听出那是悲剧。
“……菠萝棉花糖沙拉……他讨厌那种东西……好一阵子不对劲……闷闷不乐……喝酒……鲁比·大鹿……西姆……他的脸色……佩奥特……麦斯卡尔仪式……奥塞奇……白鹿皮裙……特蕾莎·贾姆普……”
“我明白，”索尔·利维宽慰道，“是了。嗯，是了。孩子会没事的。孩子会没事的。至于扬西——你是知道他的——扬西。你觉得他又走了吗？我是说——走了？”
“我不知道。”顿了顿，“是。”
三点钟过了。他们望见了奥塞奇保留地——贫瘠的田庄和木棚屋零零落落，摊在光秃秃、不见姿色的草原上。
黑暗。黎明前那种彻头彻尾的黑暗。寂静，只有他们坐骑飞驰的蹄声和车轮的呼呼与磕碰。接着，萨布拉迟疑地把马放慢，一时还拿不定主意怎样才好，他们听见了——麦斯卡尔歌谣那诡谲摇曳的调子，葫芦响器被用力而单调地摇出的冰雹般的嗒嗒声；而在这一切的底下、上头、四周，回荡着、纠缠着、不祥地响着的，是鹿皮鼓的鼓点。

===Original===

Through the still, cool night air of the prairie it came to them﻿—to the overwrought woman, and to the little peaceful Jew. Barbaric sounds, wild, sinister. She pulled up the horses. They sat a moment, listening. Listening. The drum. The savage sound of the drum.
Fear was gnawing at her vitals, wringing her very heart with clammy fingers, yet Sabra spoke matter-of-factly, her voice holding a hard little note because she was trying to keep it from quavering.
“He’ll be in the Mescal teepee next to Big Elk’s House. They built it there when he was Chief, and they still use it regularly for the ceremony. Yancey showed it to me once, when he drove me out here.” She stopped and cleared her throat, for her voice was suddenly husky. She wondered, confusedly, if that sound was the drum or her own heart beating. She gave a little cracked laugh that bordered on hysteria. “A drum in the night. It sounds so terrible. So savage.”
Sol Levy took the reins from her shaking fingers. “Nothing to be frightened about. A lot of poor ignorant Indians trying to forget their misery. Come.” Perhaps no man ever made a more courageous gesture, for the little sensitive Jew was terribly frightened.
Uncertainly, in the blackness, they made their way toward the drum beat. Nearer and nearer, louder and louder. And yet all about, darkness, silence. Only that pulsing cry and rattle and beat pounding through the night like the tide. What if he is not there? thought Sabra.
Sol Levy pulled up in the roadway before the trampled yard that held the Mescal teepee, round, to typify the sun, built of wood, larger than any other building on the Reservation. The horses were frightened, restive. All about in the blackness you heard the stamp of other horses’ hoofs, heard them crunching the dried herbage of the autumn prairie. With difficulty he groped his way to a stump that served as hitching post, tied the horses. As he helped Sabra down her knees suddenly bent, and he caught her as she sank. “Oh! It’s all right. Stiff, I guess﻿—from the ride.” She leaned against him a moment, then straightened determinedly. He took her arm firmly. Together they made their way toward the tent-shaped wooden teepee.
Two great, silent blanketed figures at the door through which the fitful flame of the sacred fire flared. The figures did not speak. They stood there, barring the way.

===Chinese===

声音穿过草原静止而清凉的夜气传到他们耳朵里——传到这个神经绷到极处的女人耳朵里，和这个小个子的、爱好和平的犹太人耳朵里。野蛮的声音，狂野，凶险。她勒住马。他们坐了片刻，听着。听着。鼓。那鼓的野蛮的声音。
恐惧啃噬着她的五脏，用黏湿的手指绞着她的心，可萨布拉说话还是就事论事的腔调，嗓音里带一点发硬的尖音，因为她正竭力不让它发颤。
“他会在麦斯卡尔帐篷里，挨着大鹿家。他做酋长那阵子他们把帐篷建在那儿，如今办仪式照旧用它。扬西带我出来时指给我看过一次。”她停下来清了清嗓子，嗓子突然哑了。她恍恍惚惚地想，那声音是鼓呢，还是她自己的心跳。她发出一声近乎歇斯底里的干笑。“夜里的鼓。听着那么可怕。那么野蛮。”
索尔·利维从她发抖的指间接过缰绳。“没什么可怕的。不过是一群穷苦无知的印第安人想法子忘了他们的苦楚。走吧。”恐怕再没有哪个男人做出过比这更勇敢的姿态了，因为这个敏感的小个子犹太人怕得厉害。
他们深一脚浅一脚，在黑暗里朝鼓声走去。越来越近，越来越响。而四周仍旧是黑暗，寂静。只有那哭喊、响器和鼓点像潮水一样搏动着一记记砸透夜。要是他不在呢？萨布拉想。
索尔·利维把车停在路上，停在一片被踩得精光的地场前——麦斯卡尔帐篷就在那里，圆形，象征太阳，木造，比保留地上任何房子都大。马受了惊，躁动不安。黑暗里四下听得见别的马的蹄声，听得见它们咀嚼秋草原枯草的声音。他费劲地摸到一根当拴马桩用的树桩前，把马拴上。他扶萨布拉下车时，她的膝盖忽然一软，他趁她瘫倒前一把捞住。“哦！没事。僵了吧——坐车坐的。”她靠了他一会儿，随即下定决心似的挺直身子。他紧紧挽住她的胳膊。两人一同朝那座像帐篷一样的木头建筑走去。
门口立着两条裹毯子的高大身影，一动不动，圣火忽明忽暗的火光从门里闪出来。那两条身影不说话。就那么站着，拦住去路。

===Original===

The little Jew felt Sabra’s arm trembling in his hand. He peered up into the faces of the silent, immobile figures.
Suddenly, “Hello, Joe!” He turned to Sabra. “It’s Joe Yellow Eyes. He was in the store only yesterday. Say, Joe, the lady here﻿—Mrs. Cravat﻿—she wants her son should come out and go home.”
The blanketed figures stood silent.
Suddenly Sabra thought, “This is ridiculous.”
She loosed her arm. She took a step forward, her profile sharp and clear in the firelight. “I am the woman of Yancey Cravat, the one you call Buffalo Head. If my son is in there I want to take him home now. It is time.”
“Sure take um home,” replied the blanket that Sol had addressed as Joe Yellow Eyes. He stood aside. Blinking, stumbling a little, Sol and Sabra entered the crowded Mescal teepee.
The ceremony was almost at an end. With daybreak it would be finished. Blinded by the light, Sabra at first could discern nothing except the central fire and the figure crouched before it. Yet her eyes went this way and that, searching for him. Gradually her vision cleared. The figures within the teepee paid no attention to those two white intruders. They stood there in the doorway, bewildered, terrified; brave.
In the center a crescent of earth about six inches high curved around a fire built of sticks so arranged that as the ashes fell they formed a second crescent within the other. A man squatted, tending this fire, watchfully, absorbedly. In the center of the crescent, upon a little star of sage twigs, lay the mescal, symbol of the rite. Facing them was the Chief, old Stump Horn, in the place of honor, the emblems of office in his hands﻿—the rattle, the wand, the fan of eagle plumes. All about the teepee crouched or lay blanketed motionless figures. Some sat with heads bowed, other gazed fixedly upon the central mescal button. All had been eating the mescal or drinking a brew in which it had steeped. Now and then a figure would slowly draw the blanket over his head and sink back to receive the vision. And the song went on, the shaking of the gourd rattle, the beat-beat of the buckskin drum. The air of the room was stifling, the room itself scrupulously clean.
At intervals around the wall, and almost level with the dirt floor, were apertures perhaps sixteen inches square. A little wooden door was shut upon most of these.

===Chinese===

小个子犹太人觉出萨布拉的胳膊在他手里发抖。他仰起脸，去看那两张沉默不动的脸。
突然，“你好啊，乔！”他转向萨布拉。“这是乔·黄眼睛。昨天还来过铺子里。喂，乔，这位太太——克拉瓦特太太——要她儿子出来，跟他回家。”
裹毯子的身影默默站着。
萨布拉突然想：“这多可笑。”
她抽出胳膊。向前一步，侧影在火光里分明而锐利。“我是扬西·克拉瓦特的女人，就是你们叫野牛头的那个人。要是我儿子在里面，我现在就要带他回家。到时候了。”
“带走他好啦，”索尔称作乔·黄眼睛的那条毯子答道。他闪到一边。索尔和萨布拉眨着眼、微微踉跄地走进挤满了人的麦斯卡尔帐篷。
仪式已近尾声。天一亮便要结束。火光刺得萨布拉一时什么也辨不出，只看见当中那堆火和火前蹲着的人影。她的眼睛却四下里转，找他。渐渐地她看清了。帐篷里的人影对这两个白人闯入者毫不理会。他们站在门口，慌乱，恐惧；而又勇敢。
当中，一道约六英寸高的新月形土埂环着柴火，柴架得讲究，灰烬落下时便在月牙里再围出一道月牙。一个男人蹲着看火，警觉，入神。月牙正中，一小丛鼠尾草细枝摆成的星形上，供着麦斯卡尔——这仪式的圣物。正对着他们的是酋长，年迈的断角，坐在尊位上，掌中握着权力的象征——响器、权杖、鹰羽扇。帐篷四下或蹲或卧着一动不动的人影。有的低头坐着，有的死死盯着当中那枚麦斯卡尔纽扣。人人都嚼过了麦斯卡尔，或是喝了它泡的汁水。不时有人影缓缓把毯子蒙上头，向后一仰，去迎接幻象。歌声继续着，葫芦响器继续摇着，鹿皮鼓一声一声。屋里空气窒闷，屋子本身却干净得一丝不苟。
四壁间隔着开了一些约十六英寸见方的洞口，几乎与泥地面齐平。多数洞口掩着小木门。

===Original===

Near each lay figures limper, more spent even than the other inert bodies. As Sabra and Sol stood, blinking, they learned the use of these openings. For suddenly nausea overcame one of the Indians crouched in the semicircle near the flame. The man crawled swiftly to one of the little doors, opened it, thrust head and shoulders out into the night air, relieved his body of the drug’s overdose.
Sabra only turned her eyes away, searching, searching. Then she saw where the boy lay under his gay striped blanket. His face was covered, but she knew. She knew well how the slim body curled in its blankets, how it lay at night, asleep. This was a different sleep, but she knew. They went to him, picking their way over the crouching figures with the fixed trancelike gaze; the recumbent forms that lay so still. She turned back the blanket. His face was smiling, peaceful, lovely.
She thought, “This is the way I should look at him if he were dead.” Then, “He is dead.” The boy lay breathing quietly. All about the room was an atmosphere of reverie, of swooning bliss. If the Indians looked at all at Sabra, at the Jew, at their efforts to rouse the boy, it was with the eyes of sleepwalkers. Their lips were gently smiling. Sometimes they swayed a little. The sacred fire leaped orange and scarlet and gold. Old Stump Horn wielded his eagle feather fan, back and forth, back and forth. The quavering cadences of the Mescal song rose and fell to the accompaniment of the gourd rattle and the unceasing drum. The white man and woman, frail both, tugged and strained at the inert figure of the boy.
“Oh, God!” whimpered Sabra. “He’s so heavy. What shall we do?” They bent again, tugged with all their strength, lifted but could not carry him.
“We must drag him,” Sol said, at last.
They took an arm each. So, dragging, tugging, past those rapt still forms, past those mazed smiling faces, they struggled with him to the door. The little beads of sweat stood out on her forehead, on her lip. She breathed in choking gasps. Her eyes were wide and staring and dreadful in their determination. The rattle. The drum beat. The high eerie song notes, wordless.
The blackness of the outer air; past the two towering motionless blanketed figures at the door. Dragging him along the earth, through the trampled weeds.
“We can’t lift him into the buggy. We can’t﻿—” She ran back to the two at the door.

===Chinese===

每个洞口近旁都躺着人影，比别的那些不动的躯壳更瘫软，更精疲力竭。萨布拉和索尔站着眨眼的工夫，便明白了这些开口的用处。因为靠近火光的半圆里，一个蹲着的印第安人突然恶心起来。那人手脚并用地爬到一扇小门前，推开，把头和肩膀探进夜气里，把身子里多服的药吐了出去。
萨布拉只把眼移开，找啊，找啊。随后她看见了他躺的地方——在那条鲜艳的条纹毯子底下。脸蒙着，可她认得。那瘦长身子在毯子里怎样蜷着，夜里睡着了怎样躺着，她再熟悉不过。这是一种不一样的睡，可她认得。他们朝他走过去，跨过一个个目光定定的、如痴如醉蹲着的人影，跨过那些躺得死静的躯体。她掀开毯子。那张脸笑着，安详，可爱。
她想：“他若是死了，我就是这样看他的。”随即又想：“他是死了。”男孩安静地躺着，呼吸着。满屋是一种恍惚的、酥软至乐的气氛。那些印第安人若还看萨布拉一眼，看那犹太人一眼，看他们唤醒男孩的努力一眼，用的也是梦游者的眼睛。他们唇上挂着温柔的笑。有时轻轻摇晃。圣火蹿起橙色、猩红和金色。老断角挥着鹰羽扇，一来，一回，一来，一回。麦斯卡尔歌谣颤巍巍的调子，和着葫芦响器和不停的鼓，起伏着。这一对白人男女，两个人都单薄，拽着拽着男孩那具死沉的身子。
“哦，上帝！”萨布拉呜咽道。“他这么沉。我们怎么办？”他们又俯下身，用尽力气抬，抬起来了，却抱不动。
“只得拖他走了。”索尔末了说。
一人架起一条胳膊。就这样，拖着，拽着，越过那些出神的静影，越过那些迷惘的笑脸，他们架着他挣扎到门口。细汗珠沁在她额头上，唇上。她一口一口噎着喘。她的眼睛睁得老大，直瞪着，那决心怕人。响器。鼓点。尖利诡异的歌音，没有词。
外面空气的黑；过门口那两条高塔般纹丝不动的裹毯身影。拖着他贴着地，蹚过踩烂的荒草。
“我们抬他不上车。我们没法——”她跑回门口那两人跟前。

===Original===

She clasped her hands before the one called Joe Yellow Eyes. She lifted her white, agonized face to him. “Help me. Help me.” She made a futile gesture of lifting.
The Indian looked at her a moment with a dead, unseeing gaze. Flecks of gold and red and yellow danced, reflected in the black pools of his eyes, and died there. Leisurely, wordless, he walked over to where the boy lay, picked him up lightly in his great arms as though he were a sack of meal, swung him into the buggy seat. He turned, then, and went back to his place at the door.
They drove back to the town of Osage. Cim’s body leaned heavily, slackly against hers; his head lay in her lap, like a little boy’s. One aching arm she held firmly about him to keep him from slipping to the floor of the buggy, so that finally it ceased to ache and became numb. The dawn came, and then the sunrise over the prairie, its red meeting the red of the Oklahoma earth, so that they drove through a fiery furnace.
She had been quiet enough until now, with a kind of stony quiet. She began to sob; a curious dry racking sound, like a hiccup.
“Now, now,” said Sol Levy, and made a little comforting noise between tongue and teeth. “So bad it isn’t. What did the boy do, he went out to see the sights on the Reservation and try what it was like to eat this dope stuff﻿—this peyote. Say, when I was a boy I did lots worse.”
She did not seem to pay much heed to this, but it must have penetrated her numbed brain at last, for presently she stopped the painful sobbing and looked down at his lovely smiling face in her lap, the long lashes, like a girl’s, resting so fragilely on the olive cheek.
“He wanted to go. I wouldn’t let him. Is is too late, Sol?”
“Go? Go where?”
“The Colorado School of Mines. Geology.”
“Too late! That kid there! Don’t talk foolish. September. This is the time to go. It just starts. Sure he’ll go.”
They drove through the yard, over Sabra’s carefully tended grass, of which she was so proud, right to the edge of the porch steps, and so, dragging again and pulling, they got him in, undressed him; she washed his dust-smeared face.
“Well,” said Sol Levy. “I guess I go and open the store and then have a good cup of coffee.”
She put out her hand. Her lower lip was caught between her teeth, sharp and tight. Her face was distorted absurdly with her effort not to cry.

===Chinese===

她在那个叫乔·黄眼睛的人面前合起双手。她朝他仰起自己惨白的、痛楚的脸。“帮我。帮我。”她做了一个徒劳的抬举姿势。
那印第安人用一种死的、什么都不看的目光望了她片刻。金色、红色、黄色的光斑在他眼睛的黑潭里跳着，映着，熄灭在那里。他慢悠悠地、一言不发地走到男孩躺着的地方，两条粗臂轻轻把他抱起，像抱一袋面粉，把他悠上车座。然后转身，回到门口他的位置上去了。
他们驱车回奥塞奇镇。西姆的身子沉沉地、松松地倚着她；他的头枕在她膝上，像个小男孩。她用一条酸痛的胳膊紧紧搂住他，不叫他滑到车板上，搂到末了那胳膊不再酸痛，麻了。天亮起来，随后草原上日出，那红碰上俄克拉何马红土的红，他们便像驱车穿过一座烈火的炉子。
直到此刻她都够安静，一种石头的安静。她开始啜泣；一种古怪的、干涩的、抽搐的声音，像打嗝。
“好了，好了，”索尔·利维说，用舌齿间发出一点安慰的细响。“没那么糟。这孩子做什么了，他不过是出去逛逛保留地，尝尝这迷魂药是什么滋味——这佩奥特。我说，我小时候干的糟心事多着呢。”
这些话她像是没大听进去，可它终究渗进了她麻木的脑子，因为不多时她止住了那痛苦的抽噎，低头看膝上那张含笑的、可爱的脸，长长的睫毛，像女孩的，那样纤弱地覆在橄榄色的脸颊上。
“他想去。我不让。还来得及吗，索尔？”
“想去？去哪儿？”
“科罗拉多矿业学院。地质。”
“来不及！那孩子！别说傻话。九月。正是去的时候。才开学呢。他当然能去。”
他们把车赶进院子，碾过萨布拉精心侍弄、引以为傲的草地，一直赶到游廊台阶边上，然后又拖又拽，把他弄进屋，脱了衣裳；她洗去他脸上抹的尘土。
“好了，”索尔·利维说，“我该去开铺子门，然后喝杯好咖啡了。”
她伸出手来。下唇咬在齿间，紧紧的。她的脸为了不哭，扭曲得近乎滑稽。

===Original===

But when he would have patted her grimed and trembling hand with his own, in a gesture of comforting, she caught his hand to her lips and kissed it.
The sound of the horses’ hoofs died away on the still morning air. She looked down at Cim. She thought, I will take a bath, and then I will have some coffee, too. Yancey has gone again. Has left me. I know that. How do I know it? Well, nothing more can happen to me now. I have had it all, and I have borne it. Nothing more can happen to me now.

===Chinese===

可当他也要用自己的手拍拍她那只污黑发抖的手、以示安慰时，她却一把抓过那只手，贴上自己的唇，吻了它。
马蹄声在寂静的晨气里远去，消逝。她低头看西姆。她想，我要洗个澡，然后我也喝点咖啡。扬西又走了。丢下我了。我知道的。我怎么知道的？唔，如今再没有什么能落到我头上了。该来的都来过了，我都受了。如今再没有什么能落到我头上了。

## XV / 十五
===Original===

Dixie Lee’s girls were riding by on their daily afternoon parade. Sabra recognized their laughter and the easy measured clatter of their horses’ hoofs before they came into view. She knew it was Dixie Lee’s girls. Somehow, the virtuous women of Osage did not laugh much, though Sabra did not put this thought into words, even in her mind. She glanced up now as they drove by. She was seated at her desk by the window in the front office of the *Oklahoma Wigwam*. Their plumes, their parasols, their brilliant-hued dresses made a gay garden of color in the monotony of Pawhuska Avenue. They rode in open phaetons, but without the usual top, so that they had only their parasols to shade their brightly painted faces from the ardent Southwest sun. The color of the parasols and plumes and dresses was changed from day to day, but they always were done in ensemble effect. One day the eyes of Osage’s male population were dazzled (and its female population’s eyes affronted) by a burst of rosy splendor shading from pale pink to scarlet. The next day they would shade from palest lavender to deepest purple. The next, from delicate lemon to orange; the day following they ran the gamut of green. They came four by four, and usually one in each carriage handled the reins, though occasionally a Negro driver occupied the front seat alone. They were not boisterous. Indeed, they conducted themselves in seemly enough fashion except perhaps for the little bursts of laughter and for the fact that they were generous with the ankles beneath the ruffled skirts. Often they carried dolls in their arms. Sometimes﻿—rarely﻿—they called to each other. Their voices were high and curiously unformed, like the voices of little children, and yet with a metallic note in them.
“Madge, looka! When we get to the end of Pawhuska we’ll race you to Coley’s Gulch and back.” These afternoon races became almost daily sporting events, and the young bloods of Osage got into the habit of stationing themselves along the road to bet on the pale pink plumes or the deep rose plumes.
“Heh, go it, Clemmie! Whip him up, Carmen. Give him the whip! Come on! Whoop‑ee! Yi!” Plumes whirling, parasols bobbing, skirts flying, shrill shouts and screams of laughter from the edge of town. But on the return drive their behavior was again seemly enough, their cheeks flushed with a natural color beneath the obvious red.

===Chinese===

迪克茜·李的姑娘们又坐着车过来了，这是她们每天下午的例行游行。人还没露面，萨布拉先听出了那笑声，听出了马蹄那从容而均匀的嗒嗒声。她知道是迪克茜·李的姑娘们。不知怎么，奥塞奇的正经女人是不大笑的——这个念头萨布拉没有说出口，连在心里也没让自己说出来。车驶过时她抬头瞥了一眼。她正坐在《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》前厅靠窗的书桌前。她们的翎毛、阳伞、色彩艳丽的衣裙，在单调的波哈斯卡大街上开出一片欢快的花团锦簇。她们坐的是敞篷马车，连车篷都不要，全凭一柄阳伞替浓妆艳抹的脸挡住西南部火辣辣的日头。阳伞、翎毛和裙子的颜色一天一换，但总是成套搭配。有一天，奥塞奇男人的眼睛被晃花了（女人的眼睛则被冒犯了）——满目玫瑰色的辉煌，从浅粉渐变到猩红。第二天又从最浅的淡紫渐变到最深的绛紫。再一天，从娇嫩的柠檬黄变到橙黄；再下一天，把绿色从头到尾演了个遍。她们四辆一组地过来，通常每辆车里有一人执缰，偶尔也有个黑人车夫独坐前座。她们并不喧闹。举止其实够得体的，除了偶尔迸出的一阵笑声，还有褶裙底下那双毫不吝惜示人的脚踝。她们怀里常常抱着洋娃娃。有时——难得——彼此喊句话。嗓音尖高，出奇地不成调，像小孩子的嗓音，却又带着一股金属味。
“玛奇，瞧着！到了波哈斯卡尽头，咱们跟你赛到科利沟再赛回来。”这种下午的赛车几乎成了每日的体育盛事，奥塞奇的纨绔子弟们养成习惯，守在路边，押淡粉翎毛赢，或者押深玫瑰翎毛赢。
“嘿，跑哇，克莱米！抽它，卡门。给它一鞭！上啊！呼——咿！呀！”翎毛旋转，阳伞颠动，裙裾翻飞，尖声的喊叫和大笑从镇边上腾起。可回程时她们的举止又够体面了，两颊在明显的脂红底下透出天然的红晕。

===Original===

Sabra’s face darkened now as she saw them driving slowly by. Dixie Lee never drove with them. Sabra knew where she was this afternoon. She was down in the back room of the Osage First National Bank talking business to the President, Murch Rankin. The business men of the town were negotiating for the bringing of the packing house and a plough works and a watch factory to Osage. Any one of these industries required a substantial bonus. The spirit of the day was the boom spirit. Boom the town of Osage. Dixie Lee was essentially a commercial woman﻿—shrewd, clear headed. She had made a great success of her business. It was one of the crude town’s industries, and now she, as well as the banker, the hardware man, the proprietor of the furniture store, the meat market, the clothing store, contributed her share toward coaxing new industries to favor Osage. That way lay prosperity.
Dixie Lee was a personage in the town. Visitors came to her house now from the cities and counties round about. She had built for herself and her thriving business the first brick structure in the wooden town; a square, solid, and imposing two-story house, its bricks formed from the native Oklahoma red clay. Cal Bixby had followed close on it with the Bixby Block on Pawhuska Avenue, but Dixie Lee had led the way. She had commissioned Louie Hefner to buy her red velvet and gold furniture and her long gilt-framed mirrors, her scarlet deep-pile carpet﻿—that famous velvet-pile carpet in which Shanghai Wiley, that bearded, cultured, and magnetic barbarian, said he sank so deep that for a terrified moment he fell into a panic, being unable to tell which was red carpet and which his own flowing red beard. Dixie herself had gone East for her statues and pictures. The new house had been opened with a celebration the like of which had never been seen in the Southwest. Sabra Cravat, mentioning no names, had had an editorial about it in which the phrases “insult to the fair womanhood of America” and “orgy rivaling the Bacchanalian revels of history” (Yancey’s library stood her in good stead these days) figured prominently. Both the Philomathean Society and the Twentieth Century Culture Club had, for the duration of one meeting at least, deserted literature and culture for the discussion of the more vital topic of Dixie Lee’s new mansion.

===Chinese===

看见她们缓缓驶过，萨布拉的脸沉了下来。迪克茜·李从不跟车同行。萨布拉知道她今天下午在哪儿。她在奥塞奇第一国民银行的后屋，正跟行长默奇·兰金（Murch Rankin）谈生意。镇上的商人们正在谈判，要把肉联厂、犁具厂和一家钟表厂招到奥塞奇来。这几样实业，哪一样都得一笔可观的赞助。时下的风气就是大发展的风气。把奥塞奇镇炒起来。迪克茜·李骨子里是个生意女人——精明，头脑清楚。她那一行经营得非常成功。那也是这个粗糙小镇的实业之一，如今她跟银行家、五金行老板、家具店老板、肉铺、服装店一样，出自己那一份力，把新实业哄到奥塞奇来。繁荣之路，正在于此。
迪克茜·李是镇上的一个人物。邻近城乡的客人如今都上她家来。她为自己、为自己蒸蒸日上的营生，在这座木板镇上盖起了第一栋砖楼——方方正正、结结实实、气派的两层楼，砖是用俄克拉何马本地的红土烧的。卡尔·比克斯比紧随其后，在波哈斯卡大街上盖起了比克斯比大楼，但开风气之先的是迪克茜·李。她委托路易·赫夫纳替她置办红丝绒描金的家具、镶金框的长镜、猩红的厚绒地毯——那张有名的丝绒地毯，“上海”怀利（Shanghai Wiley），那个蓄须、风雅、魅力四射的蛮子，说他陷进去那么深，一时竟慌了神，分不清哪是红地毯、哪是他自己飘洒的红胡子。雕像和画儿，迪克茜亲自去东部采办。新宅开张那天大操大办，西南部前所未见。萨布拉·克拉瓦特不点名地写了一篇社论，其中“对美国良家妇女的侮辱”和“堪与史上巴克斯狂欢相比的放纵”这样的字眼（这些日子扬西那间书房可帮了她大忙）赫然在目。菲洛马西安会和二十世纪文化俱乐部，至少在一个会期里，撇下了文学与文化，转而讨论迪克茜·李新宅这个更要紧的题目。

===Original===

It was﻿—this red brick brothel﻿—less sinister than these good and innocent women suspected. Dixie Lee, now a woman of thirty or more, ruled it with an iron hand. Within it obtained certain laws and rules of conduct so rigid as to be almost prim. In a crude, wild, and nearly lawless country the brick mansion occupied a strange place, filled a want foreign to its original purpose. It was, in a way, a club, a rendezvous, a salon. For hundreds of men who came there it was all they had ever known of richness, of color, of luxury. The red and gold, the plush and silk, the perfume, the draperies, the white arms, the gleaming shoulders sank deep into their hard-bitten senses, long starved from years on windswept ranches, plains dust bedeviled, prairies baked barren by the fierce Southwest sun. Here they lolled, sunk deep in rosy comfort, while they talked Territory politics, swapped yarns of the old cattle days, played cards, drank wines which tasted like sweet prickling water to their whisky-scarred palates. They kissed these women, embraced them fiercely, thought tenderly of many of them, and frequently married them; and these women, once married, settled down contentedly to an almost slavish domesticity.
A hard woman, Dixie Lee; a bad woman. Sabra was morally right in her attitude toward her. Yet this woman, as well as Sabra, filled her place in the early life of the Territory.
Now, as the laughter sounded nearer and the equipages came within her view, Sabra, seated at her desk in the newspaper office, put down the soft pencil with which she had been filling sheet after sheet of copy paper. She wrote easily now, with no pretense to style, but concisely and with an excellent sense of news values. The *Oklahoma Wigwam* had flourished in these last five years of her proprietorship. She was thinking seriously of making it a daily instead of a weekly; of using the entire building on Pawhuska Avenue for the newspaper plant and building a proper house for herself and the two children on one of the residence streets newly sprung up﻿—streets that boasted neatly painted houses and elm and cottonwood trees in the front yards.
Someone came up the steps of the little porch and into the office. It was Mrs. Wyatt. She often brought club notices and social items to the *Wigwam*: rather fancied herself as a writer; a born woman’s club corresponding secretary.
“Well!”

===Chinese===

其实——这座红砖窑子——并不像这些善良无辜的女人们疑心的那么邪恶。迪克茜·李如今已三十开外，用铁腕统治着它。宅子里奉行着一套法规和行止的规矩，严得近乎古板。在这样一个粗野、荒僻、几乎无法无天的地方，这座砖楼占着一个奇怪的位置，填补了一种它本分之外的需求。某种程度上，它是个俱乐部，个聚会处，个沙龙。对成百上千来这里的男人，这就是他们平生所知的全部富丽、全部色彩、全部奢华。那红与金，那长毛绒与绸缎，那香水，那帷幔，那雪白的手臂，那莹亮的肩膀，深深沉入他们久饿的感觉——那些感觉在风吹日晒的牧场上饿了多年，被平原的尘土磨着，被西南部毒日烤得焦渴。他们在这儿懒懒地半躺着，深陷进玫瑰色的安逸里，谈领地政治，摆当年赶牛的老龙门阵，打牌，喝那种在他们被威士忌烧坏的舌头上只像甜丝丝冒泡的清水的葡萄酒。他们亲吻这些女人，狠狠地搂她们，对她们中不少人存着温情，而且常常娶了她们；而这些女人一旦嫁了人，便心满意足地安顿下来，操持家务，近乎奴婢一般。
迪克茜·李是个狠女人；是个坏女人。萨布拉对她的态度，在道义上没有错。然而这个女人，同萨布拉一样，在领地早期的岁月里占着她的位置。
这时，笑声近了，车队进了她的视野，萨布拉在报馆的书桌前放下那支软铅笔——她已用它写满了一页又一页稿纸。她如今笔下流畅，不摆文体架子，但简洁明快，对新闻价值的感觉极佳。在她主持的这最后五年里，《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》兴旺发达。她正认真考虑把它改成日报，不再出周报；把波哈斯卡大街整栋楼都用作报馆的厂房，再在一条新起的住宅街上给自己和两个孩子盖一栋像样的房子——那些街上已有粉刷齐整的房舍，前院里种着榆树和三角叶杨。
有人走上小门廊的台阶，进了报馆。是怀亚特太太。她常给《维格沃姆报》送社团启事和社交消息：颇自命为笔杆子；天生一个妇女俱乐部通讯干事。
“哎呀！”

===Original===

she exclaimed now, simply, but managing to put enormous bite and significance into the monosyllable. Her glance followed Sabra’s. Together the two women, tight lipped, condemnatory, watched the gay parade of Dixie Lee’s girls go by.
The flashing company disappeared. A whiff of patchouli floated back to the two women standing by the open window. Their nostrils lifted in disdain. The sound of the horses’ hoofs grew fainter.
“It’s a disgrace to the community”﻿—Mrs. Wyatt’s voice took on its platform note﻿—“and an insult to every wife and mother in the Territory. There ought to be a law.”
Sabra turned away from the window. Her eyes sought the orderly rows of books, bound neatly in tan and red﻿—Yancey’s law books, so long unused now, except, perhaps, for occasional newspaper reference. Her face set itself in lines of resolve. “Perhaps there is.”
It had taken almost three of those five years to bring those lines into Sabra Cravat’s face. They were not, after all, lines. Her face was smooth, her skin still fresh in spite of dust and alkali water and sun and wind. It was, rather, that a certain hardening process had taken place﻿—a crystallization. Yancey had told her, tenderly, that she was a charming little fool, and she had believed it﻿—though perhaps with subconscious reservations. It was not until he left her, and the years rolled round without him, that she developed her powers. The sombrero had ridden gayly away. The head under the sunbonnet had held itself high in spite of hints, innuendoes, gossip.
A man like Yancey Cravat﻿—spectacular, dramatic, impulsive﻿—has a thousand critics, scores of bitter enemies. As the weeks had gone by and Yancey failed to return﻿—had failed to write﻿—rumor, clouded by scandal, leaped like prairie fire from house to house in Osage, from town to town in the Oklahoma Country, over the Southwest, indeed. All the old stories were revived, and their ugly red tongues licked a sordid path through the newly opened land.
They say he is living with the Cherokee squaw who is really his wife.
They say he was seen making the Run in the Kickapoo Land Opening in 1895.
They say he killed a man in the Cherokee Strip Run and was caught by a posse and hung.
They say he got a section of land, sold it at a high figure, and was seen lording it around the bar of the Brown Palace Hotel in Denver, in his white sombrero and his Prince Albert coat.

===Chinese===

她只说了这么一个字，却硬是让这个单音节字咬出了千钧的分量。她的目光随着萨布拉的目光望去。两个女人紧抿着嘴，满脸谴责，一同看着迪克茜·李的姑娘们那花团锦簇的车队驶过。
那队流光溢彩的人马过去了。一缕广藿香味飘回到站在敞开的窗边的两个女人这里。她们的鼻孔不屑地翕动着。马蹄声渐渐远了。
“这是全镇的耻辱”——怀亚特太太的嗓音摆出了演讲腔——“是对领地上每一个妻子和母亲的侮辱。该立个法。”
萨布拉从窗前转过身。她的目光落在那一排排整整齐齐的书上，棕黄色和红色的书皮装订得干净利落——扬西的法律书，如今久已不用了，或许偶尔查个报纸要用的条目。她的脸绷出了决断的线条。“也许已经有了。”
把这样的线条带上萨布拉·克拉瓦特的脸，几乎用去了那五年中的三年。那其实算不上皱纹。她的脸是光洁的，尽管风沙、碱水、日头和风，皮肤依然鲜润。倒不如说是一种变硬的过程在她身上完成了——一种结晶。扬西曾温柔地对她说，她是个迷人的小傻瓜，她也信了——尽管潜意识里也许有所保留。直到他离开了她，年复一年没有他，她才把自己的本事施展出来。那顶阔边帽兴冲冲地骑马走了。太阳帽底下那颗头却始终昂着，任凭人们影射、讥讽、嚼舌根。
扬西·克拉瓦特这样的人——招摇，戏剧化，冲动——有一千个批评者，几十个切齿的仇敌。几个星期过去，扬西没有回来——也没有来信——流言裹着丑闻，像草原野火，在奥塞奇从一家烧到一家，在俄克拉何马地从一镇烧到一镇，乃至烧遍整个西南部。所有的老故事都翻了出来，它们丑恶的红舌头，一路舔过这片新开之地。
都说他跟那个切罗基婆娘住在一起，那才是他明媒正娶的老婆。
都说1895年基卡普土地开放抢地时，有人瞧见他也去跑了。
都说他在切罗基长条地抢地时杀了人，被民团抓住，绞死了。
都说他到手了一整块地，卖了个大价钱，有人瞧见他穿着长礼服、戴着白阔边帽，在丹佛布朗宫饭店的酒吧里称王称霸。

===Original===

They say Dixie Lee is his real wife, and he left her when she was seventeen, came to Wichita, and married Sabra Venable; and he is the one who has set Dixie up in the brick house.
They say he drank five quarts of whisky one night and died and is buried in an unmarked grave in Horseshoe Ranch, where the Doolin gang held forth.
They say he is really the leader of the Doolin gang.
They say. They say. They say.
It is impossible to know how Sabra survived those first terrible weeks that lengthened into months that lengthened into years. There was in her the wiry endurance of the French Marcys; the pride of the Southern Venables. Curiously enough, in spite of all that had happened to her she still had that virginal look﻿—that chastity of lip, that clearness of the eye, that purity of brow. Men come back to the women who look as Sabra Cravat looked, but the tempests of men’s love pass them by.
She told herself that he was dead. She told the world that he was dead. She knew, by some deep and unerring instinct, that he was alive. Donna had been so young when he left that he now was all but wiped from her memory. But Cim, strangely enough, spoke of Yancey Cravat as though he were in the next room. “My father says﻿ ﻿…” Sometimes, when Sabra saw the boy coming toward her with that familiar swinging stride, his head held down and a little thrust forward, she was wrenched by a physical pang of agony that was almost nausea.
She ran the paper competently; wrung from it a decent livelihood for herself and the two children. When it had no longer been possible to keep secret from her parents the fact of Yancey’s prolonged absence, Felice Venable had descended upon her prepared to gather to the family bosom her deserted child and to bring her, together with her offspring, back to the parental home. Lewis Venable had been too frail and ill to accompany his wife, so Felice had brought with her the more imposing among the Venables, Goforths, and Vians who chanced to be visiting the Wichita house at the time of her departure. Osage had looked upon these stately figures with much awe, but Sabra’s reception of them had been as coolly cordial as her rejection of their plans for her future was firm.

都说迪克茜·李才是他原配，他在她十七岁上甩了她，跑到威奇托娶了萨布拉·维纳布尔；把迪克茜安顿进那座砖楼的，正是他。
都说他一夜灌下五夸特威士忌，死了，埋在马蹄牧场一座没字的坟里，杜林帮就在那儿盘踞。
都说他就是杜林帮的头子。
都说。都说。都说。
萨布拉是怎么熬过最初那些可怕的星期——星期拖成月，月拖成年——没人说得清。她身上有法国马西家坚韧的耐力；有南方维纳布尔家的骄傲。奇怪的是，尽管遭了这一切，她脸上仍带着那种处子气——唇的贞静，眼的澄澈，额的纯净。男人会回到长得像萨布拉·克拉瓦特这样的女人身边来，可男人们爱的风暴偏偏绕开她们。
她对自己说，他死了。她对世人说，他死了。可她凭某种深沉而不误的本能知道，他活着。他走时唐娜太小，如今他已差不多从她记忆里抹掉了。可是西姆，说来奇怪，提起扬西·克拉瓦特来，就像他人就在隔壁屋里。“我爸爸说……”有时，萨布拉看见那孩子迈着熟悉的、大摇大摆的步子朝她走来，头低着、微微前倾，她会猛地被一种近乎恶心的、肉体般的剧痛拧住。
她把报纸办得井井有条；靠它为自己和两个孩子挤出一份体面的生计。扬西久久不归的事再瞒不住父母时，费利斯·维纳布尔便大举驾临，预备把被弃的爱女收回家族的怀抱，连她带她的孩子一并接回娘家去。刘易斯·维纳布尔身体太弱，经不起旅途，没陪妻子同来，费利斯便带来了维纳布尔、戈福思、维安几家碰巧在她动身时客居威奇托宅子里的、最有气派的几位。奥塞奇人望着这些仪态庄严的人物，敬畏得很，可萨布拉接待他们时不冷不热，客气而疏远，一如她回绝他们为她安排的前程时那样斩钉截铁。

===Original===

“I intend to stay right here in Osage,” she announced, quietly, but in a tone that even Felice Venable recognized as inflexible, “and run the paper, and bring up my children as their father would have expected them to be brought up.”
“Their father!” Felice Venable repeated, in withering accents.
The boy Cimarron, curiously sensitive to sounds and moods, stood before his grandmother, his head thrust forward, his handsome eyes glowing. “My father is the most famous man in Oklahoma. The Indians call him Buffalo Head.”
Felice Venable pounced on this. “If that’s what you mean by bringing them up as their father﻿ ﻿…”
The meeting degenerated into one of those family bickerings. “I do wish, Mamma, that you wouldn’t repeat everything I say and twist it by your tone into something poisonous.”
“*I* say! I can’t help it if the things you say sound ridiculous when they are repeated. I simply mean﻿—”
“I don’t care what you mean. I mean to stay here in Osage until Yancey﻿—until﻿—” She never finished that sentence.
The Osage society notes became less simple. From bare accounts of quiltings, sewing bees, and church sociables they blossomed into flowery imitations of the metropolitan dailies’ descriptions of social events. Refreshments were termed elegant. Osage matrons turned from the sturdy baked beans, cole slaw, and veal loaf of an earlier day to express themselves in food terms culled from the pictures in the household magazines. They heard about fruit salads. They built angel-food cakes whose basis was the whites of thirteen eggs, and their husbands, at breakfast, said, “What makes these scrambled eggs so yellow?” Countrified costumes were described in terms of fashion. The wilted prairie flowers that graced weddings and parties were transformed into rare hothouse blooms by the magic touch of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* hand press. Sabra cannily published all the brilliant social news items that somewhat belatedly came her way via the ready-print and the paper’s scant outside news service.

“我打算就待在奥塞奇，”她平静地宣布，但那口气连费利斯·维纳布尔也听得出毫无转圜，“办我的报，按他们父亲期望的样子把我的孩子带大。”
“他们的父亲！”费利斯·维纳布尔用能把人冻死的腔调重复了一遍。
西马龙这孩子对声响和情绪出奇地敏感，站到外祖母面前，头前倾着，俊秀的眼睛放着光。“我爸爸是俄克拉何马最有名的人。印第安人叫他野牛头。”
费利斯·维纳布尔一把揪住这句话。“这就是你说的按他们父亲期望的样子带大……”
这场会面退化成了那种家常拌嘴。“妈妈，我真希望你别我说什么你就重复什么，还用你的腔调把它拧成毒药。”
“我说话！你说的那些话一重复出来显得可笑，这能怪我吗。我不过是说——”
“你是什么意思我不在乎。我就是要待在奥塞奇，等扬西——等到——”这句话她从来没能说完。
奥塞奇的社交栏不再那么朴素了。从拼布会、缝纫会、教会联谊的干巴巴记事，一变而为花团锦簇的仿作，仿的是大都会日报对社交盛事的描写。茶点一律称“精致”。奥塞奇的主妇们撇下早年间结实的烤豆子、凉拌卷心菜、小牛肉糕，改用家庭杂志画页上拣来的美食辞令表达自己。她们听说了水果沙拉。她们烤天使蛋糕，底子是十三个蛋清，她们的丈夫吃早饭时问：“这炒鸡蛋怎么这么黄？”土气的衣装被用时装术语描写一番。装点婚礼和宴会的那种蔫头蔫脑的草原野花，经《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的手摇印刷机一点，就变成了温室珍卉。萨布拉精明地刊出一切经由预印版和本报可怜的一点外埠新闻渠道迟到她手里的精彩社交消息。

===Chinese===

> “纽波特，10月4日——纽波特多年来最盛大的一场婚礼，今日于老三一教堂隆重举行。新人为乔治娜·哈伍德小姐与哈罗德·布莱克先生，两家均属‘四百人家’魔圈之内的门第。新娘着象牙白缎礼服，缀以至珍针绣花边的褶裥与绉边，花边头纱以珍珠钻石的冠冕绾住。礼成之后，有盛大冷餐……”

===Original===

The feminine population of Osage﻿—of the county﻿—felt that it had seen the ivory satin, the point lace, the tiara of pearls and diamonds, as these splendors moved down the aisle of old Trinity on the person of Miss Georgina Harwood of Newport. They derived from it the vicarious satisfaction that a dieting dyspeptic gets from reading the cook book.
Sabra was, without being fully aware of it, a power that shaped the social aspect of this crude Southwestern town. The Ladies of the new Happy Hour Club, on her declining to become a member, pleading lack of time and press of work (as well she might), made her an honorary member, resolved to have her influential name on their club roster, somehow. They were paying unconscious tribute to Oklahoma’s first feminist. She still ran the paper single handed, with the aid of Jesse Rickey, the most expert printer in the Southwest (when sober), and as good as the average when drunk.
Sabra, serene in the knowledge that the attacked could do little to wreak vengeance on a woman, printed stories and statements which for boldness and downright effrontery would have earned a male editor a horsewhipping. She publicly scolded the street loafers who, in useless sombreros and six-shooters and boots and spurs, relics of a bygone day, lolled limply on Pawhuska Avenue corners, spitting tobacco juice into the gutter. Sometimes she borrowed Yancey’s vigorous and picturesque phraseology. She denounced a local politician as being too crooked to sleep in a roundhouse, and the phrase stuck, and in the end defeated him. Law, order, the sanctity of the home, prunes, prisms. Though the Gyp Hills and the Osage Hills still were as venomous with outlaws as the Plains were with rattlesnakes; though the six-shooter still was as ordinary a part of the Oklahoma male costume as boots or trousers; though outlawry still meant stealing a horse rather than killing a man; though the Territory itself had been settled and peopled, in thousands of cases, by men who had come to it, not in a spirit of adventure, but from cowardice, rapacity, or worse, Sabra Cravat and the other basically conventional women of the community were working unconsciously, yet with a quiet ferocity, toward that day when one of them would be able to say, standing in a doorway with a stiff little smile:
“Awfully nice of you to come.”

奥塞奇的女性——全县的女性——觉得那象牙白的缎子、那针绣花边、那珍珠钻石的冠冕，自己都亲眼见过了，看着这一派富丽堂皇伴着纽波特的乔治娜·哈伍德小姐走过老三一教堂的甬道。她们从中得到的替代性满足，好比忌口的病人读菜谱。
萨布拉自己并不全然察觉，她已是塑造这个粗陋西南小镇社交面貌的一股力量。新成立的欢乐时光俱乐部的太太们，在她以无暇及事忙为由（也确是实情）谢绝入会后，硬授了她一个名誉会员，横竖要把她那有影响的名字弄上会员名册。她们是在向俄克拉何马第一位女权主义者不自觉地致敬。她仍是一手办报，靠杰西·里基帮衬——西南部最老到的排字工（清醒的时候），喝醉了也不输于常人。
萨布拉心里笃定：挨骂的人对一个女人下不了狠手报复。于是她刊出的报道和言论，那份大胆、那份泼皮无赖，搁在男主编身上早换一顿马鞭了。她公开申斥街上的闲汉——他们戴着无用的阔边帽，挎着六响枪，蹬着靴子马刺，一身前朝遗物，蔫蔫地瘫在波哈斯卡大街的街角，把嚼烟的汁水啐进阴沟。有时她借用扬西那有力的、有声有色的措辞。她骂本镇一个政客“歪得连圆形机车库都睡不进去”，这话传开了，末了断送了他的前程。法律，秩序，家庭的神圣，李子干，棱镜。尽管吉普山和奥塞奇山里亡命徒之猖獗，一如平原上响尾蛇之密布；尽管六响枪在俄克拉何马男人的行头里仍如靴子裤子一样寻常；尽管“犯法”仍是指偷马而非杀人；尽管这片领地成千上万的居民，当年来时怀的并非冒险精神，而是懦怯、贪婪，或更糟——萨布拉·克拉瓦特和镇上其他本性规矩的女人们，正不自觉地、却又以一种安静的凶猛，朝着那一天努力：到那天，她们中的一个便能站在自家门口，端着一丝拘谨的小笑，说出：
“您能来，太好了。”

===Original===

“Awfully nice of you to ask me,” the other would reply.
When that day came, Osage would no longer need to feel itself looked down upon by Kansas City, Denver, Chicago, St. Louis, and San Francisco.
Slowly, slowly, certain figures began to take on the proportions of personalities. No one had arisen in the Territory to fill Yancey Cravat’s romantic boots. Pat Leary was coming on as a Territory lawyer, with an office in the Bixby Block and the railroads on which he had worked as section hand now consulting him on points of Territorial law. In his early railroad days he had married an Osage girl named Crook Nose. People shook their heads over this and said that he regretted it now, and that a lawyer could never hope to get on with this marital millstone round his neck.
There still was very little actual money in the Territory. People traded this for that. Sabra often translated subscriptions to the *Oklahoma Wigwam*﻿—and even advertising space﻿—into terms of fresh vegetables, berries, wild turkey, quail, prairie chickens, dress lengths, and shoes and stockings for the children.
Sol Levy’s store, grown to respectable proportions now, provided Sabra with countless necessities in return for the advertisement which, sent through the country via the *Oklahoma Wigwam*, urged its readers to Trade at Sol Levy’s. Visit the Only Zoo in the Territory. This invitation, a trifle bewildering to the uninitiated, was meant to be taken literally. In the back of his store Sol Levy kept a sizable menagerie. It had started through one of those chance encounters. A gaunt and bearded plainsman had come into the store one day with the suggestion that the proprietor trade a pair of pants for a bear cub. The idea had amused Sol Levy; then he had glanced out into the glare of Pawhuska Avenue and had seen the man’s ocherous wife, his litter of spindling children, huddled together in a crazy wagon attached by what appeared to be ropes, strings, and bits of nail and wire, to horses so cadaverous that his amusement was changed to pity. He gave the man the pants, stockings for the children, and﻿—the sentimentalist in him﻿—a piece of bright-colored cotton stuff for the woman.

===Chinese===

“多谢您相邀，”另一位便这样回答。
到了那一天，奥塞奇就用不着再觉得被堪萨斯城、丹佛、芝加哥、圣路易斯和旧金山俯视了。
慢慢地，慢慢地，一些人开始显出人物的分量。领地里没有冒出谁来填补扬西·克拉瓦特那双传奇的马靴。帕特·利里作为领地律师渐渐起来了，在比克斯比大楼里开着事务所，当年雇他当养路工的那几条铁路，如今反过来向他请教领地法律问题。早年在铁路上混的时候，他娶了个名叫歪鼻子的奥塞奇姑娘。人们为此摇头，说他如今悔了，说一个律师脖子上套着这么个婚姻磨盘，休想有出息。
领地里实在的现钱还是很少。人们以物易物。萨布拉常把《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的订费——连广告版面也算——折算成新鲜蔬菜、浆果、野火鸡、鹌鹑、草原榛鸡、一幅幅的衣料，还有孩子们的鞋袜。
索尔·利维的铺子如今已长成一副可观的规模，供给萨布拉数不清的必需品，交换条件是广告——那广告经《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》传遍乡野，劝读者光顾索尔·利维铺子。请参观领地唯一的动物园。这条启事，外行人看着有点糊涂，它是要你按字面当真的。铺子后头，索尔·利维养着一批规模可观的兽群。起头也是一场偶然。有一天，一个瘦削的、络腮胡的草原汉子进店来，提议拿一对熊崽跟掌柜的换条裤子。这主意逗乐了索尔·利维；可他往波哈斯卡大街白晃晃的日头里一望，看见了那汉子黄尘满面的老婆、一群细脚伶仃的孩子，挤在一辆破得发疯的马车上，车跟马之间连着的，瞧着像是绳子、线头、钉子和铁丝，那几匹马瘦得像骷髅——他的好笑变成了怜悯。他给了那汉子裤子，给了孩子们长袜，还——他心里那点感伤主义作祟——给了那女人一块颜色鲜亮的布。
