记住“缅因号”，让西班牙见鬼去！《今夜老城有一场好热闹》。他成了这场战争的象征、俄克拉何马地方的象征、领地的象征、西南部的象征——性烈，浪漫，冒险。
“嗨，扬西！嗬，我说，你上哪儿去了，你这头惊了群的老犍牛！”
“好啊，西马龙！你的白帽子哪儿去了？”
“有你跟这个罗斯福下场打仗，我看西班牙人要后悔哥伦布生下来了。”
扬西这边则是：“你好啊，克林特！好啊，山姆！嗬，这不是格拉特吗，见鬼！你‘好’啊，艾克，你这老偷马贼！”
那高大的身躯比这些高挑的平原汉子还要高出头来；那双俊目灼灼有光；那圆润的嗓音施展着魔法。浪子成了英雄；弃儿凯旋归来。
阿拉斯加。俄克拉何马自己正忙着长个儿的阵痛，倒也没有漏听阿拉斯加和淘金热的风声。“阿拉斯加！得了吧，你压根儿没去过阿拉斯加！听说你当印第安人去了。听说你跟杜林帮一块儿埋在布特希尔了。”
他掏出那个小皮袋。众人围拢来，他把那亮闪闪的一小堆倒在他们放光的眼前——那财宝，整个西南部的历史都跟它绞在一起。金子。为它，群山和平原被钻得千疮百孔；人们为它挨饿、火并、焦渴；为它死掉；为它被杀；为着找到它的指望，把名誉、家园、幸福都搭了进去。而这来自遥远阿拉斯加的宝贝黄色玩意儿，正从扬西·克拉瓦特纤细白皙的指缝里簌簌漏下。
“真他妈的，扬西，好事全叫你一个人占了。”
这位奥德修斯就这样站着，为他们编织他史诗里的新篇章。他们听着，惊叹着，信着，被艶羡、钦佩和同样去冒险的渴望激动着。他讲着，笑着，比划着，来回踱着步，谁也不曾想念他长礼服的下摆——如今替它扬威的，是铜扣子、贴袋、金线绣饰，和交叉马刀的闪光。
“运气！你管这叫运气，莫特？冻僵，饿瘪，迷路，雪盲！整整一个冬天独个儿关在一间屋的小木屋里，雪堆到屋顶，几个月没一个活人说话。你信得过的搭档，骗走你的地权，还顺手抢了你的金子——这也叫运气！就剩这一把了。

===Original===

I was going to see Sabra covered in gold like an Aztec princess.”
The eyes of listening Osage swung to the prim blue serge figure of the cheated Aztec princess, encountered the level gaze, the unsmiling lips; swung back again hastily to the dashing, the martial figure of the lately despised wanderer.
A tale of another world; a story of a land so remote from the brilliant scarlet and orange of the burning Southwest country that the very sound of the words he used in describing it fell with a strange cadence on the ears of the eager listeners. And as always when Yancey was telling the tale, he filled his hearers with a longing for the place he described; a longing that was like a nostalgia for something they had never known. Well, folks, winters at fifty below zero. Two hours of bitter winter sunshine, and then blackness. Long splendid summer days in May and June, with twenty hours of sunshine and four hours of twilight. Sabra, listening with the others, found this new vocabulary as strange, as terrifying, as the jargon of the Oklahoma country had been to her when first she had encountered it years ago.
Yukon. Chilkoot Pass. Skagway. Kuskokwim. Klondike. Moose. Caribou. Huskies. Sledges. Nome. Sitka. Blizzards. Snow blindness. Frozen fingers. Pemmican. Cold. Cold. Cold. Gold. Gold. Gold. To the fascinated figures crowded into the stuffy rooms of this little frame house squatting on the sunbaked Oklahoma prairie he brought, by the magic of his voice and his eloquence, the relentless movement of the glaciers, the black menace of icy rivers, the waste plains of blinding, treacherous snow. Two years of this, he said; and looked ruefully down at the stump that had been his famous trigger finger.
They, too, looked. Two years. Two years, and he had been gone five. That left three unaccounted for, right enough. The old stories seeped up in their minds. Their eyes, grown accustomed to the uniform, were less dazzled now. They saw the indefinable break that had come to the magnificent figure﻿—not a break, really, but a loosening, a lowering of the resistance such as comes to steel that has been too often in the flaming furnace. You looked at the massive shoulders﻿—they did not droop. The rare glance still pierced you like a sword thrust. The buffalo head, lowered, menaced you; lifted, thrilled you. Yet something had vanished.
“Where’d you join up, Yancey?”
“San Antonio.

===Chinese===

我原是要叫萨布拉浑身披金的，像一位阿兹特克公主。”
听着的奥塞奇人的目光刷地转向那位被骗的阿兹特克公主——一身规矩的蓝喬嗙——撞上一道平平的注视、两片不笑的嘴唇；又慌忙荡回那位刚刚还叫人鄙弃的浪人那身英武的戎装上去。
另一个世界的故事；讲的是一片同西南部烈日下猩红橙黄的土地相隔万里的地方，他讲述时用的那些字眼，落在热心的听客耳朵里，自有一种奇怪的调子。而且扬西讲故事时向来如此：他让听的人心里涨满对那地方的渴望；那渴望像一种乡愁，愁的是他们从来不识的东西。可不是嘛，乡亲们，零下五十度的冬天。冬天里两个钟头苦涩的日头，然后就是黑。五月六月漫长壮丽的白昼，二十个钟头太阳、四个钟头暮色。萨布拉跟众人一起听着，觉得这套新词汇之陌生、之骇人，一如多年前她头一回撞上俄克拉何马地方的行话时那样。
育空。奇尔库特山口。斯卡圭。卡斯科奎姆。克朗代克。驼鹿。驯鹿。爱斯基摩犬。雪橇。诺姆。锡特卡。暴风雪。雪盲。冻掉的手指。干肉饼。冷。冷。冷。金。金。金。挤在这间蹲在俄克拉何马烈日草原上的小板房闷热屋子里的着了迷的人们，被他的嗓音和口才一施魔法，眼前便有了冰川无情的移动，冰河黑色的凶险，还有那白得晃眼、暗藏杀机的茫茫雪原。这样的日子过了两年，他说；然后愁眉苦脸地低头看看那截残指——他那根有名的扣扳机的指头。
他们也看。两年。两年——可他走了五年。还有三年下落不明，这笔账明摆着。那些老故事又从他们心里渗上来。他们的眼睛惯了那身军装，不那么晃眼了。他们看出那堂堂身躯上来了种说不清的折损——其实算不上折损，而是一种松动，一种抵抗力的下降，好比一块回炉太多次的钢。你看那宽阔的肩膀——并没有塌。那一记难得一瞥仍旧像剑锋一样刺透你。那颗野牛头，低下来，威慑你；扬起来，让你激奋。可有什么东西没了。
“你是在哪儿入伍的，扬西？”
“圣安东尼奥。

===Original===

Leonard Wood’s down there﻿—Colonel Wood now﻿—and young Roosevelt, Lieutenant Colonel. He’s been drilling the boys. Most of them born on a horse and weaned on a Winchester. We’re better equipped than the regulars that have been at it for years. Young Roosevelt’s to thank for that. They were all for issuing us winter clothing, by God, to wear through a summer campaign in the tropics﻿—those nincompoops in Washington﻿—and they’d have done it if it hadn’t been for him.”
Southwest Davis spoke up from the crowd. “That case, you’ll be leaving right soon, won’t you? Week or so.”
“Week!” echoed Yancey, and looked at Sabra. “I go back to San Antonio tomorrow. The regiment leaves for Tampa next day.”
He had not told her before. Yet she said nothing, gave no sign. She had outfaced them with her pride and her spirit for five years; she would give them no satisfaction now. Five years. One day. San Antonio﻿—Tampa﻿—Cuba﻿—the Philippines﻿—War. She gave no sign. Curiously, the picture that was passing in her mind was this: she saw herself, as though it were someone she had known in the dim, far past, standing in the cool, shady corridor of the Mission School in Wichita. She saw, through the open door, the oblong of Kansas sunshine and sky and garden; there swept over her again that wave of nostalgia she had felt for the scene she was leaving; she was shaken by terror of this strange Indian country to which she was going with her husband.
“… but here in this land, Sabra, my girl, the women, they’ve been the real hewers of wood and drawers of water. You’ll want to remember that.”
Sabra remembered it now, well enough.
Slowly the crowd began to disperse. The men had their business; the women their housework. Wives linked their arms through those of husbands, and the gesture was one of perhaps not entirely unconscious cruelty, accompanied as it was by a darting glance at Sabra.
“Rough Rider uniform, sack of gold, golden voice, and melting eye,” that glance seemed to say. “You’re welcome to all the happiness you can get from those. Security, permanence, home, husband﻿—I wouldn’t change places with you.”
“Come on, Yancey!” shouted Strap Buckner. “Over to the Sunny Southwest and have a drink. We got a terrible lot of drinking to do, ain’t we, boys? Come on, you old longhorn. We got to drink to you because you’re back and because you’re going away.”
“And to the war!” yelled Bixler.

===Chinese===

伦纳德·伍德在底下——如今是伍德上校——还有小罗斯福，中校。他一直在操练弟兄们。这帮人多半生在马背上，是叼着温彻斯特枪断的奶。我们的装备比干了那么些年的正规军还好。这得谢小罗斯福。那帮华盛顿的糊涂虫，天知道，一心要给我们发冬装，穿着到热带去打夏季战役——要不是他拦着，他们真干得出来。”
索斯韦斯特·戴维斯从人群里开口。“这么说，你很快就要走了，是吧？个把星期。”
“星期！”扬西应了一声，看着萨布拉。“我明天就回圣安东尼奥。后天团里开拔去坦帕。”
他事先没跟她说过。可她什么也没说，不动声色。五年来她凭骄傲和心气把他们全都顶住了；如今也不会让他们称心。五年。一天。圣安东尼奥——坦帕——古巴——菲律宾——战争。她不动声色。奇怪的是，此刻掠过她脑海的画面是这样：她看见自己——仿佛是久远往昔认识的另一个人——站在威奇托教会学校清凉的回廊里。透过敞开的门，她看见堪萨斯的阳光、天空和花园那长长的一方；离别的乡愁又一潮一潮涌上来；又要跟着丈夫去那陌生的印第安人的地界，恐惧又把她摇撼。
“……可是在这块地上，萨布拉，我的姑娘，女人们，才是真正砍柴挑水的人。这话你该记着。”
萨布拉如今记得够清楚的了。
人渐渐散了。男人们有营生；女人们有家务。妻子们把胳膊挎进丈夫的臂弯，那姿态里带着一种也许并非全然无心的残忍——随之一道飞快的眼神，直射萨布拉。
“莽骑兵的军装，一袋金子，金嗓子，热乎乎的眼睛，”那眼神像在说。“这些东西能给你多少快活，都归你去受用。安稳，长久，家，丈夫——我可不跟你换。”
“走，扬西！”斯特拉普·巴克纳嚷道。“上‘阳光西南’喝一杯去。咱们欠下的酒债可了不得呢，是不是，弟兄们？来呀，你这头老长角牛。得敬你酒——为你回来，也为你又要走。”
“也为战争干杯！”比克斯勒喊。

===Original===

“And the Rough Riders!”
“And Alaska!”
Their boots clattered across the board floor of the newspaper office. They swept the towering figure in its khaki uniform with them. He turned, waved his hat at her. “Back in a minute, honey.” They were gone.
Sabra turned to the children, Cim and Donna, flushed, both, with the unwonted excitement; out of hand. Her face set itself with that look of quiet resolve. “Half the morning’s gone. But I want you to go along to school, anyway. Now, none of that! It’s no use your staying around here. The paper must be got out. Jesse’ll be no good to me the rest of the day. It’s easy to see that. I’ll write a note to your teachers.﻿ ﻿… Run along now. I must go to court.”
She actually had made up her mind that she would see the day through as she had started it. The Dixie Lee case, seething for weeks, was coming to a crisis this morning﻿—this very minute. She would be late if she did not hasten. She would not let the work of months go for nothing because this man﻿—this stranger had seen fit to stride into her life for a day.
She pinned on her hat, saw that her handbag contained pencil and paper, hurried into the back room that was printing shop, composing room, press room combined. She had been right about Jesse Rickey. That consistently irresponsible one was even now leaning a familiar elbow on the polished surface of the Sunny Southwest bar as he helped toast the returned wanderer or the departing hero or the war in the semi-tropics, or the snows of Alaska “﻿—or God knows what!” concluded Sabra, in her mind.
Cliff Means, the ink-smeared printer’s devil who, at fifteen, served as Jesse Rickey’s sole assistant in the mechanical end of the *Wigwam* office, looked up from his case rack as Sabra entered.
“It’s all right, Mis’ Cravat. I got the head all set up like you said. ‘Vice Gets Death Blow. Reign of Scarlet Woman Ends. Judge Issues Ban.’ Even if Jesse don’t﻿—even if he ain’t﻿—why, you and me can set up the story this afternoon so we can start the press goin’ for Thursday. We ain’t been late with the paper yet, have we?”
“Out on time every Thursday for five years,” Sabra said, almost defiantly.
Suddenly, sharp and clear, Yancey’s voice calling her from the office porch, from the front office, from the print-shop doorway; urgent, perturbed. “Sabra! Sabra! Sabra!”
He strode into the back shop. She faced him. Instinctively she knew.

===Chinese===

“也为莽骑兵！”
“也为阿拉斯加！”
皮靴咚咚地踏过报馆的木地板。他们裹着那卡其军装的高大身影一拥而去。他回头朝她挥挥帽子。“马上回来，亲爱的。”人走了。
萨布拉转向孩子们。西姆和唐娜，两个都让这不寻常的热闹烧得满脸通红；收不住了。她的脸摆出那种安静决断的神情。“半个上午没了。可我还是 要你们上学去。行了，别闹了！你们在这儿待着也没用。报纸得出。杰西今天剩下的大半天指望不上了。这还看不出来。我给你们老师写个条。……快走吧。我得上法院去。”
她其实已经打定主意，这一天要怎么开始的就怎么过完。迪克茜·李的案子沸沸扬扬闹了几个星期，今天上午——此刻此刻——就要见分晓。再不赶紧就要迟到了。她不能因为这个人——这个陌生人一时兴起大步闯进她生活一天，就让几个月的辛劳付诸流水。
她别上帽子，看一眼手袋里铅笔纸都在，匆匆走进后面那间印刷、排版、印压三合一的屋子。关于杰西·里基她没料错。那个一贯没正形的，这会儿正把他那只熟惯的胳膊肘支在“阳光西南”酒馆擦得锃亮的吧台上，跟着干杯——敬归来的浪子，或者敬将要离去的英雄，或者敬半热带的战争，或者敬阿拉斯加的雪“——或者天知道什么！”萨布拉在心里下了结论。
克利夫·米恩斯，那个满脸油墨的印刷学徒，十五岁，是《维格沃姆报》馆机械方面杰西·里基唯一的帮手，萨布拉进来时，他从活字架上抬起头。
“没事，克拉瓦特太太。标题我照您说的全排好了。《恶行遭致命一击 红衣女人的统治到头 法官颁下禁令》。就算杰西不——就算他没——嘷，您跟我下午也能把正文排出来，星期四就能开印。咱们报纸还没误过期呢，对不对？”
“五年，期期星期四准时出报，”萨布拉说，几乎带着挑战。
突然，又急又清楚，扬西的声音在喊她——从报馆门廊，从前厅，从印刷间门口；急迫，慌乱。“萨布拉！萨布拉！萨布拉！”
他大步走进后面的工房。她迎着他。她本能地知道了。

===Original===

“What’s this about Dixie Lee?” His news-trained eye leaped to the form. He read the setup head, upside down, expertly. “When’s this case come up?”
“Now.”
“Who’s defending her?”
“Nobody in town would touch the case. They say she got a lawyer from Denver. He didn’t show up. He knew better than to take her money.”
“Prosecuting?”
“Pat Leary.”
Without a word he turned. She caught him at the door, gripped his arm. “Where are you going?”
“Court.”
“What for? What for?” But she knew. She actually interposed her body between him and the street door then, as though physically to prevent him from going. Her face was white. Her eyes stared enormous.
“You can’t take the case of that woman.”
“Why not?”
“Because you can’t. Because I’ve been fighting her. Because the *Wigwam* has come out against all that she stands for.”
“Why, Sabra, honey, where are you thinking of sending her?”
“Away. Away from Osage.”
“But where?”
“I don’t know. I don’t care. Things have changed since you went away. Went away and left me.”
“Nothing’s changed. It’s all the same. Dixie’s been stoned in the market place for two thousand years and more. Driving her out is not going to do it. You’ve got to drive the devil out of﻿—”
“Yancey Cravat, are you preaching to me? You who left your wife and children to starve, for all you cared! And now you come back and you take this creature’s part against every respectable woman in Osage﻿—against me!”
“I know it. I can’t help it, Sabra.”
“I’ll tell you what I think,” cried Sabra﻿—the Sabra Cravat who had been evolved in the past five years. “I think you’re crazy! They’ve all said so. And now I know they are right.”
“Maybe so.”
“If you dare to think of disgracing me by defending her. And your children. I’ve fought her for months in the paper. A miserable creature like that! Your own wife﻿—a laughing stock﻿—for a﻿—a﻿—”
“The Territory’s rotten. But, by God, every citizen’s still got the legal right to fight for existence!” He put her gently aside.
She went mad. She became a wildcat. She tried to hold him. She beat herself against him. It was like an infuriated sparrow hurling itself upon a mastodon. “If you dare! Why did you come back? I hate you. What’s she to you? I say you won’t. I’d rather see you dead. I’d kill you first. That scum! That filth! That harlot!”
Her dignity was gone.

===Chinese===

“迪克茜·李这是怎么回事？”他那双新闻行家的眼睛一下跳到版台上。他把排好的标题倒着读了出来，熟练之至。“这案子什么时候开庭？”
“就现在。”
“谁给她辩护？”
“镇上没人肯沾这案子。说是从丹佛请了个律师。人没来。他没那么傻，收她的钱。”
“控方呢？”
“帕特·利里。”
他一言不发，转身就走。她在门口追上他，抓住他的胳膊。“你去哪儿？”
“法院。”
“去干什么？去干什么？”可她知道。她竟真的把身子横在他和临街的门之间，仿佛要凭血肉之躯拦住他。她的脸白了。眼睛瞪得老大。
“你不能接那女人的案子。”
“为什么不能？”
“因为你就是不能。因为我一直在跟她斗。因为《维格沃姆报》公开反对她所代表的一切。”
“嘷，萨布拉，亲爱的，你想把她发落到哪儿去呢？”
“走。离开奥塞奇。”
“去哪儿呢？”
“我不知道。我也不管。你走以后，世道变了。一走了之，扔下我。”
“什么都没变。还是老样子。迪克茜这样的人在集市上被人扔石头，扔了两千多年了。把她赶走，赶不走那个。你得把魔鬼从——”
“扬西·克拉瓦特，你这是在给我布道？你这个人，老婆孩子饿不饿死都不管！现在回来了，倒替这号货色撑腰，跟奥塞奇每一个正经女人作对——跟我作对！”
“我知道。我由不得自己，萨布拉。”
“我告诉你我怎么想，”萨布拉嚷道——这五年里炼出来的那个萨布拉·克拉瓦特。“我看你疯了！他们全这么说。如今我知道他们说得对。”
“也许吧。”
“你要敢想替她辩护丢我的脸。还有你的孩子。我在报上跟这可怜虫斗了好几个月！这种下贱东西！让你自己的老婆——沦为一笑柄——为了一个——一个——”
“这领地烂透了。可是，老天在上，每一个公民都还有为生存而战的法律权利！”他轻轻把她拨到一边。
她疯了。她成了一只野猫。她想拽住他。她拿自己往他身上撞。像一只暴怒的麻雀扑向一头乳齿象。“你敢！你回来干什么？我恨你。她算你什么人？我说不行就不行。我宁愿看你死。我先杀了你。那渣滓！那脏东西！那娼妇！”
她的体面荡然无存。

===Original===

He lifted her, scratching, kicking, clawing, set her gently down in the chair in front of her desk. The screen slammed. His quick, light step across the porch, down the stair. Crumpled, tearstained, wild as she was, and with her hat on one side she reached automatically for her pencil, a pad of copy paper, and wrote a new head. Vice Again Triumphs Over Justice. Then, with what composure she could summon, she sped down the dusty road to where the combination jail and courthouse﻿—a crude wooden building﻿—sat broiling in the sun.
Because of the notoriety of the defendant the inadequate little courtroom would have been crowded enough in any case. But the news of Yancey’s abrupt departure from the Sunny Southwest Saloon﻿—and the reason for it﻿—had spread from house to house through the little town with the rapidity of a forest fire leaping from tree to tree. Mad Yancey Cravat’s latest freak. Men left their offices, their stores; women their cooking, their cleaning. The courtroom, stifling, fly infested, baked by the morning sun, was packed beyond endurance. The crowd perched on the window sills, stood on boxes outside the windows, suffocated in the doorway, squatted on the floor. The jury so hastily assembled, Pat Leary in a solemn suit of black, Dixie Lee with her girls, even Judge Sipes himself seemed in momentary danger of being trampled by the milling mob. It was a travesty of a courtroom. The Judge nervously champing his cud of tobacco, the corners of his mouth stained brown; Pat Leary neat, tight, representing law and order in his glittering celluloid collar; Dixie Lee, with a sense of the dramatic, all in black, her white cheeks unrouged, her dark abundant hair in neat smooth bands under the prim brim of her toque. But her girls were in full panoply of plumes. It was rather exhilarating to see them in that assemblage of drab respectability.
The jury was a hard-faced lot for the most part. Plucked from the plains or the hills; halting of speech, slow of mind, quick on the trigger. Two or three in overalls; one or two in the unaccustomed discomfort of store clothes. The rest in the conventional boots, corduroys or jeans, and rough shirt. A slow, rhythmic motion of the jaw was evidence that a generous preliminary bite of plug served as a precaution to soothe the nerves and steady the judgment.

===Chinese===

他把她抱起来——她又抓又踢又挠——轻轻放到她书桌前的椅子上。纱门砰地一声。他那轻快的脚步穿过门廊，下了台阶。她衣衫皱乱，泪痕满面，头发帽子都歪了，手却下意识地伸向铅笔和一叠稿纸，写下一个新标题。《恶行再度战胜正义》。然后，竭力镇定着，她顺着尘土飞扬的路快步奔向那座监狱兼法院——一栋粗陋的木楼——正趴在太阳底下捱烤。
单说被告的名气，那间不顶用的小法庭本来就够挤的了。可扬西突然撇下“阳光西南”酒馆——以及为什么——的消息，像林火从这棵树跳上那棵树，眨眼传遍了小镇家家户户。疯子扬西·克拉瓦特又出新花样了。男人们掠下事务所、掠下铺子；女人们掠下炉灶、掠下扫帚。法庭里又闷、苍蝇又多、上午的太阳烤着，人挤得水泄不通。人群蹲在窗台上，站到窗外的木箱上，堵在门口透不过气，索性席地而坐。仓促凑起来的陪审团，一身肃穆黑西装的帕特·利里，带着姑娘们的迪克茜·李，连斯皮斯（Sipes）法官本人，一时都像有被乱拱乱撞的人群踩倒的危险。这简直是对法庭的一场戏仿。法官紧张地嚼着嘴里的烟草，嘴角染成褐色；帕特·利里整洁、紧巴，戴一只亮闪闪的赛璐璐领子，代表着法律与秩序；迪克茜·李很有戏剧感，一身黑，白脸不施脂粉，浓密的黑发在女帽规矩的帽檐下梳得平平整整。可她的姑娘们却是翎毛全副武装。在那一屋子灰扑扑的体面人中看见她们，倒真叫人精神一振。
陪审团多半是一张张硬面孔。从平原上、从山地里拽来的；说话讷讷，脑子慢，扳机快。两三个穿工装裤；一两个穿成衣，浑身不自在。其余都是寻常的靴子、灯芯绒或劳动布，粗布衬衫。下巴一慢一慢有节奏地动着，可见事先狠狠咬下的一大口嚼烟正在起作用——安神，定断。

===Original===

This legal farce had already begun before Yancey made his spectacular entrance.

===Chinese===

这场法律闹剧，在扬西那轰动的一步踏进来之前，就已经开场了。

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

“Case of the Territory of Oklahoma versus Dixie Lee!” (So they had made it a Territorial case.﻿ ﻿…) “Counsel for the Territory of Oklahoma!” Pat Leary stood up. “… for the defense.” No one. The close-packed courtroom was a nightmare of staring eyes and fish-like mouths greedily devouring Dixie Lee’s white, ravaged face. Oddly enough, compared to these, she seemed pure, aloof, exquisite. “The defendant having failed to provide herself with counsel, it is my duty, according to the laws of the gover’ment of the United States and the Territory of Oklahoma to appoint counsel for the defendant.” He shifted his quid, the while his cunning, red-rimmed eyes roved solemnly through the crowd seeking the shyster, Gwin Larkin. A stir in the close-packed crowd; a murmur. “I hereby appoint﻿—” The murmur swelled. “Order in the court!”
“Your Honor!”
Towering above the crowd, forging his way through like some relentless force of nature, came the great buffalo head, the romantic Rough Rider hat with its turned-up brim caught by the crossed sabers; the massive khaki-clad figure. It was dramatic, it was melodramatic, it was ridiculous. It was superb. The fish faces turned their staring eyes and their gasping mouths away from the white-faced woman and upon him. Here was the kind of situation that the Southwest loved and craved; here was action, here was blood-and-thunder, here was adventure. Here, in a word, was Cimarron.
He stood before the shoddy judge. He swept off his hat with a gesture that invested it with plumes. “If it please Your Honor, I represent the defendant, Dixie Lee.”
No Territorial judge, denying Yancey Cravat, would have dared to face that crowd. He cast another glance round﻿—a helpless, baffled one, this time﻿—waved the approaching Gwin Larkin back with a feeble gesture, and prepared to proceed with the case according to the laws of the Territory. Certainly the look that he turned on Sabra Cravat as she entered a scant ten minutes later, white faced, resolute, and took her place as representative of the press, was one of such mingled bewilderment and reproach as would have embarrassed anyone less utterly preoccupied than the editor and publisher of the *Oklahoma Wigwam*.
Objection on the part of the slick Pat Leary. Overruled, perforce, by the Judge. A shout from the crowd. Order! Bang! Another shout.

===Chinese===

“俄克拉何马领地诉迪克茜·李案！”（这么说他们把它办成了领地级的案子。……）“俄克拉何马领地之公诉人！”帕特·利里站了起来。“……辩方。”没有人。挤得密不透风的法庭活像一场噩梦：无数瞪着的眼睛、鱼一样一开一合的嘴，贪婪地吞咬着迪克茜·李那张惨白的、被毁损的脸。说来奇怪，同这些脸比起来，她倒显得纯净、高傲、精致。“被告未能自行延请辩护人，依照美利坚合众国政府及俄克拉何马领地之法律，本庭有责为被告指定辩护人。”他嘴里倒换着嚼烟，那双狡黠的红边眼睛煞有介事地在人群里逡巡，找那个讼棍格温·拉金（Gwin Larkin）。人群里一阵骚动；一阵嘀咕。“本庭兹指定——”嘀咕声涨起来。“法庭肃静！”
“法官阁下！”
高过人群一头，像某种势不可挡的自然力排开众人，那颗大野牛头过来了，浪漫的莽骑兵帽，掀起一边的帽檐别着交叉马刀；那高大的一身卡其。这很戏剧，这很闹剧，这很可笑。这妙极了。那些鱼脸把瞪着的眼睛和喘息的嘴从那白脸女人身上挪开，转向他。这正是西南部热爱渴盼的那路场面；这里有动作，有血与雷，有冒险。一句话，这里有西马龙。
他站到那个蹩脚法官面前。他一挥手摘下帽子，那手势凭空给帽子插上了翎毛。“如蒙法官俯允，本人为被告迪克茜·李之辩护人。”
领地的法官要驳回扬西·克拉瓦特，是不敢面对那样一群人的。他又环视一圈——这回是无可奈何、不知所措的一圈——有气无力地一挥手，让正走上前来的格温·拉金退回去，然后预备按领地的法律继续审案。十分钟后萨布拉·克拉瓦特进来，脸色苍白，神色决绝，以报界代表身份落座；法官投向她的那一眼，又惶惑又责备，那样的眼神，除了心无旁骛的《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》发行人兼主编，谁看了都要窘迫。
圆滑的帕特·利里提出异议。法官不得已，驳回。人群一声喤叫。肃静！砰！又是一声喤叫。

===Original===

Law in a lawless community not yet ten years old; a community made up, for the most part, of people whose very presence there meant impatience with the old order, defiance of the conventions. Ten minutes earlier they had been all for the cocky little Leary, erstwhile station agent; eager to cast the first stone at the woman in the temple. Now, with the inexplicable fickleness of the mob, the electric current of sympathy flowed out from them to the woman to be tried, to the man who would defend her. Hot and swift and plenty of action﻿—that was the way the Southwest liked its justice.
Pat Leary. Irish, ambitious, fiery. His temper, none too even at best, had been lost before he ever rose. The thought of Yancey ahead of him, the purity brigade behind him, spurred him to his frantic, his disorderly charge.
His years as section hand on the railroad had equipped him with a vocabulary well suited to scourge this woman in black who sat so quietly, so white faced, before him, for all the crowd to see. Adjective on adjective; vituperation; words which are considered obscenity outside the Bible and the courtroom.
“… all the vicious influences, your Honor, with which our glorious Territory is infested, can be laid at this woman’s leprous door.﻿ ﻿… A refuge for the evil, for the diseased, for the criminal﻿ ﻿… waxed fat and sleek in her foul trade, on the money that should have been spent to help build up, to ennoble this fair Southwest land of ours﻿ ﻿… scavenger﻿ ﻿… vilest of humans﻿ ﻿… disgrace to the fair name of woman.﻿ ﻿…” Names, then, that writhed from his tongue like snakes.
A curious embarrassment seized the crowd. There were many in the packed room who had known the easy hospitality of Dixie’s ménage; who had eaten at her board, who had been broken in Grat Gotch’s gambling place and had borrowed money from Dixie to save themselves from rough frontier revenge. She had plied her trade and taken the town’s money and given it out again with the other merchants of the town. The banker could testify to that; the mayor; this committee; that committee. Put Dixie Lee’s name down for a thousand. Part of the order of that disorderly, haphazard town.
Names. Names. Names. The dull red of resentment deepened the natural red of their sunburned faces. The jurors shifted in their places. A low mutter, ominous, like a growl, sounded its distant thunder. Blunt. Sharp. Ruthless.

===Chinese===

法律，落在一个立起还不足十年、无法无天的社会里；这个社会，多数人的落脚此地本身就意味着对旧秩序的不耐、对常规的藐视。十分钟前，他们还全站在那个神气活现的小个子利里一边——从前他是火车站站长——巴不得往圣殿里的女人扔头一块石头。此刻，怀着乌合之众那说不清的善变，同情的电流又从他们身上涌向那受审的女人、涌向那要为她辩护的男人。火辣、迅猛、好戏连台——西南部喜欢的司法，就是这个味儿。
帕特·利里。爱尔兰人，有野心，火性子。他那脾气，最好的时候也平不了，还没起身就先丢了。想到前面压着个扬西，后面催着纯洁旅，他一口气冲进了他那疯狂而失序的控诉。
铁路上养路工那些年，给他攒下了一肚子词汇，正够用来鞭笞眼前这个黑衣女人——她那样安静、那样脸白地坐在他面前，任全镇人看。形容词擦着形容词；罵骂；还有那些字眼——出了《圣经》和法庭，人们就算作淫词的话。
“……毒害我们光荣领地的一切恶势力，法官阁下，都可以记在这个女人那烂疮般的门上。……她是恶人的渊薮，病者的巢穴，罪徒的窝藏……靠她那肮脏行当养得脑满肠肥，花的是本该用来建设、用来教化我们这片美好西南土地的钱……食腐的秃鹫……人渣中的人渣……玷污女人这个美好名声的败类。……”接着是一个个的骂名，从他舌头上蛇一样扭出来。
一阵古怪的难堪攏住了人群。这满屋子的人里，许多领受过迪克茜家的自在款待；在她桌上吃过饭；在格拉特·戈奇的赌场里输光了，又跟迪克茜借钱，躲开了边疆粗暴的私刑。她做她的营生，收进镇上的钱，又跟镇上别的商人一样把钱花出去。这个银行家可以作证；市长可以；这个委员会可以，那个委员会也可以。给迪克茜·李记上一千块的捐。这本是那个杂乱无章的小镇秩序的一部分。
骂名。骂名。骂名。愤慊的暗红加深了他们那张风吹日晒脸膛上本来的红。陪审员们在座位上挪动起来。一阵低低的嘀咕，不祥，像兽的低吼，滚过远雷。钝。利。狠。

===Original===

Younger than Yancey, less experienced, he still should have known better. These men of the inadequate jury, these men in the courtroom crowd, had come of a frontier background, had lived in the frontier atmosphere. In their rough youth, and now, women were scarce, with the scarcity that the hard life predicated. And because they were scarce they were precious. No woman so plain, so hard, so undesirable that she did not take on, by the very fact of her sex, a value far beyond her deserts. The attitude of a whole nation had been touched by this sentimental fact which was, after all, largely geographic. For a full century the countries of Europe, bewildered by it, unable to account for it, had laughed at this adolescent reverence of the American man for the American woman.
Here was Pat Leary, jumping excitedly about, mouthing execrations, when he himself, working on the railroads ten years before, had married an Indian girl out of the scarcity of girls in the Oklahoma country. Out of the corner of his eye, as he harangued, he saw the great lolling figure of Yancey Cravat. The huge head was sunk on the breast; the eyelids were lowered. Beaten, Pat Leary thought. Defeated, and he knows it. Cravat, the windbag, the wife deserter. He finished in a burst of oratory so ruthless, so brutal that he had the satisfaction of seeing the painful, unaccustomed red surge thickly over Dixie Lee’s pale face from her brow down to where the ladylike white turnover of her high collar met the line of her throat.
The pompous little Irishman seated himself, chest out, head high, eye roving the crowd and the bench, lips open with self-satisfaction. A few more cases like this and maybe they’d see there was material for a Territory governor right here in Osage.
The crowd shifted, murmured, gabbled. Yancey still sat sunk in his chair as though lost in thought. The gabble rose, soared. “He’s given it up,” thought Sabra, exulting. “He sees how it is.”
The eyes of the crowd so close packed in that suffocating little courtroom were concentrated on the inert figure lolling so limply in its chair. Perhaps they were going to be cheated of their show after all.
Slowly the big head lifted, the powerful shoulders straightened, he rose, he seemed to rise endlessly, he walked to Judge Sipes’s crude desk with his light, graceful stride. The lids were still cast down over the lightning eyes.

===Chinese===

他比扬西年轻，阅历也浅，本也该更明白些。那班不顶事的陪审员，法庭里的这班人，都是边疆生边疆长的，浸在边疆的空气里。在他们粗粝的青年时代，直到如今，女人都缺，缺得跟苦日子成正比。因为缺，所以金贵。再平庸、再粗粝、再不讨人喜欢的女人，单凭她是个女人，就平添了远超她本身的价值。一整个民族的态度都被这个感伤的事实熏染过——这事实，说到底，多半是地理造成的。整整一个世纪，欧洲各国被它弄得莫名其妙、无从解释，只好笑话美国男人对美国女人这种少男式的崇敬。
眼前这位帕特·利里，兴奋得上蹿下跳，满嘴咒骂——其实他本人十年前在铁路上做工时，就因为俄克拉何马地方姑娘稀缺，娶了个印第安姑娘。他振臂控诉的当口，眼角瞥见扬西·克拉瓦特那懒懒后仰的大身架。那颗大脑袋垂在胸前；眼皮耷拉着。败了，帕特·利里想。输了，他自己也知道。克拉瓦特，那个空谈家，那个抛妻的负心汉。他的收尾一阵滔滔雄辩，狠到无状、毒到蛮横，他于是称心如意地看见，一层痛楚的、不惯有的红潮，从迪克茜·李的额头一路涌下来，直涌到那高领翻边的淑女白领口贴上脖颈的地方。
这个神气活现的小个子爱尔兰人落了座，胸脯挺着，头昂着，眼扫着人群和法官席，嘴刮着，一肚子得意。这样的案子再多办几桩，也许他们就能看出，奥塞奇本地就有当领地州长的料。
人群挪动着，嘀咕着，嘈嘈切切。扬西还陷在椅子里，仿佛沉入了深思。嘈杂声涨起来，升上去。“他认输了，”萨布拉想，心里发着胜利的快。“他看明白形势了。”
挤在那间窒息的小法庭里的人群，千百只眼睛都钉在那瘫软在椅子里的不动身形上。莫非他们这场好戏终究要落空。
那颗大脑袋缓缓抬起来，那副有力的肩膀缓缓撑直，他站起身——他仿佛起个没完——迈着轻盈优雅的步子，走到斯皮斯法官那张粗陋的案前。眼皮依旧罩着那双闪电似的眼睛。

===Original===

He stood a moment, that singularly sweet and winning smile wreathing his lips. He began to speak. The vibrant voice, after Leary’s shouts, was so low pitched that the crowd held its breath in order to hear.
“Your Honor. Gentlemen of the Jury. I am the first to bow to achievement. Recognition where recognition is due﻿—this, gentlemen, has ever been my way. May I, then, before I begin my poor plea in defense of this lady, my client, most respectfully call your attention to that which, in my humble opinion, has never before been achieved, much less duplicated, in the whole of the Southwest. Turn your eye to the figure which has so recently and so deservedly held your attention. Gaze once more upon him. Regard him well. You will not look upon his like again. For, gentlemen, in my opinion this gifted person, Mr. Patrick Leary, is the only man in the Oklahoma Territory﻿—in the Indian Territory﻿—in the whole of the brilliant and glorious Southwest﻿—nay, I may even go so far as to say the only man in this magnificent country, the United States of America!﻿—of whom it actually can be said that he is able to strut sitting down.”
The puffed little figure in the chair collapsed, then bounded to its feet, red faced, gesticulating. “Your Honor! I object!”
But the rest was lost in the gigantic roar of the delighted crowd.
“Go it, Yancey!”
“That’s the stuff, Cimarron!”
Here was what they had come for. Doggone, there was nobody like him, damn if they was!
Even today, though more than a quarter of a century has gone by, there still are people in Oklahoma who have kept a copy, typed neatly now from records made by hand, of the speech made that day by Yancey Cravat in defense of the town woman, Dixie Lee. “Yancey Cravat’s Plea for a Fallen Woman,” it is called; and never was speech more sentimental, windy, false, and utterly moving. The slang words “hokum” and “bunk” were not then in use, but even had they been they never would have been applied, by that appreciative crowd, at least, to the flowery and impassioned oratory of the Southwest Silver Tongue, Yancey Cravat.
Cheap, melodramatic, gorgeous, impassioned. A quart of whisky in him; an enthralled audience behind him; a white-faced woman with hopeless eyes to spur him on; the cry of his wronged and righteous wife still sounding in his ears﻿—Booth himself, in his heyday, never gave a more brilliant, a more false performance.

===Chinese===

他站了片刻，唇边漾起那一丝特有的甜润动人的笑。他开口了。那锿锵的嗓音，接在利里的咆哮之后，压得那么低，人群屏住呼吸才能听见。
“法官阁下。陪审团的先生们。我素来第一个向成就鞠躬。该承认处便承认——先生们，这向来是我的为人。那么，在我开始为这位女士、我的当事人那可怜的申辩之前，可否容我敬请诸位注意一件，依敝人浅见，整个西南部前所未见、更遑论复制的成就。请诸位把目光投向方才那样当之无愧地吸引了诸位的那个人物。再看他一眼。仔细看他。像他这样的，你们再不会见到了。因为，先生们，依我看，这位有才华的人士，帕特里克·利里先生，是俄克拉何马领地——印第安领地——整个辉煌光荣的西南部——不，我甚至可以说，是这个了不起的国家、美利坚合众国——唯一一个当真能够坐着昂首阔步的人。”
椅子上那个鼓着气的小身个瘪了下去，随即又弹起来，涨红着脸，直比划。“法官阁下！我抗议！”
可后半句淹没在人群欣喜的巨吼里了。
“说得好，扬西！”
“就是这个味儿，西马龙！”
他们要的就是这个。他娘的，没人比得上他，比得上才怪！
直到今天，四分之一个多世纪过去了，俄克拉何马仍有人保存着一份当年扬西·克拉瓦特为镇上的女人迪克茜·李辩护那篇演说的抄本——如今已从手抄誊成了整整齐齐的打字稿。题目叫《扬西·克拉瓦特为一个堕落女人的请求》；再没有哪篇演说比它更滥情、更空洞、更虚假，也更动人了。“煽情”“扯淡”这两个俚词当时还没造出来，可就算造出来了，至少那班有欣赏力的听众，也绝不会把它们用在“西南部银舌头”扬西·克拉瓦特这篇辞藻华丽、慷慨激昂的雄辩上。
廉价，闹剧，华丽，激昂。一夸特威士忌在肚里；一群如痴如醉的听众在身后；一个白脸绝望的女人在眼前鞭策着他；他那受了委屈、占着理的妻子的哭喊还在耳边——就是布斯本人在鼎盛年代，也没演过比这更精彩、也更虚假的一场戏。

===Original===

“Your Honor! Gentlemen of the Jury! You have heard with what cruelty the prosecution has referred to the sins of this woman, as if her condition was of her own preference. A dreadful﻿—a vicious﻿—a revolting picture has been painted for you of her life and surroundings. Tell me﻿—tell me﻿—do you really think that she willingly embraced a life so repellent, so horrible? No, gentlemen! A thousand times, no! This girl was bred in such luxury, such refinement, as few of us have known. And just as the young girl was budding into womanhood, cruel fate snatched all this from her, bereft her of her dear ones, took from her, one by one, with a terrible and fierce rapidity, those upon whom she had come to look for love and support. And then in that moment of darkest terror and loneliness, came one of our sex, gentlemen. A wolf in sheep’s clothing. A fiend in the guise of a human. False promises. Lies. Deceit so palpable that it would have deceived no one but a young girl as innocent, as pure, as starry eyed as was this woman you now see white and trembling before you. One of our sex was the author of her ruin, more to blame than she. What could be more pathetic than the spectacle she presents? An immortal soul in ruin. The star of purity, once glittering on her girlish brow, has set its seal, and forever. A moment ago you heard her reviled, in the lowest terms a man can employ toward a woman, for the depths to which she has sunk, for the company she keeps, for the life she leads. Yet where can she go that her sin does not pursue her? You would drive her out. But where? Gentlemen, the very promises of God are denied her. Who was it said, ‘Come unto me all ye that are heavy laden, and I will give you rest’? She is indeed heavy laden, this trampled flower of the South, but if at this instant she were to kneel down before us all and confess her Redeemer, where is the Church that would receive her, where the community that would take her in? Scorn and mockery would greet her; those she met of her own sex would gather their skirts the more closely to avoid the pollution of her touch. Our sex wrecked her once pure life. Her own sex shrinks from her as from a pestilence. Society has reared its relentless walls against her. Only in the friendly shelter of the grave can her betrayed and broken heart ever find the Redeemer’s promised rest.

===Chinese===

“法官阁下！陪审团的先生们！诸位已经听到，控方何等残忍地提起这女人的罪，仿佛她的境遇是她自己的拣选。他们为诸位描画了她生活和周遭的一幅图画——可怕的——恶毒的——叫人作呕的。告诉我——告诉我——诸位当真以为，她是心甘情愿抱上这样一种可憎、这样可怕的生活的吗？不，先生们！一千个不！这姑娘生养在那样奢华、那样文雅的环境里，我们中间鲜有人见识过。而正当这少女含苞欲放，残酷的命运把这一切从她手里夺走了，夺走了她的至亲，以可怕而凶猛的速度，把她倚靠爱怜倚靠扶持的人，一个一个从她身边带走。而就在那最黑暗的恐怖与孤独里，先生们，来了我们男性中的一个。一只披着羊皮的狼。一个顶着人形的恶魔。虚假的许诺。谎言。那骗局其实浅露得很，骗不了任何人——除了一个像你们眼前这面色苍白、浑身发抖的女人当年那样天真、那样纯洁、那样满眼星光的少女。毁掉她的，是我们男性中的一个，他比她更有罪。还有比她此刻这景象更可怜的吗？一个不朽的灵魂，毁掉了。纯洁之星，曾在她少女的额头上闪亮，如今沉落了，永远沉落了。方才诸位听到，人们用一个男人对一个女人所能用的最下流的话辱骂她，骂她堕落的深渊，骂她交往的人，骂她过的日子。可是天下之大，哪里有她去了而罪不追着她的地方？你们要赶她走。赶到哪里去？先生们，连上帝的应许于她都是落空的。是谁说过，‘凡劳苦担重担的人，可以到我这里来，我就使你们得安息’？她的担子实在沉重，这朵被践踏的南方之花，可是倘若此刻她当着我们众人跪下，认她的救主，哪一座教堂肯接纳她？哪一处乡邻肯收留她？迎接她的只有讥嘲和哄笑；她的同性迎面遇见她，只会把裙子拢得更紧，生怕沾上她的秽气。我们男性毁了她原本纯洁的一生。她的同性避她如避瘟疫。社会朝她砌起了无情的墙。只有在坟墓那友好的荫庇里，她这颗被辜负、被碾碎的心，才能寻得救主应许的安息。

===Original===

The gentleman who so eloquently spoke before me told you of her assumed names, of her sins, of her habits. He never, for all his eloquence, told you of her sorrows, her agonies, her hopes, her despairs. But I could tell you. I could tell you of the desperate day﻿—the red-letter day in the banner of the great Oklahoma country﻿—when she tried to win a home for herself where she could live in decency and quiet.﻿ ﻿… When the remembered voices of father and mother and sisters and brothers fall like music on her erring ears﻿ ﻿… who shall tell what this heavy heart, sinful though it may seem to you and to me﻿ ﻿… understanding, pity, help, like music on her erring soul﻿ ﻿… oh, gentlemen﻿ ﻿… gentlemen﻿ ﻿…”
But by this time the gentlemen, between emotion and tobacco juice, were having such difficulty with their Adam’s apples as to make a wholesale strangling seem inevitable. The beautiful flexible voice went on, the hands wove their enchantment, the eyes held you in their spell. The pompous figure of little Pat Leary shrank, dwindled, disappeared before their mind’s eye. The harlot Dixie Lee, in her black, became a woman romantic, piteous, appealing. Sabra Cravat, her pencil flying over her paper, thought grimly:
“It isn’t true. Don’t believe him. He is wrong. He has always been wrong. For fifteen years he has always been wrong. Don’t believe him. I shall have to print this. How lovely his voice is. It’s like a knife in my heart. I mustn’t look at his eyes. His hands﻿—what was that he said?﻿—I must keep my mind on﻿ ﻿… music on her erring soul﻿ ﻿… oh, my love﻿ ﻿… I ought to hate him﻿ ﻿… I do hate him.﻿ ﻿…”
Dixie Lee’s head drooped on her ravaged breast. Even her plumed satellites had the wit to languish like crushed lilies and to wipe their eyes with filmy handkerchiefs the while they sniffled audibly.
It was finished. Yancey walked to his seat, sat as before, the great buffalo head lowered, the lids closed over the compelling eyes, the beautiful hands folded, relaxed.
The good men and true of the jury filed solemnly out through the crowd that made way for them. As solemnly they crossed the dusty road and repaired to draw at the roadside, where they squatted on such bits of rock or board as came to hand. Solemnly, briefly, and with utter disregard of its legal aspect, they discussed the case﻿—if their inarticulate monosyllables could be termed discussion.

===Chinese===

刚才在我之前发言的那位先生口才真好，他告诉了诸位她用过的假名、她的罪、她的习惯。可是任凭他口若悬河，他始终没有告诉诸位她的悲苦、她的煎熬、她的盼望、她的绝望。我却能告诉诸位。我能告诉诸位那个绝望的日子——伟大的俄克拉何马地方那面大旗上红字标出的日子——那天她曾想为自己捾一个家，一个能过体面安稳日子的家。……当父亲母亲兄弟姐妹的声音，像音乐一样，落在她迷途的耳中……谁能说得清，这颗沉重的心，在你们在我看来纵然有罪……理解，怜悯，援手，像音乐一样落在她迷途的灵魂上……哦，先生们……先生们……”
可到这时，诸位先生被激动的情绪和满嘴的烟草汁夹攻，喉结上下滚动得那样艰难，眼看要集体噎住。那美丽而富于变化的嗓音继续着，那双手编织着魅惑，那双眼睛把人定在咒里。小个子帕特·利里那神气活现的身影，在他们心目中缩小了，萎缩了，消失了。娼妓迪克茜·李，穿着她的黑衣裳，变成了一个传奇的、哀婉的、楚楚动人的女人。萨布拉·克拉瓦特，铅笔在纸上飞着，冷冷地想：
“这不是真的。别信他。他错了。他一向是错的。十五年了，他一向是错的。别信他。这篇我还得登出去。他的声音多好听。像一把刀扎在我心上。我不能看他的眼睛。他的手——他方才说什么来着？——我得把心思放在……音乐一样落在她迷途的灵魂上……哦，我的人……我该恨他……我恨他。……”
迪克茜·李的头垂到她那憔悴的胸前。连她那班插翎毛的卫星们也聪明地做出残花败柳的憔悴样，用薄纱手绢揩着眼角，还煞有介事地抽着鼻子。
完了。扬西走回座位，像先前那样坐下，那颗大野牛头低着，眼皮罩住那双摄人的眼睛，漂亮的双手交叠着，松弛下来。
陪审团那些善良正直的先生们鱼贯而出，人群肃然让开一条路。他们又那样肃然地穿过尘土路，到路边去商量，蹲在随手捡来的石块或木板上。肃然地、简短地、全然不顾其法律层面地讨论着案情——如果他们那几个含混的单音节字也算讨论的话。

===Original===

The courtroom throng, scattering for refreshment, had barely time to down its drink before the jury stamped heavily across the road and into the noisome courtroom.
“… find the defendant, Dixie Lee, not guilty.”

===Chinese===

法庭里的人四散去打尖，酒还没来得及下肚，陪审团已咚咚地踏过马路，回到那间浊气熏天的法庭。
“……裁定被告迪克茜·李，无罪。”

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

It was as though Osage and the whole Oklahoma country now stopped and took a deep breath. Well it might. Just ahead of it, all unknown, waited years of such clangor and strife as would make the past years seem uneventful in comparison. Ever since the day of the Run, more than fifteen years ago, it had been racing helter-skelter, devil take the hindmost; shooting into the air, prancing and yelping out of sheer vitality and cussedness. A rough roof over its head; coarse food on its table; a horse to ride; a burning drink to toss down its throat; border justice; gyp water; a girl to hug; mud roads to the edge of the sunbaked prairie, and thereafter no road; grab what you need; fight for what you want﻿—the men who had come to the wilderness of the Oklahoma country had expected no more than this; and this they had got. A man’s country it seemed to be, ruled by men for men. The women allowed them to think so. The word feminism was unknown to the Sabra Cravats, the Mrs. Wyatts, the Mrs. Hefners, the Mesdames Turket and Folsom and Sipes. Prim, good women and courageous, banded together by their goodness and by their common resolve to tame the wilderness. Their power was the more tremendous because they did not know they had it. They never once said, during those fifteen years, “We women will do this. We women will change that.” Quietly, indomitably, relentlessly, without even a furtive glance of understanding exchanged between them, but secure in their common knowledge of the sentimental American male, they went ahead with their plans.
The Philomathean Club. The Twentieth Century Culture Club. The Eastern Star. The Daughters of Rebekah. The Venus Lodge.
“Ha-ha!” and “Ho-ho!” roared their menfolk. “What do you girls do at these meetings of yours? Swap cooking receipts and dress patterns?”
“Oh, yes. And we talk.”
“I bet you do. Say, you don’t have to tell any man that. Talk! Time about ten of you women folks start gabblin’ together I bet you get the whole Territory settled﻿—politics, Injuns, land fights, and all.”
“Just about.”
Yancey had come home from the Spanish-American War a hero. Other men from Osage had been in the Philippines. One had even died there (dysentery and ptomaine from bad tinned beef). But Yancey was the town’s Rough Rider. He had charged up San Juan Hill with Roosevelt.

===Chinese===

此刻的奥塞奇和整个俄克拉何马地方，仿佛停下来深深吸了一口气。也确实该歇口气了。就在前头，它全然不知的地方，等着它的年月，喧嚣与争斗之烈，将使过去的岁月相形之下显得平安无事。自打十五年前抢地那一天起，它就一直撒着欢儿狂奔，落后者遭殃；凭着一身的元气和无赖劲儿蹦着跳着、叫着喊着。头上一顶粗陋的屋顶；桌上一餐粗食；胯下一匹马；喉咙里灌一口烧酒；边疆的公道；石膏碱水；怀里搂一个姑娘；泥路修到烈日草原边上，再往前便没有路；要什么，抢；想什么，争——到俄克拉何马这片荒野来的人们，指望的本就不过如此；而他们得到的也正是如此。这看起来是一片男人的土地，男人统治、为男人统治。女人们由他们这么想。“女权主义”这个词，萨布拉·克拉瓦特们、怀亚特太太们、赫夫纳太太们、特基特太太福尔索姆太太斯皮斯太太们，都不认识。规矩的好女人，勇敢的女人，因着各自的好、因着一个共同的驯服荒野的决心，结成一伙。她们的力量恰恰因为不自知而愈发可怕。那十五年里，她们从没说过一句“我们女人要做成这个。我们女人要改变那个”。悄悄地，不屈不挠地，不留情面地，连心照不宣的一瞥都不必交换，只凭着对美国感伤男人的一致了解，她们按着各自的计划稳步推进。
菲洛马西安俱乐部。二十世纪文化俱乐部。东方之星会。丽贝卡之女会。维纳斯分会。
“哈哈！”“呵呵！”她们的男人嚷道。“你们这帮丫头在那些会上干什么？换烹调方子和衣裳样子？”
“哦，是啊。还聊天。”
“这个我信。嘷，这话不用告诉任何男人。聊天！你们娘儿们十个凑一块儿开聊，我看整个领地的事都让你们聊定了——政治啦，印第安人啦，争地啦，全套。”
“差不多吧。”
扬西从美西战争回来，成了英雄。奥塞奇也有别的人去过菲律宾。有一个还死在了那儿（坏罐头牛肉吃出来的病疾和尸碱中毒）。可扬西是全镇的莽骑兵。他跟着罗斯福冲上过圣胡安山。

===Original===

Osage, knowing Yancey and never having seen Roosevelt, assumed that Yancey Cravat﻿—the Southwest Cimarron﻿—had led the way, an ivory- and silver-mounted six-shooter in either hand, the great buffalo head lowered with such menace that the little brown men had fled to their jungles in terror.
His return had been the occasion for such a celebration as the town had never known and never would know again, they assured each other, between drinks, until the day when statehood should come to the Territory. He returned a captain, unwounded, but thin and yellow, with the livery look that confirmed the stories one had heard of putrid food, typhoid, dysentery, and mosquitoes more deadly, in this semitropical country, than bullets or cannon.
Poisoned and enfeebled though he was, his return seemed to energize the crude little town. Wherever he might be he lived in a swirl of events that drew into its eddy all that came within its radius. Hi, Yancey! Hi, Clint! He shed the khaki and the cocked hat and actually appeared again in the familiar white sombrero, Prince Albert, and high-heeled boots. Osage breathed a sigh of satisfaction. His dereliction was forgiven, the rumors about him forgotten﻿—or allowed to subside, at least. Again the editorial columns of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* blazed with hyperbole.
It was hard for Sabra to take second place (or to appear to take second place) in the office of the *Wigwam*. She had so long ruled there alone. Her word had been law to the wavering Jesse Rickey and to the worshiping Cliff Means. And now to say, “You’d better ask Mr. Cravat.”
“He says leave it to you. He’s went out.”
Yancey did a good deal of going out. Sabra, after all, still did most of the work of the paper without having the satisfaction of dictating its policy. A linotype machine, that talented iron monster, now chattered and chittered and clanked in the composing room of the *Wigwam*. It was the first of its kind in the Oklahoma country. Very costly and uncannily human, Sabra never quite got over her fear of it. The long arm reached down with such leisurely assurance, snatched its handful of metal, carried it over, descended, dropped it. It opened its capacious maw to be fed bars of silvery lead which it spat forth again in the shape of neat cakes of type. Its keys were like grinning teeth. It grunted, shivered, clumped, spoke﻿—or nearly.

奥塞奇认识扬西，却没见过罗斯福，于是认定是扬西·克拉瓦特——西南部的西马龙——一路打头，双手各握一支象牙柄银箍的六响枪，那颗大野牛头低垂得杀气腾腾，吓得小个子棕色人逃回丛林里去了。
他回来那阵，全镇大操大办了一场，那样的盛况镇上没见识过，往后也不会再有了——他们一杯接一杯地彼此保证——直到领地建州的那天为止。他回来时是个上尉，没挂彩，又瘦又黄，一副肝火虚损、蜡黄蜡黄的脸色，印证了人们听说的那些传闻：腐烂的伙食、伤寒、病疾，还有在这半热带地方比枪弹炮弹更要命的蚊子。
尽管中了毒、亏了元气，他的归来却仿佛给这个粗陋的小镇充了电。不论他人在哪儿，身边总卷着一团事件的风，凡进这风暴半径的，无不被卷进漩涡。嗨，扬西！嗨，克林特！他脱下卡其和歪帽，当真又穿起了那身熟悉的白阔边帽、长礼服、高跟马靴。奥塞奇满意地舒了口气。他的荒唐被宽恕了，关于他的流言被忘了——至少，容它平息了。《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的社论栏又烧起了夸张的火。
在《维格沃姆报》馆里屈居第二（或者显得屈居第二），对萨布拉是件难受的事。她独自当家了那么久。她的话，对摇摆的杰西·里基是法，对崇拜她的克利夫·米恩斯更是法。可如今只落得说一句：“你最好问克拉瓦特先生去。”
“他说交给你。他出去了。”
扬西出去的时候多。说到底，报纸的活儿大半还是萨布拉干，却没有定方针的痛快。一台莱诺铸排机，那台有才华的铁怪物，如今在《维格沃姆报》的排字间里喳喳、嗒嗒、哐当响着。整个俄克拉何马地方它是头一台。价钱昂贵，又像人像得瘆人，萨布拉始终没能不怕它。那条长臂从容笃定地探下来，抓起一把铅字，举过去，落下，撒下。它张开宽大的口，喂进去一条条银亮的铅，再吐出来，已成一块块齐整的活字版。它的键盘像越来越笑的牙。它哼哧，哆嗦，咚咚地走，说话——或者差不多说出来。

===Original===

“I never come near it,” Sabra once admitted, “that I don’t expect the thing to reach down with its iron arm and clap me on the shoulder and clatter, ‘Hello, Sabra!’ ”
She was proud of the linotype machine, for it had been her five years at the head of the *Wigwam* that had made it possible. It was she who had gone out after job printing contracts; who had educated the local merchants to the value of advertising. Certainly Yancey, prancing and prating, had never given a thought to these substantial foundations on which the entire business success of the paper rested. They now got out with ease the daily *Wigwam* for the Osage townspeople and the weekly for county subscribers. Passing the windows of the *Wigwam* office on Pawhuska Avenue you could hear the thump and rattle of the iron monster. Between them Jesse Rickey and Cliff Means ran the linotype. Often they labored far into the night on job work, and the late passerby would see the little light burning in the printing shop and hear the rattle and thump of the machine. In a pinch Sabra herself could run it. Yancey never went near it, and, strangely enough, young Cim had a horror of it, as he had of most things mechanical. After one attempt at the keyboard, during which he had hopelessly jammed the machine’s delicate insides, he was forbidden ever to go near it again. For that matter, Cim had little enough taste for the newspaper business. He pied type at the case rack. He had no news sense. He had neither his father’s gift for mingling with people and winning their confidence nor his mother’s more orderly materialistic mind. He had much of Yancey Cravat’s charm, and something of the vagueness of his grandfather, old Lewis Venable (dead these two years), but combining the worst features of both.
“Stop dreaming!” Sabra said to him, often and often. “What are you dreaming about?”
She had grown to love the atmosphere of the newspaper office and resented the boy’s indifference to it. She loved the very smell of it﻿—the mixed odor of hot metal, printer’s ink, dust, white paper, acid, corncob pipe, and cats.
“Stop dreaming!” Yancey hearing her thus admonishing Cim, whirled on her in one of his rare moments of utter rage. “God a’mighty, Sabra! That’s what Ann Hathaway said to Shakespeare. Don’t you women know that ‘Dreams grow holy put in action; work grows fair through starry dreaming’? Leave the boy alone!

“我每回走到它跟前，”萨布拉有一次承认，“都等着那东西伸出铁胳膊拍我的肩膀，吧嗒吧嗒地说：‘你好啊，萨布拉！’”
她为这台莱诺铸排机骄傲，因为正是她主掌《维格沃姆报》的那五年才置得起它。是她一家家去攽零活印刷的合同；是她把本地商人教得懂得广告的价值。至于扬西，蹦跶归蹦跶，高谈归高谈，报纸生意全部成功所系的这些坚实根基，他从来没往心里去过。如今他们轻松地出着日报《维格沃姆报》，给奥塞奇镇民；出着周报，给县里的订户。走过波哈斯卡大街《维格沃姆报》馆的窗前，你听得见那铁怪物的哐哐咚咚。杰西·里基和克利夫·米恩斯两人轮着开莱诺机。他们常常为了一批零活干到深夜，晚归的过路人会看见印刷间里亮着一星小灯，听见机器哐哐咚咚。紧要关头，萨布拉自己也能开。扬西从不走近它；说来奇怪，小西姆还怕它，像怕大多数机器玩意儿一样。他在键盘上试过一回，把机器精密的五脏堵了个死，从此便被禁绝靠近。说起来，西姆对报馆这一行本也没多大兴致。他在活字架前尽排乱字。他没有新闻嗅觉。他既没有父亲那种周旋于人、取信于人的天分，也没有母亲那颗更有条理的、实打实的头脑。他有不少扬西·克拉瓦特式的魅力，又有点外公老刘易斯·维纳布尔（已故两年）那种心不在焉，只是取了两人各差的一面。
“别发呆了！”萨布拉常常常常对他说。“你发什么呆呢？”
“别发呆了！”扬西听见她这样训西姆，一时罕见地大怒，朝她劈头转过来。“老天爷，萨布拉！安·哈撒韦对莎士比亚说的就是这话。你们这些女人难道不懂‘梦想付诸行动便成神圣；劳作因星光之梦而臻美好’？别管这孩子！让他做梦！让他做梦！”
“一家有一个做星星梦的就够了，”萨布拉尖刻地回敬。

===Original===

Let him dream! Let him dream!”
“One starry dreamer in a family is enough,” Sabra retorted, tartly.
Five years had gone by﻿—six years since Yancey’s return. Yet, strangely enough, Sabra never had a feeling of security. She never forgot what he had said about Wichita. “Almost five years in one place. That’s the longest stretch I’ve ever done, honey.” Five years. And this was well into the sixth. He had plunged head first into the statehood fight, into the Indian Territory situation. The anti-Indian faction was bitterly opposed to the plan for combining the Oklahoma Territory and the Indian Territory under the single state of Oklahoma. Their slogan was “The White Man’s State for the White Man.”
“Who brought the Indian here to the Oklahoma country in the first place?” shouted Yancey in the editorial columns of the *Wigwam*. “White men. They hounded them from Missouri to Arkansas, from Arkansas to southern Kansas, then to northern Kansas, to northern Oklahoma, to southern Oklahoma. You white men sold them the piece of arid and barren land on which they now live in squalor and misery. It isn’t fit for a white man to live on, or the Indians wouldn’t be living on it now. Deprived of their tribal laws, deprived of their tribal rites, herded together in stockades like wild animals, robbed, cheated, kicked, hounded from place to place, give them the protection of the country that has taken their country away from them. Give them at least the right to become citizens of the state of Oklahoma.”
He was obsessed by it. He traveled to Washington in the hope of lobbying for it, and made quite a stir in that formal capital with his white sombrero, his Prince Albert, his Texas star boots, his great buffalo head, his charm, his grace, his manner. Roosevelt was characteristically cordial to his old campaign comrade. Washington ladies were captivated by the flowery speeches of this romantic, this storybook swaggerer out of the Southwest.
It was rumored on good authority that he was to be appointed the next Governor of the Oklahoma Territory.
“Oh, Yancey,” Sabra said, “do be careful. Governor of the Territory! It would mean so much. It would help Cim in the future. Donna, too. Their father a governor.” She thought, “Perhaps everything will be all right now. Perhaps all that I’ve gone through in the last ten years will be worth it, now. Perhaps it was for this. He’ll settle down.

===Chinese===

又五年过去了——扬西回来后已六年。然而奇怪的是，萨布拉从没有过安稳感。她从没忘掉他说威奇托的那句话。“在一个地方差不多待了五年。这是我干过的最长的刑期了，亲爱的。”五年。而这一回已进入第六个年头。他一头扎进了建州之争，扎进了印第安领地的局面。反印第安人的一派，死命反对把俄克拉何马领地同印第安领地合成一个俄克拉何马州的方案。他们的口号是：“白人的州归白人。”
“当初是谁把印第安人弄到俄克拉何马这地方来的？”扬西在《维格沃姆报》的社论栏里呐喊。“是白人。白人把他们从密苏里捾到阿肯色，从阿肯色捾到南堪萨斯，又捾到北堪萨斯，捾到北俄克拉何马，捾到南俄克拉何马。是你们白人卖给他们那块干旱贫瘠的地，如今他们就在那上面过脏日子、熬苦日子。那地连白人都住不得——要不然现在住在那上面的怎么是印第安人。部族的法律被剥夺了，部族的仪典被剥夺了，像野牲口一样圈在栅栏里，被抢，被骗，被踢，被一处一处地捾——那么，就请把那个夺走他们家园的国家的保护，给他们吧。至少，给他们做俄克拉何马州公民的权利。”
他为这事入了魔。他跑到华盛顿去游说，凭着他那顶白阔边帽、长礼服、得克萨斯星靴，那颗大野牛头，那股魅力、那份风度、那副气派，在那个一本正经的首都闹出不小的动静。罗斯福对这位老战友热诚得一如既往，很有他的本色。华盛顿的太太们则被这个浪漫人物、这个从西南部走出来的传奇故事里的招摇人物的锦绣演说迷住了。
有可靠的消息说，他要被任命为下一任俄克拉何马领地总督了。
“哦，扬西，”萨布拉说，“千万小心行事。领地总督！这意义太大了。对西姆的前程有帮助。唐娜也是。他们的父亲是一位总督。”她想：“也许现在一切都会好起来了。也许我这十年吃的苦，如今都值了。也许就是为了这个。他会安顿下来了。”

===Original===

﻿ ﻿… Mamma can’t say now﻿ ﻿… and all the Venables and the Vians and the Goforths and the Greenwoods.﻿ ﻿…” She had had to endure their pity, even from a distance, all these years.
The rumor took on substance. My husband, Yancey Cravat, Governor of the Territory of Oklahoma. And then, when statehood came, as it must in the next few years, perhaps Governor of the state of Oklahoma. Why not!
At which point Yancey blasted any possibility of his appointment to the governorship by hurling a red-hot editorial into the columns of the *Wigwam*. The gist of it was that the hundreds of thousands of Indians now living on reservations throughout the United States should be allowed to live where they pleased, at liberty. The whites of the Oklahoma Territory and the Indian Territory, with an Indian population of about one hundred and twenty thousand of various tribes﻿—Poncas, Cherokees, Chickasaws, Creeks, Osages, Kiowas, Comanches, Kaws, Choctaws, Seminoles, and a score of others﻿—read, emitted a roar of rage, and brandishing the paper ran screaming into the streets, cursing the name of Yancey Cravat.
Sabra had caught the editorial in the wet proof sheet. Her eye leaped down its lines.

> “Herded like sheep in a corral﻿—no, like wild animals in a cage﻿—they are left to rot on their reservations by a government that has taken first their land, then their self-respect, then their liberty from them. The land of the free! When the very people who first dwelt on it are prisoners! Slaves, but slaves deprived of the solace of work. What hope have they, what ambition, what object in living! Their spirit is broken. Their pride is gone. Slothful, yes. Why not? Each month he receives his dole, his pittance. Look at the Osage Nation, now dwindled to a wretched two thousand souls. The men are still handsome, strong, vital; the women beautiful, dignified, often intelligent. Yet there they huddle in their miserable shanties like beaten animals eating the food that is thrown them by a great﻿—a munificent﻿—government. The government of these United States! Let them be free. Let the Red Man live a free man as the White Man lives.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“……妈妈这会儿可没话说了……还有维纳布尔家、维安家、戈福思家、格林伍德家的一大家子。……”这些年，他们的怜悯她一直得受着，哪怕隔着几百里。
流言有了实体。我的丈夫，扬西·克拉瓦特，俄克拉何马领地总督。往后，等建州到来——不出几年必定要来——也许就是俄克拉何马州州长。有何不可！
偏在这当口，扬西把一篇烧得通红的社论捧进《维格沃姆报》的版面，把他任总督的任何可能炸了个粉碎。要旨是：如今住在全国各保留地的成千上万印第安人，应当获准自由地、爱住哪儿住哪儿。俄克拉何马领地和印第安领地的白人们——这两块地方有大约十二万印第安人，分属许多部族：波尼、切罗基、奇克索、克里克、奥塞奇、基奥瓦、科曼奇、卡乌、乔克托、塞米诺尔，还有二十来个别的——读了，发出一声怒吼，挥舞着报纸尖叫着冲上街头，咒骂扬西·克拉瓦特的名字。
萨布拉在湿漉漉的校样上撞见了这篇社论。她的眼睛顺着字行急急而下。

> “像圈栏里的羊一样被圈着——不，像笼子里的野兽一样——任由一个政府把他们丢在保留地上烂掉；这个政府先夺了他们的土地，再夺了他们的自尊，又夺了他们的自由。自由的国土！而最早住在国土上的那些人竟是囚徒！是奴隶，还是被剥夺了劳作慰藉的奴隶。他们还有什么指望，什么抱负，活着还有什么目标！他们的精神垮了。他们的骄傲没了。说他们懒，是懒。能不懒吗？每个月他领他的贩济，他那一点施舍。看看奥塞奇部族吧，如今凋零到只剩可怜的两千人。男人依旧俊美、强壮、有生气；女人美丽、端庄，常常还有智慧。可他们就在那儿缩在凄惨的棚屋里，像捾了打的牲口，吃着那个伟大的——慷慨的——政府扔给它们的食物。这美利坚合众国的政府！放他们自由吧。让红种人像白种人一样，做个自由人。……”

===Original===

Much that he wrote was true, perhaps. Yet the plight of the Indian was not as pitiable as Yancey painted it. He cast over them the glamour of his own romantic nature. The truth was that they themselves cared little﻿—except a few of their tribal leaders, more intelligent than the rest. They hunted a little, fished, slept, visited from tribe to tribe, the Poncas visiting the Osages, the Osages the Poncas, gossiping, eating, holding powwows. The men were great poker players, having learned the game from the white man, and spent hours at it.
They passed through the town of Osage in their brilliant striped blankets, sometimes walking, sometimes on sorry nags, sometimes in rickety wagons laden with pots, poles, rags, papooses, hounds. The townspeople hastily removed such articles as might please the pilfering fancy.
Sabra picked up the proof sheet, still damp from the press, and walked into Yancey’s office. Her face was white, set.
“You’re going to run this, Yancey?”
“Yes.”
“You’ll never be Governor of the Territory.”
“Never.”
She stood a moment, her face working. She crushed the galley proof in her hand so that her knuckles stood out, white.
“I’ve forgiven you many, many things, God knows, in the last ten years. I’ll never forgive you for this. Never.”
“Yes, you will, honey. Never is a long time. Not while I’m alive, maybe. But some day, a long time from now﻿—though not so very long, maybe﻿—you’ll be able to turn back to the old files of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* and lift this editorial of mine right out of it, word for word, and run it as your own.”
“Never.﻿ ﻿… Donna﻿ ﻿… Cim﻿ ﻿…”
“I can’t live my children’s lives for them, Sabra, honey. They’ve got to live their own. I believe what I believe. This town is rotten﻿—the Territory﻿—the whole country. Rotten.”
“You’re a fine one to say what is or isn’t rotten. You with your whisky and your Indians and your women. I despise you. So does everyone in the town﻿—in the Territory.”
“ ‘A prophet is not without honor, save in his own country and in his own home.’ ” A trifle sonorously.
She never really knew whether he had done this thing with the very purpose of making his governorship impossible. It was like him.
Curiously enough, the editorial, while it maddened the white population of the Territory, gained the paper many readers. The *Wigwam* prospered. Osage blossomed.

===Chinese===

他写的这些，或许大半是真的。然而印第安人的境况并不像扬西画的那么可怜。他把自己浪漫天性的光华罩在了他们身上。实情是，他们自己倒不大在乎——除了他们几个部族头人，比旁人明白些的。他们打一点猎，捕一点鱼，睡觉，互相走访，波尼人串奥塞奇人，奥塞奇人串波尼人，闲话，吃喝，开帕瓦。男人们是扑克高手——这把戏是跟白人学的——一坐就是几个钟头。
他们披着艳丽的条纹毯子穿过奥塞奇镇，有时步行，有时骑着一匹匹可怜的驽马，有时坐着摇摇欲散的大车，车上堆着锅、杆子、破布、娃娃和猎狗。镇民们慌忙把那些可能招小手的东西收进屋去。
萨布拉拿起那页还带着机器潮气的校样，走进扬西的办公室。她脸白着，绷着。
“你要登这篇，扬西？”
“登。”
“你就永远当不上领地总督了。”
“永远当不上。”
她站了片刻，脸一阵阵地抽。她把长条校样攥在手里，攥得指节发白。
“上帝知道，这十年里我饫了你多少多少事。这一件我永远不饫你。永远。”
“会的，亲爱的。‘永远’可是个漫长的词。我在世时未必，也许。可是将来有一天，隔上好些年——也许也没那么久——你会翻开《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的旧合订本，把我这篇社论原封不动、一字不差地拤出来，署上你自己的名字登出去。”
“永远不。……唐娜……西姆……”
“儿女的日子我替他们过不了，萨布拉，亲爱的。日子得他们自己过。我信我信的东西。这个镇子烂了——领地——整个国家。烂了。”
“你说什么烂不烂，亏你说得出口。凭你那威士忌、那印第安人、那些女人。我看不起你。全镇的人都看不起——全领地。”
“‘大凡先知，除了本地本家之外，没有不被人尊敬的。’”他说得有点锿锵。
她始终没能真的弄清，他做这件事，是不是恰恰存心断了自己的总督路。很像他的为人。
说来奇怪，这篇社论虽然激怒了领地的白人，却给报纸添了许许多多订户。《维格沃姆报》兴旺了。奥塞奇开花了。

===Original===

The town was still rough, crude, wide open, even dangerous. But it began to take on an aspect of permanence. It was no longer a camp; it was a town. It began to build schools, churches, halls. Arkansas Grat’s gambling tent had long ago been replaced by a solid wooden structure, just as gambling terms of the West and the Southwest had slowly been incorporated into the language of daily use. I’m keeping cases on him﻿ ﻿… standing pat﻿ ﻿… bluffing﻿ ﻿… bucking the tiger. Terms filched from the gaming table; poker and faro and keno.
Sol Levy’s store﻿—the Levy Mercantile Company﻿—had two waxen ladies in the window, their features only slightly affected by the burning Southwest sun. Yancey boomed Sol Levy for mayor of Osage, but he never had a chance. It was remarkable how the *Oklahoma Wigwam* persisted, though its position in most public questions was violently unpopular. Perhaps it, like Yancey, had a vitality and a charm that no one could withstand.
Although Sol Levy was still the town Jew, respected, prosperous, the town had never quite absorbed this Oriental. A citizen of years’ standing, he still was a stranger. He mingled little with his fellow townsmen outside business hours. He lived lonesomely at the Bixby House and ate the notoriously bad meals served by Mrs. Bixby. He was shy of the town women though the Women of the Town found him kindly, passionate, and generous. The business men liked him. They put him on committees. Occasionally Sabra or some other woman who knew him well enough would say, half playfully, half seriously, “Why don’t you get married, Sol? A nice fellow like you. You’d make some girl happy.”
Sometimes he thought vaguely of going to Wichita or Kansas City or even Chicago to meet some nice Jewish girl there, but he never did. It never entered his head to marry a Gentile. The social life of the town was almost unknown to him. Sometimes if a big local organization﻿—the Elks, the Odd Fellows, the Sons of the Southwest﻿—gave a benefit dance, you would glimpse him briefly, in the early part of the evening, standing shyly against the wall or leaning half hidden in the doorway, a darkling, remote, curiously Oriental figure in the midst of these robust red-faced plainsmen and ex-cowmen.
“Come on, Sol, mix in! Grab off one of the girls and get to dancin’, why don’t you? What you scairt of?” But Sol remained aloof.

===Chinese===

镇子仍然粗野、糙陋、门户大开，甚至危险。但它开始有了长久打算的模样。它不再是营盘；它是镇子了。它开始盖学校、教堂、会堂。阿肯色格拉特的赌帐篷早已换成了一座结结实实的木建筑，正如西部和西南部的赌桌术语慢慢渗进了日常用语。我盯着他呢……牌不变……吓唬……斗老虎。全是从赌桌上偷来的词；扑克、法罗、基诺。
索尔·利维的铺子——利维商贸公司——橱窗里立着两位蜡人小姐，眉眼只让西南部的毒日轻微地伤了一点。扬西敲锣打鼓地捧索尔·利维竞选奥塞奇镇长，可他从来没有机会。《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》居然立得住，倒也奇了——它在大多数公共问题上都站在极不得人心的一边。也许它跟扬西一样，有一股没人顶得住的生气和魅力。
索尔·利维虽然还是镇上的犹太人，受人尊敬，家道殷实，镇子却始终没能消化这个东方人。多年老市民了，他仍然是个外乡人。下了班他极少同镇民来往。他孤零零地住在比克斯比旅馆，吃着比克斯比太太那出了名糟糕的伙食。他怕镇上的女人们，虽然“镇上的那些女人”倒觉得他厚道、热心、大方。商人们喜欢他。他们拉他进委员会。偶尔萨布拉或哪个跟他够熟的女人会半打趣半当真地说：“你怎么不娶个媳妇呢，索尔？你这么个好男人。准能让哪个姑娘幸福。”
有时他也模模糊糊想过，去威奇托、堪萨斯城甚至芝加哥，见见那里哪位犹太好姑娘，可他从来没去。娶个外教女人，这念头就没进过他的脑子。镇上的社交生活他几乎一无所知。有时本地哪个大组织——麋鹿会、互助会、西南部之子会——办一场募捐舞会，你会短暂地瞥见他，在晚会起头的时候，膞腆地靠墙站着，或半个身子藏在门框里，在这帮红脸膛、身板壮实的平原汉子和前牛仔中间，一个阴翳的、疏远的、说不清多么东方的身影。
“来呀，索尔，凑热闹呀！随便拽个姑娘跳起来，怎么不跳？你怕什么？”可索尔始终冷在一边。

===Original===

He regarded the hot, sweaty, shouting dancers with a kind of interested bewilderment and wonder, much as the dancers themselves sometimes watched the Indians during one of the Festival Dances on the outlying reservations. On occasion he made himself politely agreeable to a stout matron well past middle age. They looked up at his tragic dark eyes; they noticed his slim ivory hand as it passed them a plate of cake or a cup of coffee. “He’s real nice when you get to know him,” they said. “For a Jew, that is.”
Between him and Yancey there existed a deep sympathy and understanding. Yancey campaigned for Sol Levy in the mayoralty race﻿—if a thing so one-sided could be called a race. The *Wigwam* extolled him.

> “Sol Levy, the genial proprietor of the Levy Mercantile Company, is the Wigwam’s candidate for mayor. It behooves the people of Osage to do honor to one of its pioneer citizens whose career, since its early days, has been marked by industry, prosperity, generosity. He comes of a race of dreamers and doers.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

他看着那些热汗淋漓、喊叫着的舞客，带着一种饶有兴味的惶惑与惊叹，倒像那些舞客有时看着外围保留地节庆舞蹈上的印第安人。偶尔他也会彬彬有礼地应酬一位早过了中年的壮硕主妇。她们抬头望望他那双忧郁的黑眼睛；注意到他递蛋糕盘或咖啡杯时那纤细的象牙色的手。“处熟了他人真好，”她们说。“就是说，对一个犹太人而言。”
他和扬西之间，存在着一种深切的相知。镇长竞选中扬西为索尔·利维奔走——如果一面倒的事情也配叫竞选的话。《维格沃姆报》把他捧上了天。

> “利维商贸公司和气生财的东家索尔·利维，即本报属意之镇长候选人。奥塞奇理应礼敬这样一位开埠元老，其创业以来，勤勉、兴旺、慷慨，有口皆碑。他出身于一个梦想家与实干家的民族。……”

===Original===

“Why, the very idea!” snorted the redoubtable virago, Mrs. Tracy Wyatt, whose husband was the opposing candidate. “A Jew for mayor of Osage! They’ll be having an Indian mayor next. Mr. Wyatt’s folks are real Americans. They helped settle Arkansas. And as for me, why, I can trace my ancestry right back to William Whipple, who was one of the signers of the Declaration of Independence.”
Sol Levy never had a chance for public honor. He, in fact, did practically nothing to further his own possible election. He seemed to regard the whole matter with a remoteness slightly tinged with ironic humor. Yancey dropped into Sol’s store to bring him this latest pronouncement of the bristling Mrs. Wyatt. Sol was busy in the back of the store, where he was helping the boy unpack a new invoice of china and lamps just received, for the Levy Mercantile Company had blossomed into a general store of parts. His head was in a barrel, and when he straightened and looked up at the towering Yancey there were bits of straw and excelsior clinging to his shirtsleeves and necktie and his black hair.
“Declaration of Independence!” he exclaimed, thoughtfully. “Tell her one of my ancestors wrote the Ten Commandments. Fella name of Moses.”
Yancey, roaring with laughter, used this in the *Wigwam*, and it naturally helped as much as anything to defeat the already defeated candidate.
Sometimes the slim, white-faced proprietor, with his friend Yancey Cravat, stood in the doorway of the store, watching the town go by. They said little. It was as though they were outsiders, looking on at a strange pageant.
“What the hell are you doing here in this town, anyway, Sol?” Yancey would say, as though musing aloud.
“And you?” Sol would retort. “A civilized barbarian.”
The town went by﻿—Indians, cowboys up from Texas, plainsmen, ranchers. They still squatted at the curb, as in the early days. They chewed tobacco and spat. The big sombrero persisted, and even the boots and spurs.
“Howdy, Yancey! Howdy, Sol! H’are you, Cim!”
There was talk of paving Pawhuska Avenue, but this did not come for years. The town actually boasted a waterworks. The *Wigwam* office still stood on Pawhuska, but it now occupied the entire house. Two years after Yancey’s return they had decided to build a home on Kihekah Street, where there actually were trees now almost ten years old.

===Chinese===

“嗬，亏想得出！”那位了不得的母老虎特雷西·怀亚特太太哼了一声，她丈夫正是对家候选人。“让犹太人当奥塞奇镇长！下回就该选个印第安人镇长了。怀亚特先生家可是地道的美国人。他们帮着开拓的阿肯色。至于我，嗬，我的家谱能一直上溯到威廉·惠普尔——《独立宣言》的签字人之一。”
公众的荣耀，索尔·利维从来就没有份。事实上，他也几乎没为那场可能的选举出过一点力。他看待整件事的态度疏远淡漠，只微微带一点冷幽默。扬西踱进索尔的铺子，把那位浑身是刺的怀亚特太太的最新高论带给他。索尔正在铺子后头忙着，帮伙计拆一箱刚到的瓷器和灯——利维商贸公司如今已出落成一家像模像样的百货铺了。他的脑袋正埋在一只桶里，等他直起身，仰头看着高塔似的扬西，衬衫袖子上、领带上、黑头发上还沾着草屑和刨花。
“《独立宣言》！”他若有所思地感叹。“告诉她，我有一位祖上写过《十诫》。一个叫摩西的家伙。”
扬西哈哈大笑，把这话登进了《维格沃姆报》，自然，这一下同别的招数加在一起，把那位早已注定落败的候选人彻底送输了。
有时，这位瘦削白脸的东家同他的朋友扬西·克拉瓦特并肩站在铺子门口，看着小镇从眼前流过。他们很少说话。倒像两个局外人，在看一场陌生的大戏。
“说到底，你到底在这镇上干什么呢，索尔？”扬西会说，像是自言自语。
“那你呢？”索尔会顶回来。“一个文明化的蛮子。”
小镇流过去——印第安人，从得克萨斯上来的牛仔，平原汉子，牧场主。他们照旧蹲在路牙子上，跟早年间一样。嚼着烟，吐着沫。大阔边帽照旧戴着，连靴子带马刺也照旧。
“好啊，扬西！好啊，索尔！你好啊，西姆！”
铺波哈斯卡大街的事也议了，可一议就是好几年。镇上居然有自来水厂可夸了。《维格沃姆报》馆还立在波哈斯卡街上，但如今占的是整栋房子。扬西回来两年后，他们决定在基赫卡街盖一处宅子，那街上如今当真有了树，都快十年了。

===Original===

Sabra had built the house as she wanted it, though at first there had been a spirited argument about this. Yancey’s idea had been, of course, ridiculous, fantastic. He said he wanted the house built in native style.
“Native! What in the world! A wickiup?”
“Well, a house in the old Southwest Indian style﻿—almost pueblo, I mean. Or Spanish, sort of, made of Oklahoma red clay﻿—plaster, maybe. Not brick. And low, with a patio where you can be out of doors and yet away from the sun. And where you can have privacy.”
Sabra made short work of that idea. Or perhaps Yancey did not persist. He withdrew his plan as suddenly as he had presented it; shrugged his great shoulders as though the house no longer interested him.
Osage built its new houses with an attached front porch gaping socially out into the street. It sat on the front porch in its shirtsleeves and kitchen apron. It called from porch to porch, “How’s your tomato plants doing? I see the Packses got out-of-town company visiting.” It didn’t in the least want privacy.
Sabra built a white frame house in the style of the day, with turrets, towers, minarets, cupolas, and scrollwork. There was a stained glass window in the hall, in purple and red and green and yellow, which, confronting the entering caller, gave him the look of being suddenly stricken with bubonic plague. There were parlor, sitting room, dining room, kitchen on the first floor; four bedrooms on the second floor, and a bathroom, actually, with a full-size bathtub, a toilet, and a marble washstand with varicose veins. In the cellar there was a hot air furnace. In the parlor were brown brocade-and-velvet settee and stuffed chairs. In the sitting room was a lamp with a leaded glass shade in the shape of a strange and bloated flower﻿—a Burbankian monstrosity, half water lily, half petunia.
“As long as we’re building and furnishing,” Sabra said, “it might as well be the best.” She had gone about planning the house, and furnishing it, with her customary energy and capability. With it all she found time to do her work on the *Wigwam*﻿—for without her the paper would have been run to the ground in six months. Osage had long since ceased to consider it queer that she, a woman, and the wife of one of its most prominent citizens, should go to work every morning like a man.

===Chinese===

房子照萨布拉的意思盖成了，虽然起初为此有过一场热闹的争执。扬西的主意，当然，荒唐，离奇。他说他要房子照本地风格盖。
“本地风格！我的天！盖个窝棚？”
“唔，一座老西南印第安样式的房子——我是说，差不多普韦布洛那样的。或者西班牙味儿的，用俄克拉何马红土——也许是抹灰泥。不用砖。矮矮的，带个院子，人在露天又晒不着日头。还能清静。”
萨布拉三下五除二把这主意打发掉了。或者，也许扬西自己没坚持。他收回他的方案，同提出时一样突然；耸了耸宽大的肩膀，仿佛这房子已引不起他的兴趣。
奥塞奇的新房子都带一个前廊，大张着口，应酬着街面。它穿着衬衫、系着围裙坐在前廊上。从这廊喊到那廊：“你家西红柿长得怎么样？我看帕克斯家来了外客。”它压根儿不要清静。
萨布拉盖了一栋时兴样式的白色木板房，带小塔、塔楼、尖顶、穹顶和卷涡花纹。门厅里有一扇紫红绿黄的彩色玻璃窗，迎面照着进门客人，把他照得像突然染上了腺鼠疫。一楼客厅、起居室、饭厅、厨房；二楼四间卧室，还有一间真正的浴室，带一只齐整的大人浴缸、一个马桶、一张“静脉曲张”纹理的大理石洗脸台。地窖里有一座热风炉。客厅里摆着棕色织锦丝绒的长靠椅和弹簧扶手椅。起居室里有一盏铅条玻璃罩的灯，灯罩是一朵臃肿的怪花——一件伯班克式的怪物，半是睡莲，半是矮牵牛。
“既然要盖要置办，”萨布拉说，“索性置最好的。”她以惯有的精力和才干，张罗着画房子、置家具。饶是这样，她还有工夫做她《维格沃姆报》的那份活——没有她，这报馆不出半年就得塔台。她一个女人，还是镇上头面人物之一的太太，每天早晨像男人一样去上班——奥塞奇早就不再觉得这事奇怪了。

===Original===

By ten every morning she had attended to her household, seen it started for the day, had planned the meals, ordered them on her way downtown, and was at her desk in the *Wigwam* office, sorting mail, reading exchanges, taking ads, covering news, writing heads, pasting up. Yancey’s contributions were brilliant but spasmodic. The necessary departmental items﻿—real estate transfers, routine court news, out-of-town district and county gleanings﻿—bored him, though he knew well that they were necessary to the success of the paper. He left these to Sabra, among many other things.
Sabra, in common with the other well-to-do housewives of the community, employed an Indian girl as a house servant. There was no other kind of help available. After her hideous experience with Arita Red Feather she had been careful to get Indian girls older, more settled, though this was difficult. She preferred Osage girls. These married young, often before they had finished their studies at the Indian school.
Ruby Big Elk had been with Sabra now for three years. A curious, big, silent girl of about twenty-two﻿—almost handsome﻿—one of six children﻿—a large family for an Osage. Sabra was somewhat taken aback, after the girl had been with her for some months, to learn that she already had been twice married.
“What became of your husbands, Ruby?”
“Died.”
She had a manner that bordered on the insolent. Sabra put it down to Indian dignity. When she walked she scuffed her feet ever so little, and this, for some inexplicable reason, seemed to add insolence to her bearing. “Oh, do lift your feet, Ruby! Don’t scuffle when you walk.” The girl made no reply. Went on scuffling. Sabra discovered that she was lame; the left leg was slightly shorter than the right. She did not limp﻿—or, rather, hid the tendency to limp by the irritating sliding sound. Her walk was straight, leisurely, measured. Sabra was terribly embarrassed; apologized to the Indian girl. The girl only looked at her and said nothing. Sabra repressed a little shiver. She had never got accustomed to the Indians.
Sabra was a bustler and a driver. As she went about the house in the morning, performing a dozen household duties before leaving for the *Wigwam* office, her quick tapping step drummed like hail on a tin roof.

===Chinese===

每天早晨十点前，她已料理完家务，安顿好一天的开头，定好饭菜，进城路上把料订好，然后坐到《维格沃姆报》馆的书桌前：分信，读交换报纸，接广告，跑新闻，写标题，拼版。扬西的贡献精彩，却时断时续。那些少不了的栏目条目——不动产过户、例行法院消息、外区外县的零星拾遗——他烦，虽然他清楚这些对报纸的成功必不可少。他把这些都丢给萨布拉，连同许多别的。
萨布拉同镇上其他家境不错的主妇一样，雇一个印第安姑娘做家务。别的帮手也雇不到。自打同阿丽塔·红羽那场可怕的经历之后，她雇人小心了，专挑年长些、稳当些的印第安姑娘，虽然这不容易。她偏爱奥塞奇姑娘。可她们嫁得早，常常印第安学校还没念完就嫁了。
鲁比·大鹿跟萨布拉已经三年了。一个古怪的、高大的、沉默的姑娘，约莫二十二岁——几乎称得上俊——六个孩子中的一个——在奥塞奇人里这算大家口。姑娘来了几个月之后，萨布拉才得知她已经嫁过两回，不免吃了一惊。
“你那两个丈夫呢，鲁比？”
“死了。”
她那做派，几乎带着傲慢。萨布拉把这归为印第安人的尊严。她走路时脚微微磨着地，而这，不知怎么，倒像更添了她的倨傲。“哎呀，抬起点脚来，鲁比！走路别磨磨磨的。”姑娘不答。照旧磨着。萨布拉后来发现她腿有毛病；左腿比右腿略短。她并不跛——或者说，用那恼人的磨地声把跛掩了过去。她走得笔直，从容，一板一眼。萨布拉窘得不行；向印第安姑娘道了歉。姑娘只看看她，什么也没说。萨布拉压下一阵小小的寒噤。她始终没能习惯印第安人。
萨布拉是个风风火火的人。早晨她在屋里转，去《维格沃姆报》馆之前办完一打家务，那急促的、笃笃的脚步，像冰雹敲着铁皮屋顶。

===Original===

It annoyed her intensely, always, to see Ruby Big Elk making up the beds with that regal manner, or moving about the kitchen with the pace and air of a Lady Macbeth. The girl’s broad, immobile face, her unspeaking eyes, her secret manner all worked a slow constant poison in Sabra. She spoke seldom; never smiled. When Sabra spoke to her about some household task she would regard her mistress with an unblinking gaze that was highly disconcerting.
“Did you understand about the grape jell, Ruby? To let it get thoroughly cool before you pour on the wax?”
Ruby would majestically incline her fine head, large, like a man’s head. The word “sinister” came into Sabra’s mind. Still, Sabra argued, she was good to the children, fed them well, never complained about the work. Sometimes﻿—on rare occasions﻿—she would dig a little pit in the back yard and build a slow hot smothered fire by some secret Indian process, and there, to the intense delight of young Cim, she would roast meats deliciously in the Indian fashion, crisp and sweet, skewered with little shafts of wood that she herself whittled down. Donna refused to touch the meat, as did Sabra. Donna shared her mother’s dislike of the Indians﻿—or perhaps she had early been impressed with her mother’s feeling about them. Sometimes Donna, the spoiled, the pampered, the imperious (every inch her grandmother Felice Venable), would feel Ruby Big Elk’s eye on her﻿—that expressionless, dead black Indian eye. Yet back of its deadness, its utter lack of expression, there still seemed to lurk a cold contempt.
“What are you staring at, Ruby?” Donna would cry, pettishly. Ruby would walk out of the room with her slow scuffling step, her body erect, her head regal, her eyes looking straight ahead. She said nothing. “Miserable squaw!” Donna would hiss under her breath. “Gives herself the airs of a princess because her greasy old father runs the tribe or something.”
Ruby’s father, Big Elk, had in fact been Chief of the Osage tribe by election for ten years, and though he no longer held this highest office, was a man much looked up to in the Osage Nation. He had sent his six children and actually his fat wife to the Indian school, but he himself steadfastly refused to speak a word of English, though he knew enough of the language. He conversed in Osage, and when necessary used an interpreter.

===Chinese===

看见鲁比·大鹿以那副帝王气派铺床，或以麦克白夫人的步子和架势在厨房里走动，萨布拉总要火冒三丈。姑娘那张宽大的、不动的脸，那双不说话的眼睛，那副讳莫如深的样子，都在萨布拉心里慢慢下着常年的毒。她少开口；从不笑。萨布拉同她交代家务时，她会一眨不眨地盯着女主人，盯得人心发毛。
“葡萄冻的事听明白了吗，鲁比？要让它彻底凉透，再浇蜡。”
鲁比会庄严地俯一下她那颗好看的头，大大的，像男人的头。“阴森”这个词浮上萨布拉的心头。不过，萨布拉替自己辩解道，她对孩子们好，喂得好，干活也从无怨言。有时——难得——她会在后院挖一个小坑，用某种印第安秘法拢一堆慢火焖烧，就在那里，把肉烤得喷香——印第安式，又脆又甜，串肉的小木签是她自己削的——馋得小西姆魂都没了。唐娜碰也不肯碰那肉，萨布拉也一样。唐娜承了她母亲对印第安人的嫌恶——或者，她打小就让母亲对印第安人的态度印上了心。有时，娇惯的、宠坏的、说一不二的唐娜（一寸一分都是她外祖母费利斯·维纳布尔），会觉出鲁比·大鹿的眼睛落在自己身上——那只无表情的、死黑的印第安人的眼睛。而在那死寂之后、那全然的空白之后，似乎仍潜伏着一丝冷冷的轻蔑。
“你看什么，鲁比？”唐娜会气恼地嚷。鲁比会迈着缓慢的、磨着地的步子走出屋去，身子笔直，头颅高贵，眼睛直视前方。一言不发。“贱婆娘！”唐娜会压着嗓子嘶嘶地骂。“仗着她那油腻的老爹管着个部族什么的，就端起公主的架子来了。”
鲁比的父亲大鹿，其实当选担任过十年奥塞奇部族的酋长，虽然如今已不在那个最高的位子上，在奥塞奇部族中仍是人人仰重的人物。他把六个孩子、连他那位胖太太都送进了印第安学校，可他自己却死活不肯说一个英文词，虽然那语言他懂得不少。他讲奥塞奇语，必要的时候用通译。

===Original===

It was a kind of stubborn Indian pride in him. It was his enduring challenge to the white man. “You have not defeated me.”
His pride did not, however, extend to more material things, and Sabra was frequently annoyed by the sight of the entire Big Elk family, the old ex-Chief, his squaw, and the five brothers and sisters, squatting in her kitchen doorway enjoying such juicy bits as Ruby saw fit to bestow upon them from the Cravat larder. When Sabra would have put a stop to this, Yancey intervened.
“He’s a wise old man. If he had a little white blood in him he’d be as great as Quanah Parker was, or Sequoyah. Everything he says is wisdom. I like to talk with him. Leave him alone.”
This did not serve to lessen Sabra’s irritation. Often she returned home to find Yancey squatting on the ground with old Big Elk, smoking and conversing in a mixture of Osage and English, for Big Elk did not refuse to understand the English language, even though he would not speak it. Yancey had some knowledge of Osage. Sabra, coming upon the two grunting and muttering and smoking and staring ahead into nothingness or (worse still) cracking some Indian joke and shaking with silent laughter, Indian fashion, was filled with fury. Nothing so maddened her.
It slowly dawned on Sabra that young Cim was always to be found lolling in the kitchen, talking to Ruby. Ruby, she discovered to her horror, was teaching Cim to speak Osage. A difficult language to the white, he seemed to have a natural aptitude for it. She came upon them, their heads close together over the kitchen table, laughing and talking and singing. Rather, Ruby Big Elk was singing a song with a curious rhythm, and (to Sabra’s ear, at least) no melody. There was a pulsation of the girl’s voice on sustained notes such as is sometimes produced on a violin when the same note is sounded several times during a single bow stroke. Cim was trying to follow the strange gutturals, slurs, and accents, his eyes fixed on Ruby’s face, his own expression utterly absorbed, rapt.
“What are you doing? What is this?”
The Indian girl’s face took on its customary expression of proud disdain. She rose. “Teach um song,” she said; which was queer, for she spoke English perfectly.
“Well, I must say, Cimarron Cravat! When you know your father is expecting you down at the office﻿—” She stopped.

===Chinese===

这是他身上一种倔强的印第安人的骄傲。这是他向白人递出的、经久不撤的挑战书。“你没有打败我。”
不过他的骄傲并不延及更实惠的事情，萨布拉便常常被搅得心烦——整个大鹿家的人，退位的老酋长，他的婆娘，还有那五个兄弟姐妹，蹲在她厨房门口，享用鲁比认为该从克拉瓦特家食柜里打发给他们的荤腥。萨布拉要拦，扬西便出面。
“这是个有智慧的老人。他身上要是有几分白人血，成就不输夸纳·帕克，不输塞阔雅。他说的话句句是道理。我喜欢同他说话。随他去。”
这对萨布拉的火气无济于事。她常常回到家，看见扬西同老鹿蹲在地上，抽着烟，用奥塞奇话掺英语聊着——因为英语大鹿虽不肯说，听却是不拒的。扬西懂些奥塞奇话。萨布拉撞见这两人咕哝着、嘟囔着、抽着烟、直勾勾望着前面的空无——或者（更糟）讲了个印第安笑话，正按印第安人的样子无声地抖着笑——她便怒不可遏。没有比这更叫她发疯的了。
萨布拉慢慢醒悟过来，小西姆总是赖在厨房里，同鲁比说话。而鲁比——她骇然发现——在教西姆说奥塞奇话。这门语言对白人很难，他却像有天生的灵性。她撞见他们两个，头凑着头伏在厨房桌上，又说又笑又唱。确切说，是鲁比·大鹿在唱一支节奏古怪的歌——至少在萨布拉耳朵里——没有旋律。姑娘的嗓音在长音上一波一波地颤，像小提琴一弓之内把同一个音拉响好几下的那种颤。西姆跟着学那些古怪的喉音、滑音和重音，眼睛盯着鲁比的脸，自己的神情全然入定，痴了。
“你们在干什么？这是什么？”
印第安姑娘的脸摆出惯常的傲慢轻蔑。她站起来。“教个歌，”她说；这就怪了，因为她英语说得地道着呢。
“嗬，真是的，西马龙·克拉瓦特！明知道你父亲在报馆等着你下去——”她停住了。

===Original===

Her quick eye had leaped to the table where lay the little round peyote disk or mescal button which is the hashish of the Indian.
She had heard about it; knew how prevalent among the Indian tribes from Nebraska down to Mexico had become the habit of eating this little buttonlike top of a Mexican cactus plant. In shape a disk about an inch and a half in diameter and a quarter of an inch thick, the mescal or peyote gave the eater a strange feeling of lightness, dispelled pain and fatigue, caused visions of marvelous beauty and grandeur. The use of it had become an Indian religious rite.
Like a fury Sabra advanced to the table, snatched up the little round button of soft green.
“Peyote!” She whirled on Cim. “What are you doing with this thing?”
Cim’s eyes were cast down sullenly. His hands in his pockets, he leaned against the wall, very limp, very bored, very infuriating and insolent.
“Ruby was just teaching me one of the Mescal Ceremony songs. Darned interesting. It’s the last song. They sing it at sunrise when they’re just about all in. Goes like this.”
To Sabra’s horror he began an eerie song as he stood there leaning against the kitchen wall, his eyes half closed.
“Stop it!” screamed Sabra. With the gesture of a tragedy queen she motioned him out of the kitchen. He obeyed with very bad grace, his going more annoying, in its manner, than his staying. Sabra followed him, silently. Suddenly she realized she hated his walk, and knew why. He walked with a queer little springing gait, on the very soles of his feet. It came over her that it always had annoyed her. She remembered that someone had laughingly told her what Pete Pitchlyn, the old Indian scout, lounging on his street corner, had said about young Cim:
“Every time I see that young Cimarron Cravat a-comin’ down the street I expect to hear a twig snap. Walks like a storybook Injun.”
In the privacy of the sitting room Sabra confronted her son, the bit of peyote still crushed in her hand.
“So you’ve come to this! I’m ashamed of you!”
“Come to what?”
She opened her hand to show the button of pulpy green crushed in her palm. “Peyote. A son of mine. I’d rather see you dead﻿—”
“Oh, for heaven’s sake, Mom, don’t get Biblical, like Dad. To hear you a person would think you’d found me drugged in a Chinese opium den.”
“I think I’d almost rather.”
“It’s nothing but a miserable little piece of cactus.

===Chinese===

她那双利眼已跳到桌上——那里躺着一小片圆的佩奥特饼，也叫麦斯卡尔纽，印第安人的大麻。
她听说过这东西；知道从内布拉斯加一直到墨西哥，各印第安部族里吃这颗小纽扣——一种墨西哥仙人掌的顶芽——已成了多普遍的习惯。形状是一枚圆片，直径约一寸半，厚约四分之一寸，麦斯卡尔也好佩奥特也好，吃下去叫人通身轻飘，驱走疼痛和疲乏，引出美妙壮丽的幻象。吃它，已成了印第安人的一种宗教仪式。
萨布拉像个复仇女神般冲到桌前，一把抓起那枚软绿色的圆纽扣。
“佩奥特！”她猛转身对西姆。“你拿这东西干什么？”
西姆阴沉着眼皮。两手插兜，靠着墙，浑身松垮，百无聊赖，那副样子气死人、狂得没边。
“鲁比刚在教我一首麦斯卡尔仪式的歌。有意思极了。是最后那首。太阳出来的时候唱，那会儿他们全都快瘫了。调调儿是这样。”
萨布拉惊恐地看着他倚着厨房墙，眯着眼睛，唱起一支鬼气森森的歌。
“住口！”萨布拉尖叫。她以悲剧女王的姿势一挥手，把他轰出厨房。他老大不情愿地照办了，那走的样子，比赖着不走还气人。萨布拉默默跟着他。忽然她意识到自己恨他走路的样子，而且明白为什么。他走路带着一种古怪的小弹跳，只用脚掌着地。她猛然想起，这走路的样子其实一直让她恼火。她记得有人笑着告诉过她，老印第安侦察兵皮特·皮奇林在他那个街角上闲靠时，说起小西姆的话：
“每回我瞧见小西马龙·克拉瓦特打街上过来，我就等着听枝子咔嚓一响。走路跟传奇故事里的印第安人一个样。”
在起居室的清静里，萨布拉当面质问儿子，手里还攢着那点捏碎的佩奥特。
“你竟落到这一步了！我替你害臊！”
“落到哪一步？”
她摊开手，给他看掌心那团捏烂的绿浆。“佩奥特。我的儿子。我宁愿看你死了——”
“哦，老天，妈，别学爸爸拿《圣经》腔说话。听你这口气，人家还当你在中国人开的鸦片馆里发现我抽大了呢。”
“我看我还真宁愿那样。”
“这不过是一小片可怜的仙人掌。

===Original===

And what was I doing but sitting in the kitchen listening to Ruby tell how her father﻿—”
“I should think a man of almost eighteen could find something better to do than sit in a kitchen in the middle of the day talking to an Indian hired girl. Where’s your pride!”
Cim’s eyes were still cast down. He still lounged insolently, his hands in his pockets. “How about these stories you’ve told me all your life about the love you Southerners had for your servants and how old Angie was like a second mother to you?”
“Niggers are different. They know their place.”
He raised the heavy eyelids then and lifted his fine head with the menacing look that she knew so well in his father. “You’re right. They are different. In the first place, Ruby isn’t an Indian hired girl. She is the daughter of an Osage chief.”
“Osage fiddlesticks! What of it!”
“Ruby Big Elk is just as important a person in the Osage Nation as Alice Roosevelt is in Washington.”
“Now, listen here, Cimarron Cravat! I’ve heard about enough. A lot of dirty Indians! Just you march yourself down to the *Wigwam* office, young man, and don’t you ever again let me catch you talking in that disrespectful manner about the daughter of the President of the United States. And if I ever hear that you’ve eaten a bite of this miserable stuff”﻿—she held out her hand, shaking a little, the mescal button crushed in her palm﻿—“I’ll have your father thrash you within an inch of your life, big as you are. As it is, he shall hear of this.”
But Yancey, on being told, only looked thoughtful and a little sad. “It’s your own fault, Sabra. You’re bound that the boy shall live the life you’ve planned for him instead of the one he wants. So he’s trying to escape into a dream life. Like the Indians. It’s all the same thing.”
“I don’t know what you’re talking about. I don’t think you know, either.”
“The Indians started to eat peyote after the whites had taken their religious and spiritual and decent physical life away from them. They had owned the plains and the prairies for centuries. The whites took those.
