他披在肩上的长发又直又硬，染成了鲜橙色，像个老滑稽剧女皇——那色彩映在那张羊皮纸似的脸上，惊人，奇诡；那脸上沟壑纵横，千道皱纹十字交叉。一只手搭在膝上。另一只手懒洋洋地挥着一把鹰羽做的巨大半月形扇子，一来一回，一来一回。两个高大的身影并肩坐着，像两尊铜铸的什么。只有眼睛在动，还有那把梦魇般的鹰羽扇，一来一回，一来一回，帝气凛然。
那两双死黑不笑的眼睛，那把摇来摇去的扇子，把萨布拉推入了无名的恐惧。“扬西！”她双唇僵硬，又喊了一声。“扬西！”
听见她声音里的恐惟，他已踏着那轻快的步子下了楼，进了屋。可一看见老鹿和他老婆，他脸上的关切换成了宽慰。他绽出他那迷人透顶的笑。
“豪！”
“豪！”大鹿嗄声道。
大鹿太太点头致意。她比她的老丈夫，也许年轻三十岁；是他的第三任妻子。她会说英语；少女时代甚至上过印第安教会学校。可是出于懒散或淡漠，她说的是那不识字印第安人的破败英语。
此刻两位又坠回木然的沉默。
“他们要什么？问他们要什么。”
扬西用奥塞奇话说了几句。大鹿回了一个单音节。
“他说什么？是什么？”
“我请他们留下同我们吃饭。他说不能。”
“也不该能。叫她说英语。她会英语。”
大鹿缓缓地转过大头，像装在机轴上，转向他那胖胖的圆脸老婆。他用本族话下了一道简短的命令。婆娘咧出一个有点怪、有点窘的笑，像个女学生——那与其说是笑，不如说是一次扭动，在他们这个种族里稀罕得比怒容更吓人。
“大鹿和我，来接你们去瓦扎赫。”
“去做什么？”萨布拉厉声问。
“四点钟大宴，大舞。你们儿子要我们来说给你们听。要你们知道，他今天早上娶了鲁比。”
她又沉默了，咧着那傻傻定住的笑。大鹿的扇子摇来摇去，摇来摇去。
“老天爷！”扬西·克拉瓦特说。他看着萨布拉，快步朝她走来，她却把他挥开。
“别。我不要——没事。”

===Original===

It was as though she shrank from his touch. She stood there, staring at the two barbaric figures staring so stonily back at her with their dead black Indian eyes. It was at times like this that the Marcy in her stood her in good stead. She came of iron stock, fit to stand the fire. Only beneath her fine dark eyes you now suddenly saw a smudge of purplish brown, as though a dirty thumb had rubbed there; and a sagging of all the muscles of her face, so that she looked wattled, lined, old.
“Don’t look like that, honey. Come. Sit down.”
Again the groping wave of her hand. “I’m all right, I tell you. Come. We must go there.”
Yancey came forward. He shook hands formally with Big Elk, with the Indian woman. Sabra, seeing him, suddenly realized that he was not displeased. She knew that no formal politeness would have prevented him from voicing his anger if this monstrous announcement had shattered him as it had her, so that her very vitals seemed to be withering within her.
“Sugar, shake hands with them, won’t you?”
“No. No.” She wet her dry lips a little with her tongue, like one in a fever. She turned, woodenly, and walked to the door, ignoring the Indians. Across the hall, slowly, like an old woman, down the porch steps, toward the shabby little car next to the big rich one. As she went she heard Yancey’s voice (was there an exultant note in it?) at the telephone.
“Jesse! Take this. Get it in. Ready!﻿ ﻿… Ex-Chief Big Elk, of the Osage Nation, and Mrs. Big Elk, living at Wazhazhe, announce the marriage of their daughter Ruby Big Elk to Cimarron Cravat, son of﻿—don’t interrupt me﻿—I’m in a hurry﻿—son of Mr. and Mrs. Yancey Cravat, of this city. The wedding was solemnized at the home of the bride’s parents and was followed by an elaborate dinner made up of many Indian and American dishes, partaken of by the parents of the bride and the groom, many relatives and numerous friends of the young﻿ ﻿…”
Sabra climbed heavily into the car and sat staring at the broad back of the car ahead of her. Chief Big Elk and his wife came out presently, unreal, bizarre in the brilliant noonday Oklahoma sunshine, ushered by Yancey. He was being charming. They heaved their ponderous bulk into the big car. Yancey got in beside Sabra. She spoke to him once only.
“I think you are glad.”
“This is Oklahoma. In a way it’s what I wanted it to be when I came here twenty years ago.

===Chinese===

她像是怕他的碰触。她站在那里，瞪着那两个野室的身影，他们也用印第安人死黑的眼睛石头似的瞪回来。每逢这种时候，她身上的马西家血就顶了用。她是铁打的门第出身，禁得起火炼。只是那双漂亮的黑眼睛底下，你此刻忽然看见一团紫褐色的阴影，像被脏拇指抹过；满脸的肌肉都往下坠，坠出皱纹，坠出老态。
“别这样，亲爱的。来。坐下。”
她的手又空虚地挥了一下。“我没事，我告诉你。走吧。我们得上那儿去。”
扬西走上前。他同大鹿、同那印第安女人一一正式握手。萨布拉看着他，忽然明白过来：他并不恼。她知道，倘若这荒唐的重雷也把他砸得粉碎——像砸得她这样，五脏六腑都像在腔子里枯萎——任何客套都拦不住他把怒火喊出来。
“甜心，同他们握个手，好吗？”
“不。不。”她用舌尖润了润干裂的嘴唇，像个发烧的人。她木然转身，朝门口走去，不理那两个印第安人。穿过门厅，慢慢地，像个老妇，下门廊台阶，走向挨着那辆豪华大车的自家那辆寒酸小车。走的时候，她听见扬西在打电话（那声音里有一丝得意？）。
“杰西！记下来。发出去。准备好了！……奥塞奇部族前酋长大鹿及夫人，现居瓦扎赫，欣然宣布其女鲁比·大鹿与西马龙·克拉瓦特之婚事，后者为——别打岔——我赶时间——为本市扬西·克拉瓦特先生及夫人之公子。婚礼于新娘父母家中隆重举行，继以盛宴，菜肴汇印第安与美利坚风味，新娘新郎双方父母暨众多亲及诸多年少友人同席……”
萨布拉沉重地爬上车，坐着，直瞪着前头那辆车的宽背。大鹿酋长和他老婆出来了，在俄克拉何马明晃晃的正午阳光里，虚幻，古怪，由扬西陪送着。他正施展着他的魅力。两个沉重的身躯把自己般进大车。扬西坐到萨布拉旁边。她只对他说了一句话。
“我看你是高兴的。”
“这里是俄克拉何马。在某种意义上，这正是我二十年前来时希望它成为的样子。

===Original===

Cim’s like your father, Lewis Venable. Weak stuff, but good stock. Ruby’s pure Indian blood and a magnificent animal. It’s hard on you now, my darling. But their children and their grandchildren are going to be such stuff as Americans are made of. You’ll see.”
“I hope I shall die before that day.”
The shabby little middle-class car followed the one whirling ahead of them over the red clay Oklahoma roads. Eating the dust of the big car just ahead.
She went through it and stood it, miraculously, until one grotesquerie proved too much for her strained nerves and broke them. But she went into the Indian house, and saw Cim sitting beside the Indian woman, and as she looked at his beautiful weak face she thought, I wish that I had never found him that day when he was lost on the prairie long ago. He came toward her, his head lowered with that familiar look, his fine eyes hidden by the lids.
“Look at me!” Sabra commanded, in the voice of Felice Venable. The boy raised his eyes. She looked at him, her face stony. Ruby Big Elk came toward her with that leisurely, insolent, scuffling step. The two women gazed at each other; rather, their looks clashed, like swords held high. They did not shake hands.
There were races, there were prizes, there was dancing. In the old Indian days the bucks had raced on foot for a prize that was a pony tethered at a distance and won by the fleetest to reach him, mount, and ride him back to the starting point. Today the prize was a magnificent motor car that stood glittering in the open field half a mile distant. Sabra thought, I am dying, I am dying. And Donna. This squaw is her sister-in-law. Miss Dignum’s on the Hudson.
Ruby’s handsome head right had bought the young couple the house just across the road from Big Elk’s﻿—a one-story red brick bungalow, substantial, ugly. They showed Sabra and Yancey through it. It was furnished complete. Mongrel Spanish furniture in the living room﻿—red plush, fringe, brass nail heads as big as twenty-dollar gold pieces. An upright piano. An oak dining-room set. A fine bathroom with heavy rich bath towels neatly hung on the racks. A shining stained oak bedroom set with a rose-colored taffeta spread. Sabra felt a wave of nausea. Cim’s face was smiling, radiant. Yancey was joking and laughing with the Indians. In the kitchen sat a white girl in a gingham dress and a kitchen apron.

“西姆像你父亲刘易斯·维纳布尔。料子软，种是好种。鲁比是纯印第安血统，一匹壮美的牲口。这会儿苦的是你，我的亲爱的。可他们的儿女、他们的孙儿，会长成美国人用的那种料子。你看着吧。”
“但愿我死在那一天之前。”
那辆寒酸的中产阶级小车，跟着前头飞驰的大车，在俄克拉何马的红土路上跑。吃着前头大车扬起的尘。
她捱过去了，也撑住了，奇迹般地——直到一件过于荒诞的事压垮了她绷紧的神经。可她还是走进了那座印第安人的房子，看见了坐在那印第安女人身边的西姆，看着他那漂亮的、软弱的脸，她想，但愿多年前他在草原上走失的那天，我从没找到过他。他朝她走来，头低垂着，带着那个熟悉的姿态，俊眼藏在眼皮底下。
“看着我！”萨布拉命令道，用的是费利斯·维纳布尔的声音。男孩抬起了眼睛。她看着他，脸是石头。鲁比·大鹿迈着那从容、傲慢、磨着地的步子朝她走来。两个女人对望；不如说，两道目光相击，像高高举起的剑。她们没有握手。
有赛跑，有奖品，有舞蹈。旧日印第安人的时代，男人们赛跑，奖品是一匹拴在远处的小马，腿最快、能跑到马跟前、翻身上马奔回起点的人赢。今天的奖品是一辆气派的汽车，停在半英里外的空场上，闪闪发光。萨布拉想，我要死了，我要死了。还有唐娜。这印第安婆娘成了她的嫂子。哈得孙河畔的迪格纳姆小姐学校。
鲁比那份可观的头权，给这对小两口买下了就在大鹿家路对面的房子——一栋单层的红砖平房，结实，丑陋。他们领着萨布拉和扬西参观了全套。家具齐全。客厅里是西班牙混血式的家具——红长毛绒，流苏，黄铜泡钉大得像二十元金币。一架立式钢琴。一套橡木饭厅家具。一间精致的浴室，架上整整齐齐挂着厚重华贵的浴巾。一套闪亮的染色橡木卧室家具，床上铺着玫瑰色塔夫绸床罩。萨布拉一阵反胃。西姆的脸笑着，放着光。扬西正同印第安人说笑。厨房里坐着一个穿格布裙、系厨房围裙的白人姑娘。

===Original===

The girl’s hair was so light a yellow as to appear almost white. Her unintelligent eyes were palest blue. Her skin was so fair as to be quite colorless. In the midst of the roomful of dark Indian faces the white face of the new Cravat hired girl seemed to swim in a hazy blob before Sabra’s eyes. But she held on. She felt Ruby’s scornful dark eyes on her. Sabra had a feeling as though she had been disemboweled and now was a hollow thing, an empty shell that moved and walked and talked.
Dinner. White servants and Negro servants to wait on them. A long table seating a score or more, and many such tables. Bowls and plates piled with food all down the length of it. Piles of crisp pork, roasted in the Indian fashion over hot embers sunk in a pit in the yard, and skewered with a sharp pointed stick. Bowls of dried corn. Great fat, black ripe olives. Tinned lobster. Chicken. Piles of dead ripe strawberries. Vast plateaus of angel-food cake covered with snow fields of icing.
Sabra went through the motions of eating. Sometimes she put a morsel into her mouth and actually swallowed it. There was a great clatter of knives and forks and dishes. Everything was eaten out of one plate. Platters and bowls were replenished. Sabra found herself seated beside Mrs. Big Elk. On her other side was Yancey. He was eating and laughing and talking. Mrs. Big Elk was being almost comically polite, solicitous. She pressed this tidbit, that dainty, on her stony guest.
Down the center of the table, at intervals, were huge bowls piled with a sort of pastry stuffed with forcemeat. It was like a great ravioli, and piles of it vanished beneath the onslaught of appreciative guests.
“For God’s sake, pretend to eat something, Sabra,” Yancey murmured, under his breath. “It’s done now. They consider it an insult. Try to eat something.”
She stirred the pastry and chopped meat that had been put on her plate.
“Good,” said Mrs. Big Elk, beside her, and pointed at the mass with one dusky maculate finger.
Sabra lifted her fork to her lips and swallowed a bit of it. It was delicious﻿—spicy, rich, appetizing. “Yes,” she said, and thought, I am being wonderful. This is killing me. “Yes, it is very good. This meat﻿—this stuffing﻿—is it chopped or ground through a grinder?”
The huge Indian woman beside her turned her expressionless gaze on Sabra. Ponderously she shook her head from side to side in negation.

===Chinese===

那姑娘的头发黄得近乎白。那双不聪明的眼睛是最淡的蓝。皮肤白得几乎没有颜色。满屋深色印第安面孔中间，克拉瓦特家新雇的白人姑娘那张白脸，在萨布拉眼前像一团模糊的光斑浮着。可她撑住了。她觉出鲁比那双轻蔑的黑眼睛落在自己身上。萨布拉有种感觉，仿佛自己已被开膛掏空，如今只剩一具空壳，会动，会走，会说话。
宴席。白人仆人和黑人仆人伺候着。一张长桌坐得下二十多人，这样的桌子还有许多张。碗碟从桌子这头摆到那头，堆满了吃食。一堆堆脆皮的猪肉，印第安式在院中坑里的炭火上烤的，用尖头木签子串着。一碗碗干玉米。一颗颗肥大熟透的黑橄榄。罐头龙虾。鸡。一堆堆熟透的草莓。一片片高地的天使蛋糕，覆着雪原一样的糖霜。
萨布拉做着吃的动作。偶尔真把一小口放进嘴里，咽了下去。刀又碗碟响成一片。所有菜都从同一个盘里吃。大碟大碗又添满。萨布拉发现自己挨着大鹿太太坐。另一边是扬西。他吃着，笑着，说着。大鹿太太客气得近乎滑稽，殷勤备至。她一样样把好菜往这位石头客人面前让。
桌子正中，每隔一段摆着大盆，盆里堆着一种肉馅酥点。像大号的意式饺子，一堆堆在答谢客人的猛攻之下消失。
“看在上帝份上，装着吃一点，萨布拉，”扬西低声说。“事已至此。他们当不吃是侮辱。多少吃一点。”
她搅了搅盘里那摊酥点和碎肉。
“好吃，”旁边的大鹿太太说，用一根深色斑点的手指点了点那堆东西。
萨布拉举起叉子送到唇边，咽下一小块。真好吃——香辛，肥润，开胃。“好吃，”她说，心里想，我真了不起。这正在杀了我。“嗯，很好吃。这肉——这馅——是剁的，还是绞肉机绞的？”
旁边那高大的印第安女人把毫无表情的目光转到萨布拉身上。沉重地把头从一边摇到另一边，表示不是。

===Original===

“Naw,” she answered, politely. “Chawed.”
The clatter of a fork dropped to the plate, a clash among the cups and saucers. Sabra Cravat had fainted.

===Chinese===

“都不是，”她客气地答道。“嚼的。”
一支叉子当啷落在盘子上，杯盘之间一阵乱响。萨布拉·克拉瓦特晕倒了。

## XXIII / 二十三
===Original===

Osage was so sophisticated that it had again become simple. The society editor of the *Oklahoma Wigwam* used almost no adjectives. In the old days, you had read that “the house was beautifully decorated with an artistic arrangement of smilax, sent from Kansas City, pink and purple asters in profusion making a bower before which the young couple stood, while in the dining room the brilliance of golden glow, scarlet salvia, and autumn leaves gave a seasonal touch.” But now the society column said, austerely, “The decorations were orchids and Pernet roses.”
Osage, Oklahoma, was a city.
Where, scarcely two decades ago, prairie and sky had met the eye with here a buffalo wallow, there an Indian encampment, you now saw a twenty-story hotel: the Savoy-Bixby. The Italian head waiter bent from the waist and murmured in your ear his secret about the veal sauté with mushrooms or the spaghetti Caruso du jour. Sabra Cravat, Congresswoman from Oklahoma, lunching in the Louis XIV room with the members of the Women’s State Republican Committee, would say, looking up at him with those intelligent dark eyes, “I’ll leave it to you, Nick. Only quickly. We haven’t much time.” Niccolo Mazzarini would say yes, he understood. No one had much time in Osage, Oklahoma. A black jackanapes in a tight scarlet jacket with brass buttons and even tighter bright blue pants, an impudent round red cap cocked over one ear, strolled through the dining room bawling, “Mistah Thisandthat! Mistah Whoandwhat!” He carried messages on a silver salver. There were separate ice-water taps in every bedroom. Servidors. Ring once for the waiter. Twice for the chambermaid. A valet is at your service.
Twenty-five years earlier anybody who was anybody in Oklahoma had dilated on his or her Eastern connections. Iowa, if necessary, was East.
They had been a little ashamed of the Run. Bragged about the splendors of the homes from which they had come.
Now it was considered the height of chic to be able to say that your parents had come through in a covered wagon. Grandparents were still rather rare in Oklahoma. As for the Run of ’89﻿—it was Osage’s *Mayflower*.

===Chinese===

奥塞奇阔到了极处，反而又变得简单。《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》的社交编辑几乎不用形容词了。搁在旧日，你会读到“宅邸装饰华美，堪萨斯城运来的蔽子藤布置得雅致，粉紫翠菊累累若盖，搭成花棚，新人立于其前；餐厅内金黄花、猩红鼠尾草与秋叶交映生辉，平添时令意趣”。可如今社交栏只冷冷一句：“饰以兰花与佩内玫瑰。”
俄克拉何马州的奥塞奇，是一座城市了。
不到二十年前，这里放眼还只有草原和天空相接，这边一处野牛打滚的泥坑，那边一营印第安帐篷；如今你看到的是一座二十层的饭店：萨伏伊-比克斯比。意大利领班从腰上躬下来，在你耳边低语他关于蘑菇煽小牛肉或当日卡鲁索式焝通心粉的秘密。萨布拉·克拉瓦特，俄克拉何马州的女联邦众议员，正在路易十四厅同州共和党妇女委员会的委员们用午餐，会抬起那双聪慧的黑眼睛望着他说：“交给你了，尼克。只是要快。我们时间不多。”尼科洛·马扎里尼会应一声是，他懂。在俄克拉何马州的奥塞奇，谁时间都不多。一个穿紧绷绷的猩红上衣、缀铜扣、套着更紧的亮蓝裤子的黑小猴，歪戴一顶无礼的红圆帽，在餐厅里踱来踱去，扯着嗓子喊：“某某老爷！某某太太！”他用银托盘传口信。每间卧室有单独的冰水龙头。传菜窗。按一声铃叫侍者。两声叫客房女仆。贴身男仆听您差遣。
二十五年前，俄克拉何马任何有头有脸的人物，都要把自己的东部关系夸上一夸。不得已时，艾奥瓦也算东部。
他们对当年那场抢地，有点羞于提起。夸的是自己出身的门庭何等显赫。
如今，能说一句“我父母是坐大篷车来的”，才算时鬻到了顶。祖辈在俄克拉何马还是稀罕物。至于1889年那场抢地——那是奥塞奇的“五月花号”。

===Original===

At the huge dinner given in Sabra Cravat’s honor when she was elected Congresswoman, and from which they tried to exclude Sol Levy over Sabra’s vigorous (and triumphant) protest, the chairman of the Committee on Arrangements explained it all to Sol, patronizingly.
“You see, we’re inviting only people who came to Oklahoma in the Run.”
“Well, sure,” said the former peddler, genially. “That’s all right. I walked.”
The Levy Mercantile Company’s building now occupied an entire square block and was fifteen stories high. In the huge plate-glass windows on Pawhuska postured ladies waxen and coquettish, as on Fifth Avenue. You went to the Salon Moderne to buy Little French Dresses, and the saleswomen of this department wore black satin and a very nice little strand of imitation pearls, and their eyes were hard and shrewd and their phrases the latest. The Osage Indian women had learned about these Little French Dresses, and they often came in with their stately measured stride: soft and flaccid from easy living, rolls of fat about their hips and thighs. They tried on sequined dresses, satin dresses, chiffon. Sometimes even the younger Osage Indian girls still wore the brilliant striped blanket, in a kind of contemptuous defiance of the whites. And to these, as well as to the other women customers, the saleswomen said, “That’s awfully good this year.﻿ ﻿… That’s dreadfully smart on you, Mrs. Buffalo Hide.﻿ ﻿… I think that line isn’t the thing for your figure, Mrs. Plenty Vest.﻿ ﻿… My dear, I want you to have that. It’s perfect with your coloring.”
The daughter of Mrs. Pat Leary (née Crook Nose) always caused quite a flutter when she came in, for accustomed though Osage was to money and the spending of it, the Learys’ lavishness was something spectacular. Handmade silk underwear, the sheerest of cobweb French stockings, model hats, dresses﻿—well, in the matter of gowns it was no good trying to influence Maude Leary or her mother. They frankly wanted beads, spangles, and paillettes on a foundation of crude color. The saleswomen were polite and acquiescent, but they cocked an eyebrow at one another. Squaw stuff. Now that little Cravat girl﻿—Felice Cravat, Cimarron Cravat’s daughter﻿—was different. She insisted on plain, smart tailored things. Young though she was, she was Oklahoma State Woman Tennis Champion.

===Chinese===

萨布拉·克拉瓦特当选联邦众议员时，为她办的那场盛大宴会——他们本想把索尔·利维排除在外，被萨布拉激烈（且得胜）地抗议了回去——宴务委员会主席屈尊向索尔解释了一切。
“你看，我们只请参加过抢地来俄克拉何马的人。”
“嗐，那当然，”当年的货郎和和气气地说。“没问题。我是走路来的。”
利维商贸公司的大楼如今占了整整一个街区，十五层高。波哈斯卡大街上巨大的橱窗里，蜡做的美人们搔首弄姿，一如第五大道。你去“摩登沙龙”买“小法兰西裙装”，这一部的女店员穿黑缎子，颈间一串很不错的仿珠，眼神精明锐利，话头最新。奥塞奇的印第安女人们打听到了这些“小法兰西裙装”，常迈着端庄的一板一眼的步子进来：优游的生活养得她们柔软松弛，胯上腿上一圈圈内肉。她们试缀亮片的裙、缎裙、雪纺。有时连年轻的奥塞奇印第安姑娘也仍披着艳丽的条纹毯，带几分对白人的轻蔑的挑硬。对她们，也同对别的女客一样，女店员们说：“这个今年好得不得了。……您穿这个精神极了，牛皮太太。……我看这条线不合您的身量，多背心太太。……亲爱的，我就要您拿这件。配您的肤色十全十美。”
帕特·利里太太（娘家歪鼻子）家的女儿一进门，总引起一阵骚动，因为奥塞奇虽已看惯了钱和花钱，利里家的豪奢仍是奇观。手绣丝内衣，蛛网一样薄的法国丝袜，原版帽子，裙子——说到裙子，想左右莫德·利里或她母亲是白费。她们明摆着要珠子、亮片、金箔，底下衬着生猛的大色。女店员客气地顺从着，眉毛却彼此一挑。印第安婆娘的货色。可克拉瓦特家那小姑娘——费利斯·克拉瓦特，西马龙·克拉瓦特的女儿——不一样。她只认素净利落的定制装。年纪虽小，她已是俄克拉何马州女子网球冠军。

===Original===

She always said she looked a freak in fluffy things﻿—like a boy dressed up in girl’s clothes. She had long, lean, muscular arms and a surprising breadth of shoulder, was slim flanked and practically stomachless. She had a curious trick of holding her head down and looking up at you under her lashes and when she did that you forgot her boyishness, for her lashes were like fern fronds, and her eyes, in her dark face, an astounding ocean gray. She was a good sport, too. She didn’t seem to mind the fact that her mother, when she accompanied her, wore the blanket and was hatless, just like any poor Kaw, instead of being one of the richest of the Osages. She was rather handsome for a squaw, in a big, insolent, slow-moving way. Felice Cravat, everyone agreed, was a chip of the old block, and by that they did not mean her father. They were thinking of Yancey Cravat﻿—old Cimarron, her grandfather, who was now something of a legend in Osage and throughout Oklahoma. Young Cim and his Osage wife had had a second child﻿—a boy﻿—and they had called him Yancey, after the old boy. Young Yancey was a bewilderingly handsome mixture of a dozen types and forbears﻿—Indian, Spanish, French, Southern, Southwest. With that long narrow face, the dolichocephalic head, people said he looked like the King of Spain﻿—without that dreadful Hapsburg jaw. Others said he was the image of his grandmother, Sabra Cravat. Still others contended that he was his Indian mother over again﻿—insolence and all. A third would come along and say, “You’re crazy. He’s old Yancey, born again. I guess you don’t remember him. There, look, that’s what I mean! The way he closes his eyes as if he were sleepy, and then when he does look at you straight you feel as if you’d been struck by lightning. They say he’s so smart that the Osages believe he’s one of their old gods come back to earth.”
Mrs. Tracy Wyatt (she who had been Donna Cravat) had tried to adopt one of her brother’s children, being herself childless, but Cim and his wife Ruby Big Elk had never consented to this. She was a case, that Donna Cravat, Oklahoma was agreed about that. She could get away with things that any other woman would be shot for. When old Tracy Wyatt had divorced his wife to marry this girl local feeling had been very much against her.

===Chinese===

她总说自己穿毛茸茸的东西像个怪物——像个男扮女装。她两条胳膊又长又瘦又结实，肩膀宽得出奇，腰身窄，几乎不见肚子。她有个古怪的习惯：头低着，从睫毛底下抬眼看人；她一这样，你就忘了她那假小子气——她的睫毛像蕨叶，她那张深色脸上的眼睛，是惊人的海灰色。她还输得起。她好像并不在意：她母亲陪她出门时，披着毯子，光着头，活像哪个穷卡乌人，而不是奥塞奇最富的人家之一。就一个印第安婆娘而言，她生得算俊——高大，傲慢，动作缓慢的那种俊。人人说费利斯·克拉瓦特是老根上劈出的新片，而这指的不是她父亲。他们心里想的是扬西·克拉瓦特——老西马龙，她的祖父，如今在奥塞奇、在整个俄克拉何马，已是一号传奇。小西姆和他的奥塞奇妻子又生了第二个孩子——男孩——取名扬西，随那位老人家。小扬西是十几种血统、十几个先人的令人目眩的混合——印第安、西班牙、法兰西、南方、西南。那张窄长的脸，那个狭长的头颅，人们说他像西班牙国王——只是没有哈布斯堡那副怕人的下巴。又有人说他像极了他的祖母萨布拉·克拉瓦特。还有人坚称他是他印第安母亲的再版——连那份傲慢都在内。再过来第三个说：“你们疯了。他是老扬西投胎。你们怕是不记得他了。看哪，看！就是这个意思！他那样合上眼，像睏了，可他一旦直直看你一眼，你就跟挨了雷打一样。都说他聪明得叫奥塞奇人相信，他们是他们的老神下凡。”
特雷西·怀亚特太太（就是从前的唐娜·克拉瓦特）自己没有生育，想领养她哥哥的一个孩子，可西姆和他的妻子鲁比·大鹿始终不肯。唐娜·克拉瓦特这个人，是个人物——这一点俄克拉何马众口一词。别的女人干了就要挨枪子的事，她都干得安然无恙。当年老特雷西·怀亚特为她离掉发妻娶了这姑娘时，本地舆论对她非常不利。

===Original===

Everyone had turned to the abandoned middle-aged wife with attentions and sympathy, but she had met their warmth and friendliness with such vitriol that they fell back in terror and finally came to believe the stories of how she had deviled and nagged old Tracy all through their marriage. They actually came to feel that he had been justified in deserting her and taking to wife this young and fascinating girl. Certainly he seemed to take a new lease on life, lost five inches around the waist line, played polo, regained something of the high color and good spirits of his old dray-driving days, and made a great hit in London during the season when Donna was presented at court. Besides, there was no withstanding the Wyatt money. Even in a country blasé of millionaires Tracy Wyatt’s fortune was something to marvel about. The name of Wyatt seemed to be everywhere. As you rode in trains you saw the shining round black flanks of oil cars, thousands of them, and painted on them in letters of white, “Wyatt Oils.” Motoring through Oklahoma and the whole of the Southwest you passed miles of Wyatt oil tanks, whole silent cities of monoliths, like something grimly Egyptian, squatting eunuch-like on the prairies.
As for the Wyatt house﻿—it wasn’t a house at all, but a combination of the palace of Versailles and the Grand Central Station in New York. It occupied grounds about the size of the duchy of Luxembourg, and on the ground, once barren plain, had been set great trees brought from England.
A mile of avenue, planted in elms, led up to the mansion, and each elm, bought, transported, and stuck in the ground, had cost fifteen hundred dollars. There were rare plants, farms, forests, lakes, tennis courts, golf links, polo fields, race tracks, airdromes, swimming pools. Whole paneled rooms had been brought from France. In the bathrooms were electric cabinets, and sunken tubs of rare marble, and shower baths glass enclosed. These bathrooms were the size of bedrooms, and the bedrooms the size of ballrooms, and the ballroom as big as an auditorium. There was an ice plant and cooling system that could chill the air of every room in the house, even on the hottest Oklahoma windy day. The kitchen range looked like a house in itself, and the kitchen looked like that of the Biltmore, only larger.

===Chinese===

人人争相亲近、同情那位被弃的中年太太，可她拿硫酸一样的刻毒回敬人们的热心和善意，把人吓得纷纷后退，末了反倒信了那些传闻：整个婚姻期间，她怎样折磨老特雷西、喋喋不休地骂他。人们当真觉得，他抛下她、娶这位年轻迷人的姑娘，是事出有因。他确实像领了新生：腰围藏了五英寸，打起了马球，找回几分当年赶车岁月的红润和兴致，唐娜进宫觐见那一季，他在伦敦大出风头。再说，怀亚特家的钱谁也拗不过。在一个人人对百万富翁都餍足的地方，特雷西·怀亚特的家产也依然令人称奇。“怀亚特”这个名字无处不在。你坐火车，看见油罐车那乌亮滚圆的黑侧腹，几千几万辆，白漆大字：“怀亚特石油”。开车穿过俄克拉何马和整个西南部，你驶过一英里又一英里的怀亚特油罐，整座整座沉默的单体巨塔之城，像什么埃及的凶物，阉人似的蹲在草原上。
至于怀亚特家的宅子——那根本不是宅子，是凡尔赛宫加纽约中央车站的合体。占地约摸卢森堡大公国那么大，那片当年寸草不生的平原上，栽下了从英国运来的大树。
一条一英里长的榆树大道通到府门前，每一棵榆树，买下、运来、栽进土里，花了一千五百美元。有奇花异木，农场，森林，湖泊，网球场，高尔夫球场，马球场，赛马场，飞机场，游泳池。整间整间镶板房是从法国整个运来的。浴室里有电疗柜，嵌地的珍稀大理石浴缸，玻璃围着的淋浴间。这些浴室有卧室大，卧室有舞厅大，舞厅有礼堂大。有一套制冰和制冷系统，俄克拉何马最热的风天，也能把全宅每一个房间的空气吹凉。厨房的炉灶看着本身像座房子，厨房像贝尔摩庄园的，只是更大。

===Original===

When you entered the dining room you felt that here should be seated solemn diplomats in gold braid signing world treaties and having their portraits painted doing it. Sixty gardeners manned the grounds. The house servants would have peopled a village.
Sabra Cravat rarely came to visit her daughter’s house, and when she did the very simplicity of her slim straight little figure in its dark blue georgette or black crêpe was startling in the midst of these marble columns and vast corridors and royal hangings. She did come occasionally, and on those occasions you found her in the great central apartment that was like a throne room, standing there before the portraits of her son’s two children, Felice and Yancey Cravat. Failing to possess either of the children for her own, Donna had had them painted and hung there, one either side of the enormous fireplace. She had meant them to be a gift to her mother, but Sabra Cravat had refused to take them.
“Don’t you like them, Sabra darling? They’re the best things Segovia has ever done. Is it because they’re modern? I think they look like the kids﻿—don’t you?”
“They’re just wonderful.”
“Well, then?”
“I’d have to build a house for them. How would they look in the sitting room of the house on Kihekah! No, let me come here and look at them now and then. That way they’re always a fresh surprise to me.”
Certainly they were rather surprising, those portraits. Rather, one of them was. Segovia had got little Felice well enough, but he had made the mistake of painting her in Spanish costume, and somehow her angular contours and boyish frame had not lent themselves to these gorgeous lace and satin trappings. The boy, Yancey, had refused to dress up for the occasion﻿—had, indeed, been impatient of posing at all. Segovia had caught him quickly and brilliantly, with startling results. He wore a pair of loose, rather grimy white tennis pants, a white woolly sweater with a hole in the elbow, and was hatless. In his right hand﻿—that slim, beautiful, speaking hand﻿—he held a limp, half-smoked cigarette, its blue-gray smoke spiraling faintly, its dull red eye the only note of color in the picture. Yet the whole portrait was colorful, moving, alive. The boy’s pose was so insolent, so lithe, so careless. The eyes followed you. He was a person.
“Looks like Ruby, don’t you think?” Donna had said, when first she had shown it to her mother.

===Chinese===

一进那间餐厅，你就觉得这里该坐满金绶带的严肃外交官，签署世界条约，还请人画下这签约的一瞬。六十名园丁打理着园囿。宅内的仆役足以住满一座村子。
萨布拉·克拉瓦特很少来女儿家做客，来时，她那瘦直小巧的身影，一身深蓝乔其纱或黑绉纱，那份朴素本身，在这些大理石柱、浩大的走廊和王家的帷墁中间，惊心动魄。她偶尔也来，来的那些场合，你会在那间如同正殿的中央大厅里找到她，站在孙辈——费利斯·克拉瓦特和扬西·克拉瓦特——的画像前。两个孩子一个也没能归她自己，唐娜便让人画了像挂在这里，在那座巨大壁炉的两侧一边一幅。本是要送给母亲的礼，可萨布拉·克拉瓦特拒不肯收。
“你不喜欢吗，萨布拉亲爱的？这是塞戈维亚画得最好的东西。是因为它们太现代？我看很像这两个孩子——你说呢？”
“好得很。”
“那为什么呢？”
“我得为它们盖一座房子。挂在基赫卡街那间的起居室里像什么话！不，让我隔三差五来这儿看看。这样，它们对我永远是一份新鲜。”
这两幅像确实叫人吃惊。不如说，其中一幅。塞戈维亚把小费利斯画得不坏，只是错在她穿了一身西班牙装束，她那棱角的轮廓和假小子的身架，实在衬不起那些华丽的蕾丝与绸缎。男孩扬西却不肯为画像装扮——其实连摆姿势都不耐烦。塞戈维亚敏捷而漂亮地抓住了他，效果惊人。他穿一条宽松的、相当脏的白网球裤，一件肘部有个窟窿的白毛线衣，光着头。右手里——那只纤细、漂亮、会说话的手——拈着一支软塌塌的半截香烟，蓝灰的烟淡淡盘旋，那点暗红，是全画唯一的色彩。可整幅画像色彩斑斓，动人，活着。那孩子的姿势那么傲慢，那么灵动，那么漫不经心。眼睛追着你。他是一个人物。
“像鲁比，你说是不是？”唐娜头一回拿给母亲看时说。

===Original===

“No!” Sabra had replied, with enormous vigor. “Not at all. Your father.”
“Well﻿—maybe﻿—a little.”
“A little! You’re crazy! Look at his eyes. His hands. Of course they’re not as beautiful as your father’s hands were﻿—are﻿ ﻿…”
It had been five years since Sabra had heard news of her husband, Yancey Cravat. And now, for the first time, she felt that he was dead, though she had never admitted this. In spite of his years she had heard that Yancey had gone to France during the war. The American and the English armies had rejected him, so he had dyed his graying hair, lied about his age, thrown back his still magnificent shoulders, and somehow, by his eyes, his voice, his hands, or a combination of all these, had hypnotized them into taking him. An unofficial report had listed him among the missing after the carnage had ceased in the shambles that had been a wooded plateau called the Argonne.
“He isn’t dead,” Sabra had said, almost calmly. “When Yancey Cravat dies he’ll be on the front page, and the world will know it.”
Donna, in talking it over with her brother Cim, had been inclined to agree with this, though she did not put it thus to her mother. “Dad wouldn’t let himself die in a list. He’s too good an actor to be lost in a mob scene.”
But a year had gone by.
The *Oklahoma Wigwam* now issued a morning as well as an afternoon edition and was known as the most powerful newspaper in the Southwest. Its presses thundered out tens of thousands of copies an hour, and hour on hour﻿—five editions. Its linotype room was now a regiment of iron men, its staff boasted executive editor, editor in chief, managing editor, city editor, editor, and on down into the dozens of minor minions. When Sabra was in town she made a practice of driving down to the office at eleven every night, remaining there for an hour looking over the layout, reading the wet galley proof of the night’s news lead, scanning the A.P. wires. Her entrance was in the nature of the passage of royalty, and when she came into the city room the staff all but saluted. True, she wasn’t there very much, except in the summer, when Congress was not in session.
The sight of a woman on the floor of the Congressional House was still something of a novelty. Sentimental America had shrunk from the thought of women in active politics.

===Chinese===

“不像！”萨布拉答得斩钉截铁。“一点也不像。像你父亲。”
“唔——也许——有一点。”
“一点！你疯了！看他的眼睛。他的手。当然，手没有你父亲的手漂亮——现在……”
萨布拉已有五年没有丈夫扬西·克拉瓦特的音讯了。而此刻，头一回，她感到他死了，虽然她从不肯承认。虽然年岁已高，她却听说，大战期间扬西去了法国。美英两军都拒收他，他便染黑花白的头发，谎报年纪，重新挺起那副依然堂皇的肩膀，不知凭着他的眼睛、嗓音、双手，还是这一切的合力，竟把他们催眠得收下了他。一份非官方的报告，在那片曾被称作阿尔贡的林木高地化为屠场、屠杀停止之后，把他列进了失踪者名单。
“他没死，”萨布拉当时说，近乎平静。“扬西·克拉瓦特死的时候会上头版，全世界都会知道。”
唐娜同弟弟西姆谈论这件事时，也倾向于同意，只是没这样对母亲说。“爸不会让自己死在一张名单上。他是好演员，不会在群戏里跑丢。”
可一年过去了。
《俄克拉何马维格沃姆报》如今除下午版还出晨版，号称西南部最有势力的报纸。它的印机一小时离出几万份，一小时接一小时——一天五版。它的莱诺机房如今是一团铁人的军团，编辑部号称有执行主编、总编辑、主编、本市编辑、编辑，往下还有几十名小卒。萨布拉在城里时，习惯每晚十一点驱车到报馆，待一个钟头，看看版式，读读当晚头条新闻的湿校样，扫扫美联社的电稿。她的驾临近乎王驾过境，一进本市编辑室，全员几乎要肃立行礼。的确，她不常在，除了夏天，国会休会的日子。
国会众议院的议席上出现一个女人，在当时仍算新鲜事。感伤主义的美国，一向缩着不敢想女人投身政治。

===Original===

Woman’s place was in the Home, and American Womanhood was too exquisite a flower to be subjected to the harsh atmosphere of the Assembly floor and the committee room.
Sabra stumped the state and developed a surprising gift of oratory.
“If American politics are too dirty for women to take part in, there’s something wrong with American politics.﻿ ﻿… We weren’t too delicate and flowerlike to cross the plains and prairies and deserts in a covered wagon and to stand the hardships and heartbreaks of frontier life﻿ ﻿… history of France peeking through a bedroom keyhole﻿ ﻿… history of England a joust﻿ ﻿… but here in this land the women have been the hewers of wood and drawers of water﻿ ﻿… thousands of unnamed heroines with weather-beaten faces and mud-caked boots﻿ ﻿… alkali water﻿ ﻿… sun﻿ ﻿… dust﻿ ﻿… wind.﻿ ﻿… I am not belittling the brave pioneer men but the sunbonnet as well as the sombrero has helped to settle this glorious land of ours.﻿ ﻿…”
It had been so many years since she had heard this﻿—it had sunk so deep into her consciousness﻿—that perhaps she actually thought she had originated this speech. Certainly it was received with tremendous emotional response, copied throughout the Southwest, the Far West, the Midwest states, and it won her the election and gained her fame that was nation wide.
Perhaps it was not altogether what Sabra Cravat said that counted in her favor. Her appearance must have had something to do with it. A slim, straight, dignified woman, yet touchingly feminine. Her voice not loud, but clear. Her white hair was shingled and beautifully waved and beneath this her soft dark eyes took on an added depth and brilliance. Her eyebrows had remained black and thick, still further enhancing her finest feature. Her dress was always dark, becoming, smart, and her silken ankles above the slim slippers with their cut-steel buckles were those of a young girl. The aristocratic Marcy feet and ankles.
Her speeches were not altogether romantic, by any means. She knew her state. Its politics were notoriously rotten. Governor after governor was impeached with musical comedy swiftness and regularity, and the impeachment proceedings stank to Washington.

===Chinese===

女人的位置在家里，美利坚的淑女是太精致的花，禁不起议院会场和委员会房间里那种粗粝的空气。
萨布拉跑遍全州竞选，磨出了一手惊人的演说天才。
“如果美国政治脏到女人不能参加，那出毛病的是美国政治。……我们横越平原、草原和荒漠，坐的是大篷车，我们捱得住边地生活的艰辛与心碎，那时我们怎么不嫌自己娇贵、不嫌自己像花……法兰西的历史是从卧室钥匙孔里看出来的……英格兰的历史是一场马上的枪术赛……可是在这片土地上，女人才是砍柴挑水的人……成千上万无名女英雄，风吹雨打的脸，泥泞裹住的靴……碱水……日头……尘土……风。……我不是要贬低勇敢的拓荒男儿，可是替我们这片光荣土地开基立业的，除了阔边帽，还有太阳帽。……”
她听这话已经太多年了——它沉进她的意识太深——也许她当真以为这篇演说是自己首创的。它确实激起了巨大的情感回应，被西南、远西、中西部各州广为转载，为她赢了选举，也为她赢了全国性的名声。
成全萨布拉·克拉瓦特的，也许不全在她说了什么。她的仪表必也有关。一个瘦削、挺直、端凝的女人，却又动人地有女人味。嗓音不响，但清晰。她的白发剪短烫出好看的波纹，衬得底下那双柔和的黑眼睛愈见深亮。眉毛仍是黑的、浓的，越发添了她最美的眉目。衣裳总是深色、得体、时鬻，纤细的鞋上缀切钢鞋扣，那双丝袜里的脚踝是少女的脚踝。马西家那双贵族的脚踝。
她的演说绝非一味浪漫。她了解她的州。那州的政治烂得有名。州长一个接一个被勤劾，快得和音乐喜剧换场一样准时，勤劾案一路奥到华盛顿。

===Original===

This governor was practically an outlaw and desperado; that governor, who resembled a traveling evangelist with his long locks and his sanctimonious face, flaunted his mistress, and all the office plums fell to her rapscallion kin. Sabra had statistics at her tongue’s end. Millions of barrels of oil. Millions of tons of zinc. Third in mineral products. First in oil. Coal. Gypsum. Granite. Livestock.
In Washington she was quite a belle among the old boys in Congress and even the Senate. The opposition party tried to blackmail her with publicity about certain unproved items in the life of her dead (or missing) husband Yancey Cravat: a two-gun man, a desperado, a killer, a drunkard, a squaw man. Then they started on young Cim and his Osage Indian wife, but Sabra and Donna were too quick for them.
Donna Wyatt leased a handsome Washington house in Dupont Circle, staffed it, brought Tracy Wyatt’s vast wealth and influence to bear, and planned a coup so brilliant that it routed the enemy forever. She brought her handsome, sleepy-eyed brother Cim and his wife Ruby Big Elk, and the youngsters Felice and Yancey to the house in Dupont Circle, and together she and Sabra gave a reception for them to which they invited a group so precious that it actually came.
Sabra and Donna, exquisitely dressed, stood in line at the head of the magnificent room, and between them stood Ruby Big Elk in her Indian dress of creamy white doeskin all embroidered in beads from shoulder to hem. She was an imposing figure, massive but not offensively fat as were many of the older Osage women, and her black abundant hair had taken on a mist of gray.
“My daughter-in-law, Mrs. Cimarron Cravat, of the Osage Indian tribe.”
“My son’s wife, Ruby Big Elk﻿—Mrs. Cimarron Cravat.”
“My sister-in-law, Mrs. Cimarron Cravat. A full-blood Osage Indian.﻿ ﻿… Yes, indeed. We think so, too.”
And, “How do you do?” said Ruby, in her calm, insolent way.
For the benefit of those who had not quite been able to encompass the Indian woman in her native dress Ruby’s next public appearance was made in a Paris gown of white. She became the rage, was considered picturesque, and left Washington in disgust, her work done. No one but her husband, whom she loved with a doglike devotion, could have induced her to go through this ceremony.
The opposition retired, vanquished.

===Chinese===

这一任州长简直是个法外亡命；那一任，披着长发、一脸假圣洁、像个巡回布道牧师的，公然带着情妇招摇，所有的肥缺都落进她那班无赖亲眷手里。萨布拉的舌尖上挂着统计数字。几百万桶石油。几百万吨锌。矿产第三。石油第一。煤。石膏。花岗岩。牲畜。
在华盛顿，她在国会老男孩们中间、甚至参议院里，也算得一位红人。反对党想拿她那位死（或失踪）丈夫扬西·克拉瓦特生平里几桩无凭无据的旧事大肆宣传，敲诈她：双枪客，亡命徒，杀人犯，酒鬼，印第安女婿。接着他们又冲着小西姆和他的奥塞奇印第安妻子下手，可萨布拉和唐娜比他们快。
唐娜·怀亚特在杜邦圆环租下一座漂亮的华盛顿宅邸，配齐人手，搬出特雷西·怀亚特庞大的财势，筹划了一场漂亮到彻底击溃敌人的一次行动。她把弟弟——那位英俊的、睡眼惺忪的西姆——和妻子鲁比·大鹿，以及费利斯和小扬西两个小的，都接到杜邦圆环的宅子里，同萨布拉一起为他们办了一场招待会，请来的一批客人显贵到当真赏了光。
萨布拉和唐娜盛装并肩站在那间富丽堂皇的大厅上首，两人中间站着鲁比·大鹿，一身乳白鹿皮的印第安盛装，从肩到摆缀满珠绣。她是个追人的形象，高大，却不像许多上了年纪的奥塞奇女人那样肥得可憎，浓密的黑发已蒙上一层灰雾。
“我的儿媳，西马龙·克拉瓦特太太，奥塞奇印第安部族的人。”
“我儿子的妻子，鲁比·大鹿——西马龙·克拉瓦特太太。”
“我的弟媳妇，西马龙·克拉瓦特太太。纯血统的奥塞奇印第安人。……是的，可不是。我们也这么看。”
而鲁比就用她那沉静、傲慢的腔调说：“你好。”
为了照顾那些一时消化不了穿本族服装的印第安女人的客人，鲁比的下一次公开露面，穿的是一件白色的巴黎礼服。她一鸣惊人，人人夸她别致，随后她厌恶透了华盛顿，功成身退。除了她那位她像忠犬一样爱着的丈夫，谁也劝不动她走完这一套仪节。
反对派搬兵回营，一敷涂地。

===Original===

Donna and Tracy Wyatt then hired a special train in which they took fifty Eastern potentates on a tour of Oklahoma. One vague and not very bright Washington matron, of great social prestige, impressed with what she saw, voiced her opinion to young Yancey Cravat, quite confused as to his identity and seeing only an attractive and very handsome young male seated beside her at a country club luncheon.
“I had no idea Oklahoma was like this. I thought it was all oil and dirty Indians.”
“There is quite a lot of oil, but we’re not all dirty.”
“We?”
“I’m an Indian.”
Osage, Oklahoma, was now just as much like New York as Osage could manage to make it. They built twenty-story office buildings in a city that had hundreds of miles of prairie to spread in. Tracy Wyatt built the first skyscraper﻿—the Wyatt building. It was pointed out and advertised all over the flat prairie state. Then Pat Leary, dancing an Irish jig of jealousy, built the Leary building, twenty-three stories high. But the sweet fruits of triumph soon turned to ashes in his mouth. The Wyatt building’s foundations were not built to stand the added strain of five full stories. So he had built a five-story tower, slim and tapering, a taunting finger pointing to the sky. Again Tracy Wyatt owned the tallest building in Oklahoma.
On the roof of the Levy Mercantile Company’s Building Sol had had built a penthouse after his own plans. It was the only one of its kind in all Oklahoma. That small part of Osage which did not make an annual pilgrimage to New York was slightly bewildered by Sol Levy’s roof life. They fed one another with scraps of gossip got from servants, clerks, stenographers who claimed to have seen the place at one time or another. It was, these said, filled with the rarest of carpets, rugs, books, hangings. Super radio, super phonograph, super player piano. Music hungry. There he lived, alone, in luxury, of the town, yet no part of it. At sunset, in the early morning, late of a star-spangled night he might have been seen leaning over the parapet of his sky house, a lonely little figure, lean, ivory, aloof, like a gargoyle brooding over the ridiculous city sprawled below; over the oil rigs that encircled it like giant Martian guards holding it in their power; beyond, to where the sky, in a veil of gray chiffon that commerce had wrought, stooped to meet the debauched red prairie.

===Chinese===

唐娜和特雷西·怀亚特随后包了一列专车，带五十位东部大老爷游历俄克拉何马。一位模糊而不大聪明的华盛顿主妇，社交地位极高，看完了所见所闻，对着小扬西·克拉瓦特发表了感想——她设弄清他的身份，只看见乡村俱乐部午宴上邻座一个俊俏好看的年轻男人。
“我没想到俄克拉何马是这个样子。我只当它遍地是油和肷脏的印第安人。”
“油确实不少，可我们并不都肷脏。”
“我们？”
“我是印第安人。”
俄克拉何马州的奥塞奇，如今已尽其所能把自己弄得像纽约了。在一座有几百平方英里草原可以摊开的城市里，他们盖二十层的写字楼。特雷西·怀亚特盖了第一座摩天楼——怀亚特大楼。它在这片平坦的草原州上被指给人看、登广告。接着帕特·利里跳着一支嫉妒的爱尔兰吉格舞，盖起了利里大楼，二十三层。可胜利的甜果很快在他嘴里变成了灰。怀亚特大楼的地基，撑不住再加五层的重压——他是这么放的风。于是他在自己楼顶又起了一座五层的塔，纤细收分，一根撕揶天空的手指。特雷西·怀亚特重新拥有俄克拉何马最高的楼。
利维商贸公司大楼的顶上，索尔按自己的图纸造了一座屋顶宅。整个俄克拉何马独此一处。奥塞奇那一小撮不每年去纽约朝圣的人，对索尔·利维的屋顶生活有点发懵。他们拿仆人、店员、速记员——都自称几时见过那地方——嘴里扒出的碎离闲话彼此喂养。据说那里铺着最珍的地毯、挂毯、书、帷奵。超等的收音机，超等的留声机，超等的自动钢琴。饿着音乐。他就住在那儿，独自一人，豪华地，属于这座城，却不是它的一部分。日落时分，清早，星斗清天的深夜，你会看见他伏在空中之宅的雉堞上，一个孤零零的小个子身影，瘦削，象牙色，冷然，像一头石怪鸟俯瞰着脚下这座荒唐摊开的城市；俯瞰着像火星巨人卫队一样四面围住它的井架；再远处，天空裹着一层商业织就的灰色雪纺纱，俯下身去，同那被蹂躚的红草原相会。

===Original===

Money was now the only standard. If Pat Leary had sixty-two million dollars on Tuesday he was Oklahoma’s leading citizen. If Tracy Wyatt had seventy-eight million dollars on Wednesday then Tracy Wyatt was Oklahoma’s leading citizen.
Osage had those fascinating little specialty shops and interior decorating shops on Pawhuska just like those you see on Madison Avenue, whose owners are the daughters of decayed Eastern aristocracy on the make. The head of the shop appeared only to special clients and then with a hat on. She wore the hat from morning until night, her badge of revolt against this position of service. “I am a lady,” the hat said. “Make no mistake about that. Just because I am a shopkeeper don’t think you can patronize me. I am not working. I am playing at work. This is my fad. At any moment I can walk out of here, just like any of you.”
Feminine Osage’s hat, by the way, was cut and fitted right on its head, just like Paris.
Sabra probably was the only woman of her own generation and social position in Osage who still wore on the third finger of her left hand the plain broad gold band of a long-past day. Synchronous with the permanent wave and the reducing diet the oil-rich Osage matrons of Sabra’s age cast sentiment aside for fashion, quietly placed the clumsy gold band in a bureau drawer and appeared with a slim platinum circlet bearing, perhaps, the engraved anachronism, “M. G.-K. L. 1884.” Certainly it was much more at ease among its square-cut emerald and oblong-diamond neighbors. These ladies explained (if at all) that the gold band had grown too tight for the finger, or too loose. Sabra looked down at the broad old-fashioned wedding ring on her own gemless finger. She had not once taken it off in over forty years. It was as much a part of her as the finger itself.
Osage began to rechristen streets, changing the fine native Indian names to commonplace American ones. Hetoappe Street became the Boston Road; very fashionable it was, too. Still, the very nicest people were building out a ways on the new section (formerly Okemah Hill) now River View. The river was the ruddy Canadian, the view the forest of oil rigs bristling on the opposite shore. The grounds sloped down to the river except on those occasions when the river rose in red anger and sloped down to the houses.

===Chinese===

如今钱是唯一的标准。星期二帕特·利里名下有六千二百万，他就是俄克拉何马的头号公民。星期三特雷西·怀亚特名下有七千八百万，那头号公民就是特雷西·怀亚特。
奥塞奇的波哈斯卡街上有了那些迷人的小精品店、室内装饰店，同麦迪逊大道上的一模一样，老板是没落的东部世家、正在往上爬的女儿们。店主人只招呼特别的客人，而且必戴着帽子。她从早到晚戴着帽子，那是她对这个伺候人位置的反抗徽章。“我是位淑女，”那帽子说。“这点你们别弄错。别因为我开铺子，就当可以拿恩主的脸色看我。我不是在干活。我是在玩干活。这是我的癖好。什么时候我兴尽了，抬脚就走，同你们哪一位一样。”
顺带一说，奥塞奇女人的帽子，是当场在头上剪裁合身的，同巴黎一样。
萨布拉大概是奥塞奇她这一辈、她这个身份的女人里，唯一还在左手无名指上戴那一枚往日宽金价戒指的。烫了永久卷、进了减脟餐的奥塞奇油田富孃们，与萨布拉同辈，纷纷为时鬻丢了感情，悄悄把笨重的金价戒指搁进衣柜抽屉，换上一只纤细的铂金圈，上面或许刻着不合时宜的“M. G.-K. L. 1884”。在方形祖母绿和长方钻石的邻居中间，它确实自在多了。这些太太们解释（若解释的话）：金价戒指不是戴得紧了，就是戴得松了。萨布拉低头看自己那根不镶宝石的手指上的宽宽的老式婚戒。四十多年她没摘下过一回。它同手指一样，长在她身上。
奥塞奇开始给街道重新命名，把好好的本乡印第安名字换成平庸的美国名字。赫托阿佩街改成了波士顿路，还时鬻得很呢。不过，最上等的人家如今正往新区（从前的奥克马山，如今的“河景”）的外围盖房子。河是那条红色的加拿大大河，景是对岸密密麻麻的井架林。宅地的草坡一路斜到河边——除了河水发红怒、倒过来斜进宅子的那几天。

===Original===

The houses themselves were Italian palazzi or French châteaux or English manors; none, perhaps, quite so vast or inclusive as Tracy Wyatt’s, but all provided with such necessities as pipe organs, sunken baths, Greek temples, ancient tapestries, Venetian glass, billiard rooms, and butlers. Pat Leary, the smart little erstwhile section hand, had a melodramatic idea. Not content with peacocks, golf links, and swimming pools on his estate he now had placed an old and weathered covered wagon, a rusted and splintered wagon tongue, the bleached skull of a buffalo, an Indian teepee, and a battered lantern on a little island at the foot of the artificial lake below the heights on which his house stood. At night a searchlight, red, green, or orange, played from the tower of the house upon the mute relics of frontier days.
“The covered wagon my folks crossed the prairies in,” Pat Leary explained, with shy pride. Eastern visitors were much impressed. It was considered a great joke in Osage, intimately familiar with Pat’s Oklahoma beginnings.
“Forgot something, ain’t you Pat, in that outfit you got rigged up in the yard?” old Bixby asked.
“What’s that?”
“Pickax and shovel,” Bixby replied, laconically. “Keg of spikes and a hand car.”
Old Sam Pack, who had made the Run on a mule, said that if Pat Leary’s folks had come to Oklahoma in a covered wagon then his had made the trip in an airplane.
All the Oklahoma millionaire houses had libraries. Yards and yards of fine leather libraries, with gold tooling. Ike Hawkes’s library had five sets of Dickens alone, handsomely bound in red, green, blue, brown, and black, and Ike all unaware of any of them.
Moving picture palaces, with white-gloved ushers, had all the big Broadway super-films. Gas filling stations on every corner. Hot dog, chili con carne, and hamburger stands on the most remote country road. The Arverne Grand Opera Company at the McKee Theater for a whole week every year, and the best of everything﻿—*Traviata*, *Bohème*, *Carmen*, *Louise*, *The Barber of Seville*. The display of jewels during that week made the Diamond Horseshoe at the Metropolitan look like the Black Hole of Calcutta.

> Social events of the week just closed were worthily concluded with the smart dancing party at which Mr. and Mrs. Clint Hopper entertained a small company at the Osage Club. The roof garden of the club﻿ ﻿…

宅子本身，不是意大利府邸就是法国城堡或英吉利庄国；也许哪一座都不及特雷西·怀亚特家的浩大齐全，但管风琴、嵌地浴池、希腊神庙、古挂毯、威尼斯玻璃、台球室和管家，一样不少。帕特·利里，那个精明的小个子昔日养路工，有个闹剧的点子。园子里有了孔雀、高尔夫球场和游泳池还不满足，他如今又在他宅子所在高地脚下人工湖心的小岛上，安放了一辆老旧风霜的大篷车、一根锈裂的辕木、一颗漂白的野牛颅骨、一顶印第安帐篷和一盏破提灯。入夜，一道探照灯——红的、绿的或橙的——从宅子的塔楼上扫过来，照着这些边地岁月的哑物。
“我祖上横越草原坐的就是这辆篷车，”帕特·利里腼腆而骄傲地解释。东部来客大为动容。奥塞奇本地人却深知帕特发家的底细，只把它当作天大的笑话。
“你那套行头里落了样东西吧，帕特——你院子里摆的那套？”老比克斯比问。
“落了什么？”
“锶头铁锹，”比克斯比懒懒地答。“还有一桶道钉和一辆手摇巡道车。”
骑骡子参加当年抢地的老萨姆·帕克说，要是帕特·利里家祖上是坐大篷车来的俄克拉何马，那他家就是坐飞机来的。
俄克拉何马的百万富翁宅子家家有书房。一码一码上好皮面的书房，烫着金花。艾克·霍克斯家的书房光狄更斯就有五套，红绿蓝棕黑，装帧华美，而艾克本人一套也没碰过。
电影宫，配戴白手套的领座员，上映百老汇一切超级大片。每个街角是加油站。最偏僻的乡路旁也有热狗、辣椒炖肉和汉堡摊。阿弗恩大歌剧团每年在麦基剧院驻演整整一星期，剧目是最好的——《茶花女》《波希米亚人》《卡门》《路易丝》《塞维利亚的理发师》。那一星期里珠宝的陈列，叫大都会歌剧院的钻石马蹄圈看着像加尔各答黑洞。

## XXIV / 二十四
===Original===

Still, oil was oil, and Indians were Indians. There was no way in which either of those native forces could quite be molded to fit the New York pattern.
The Osages still whirled up and down the Oklahoma roads, and those roads, for hundreds of miles, were still unpaved red prairie dust. They crashed into ditches and draws and culverts as of old, walked back to town and, entering the automobile salesroom in which they had bought the original car, pointed with one dusky finger at a new and glittering model.
“ ’Nother,” they said, succinctly. And drove out with it.
It was common news that Charley Vest had smashed eight Cadillac cars in a year, but then Charley had a mysterious source through which he procured fire water. They bought airplanes now, but they were forbidden the use of local and neighboring flying fields after a series of fatal smashes. They seemed, for the most part (the full bloods, at least), to be totally lacking in engine sense.
They had electric refrigerators﻿—sometimes in the parlor, very proud. They ate enormously and waxed fatter and fatter. The young Osages now wore made-to-order shirts with monograms embroidered on them the size of a saucer. The Osages had taken to spending their summers in Colorado Springs or Manitou. At first the white residents of those cities had refused to rent their fine houses, furnished, to the Indians for the season. But the vast sums offered them soon overcame their reluctance. The Indian problem was still a problem, for he was considered legitimate prey, and thousands of prairie buzzards fed on his richness.
Sabra Cravat had introduced a bill for the further protection of the Osages, and rather took away the breath of the House assembled by advocating abolition of the Indian Reservation system. Her speech, radical though it was, and sensational, was greeted with favor by some of the more liberal of the Congressmen. They even conceded that this idea of hers, to the effect that the Indian would never develop or express himself until he was as free as the Negro, might some day become a reality. These were the reformers﻿—the long-hairs﻿—fanatics.
Oklahoma was very proud of Sabra Cravat, editor, Congresswoman, pioneer. Osage said she embodied the finest spirit of the state and of the Southwest.

===Chinese===

话说回来，油终归是油，印第安人终归是印第安人。这两种本乡本土的势力，谁也没法真捏成纽约的样子。
奥塞奇人照旧在俄克拉何马的路上飞驰，而那些路，几百英里几百英里，照旧是没铺过的红草原尘土。他们照旧一头撞进沟壕涵洞，照旧步行回镇，走进当初买第一辆车的车行，一根深色的手指点着一辆崭新的锃亮的车型。
“再，”他们说，干脆之至。然后开着新车走了。
查利·背心一年撞坏了八辆凯迪拉克，这是人人皆知的新闻，不过查利有个神秘的门路，弄得到烈酒。他们如今买飞机了，但连串致命的坠机之后，本地的和邻州的机场都禁止他们使用。他们（至少纯血统的那些）看起来全然没有机械的直觉。
他们有了电冰箱——有时就摆在客厅里，得意得很。他们吃得海量，越来越肥。年轻的奥塞奇人如今穿定制衬衫，绣着茶碟那么大的姓名花押。奥塞奇人兴起到科罗拉多斯普林斯或马尼托消夏。起初那两城的白人居民不肯把自己带家具的好宅子租给印第安人过季。可他们开出的巨款，很快就把这不情愿化开了。印第安人问题仍旧是个问题，因为他被看作合法的猎物，成千上万的草原秃鵰靠他的富肉饕饱。
萨布拉·克拉瓦特提出了一项进一步保护奥塞奇人的法案，又以主张废除印第安保留地制度，惊得满院屏息。她那篇演说，尽管激进，尽管耸动，却博得了几位更开明的议员的好感。他们甚至承认，她这个见解——印第安人一天不活得同黑人一样自由，就一天不能发展自己、表现自己——说不定有一天会成为现实。这些是改革派——长发派——狂热分子。
俄克拉何马为萨布拉·克拉瓦特骄傲：主编，众议员，拓荒先驱。奥塞奇人说，她体现了这个州、整个西南部最精粹的精神。

===Original===

When ten of Osage’s most unctuous millionaires contributed fifty thousand dollars each for a five-hundred-thousand-dollar statue that should embody the Oklahoma Pioneer no one was surprised to hear that the sculptor, Masja Krbecek, wanted to interview Sabra Cravat.
Osage was not familiar with the sculpture of Krbecek, but it was impressed with the price of it. Half a million dollars for a statue!
“Certainly,” said the committee, calmly. “He’s the best there is. Half a million is nothing for his stuff. He wouldn’t kick a pebble for less than a quarter of a million.”
“Do you suppose he’ll do her as a pioneer woman in a sunbonnet? Holding little Cim by the hand, huh? Or maybe in a covered wagon.”
Sabra received Krbecek in a simple (draped) dress. He turned out to be a quiet, rather snuffy little Pole in eyeglasses, who looked more like a tailor﻿—a “little” tailor﻿—than a sculptor. His eye roamed about the living room of the house on Kihekah. The old wooden house had been covered with plaster in a deep warm shade much the color of the native clay; the gimcrack porch and the cupolas had been torn away and a great square veranda and a terrace built at the side, away from the street and screened by a thick hedge and an iron grille. It was now, in fact, much the house that Yancey had planned when Sabra first built it years ago. The old pieces of mahogany and glass and silver were back, triumphant again over the plush and brocade with which Sabra had furnished the house when new. The old, despised since pioneer days, was again the fashion in Osage. There was the DeGrasse silver, the cake dish with the carefree cupids, the mantelpiece figures of china, even the handwoven coverlet that Mother Bridget had given her that day in Wichita so long ago. Its rich deep blue was unfaded.
“You are very comfortable here in Oklahoma,” said Masja Krbecek. He pronounced it syllable by syllable, painfully. O‑kla‑ho‑ma.
“It is a very simple home,” Sabra replied, “compared to the other places you have seen hereabouts.”
“It is the home of a good woman,” said Krbecek, dryly.
Sabra was a trifle startled, but she said thank you, primly.
“You are a Congress member, you are editor of a great newspaper, you are well known through the country. You American women, you are really amazing.”
Again Sabra thanked him.

===Chinese===

奥塞奇十位最油光满面的百万富翁各拥五万，凑一座五十万美元的雕像，要它体现“俄克拉何马拓荒者”——听说雕塑家马夏·克尔贝切克（Masja Krbecek）想见萨布拉·克拉瓦特，谁也不奇怪。
奥塞奇不熟克尔贝切克的雕塑，却领教它的价钱。一座雕像，五十万美元！
“当然，”委员会从容地说。“他是天下头一块牌子。他的东西五十万不算什么。少于二十五万，他连块石子都不踢。”
“你看他会把她塑成戴太阳帽的拓荒妇人吗？牵着小西姆的手，呃？要不就塑在大篷车里。”
萨布拉穿一条素净（有褶）的裙子接待了克尔贝切克。原来他是位安静、有点鼻音的小个子波兰人，戴眼镜，看着更像裁缝——“小”裁缝——而不是雕塑家。他的眼睛在基赫卡街宅子的客厅里逡巡。老木屋已抹上了深暖色的灰泥，颜色同本地红土相仿；华而不实的门廊和尖顶小塔拆了，侧面临街的背面加建了一方宽大的露台和一座平台，隔着厚密的树篱和铁栅。如今，它其实正是扬西当年画的那个样子——多年前萨布拉头一回盖这房子时他提过而没被采纳的那座。老的红木、玻璃和银器回来了，重新压倒了萨布拉当年新置办宅子时用的长毛绒和织锦。开埠以来被瞧不起的老东西，在奥塞奇又成了时鬻。德格拉斯家的银器在，那件无忧小爱神环绕的蛋糕碟在，瓷制的壁炉摆件在，连那么久以前在威奇托布里吉特嬷嬷送她的手织床罩也在。它那深沉的宝蓝色，一丝未褪。
“您在俄克拉何马住得很舒坦，”马夏·克尔贝切克说。他一个音节一个音节、费力地念：俄——克——拉——何——马。
“同您在这一带看过的别处相比，这是个很朴素的家，”萨布拉答。
“这是一个好女人的家，”克尔贝切克干巴巴地说。
萨布拉微微一怔，只规规矩矩道了谢。
“您是国会议员，您是一家大报的主编，您全国知名。你们美国女人，真叫人惊叹。”
萨布拉又谢了他。

===Original===

“Tell me, will you, my dear lady,” he went on, “some of the many interesting things about your life and that of your husband, this Yancey Cravat who so far preceded his time.”
So Sabra told him. Somehow, as she talked, the years rolled back, curtain after curtain, into the past. The Run. Then they were crossing the prairie, there was the first glimpse of the mud wallow that was Osage, the church meeting in the tent, the Pegler murder, the outlaws, the early years of the paper, the Indians, oil. She talked very well in her clear, decisive voice. At his request she showed him the time-yellowed photographs of Yancey, of herself. Krbecek listened. At the end, “It is touching,” he said. “It makes me weep.” Then he kissed her hand and went away, taking one or two of the old photographs with him.
The statue of the Spirit of the Oklahoma Pioneer was unveiled a year later, with terrific ceremonies. It was an heroic figure of Yancey Cravat stepping forward with that light graceful stride in the high-heeled Texas star boots, the skirts of the Prince Albert billowing behind with the vigor of his movements, the sombrero atop the great menacing buffalo head, one beautiful hand resting lightly on the weapon in his two-gun holster. Behind him, one hand just touching his shoulder for support, stumbled the weary, blanketed figure of an Indian.

===Chinese===

“那么，亲爱的夫人，请您给我讲讲，”他接着说，“讲讲您一生中，还有您那位远远走在时代前头的丈夫——这位扬西·克拉瓦特——一生中的种种趣事。”
于是萨布拉讲给他听。不知怎么，她讲着讲着，岁月一幕一幕倒卷回去，回到往昔。抢地。随后他们正穿过草原，第一眼望见那还是泥塘的奥塞奇，帐篷里的教会聚会，佩格勒凶案，亡命徒，报纸的早年，印第安人，石油。她讲得极好，嗓音清晰、决断。应他的请求，她给他看了扬西的照片、她自己的照片，都泛黄了。克尔贝切克听着。末了，他说：“真动人。叫我想流泪。”然后吻了她的手，告辞了，随身带走了一两张旧照片。
一年之后，《俄克拉何马拓荒者之魂》纪念碑隆重揭幕，典礼盛况空前。那是扬西·克拉瓦特的一座英雄像：他正迈着轻盈优雅的步子前行，蹬着高跟的得克萨斯星靴，长礼服的衣摆随他大步的动作向后翻飞，阔边帽戴在那颗威慑的野牛头上，一只漂亮的手轻轻搭在双枪枪套的枪柄上。他身后，一只手刚刚够着他的肩借力，跌跌撞撞跟着一个披毯的、疲惫的印第安人身影。

## XXV / 二十五
===Original===

Sabra Cravat, Congresswoman from Oklahoma, had started a campaign against the disgraceful condition of the new oil towns. With an imposing party of twenty made up of front-page oil men, Senators, Congressmen, and editors, she led the way to Bowlegs, newest and crudest of the new oil strikes.
Cities like Osage were suave enough in a surface way. But what could a state do when oil was forever surging up in unexpected places, bringing the days of the Run back again? At each newly discovered pool there followed the rush and scramble. Another Bret Harte town sprang up on the prairie; fields oozed slimy black; oil rigs clanked; false-front wooden shacks lined a one-street village. Dance halls. Brothels. Gunmen. Brawls. Heat. Flies. Dirt. Crime. The clank of machinery. The roar of traffic boiling over a road never meant for more than a plodding wagon. Nitroglycerin cars bearing their deadly freight. Overalls, corduroys, blue prints, engines. The human scum of each new oil town was like the scum of the Run, but harder, crueler, more wolfish and degraded.
The imposing party, in high-powered motor cars, bumped over the terrible roads, creating a red dust barrage.
“It is all due to our rotten Oklahoma state politics,” Sabra explained to the great Senator from Pennsylvania who sat at her right and the great editor from New York who sat at her left in the big luxurious car. “Our laws are laughed at. The Capitol is rotten with graft. Anything goes. Oklahoma is still a Territory in everything but title. This town of Bowlegs. It’s a throwback to the frontier days of forty years ago﻿—and worse. It’s like the old Cimarron. People who have lived in Osage all their lives don’t know what goes on out here. They don’t care. It’s more oil, more millions. That’s all. Any one of you men, well known as you are, could come out here, put on overalls, and be as lost as though you had vanished in the wilderness.”
The Pennsylvania Senator laughed a plump laugh and with the elbow nearest Sabra made a little movement that would have amounted to a nudge﻿—in anyone but a Senator from Pennsylvania. “What they need out here is a woman Governor﻿—eh, Lippmann!” to the great editor.
Sabra said nothing.

===Chinese===

萨布拉·克拉瓦特，俄克拉何马州联邦众议员，发动了一场讨伐新油田镇肷脏乱象的运动。她领着一支煊赫的队伍，二十人，由上过头版的石油大亨、参议员、众议员和主编们组成，一路开向鲍利格斯（Bowlegs）——新油田里最新、最糙的一处。
像奥塞奇那样的城市，面上已够光鲜了。可是油不断在意想不到的地方涌上来，把抢地年代一次次带回来，一个州又有什么办法？每一处新发现的油藏后面，跟着就是那场哄抢。草原上又冒出一座布雷特·哈特式的镇子；田里渗着黑亮的油；井架哐啷作响；一条街的村子，两边排满假门脸的木板棚屋。舞厅。窑子。枪手。斗殴。热。苍蝇。肷脏。罪恶。机器的哐啷。车流的轰沸，滚过一条本只堪慢牛车走的路。运着致命货物的硝化甘油车。工装裤，灯芯绒，蓝图图纸，发动机。每座新油田镇的人渣，同当年抢地的人渣一模一样，只是更硬，更狠，更豺狼，更败坏。
这支煊赫的队伍乘着大马力汽车，在烂路上颠簸，扬起一道红尘的帷幕。
“这全得怪我们俄克拉何马烂透了的政治，”萨布拉对坐在她右边的宾夕法尼亚大参议员和坐在左边的那位纽约大主编解释，就在那辆宽大的豪华汽车里。“我们的法律让人取笑。州议会烂透了食污。什么事都于得出来。俄克拉何马除了名分，处处还是一片领地。这座鲍利格斯镇，是四十年前沿边岁月的倒退——而且更糟。就像当年的西马龙。在奥塞奇住了一辈子的人，不知道外头这些地方是什么样子。他们也不管。又是油，又是几百万。就这么回事。你们哪一位，尽管名满天下，到了这儿，套上一条工装裤，就跟消失在荒野里一样没了影。”
宾夕法尼亚参议员胖胖地笑了，用靠萨布拉这边的那只肘子做了一个小小的动作——在旁人身上这就是撞一下胳膊肘了，但宾夕法尼亚参议员不会。“这地方需要的就是一位女州长——呢，李普曼！”他对那位大主编说。
萨布拉没说话。

===Original===

On the drive out from Osage they stopped for lunch in an older oil town hotel dining room﻿—a surprisingly good lunch, the Senators and editors were glad to find, with a tender steak, and little green onions, and near beer, and cheese, and coffee served in great thick cups, hot and strong and refreshing. The waitress was deft and friendly: a tall angular woman with something frank and engaging about the two circles of vermilion on the parchment of her withered cheeks.
“How are you, Nettie?” Sabra said to her.
“I’m grand, Mis’ Cravat. How’s all your folks?”
The Senator from Ohio winked at Sabra. “You’re a politician, all right.”
Arrived at Bowlegs, Sabra showed them everything, pitilessly. The dreadful town lay in the hot June sun, a scarred thing, flies buzzing over it, the oil drooling down its face, a slimy stream. A one-street wooden shanty town, like the towns of the old Territory days, but more sordid. A red-cheeked young Harvard engineer was their official guide: an engaging boy in bone-rimmed glasses and a very blue shirt that made his pink cheeks pinker. That is what I wanted my Cim to be, Sabra thought with a great wrench at her heart. I mustn’t think of that now.
The drilling of the oil. The workmen’s shanties. The trial of a dance-hall girl in the one-room pine shack that served as courtroom. The charge, nonpayment of rent. The little room, stifling, stinking, was already crowded. Men and women filled the doorway, lounged in the windows. The judge was a yellow-faced fellow with a cud of tobacco in his cheek, and a Sears-Roebuck catalogue and a single law book on a shelf as his library. It was a trial by jury. The jurors were nine in number, their faces a rogues’ gallery. There had happened to be nine men loafing near by. It might have been less or more. Bowlegs did not consider these fine legal points. They wore overalls and shirts. The defendant was a tiny rat-faced girl in a soiled green dress that parodied the fashions, a pathetic green poke bonnet, down-at-heel shoes, and a great run in her stocking. Her friends were there﻿—a dozen or more dance-hall girls in striped overalls and jockey caps or knee-length gingham dresses with sashes. Their ages ranged from sixteen to nineteen, perhaps. It was incredible that life, in those few years, could have etched that look on their faces.
The girls were charming, hospitable.

===Chinese===

从奥塞奇出来的路上，他们在一座老油田镇的旅馆餐厅停下吃午饭——参议员们和主编们惊喜地发现，这顿饭好得出奇：嫩的牛排，小绿洋葱，近似啤酒，干酪，还有盛在厚厚大杯里的咖啡，滚烫，酽，提神。女招待利落而亲热：一个高挑、棱角分明的女人，干枯的双颊那片羊皮纸上两点朱红，透着直爽讨喜。
“你好啊，内蒂？”萨布拉对她说。
“好得很哪，克拉瓦特太太。您一家子都好？”
俄亥俄的参议员朝萨布拉挤挤眼。“您真是个政治家。”
到了鲍利格斯，萨布拉毫不留情地把一切指给他们看。这座可怕的镇子躺在六月的烈日下，一个伤痕累累的东西，苍蝇嗡嗡地罩着它，油涎顺着它的脸淌下来，黏黏一道。一条街的木板棚屋镇，同旧领地年月那些镇子一样，只是更腌臜。一位红脸膛的年轻哈佛工程师做他们的向导：一个讨人喜欢的小伙子，骨框眼镜，一件很蓝的衬衫，衬得粉红的脸更加粉红。这正是我想让西姆成为的样子，萨布拉想，心里猛地一絞。这会儿不能想这个。
打井。工人的棚屋。在一间充作法庭的单间松木棚里，一场对舞厅姑娘的审判。罪名：欠租。小屋又闷又臭，已经挤满了。男女堵在门口，倚在窗上。法官是个黄脸汉子，腮帮子里一块嚼烟，一架西尔斯-罗伯克商品目录和一本孤零零的法律书立在架板上，就是他的全部藏书。这是陪审团审判。陪审员九名，一张张脸像流氓陈列馆。只因当时附近恰好有九个闲汉。多几个少几个也难说。鲍利格斯不讲究这些法律细目。他们穿工装裤和衬衫。被告是个 tiny 鼠脸的小个子姑娘，一身脏了的绿裙，滑稽地模仿着时新样式，一顶可怜的绿色遮阳帽，鞋跟磨塌了，袜子上抽了一大股丝。她的朋友们都在——十几个舞厅姑娘，穿条纹工装裤戴赛马帽的，或系腰带的及膝格裙的。年纪约在十六到十九之间。叫人不敢相信，生活竟能在那短短几年里，就把那副神情蚀刻上她们的脸。
姑娘们可亲，好客。

===Original===

They made way for the imposing visitors. “Come on in,” they said. “How-do!”﻿—like friendly children. The midafternoon sun was pitiless on their sick eyes, their bad skin, their unhealthy hair. Clustered behind the rude bench on which the jury sat, the girls, from time to time, leaned a sociable elbow on a juryman’s shoulder, occasionally enlivening the judicial proceedings by a spirited comment uttered in defense of their sister, and spoken in the nearby ear or aloud, for the benefit of the close-packed crowd.
“She never done no such thing!”
“He’s a damn liar, an’ I can prove it.”
No one, least of all the tobacco-chewing judge, appeared to find these girlish informalities at all unusual in the legal conduct of the case.
In the corner of the little room was a kind of pen made of wooden slats, like a sizable chicken coop, and in it, on the floor, lay a man.
“What’s he there for?” Sabra asked one of the girls. “What is that?”
“That’s Bill. He’s in jail. He shot a man last night, and he’s up for carrying concealed weapons. It ain’t allowed.”
“I’m going to talk to him,” said Sabra. And crossed the room, through the crowd. The jurors had just filed out. They repaired to a draw at the side of the road to make their finding. Two or three of the dance-hall girls, squatted on the floor, were talking to Bill through the bars. They asked Sabra her name, and she told them, and they gave her their own. Toots. Peewee. Bee.
The face of the boy on the floor was battered and blood-caked. There was a festering sore on his left hand, and the hand and arm were swollen and angry looking.
“You were carrying a concealed weapon?” Sabra asked, squatting there with the girls. A Senator or two and an editor were just behind her.
An injured look softened Bill’s battered features. He pouted like a child. “No, ma’am. I run the dance hall, see? And I was standing in the middle of the floor, working, and I had the gun right in my hand. Anybody could see. I wasn’t carrying no concealed weapon.”
The jury filed back. Not guilty. The rat-faced girl’s shyster lawyer said something in her ear. She spoke in a dreadful raucous voice, simpering.
“I sure thank you, gents.”
The dance-hall girls cheered feebly.
Out of that fetid air into the late afternoon blaze. “The dance halls open about nine,” Sabra said. “We’ll wait for that. In the meantime I’ll show you their rooms.

===Chinese===

她们给煊赫的来客让路。“进来呀，”她们说。“好啊！”——像一群友善的孩子。下下午的太阳毫不留情地照着她们病恹恹的眼睛、糟糕的皮肤、不健康的头发。她们簇在陪审团坐的那条粗板凳后面，不时把一只交际的胳膊肘搭上哪位陪审员的肩，偶尔用一句护卫姐妹的火辣议论给司法程序添些生气——凑在耳边说，或者干脆大声说，让挤得紧紧的人群都听见。
“她才没干过那种事！”
“他是个天大的骗子，我拿得出证据。”
没有谁——尤其是那位嚼烟的法官——觉得这些姑娘家的随便插话，在这案子的司法程序里有什么不寻常。
小屋的角落里有一个木板条钉的围栏，像只可观的鸡笼，里面地板上躺着一个人。
“他在那里干什么？”萨布拉问其中一个姑娘。“那是什么？”
“那是比尔。他蹲监狱呢。他昨晚上开枪打了人，这会儿吃官司，罪名是私藏武器。这不允许。”
“我要同他说说话，”萨布拉说。她穿过屋子，穿过人群。陪审员们正鱼贯而出，到路边的沟里去合议。两三个舞厅姑娘蹲在地上，隔着栅栏同比尔说话。她们问萨布拉的名字，她说了，她们也把自己的报给她。图茨。皮维。小蜂。
地板上那小伙子的脸被打烂了，血结成饼。他左手上有一处溃烂的疮，手和小臂肿着，颜色凶红。
“你私藏了武器？”萨布拉同姑娘们一起蹲在那里问。一两位参议员和一位主编就在她身后。
一副受了大委屈的神情软化了比尔那张打烂的脸。他像孩子一样撅起嘴。“没有，太太。我是开舞厅的，明白吗？我当时正站在场子当中，干活呢，枪就在我手里攢着。谁都看得见。我可没私藏什么武器。”
陪审团鱼贯而回。无罪。鼠脸姑娘的讼棍律师在她耳边说了句什么。她开口了，嗓音可怕地嘶哑，假模假式地笑着。
“真多谢各位先生了。”
舞厅姑娘们有气无力地欢呼了一阵。
从那恶浊的空气里出来，走进傍晚的白光。“舞厅九点钟开门，”萨布拉说。“我们等一等。趁这工夫我带你们看看她们的屋子。”

===Original===

Their rooms﻿—” she looked about for the fresh-cheeked Harvard boy. “Why, where﻿—”
“There’s some kind of excitement,” said the New York editor. “People have been running and shouting. Over there in that field we visited a while ago. Here comes our young friend now. Perhaps he’ll tell us.”
The Harvard boy’s color was higher still. He was breathing fast. He had been running. His eyes shone behind the bone-rimmed spectacles.
“Well, folks, we’ll never have a narrower squeak than that.”
“What?”
“They put fifty quarts in the Gypsy pool but before she got down the oil came up﻿—”
“Quarts of what?” interrupted an editorial voice.
“Oh﻿—excuse me﻿—quarts of nitroglycerin.”
“My God!”
“It’s in a can, you know. A thing like a can. It never had a chance to explode down there. It just shot up with the gas and oil. If it had hit the ground everything for miles around would have been shot to hell and all of us killed. But he caught it. They say he just ran back like an outfielder and gauged it with his eye while it was up in the air, and ran to where it would fall, and caught it in his two arms, like a baby, right on his chest. It didn’t explode. But he’s dying. Chest all caved in. They’ve sent for the ambulance.”
“Who? Who’s he?”
“I don’t know his real name. He’s an old bum that’s been around the field, doing odd jobs and drinking. They say he used to be quite a fellow in Oklahoma in his day. Picturesque pioneer or something. Some call him old Yance and I’ve heard others call him Sim or Simeon or﻿—”
Sabra began to run across the road.
“Mrs. Cravat! You mustn’t﻿—where are you going?”
She ran on, across the oil-soaked field and the dirt, in her little buckled high-heeled slippers. She did not even know that she was running. The crowd was dense around some central object. They formed a wall﻿—roustabouts, drillers, tool dressers, shooters, pumpers. They were gazing down at something on the ground.
“Let me by! Let me by!” They fell back before this white-faced woman with the white hair.
He lay on the ground, a queer, crumpled, broken figure. She flung herself on the oil-soaked earth beside him and lifted the magnificent head gently, so that it lay cushioned by her arm. A little purplish bubble rose to his lips, and she wiped it away with her fine white handkerchief, and another rose to take its place.
“Yancey! Yancey!”

===Chinese===

“她们的屋子——”她四下找那个红脸膛的哈佛小伙。“呀，人呢——”
“那边好像出了什么事，”纽约主编说。人们一直在跑，在喊。就在咱们刚才去过的那片场子。咱们的年轻朋友来了。也许他能告诉咱们。
哈佛小伙的脸更红了。他喘得急。他一路跑来的。骨框眼镜后面，眼睛闪着光。
“哎呀，各位，咱们这辈子再没有比这更险的了。”
“怎么了？”
“他们往吉普赛油层下了五十夸特，可它还没到底，油就上来了——”
“多少什么？”一个编辑的声音打断他。
“哦——恕我直言——五十夸特硝化甘油。”
“我的上帝！”
“它是装在一个罐子里的，您知道。一个罐子似的东西。它在底下根本没来得及炸。它直接随天然气和油一起喷上来了。它要是落了地，方圆多少英里全得崩上天，咱们谁也活不成。可他接住了。他们说，他像个外野手似的往后跑，眼睛在半空中就把落点量好了，跑到它要落的地方，两条胳膊一接——像接个婴儿，正正搂在胸口。它没炸。可他不行了。胸口全塌了。已经派人去叫救护车了。”
“谁？他是谁？”
“真名我不知道。是个老流浪汉，一直在油田上转，打打零工，喝喝酒。据说他当年在俄克拉何马也是号人物。什么拓荒怪杰之类的。有人叫他老扬斯，我还听人叫他西姆、西蒙或者——”
萨布拉拔腿跑过了马路。
“克拉瓦特太太！您不能——您去哪儿？”
她跑着，跑过浸透油的土地和尘土，穿着她那双缀带扣的高跟小拖鞋。她甚至不知道自己在跑。人群密密地围着什么东西。他们围成一道墙——杂工，钻工，修钻头的，放炮的，泵工。他们都低头望着地上的什么。
“让我过去！让我过去！”在这个白发、白脸的女人面前，他们退开了。
他躺在地上，一个古怪的、皱缩的、破碎的身影。她扑倒在他身边浸透油的地上，轻轻抬起那颗堂皇的头，让它枕在自己臂弯里。一点紫红的水泡浮上他的唇，她用她那方精细的白手绢拭去，又有一点浮上来，接着那一点。
“扬西！扬西！”

===Original===

He opened his eyes﻿—those ocean-gray eyes with the long curling lashes like a beautiful girl’s. She had thought of them often and often, in an agony of pain. Glazed now, unseeing.
Then, dying, they cleared. His lips moved. He knew her. Even then, dying, he must speak in measured verse.
“ ‘Wife and mother﻿—you stainless woman﻿—hide me﻿—hide me in your love!’ ”
She had never heard a line of it. She did not know that this was Peer Gynt, humbled before Solveig. The once magnetic eyes glazed, stared; were eyes no longer.
She closed them, gently. She forgave him everything. Quite simply, all unknowing, she murmured through her tears the very words of Solveig.
“Sleep, my boy, my dearest boy.”

===Chinese===

他睁开眼睛——那双海灰色的眼睛，长长的、鬈曲的睫毛像一位美丽的姑娘。多少次，她在痛苦的熬煎里想起过它们。此刻已浑浊，无神。
随后，在死亡里，它们清了一清。他的唇动了。他认出了她。即便此时，弥留之际，他也必要以韵律齐整的诗句开口。
“‘妻子和母亲——你无玷的女人——把我藏起——在你的爱里把我藏起！’”
她从未听过这一行。她不知道这是《培尔·金特》，是匍匐在索尔薇格面前的培尔。那双曾经摄人的眼睛浑浊了，直瞪着；已不再是眼睛。
她轻轻合上它们。她饶恕了他的一切。全然浑然不觉地，她噙着泪，喃喃说出的，恰是索尔薇格的话。
“睡吧，我的孩子，我最亲爱的孩子。”
